# KNOWLEDGE BASE — Stanley Cavell, *The World Viewed: Reflections on the Ontology of Film* (Enlarged Edition, 1979)

> Internal knowledge base. Source: OCR of the Harvard University Press **Enlarged Edition** PDF (277 PDF pp.), read against the book's own pagination (PDF page − 24 = book page in the Arabic-numbered body). Page numbers in brackets **[p.N]** refer to the Enlarged-Edition book pagination. Quotations are **verbatim** from the text (OCR lightly normalized for obvious scanning glitches); where a passage is paraphrased it is marked **[paraphrase]**. The marker **[재구성]** flags an *interpretive reconstruction* added by this note where Cavell's own text is elliptical — his words and the reconstruction are always kept typographically distinct.
>
> **Scope & stance.** This note is a *standalone* digest of Cavell only. It deliberately makes **no cross-links** to the other materials in this project (the Walton transparency note, the Bazin–Cavell automatism memo): the integration is reserved for separate, later work. Coverage is **uniform and full** — every chapter (Foreword, Preface, Ch 1–19) and the whole of the 1979 essay "More of The World Viewed" are digested at the same depth.

---

## 0. Bibliographic frame

- **Author:** Stanley Cavell (b. 1926), American philosopher (Harvard), working at the intersection of ordinary-language philosophy (Austin, Wittgenstein), moral perfectionism, Romanticism, and the arts. *The World Viewed* is his only sustained book on film and a founding text of philosophical (as opposed to semiotic or psychoanalytic) film theory.
- **Two texts in one volume.** (i) *The World Viewed*, first published **1971**; (ii) the **Enlarged Edition** (Harvard UP, **1979**), which reprints the 1971 text *unaltered* (errors of memory included, deliberately) and adds a new **Foreword to the Enlarged Edition** and a long closing essay, **"More of The World Viewed"** (first published in the *Georgia Review*, **1974**, as a reply to critics). Copyright dates on the volume: © 1971, 1974, 1979.
- **Subtitle / self-description:** "Reflections on the Ontology of Film." Cavell calls the book "a kind of **metaphysical memoir** — not the story of a period of my life but an account of the conditions it has satisfied" [p.xix].
- **Dedication:** "To my mother and father."
- **Epigraph:** Thoreau, *Walden*: "Why do precisely these objects which we behold make a world?" (The book's title and epigraph encode its question: what is it for film to give us *a world* / *the world*?)
- **Structure:** a Foreword (1979) and Preface (1971) + **19 short untitled-by-number chapters** (pp. 3–161) + the essay **"More of The World Viewed"** (pp. 162–230) with substantive endnotes (pp. 231–246) + index. The 19 chapters move in three waves: **(A) the ontology** of the photographic image and the screen (chs. 2–4, with ch. 1 setting the autobiographical method); **(B) the "myths"** — film's classical types, genres, and their exhaustion (chs. 5–10); **(C) the medium, automatism, and modernism** (chs. 11–19). "More of The World Viewed" then re-states and defends the whole, centrally against Alexander Sesonske.
- **Cavell's principal interlocutors inside the book:**
  - *Realist film theory he starts from and amends:* **Erwin Panofsky** ("the medium of the movies is physical reality as such") and **André Bazin** ("cinema is committed to communicate only by way of what is real"; the "myth of total cinema"; the photographic "mold/impression"). Cavell honors but corrects both: not "reality as such" but *projections of* reality, and *our absence from it*.
  - *Modernism (the decisive influence):* **Michael Fried** and **Clement Greenberg** — the vocabulary of *medium*, *acknowledgment*, *flatness/the support*, *presentness*. Cavell: "I would have written a little book about film if Michael Fried and I had never met. But it would not have been this one" [p.xxv].
  - *The "myths":* **Charles Baudelaire**, *The Painter of Modern Life* (modernity, the Dandy, the Woman, the Military Man, cosmetics, fashion) read as an unwitting "wish for photography."
  - *Philosophical spine:* **Heidegger** (world, *Dasein*, "knowing as access," "willingness for anxiety," *Days of Heaven*'s "radiance"), **Wittgenstein** (criteria, "seeing-as"/physiognomy, acknowledgment), **Kant** ("existence is not a predicate"), **Rousseau** (the *Letter to d'Alembert* and the level of "viewing"), **Tolstoy** (*What Is Art?*), and the post-Kantian diagnosis of **skepticism / estrangement** (Hegel, Marx, Kierkegaard, Nietzsche).
  - *Film criticism he claims kinship with:* **Robert Warshow**, **Walter Benjamin**, Bazin; also Agee, Farber, Kael, Tyler, Sarris.
  - *The critic answered in "More of The World Viewed":* **Alexander Sesonske** (objections re cartoons, types vs. characters, acting, "the myth of immaculate conception," fictionality).

---

## 1. The central project & master thesis

**The question.** Not "what is good in film?" but **"What is film?"** — pursued as an *ontology*: what kind of thing is a photograph, a screen, a projected world, a screened human being? Cavell's wager (stated in ch. 1) is that the question of film's **nature** and the question of its **importance** are "grammatically related" — to say what film *is* is to say why it has mattered to us [pp.3–4].

**The ontological core (chs. 2–4).** The basis of the medium is photographic, and "a photograph is *of* reality or nature" [p.16] — so the governing question becomes **"What happens to reality when it is projected and screened?"** [p.16]. Cavell's answers, in order:

1. **Automatism.** "Photographs are not hand-made; they are manufactured. And what is manufactured is an *image of the world*" [p.21]. Photography "**overcame subjectivity** in a way undreamed of by painting … **by automatism, by removing the human agent from the task of reproduction**" [p.23]. Where painting must *acknowledge* the modern self's isolation, the camera mechanically *bypasses* it.
2. **Presentness and absence.** "Painting … accepts the recession of the world. **Photography maintains the presentness of the world by accepting our absence from it.** The reality in a photograph is present to me while I am not present to it; and a world I know, and see, but to which I am nevertheless not present … is **a world past**" [p.23]. This is the modern wish photography satisfies: "the human wish, intensifying in the West since the Reformation, **to escape subjectivity and metaphysical isolation**" [p.21].
3. **"A painting is a world; a photograph is of the world"** [p.24]. Test: you can always ask what lies *behind* or *beyond* an object in a photograph (it has an answer in reality); the question is senseless for a painting, whose world "finds its limits" at the frame.
4. **The screen screens.** "A screen is a barrier. What does the silver screen screen? It screens me from the world it holds — that is, makes me invisible. And it screens that world from me — that is, screens its existence from me" [p.24]. And the Kantian hinge: **"That the projected world does not exist (now) is its only difference from reality. … Existence is not a predicate"** [p.24].

**Film as a moving image of skepticism.** Because film gives us a world *complete without us, present to us, to which we are not present*, it *realizes* the structure of philosophical skepticism (our conviction in, yet separation from, the external world and other minds). In "More of The World Viewed" Cavell names it: **film is "a moving image of skepticism"** — our senses are "satisfied of reality while reality does not exist," because "**viewing it is all it takes**" [≈pp.188–189]. The wish film satisfies is "the wish for the magical reproduction of the world by enabling us **to view it unseen**" [p.101]; *viewing unseen* is "exactly the price of skepticism" [≈p.196].

**Two senses of "automatism" (the book's pivot term — ch. 14).** Cavell deliberately fuses (a) the **photographic** automatism — film's mechanical, hand-free reproduction of the world — with (b) the **modernist** sense of *an* automatism — an inherited convention/form/"a something" (the sonata, the fugue, a genre) on which a tradition lives and which an artist relies upon. His thesis: film **was *given* its automatism** rather than having to invent one, which is why it could be "the one live traditional art" for so long — and why, when its inherited automatisms (the classical "media") exhausted themselves, film entered **modernism**, where each work must now discover/establish its automatisms anew.

**The modernist turn (chs. 10–19).** The classical Hollywood **"myths"** (the public world of the Military Man, the private mystery of the Woman, the isolated Dandy/Western hero) and the **"media"** (cycles, genres, types) that secured our *conviction in presentness* have ended; "within the last decade film has been moving into the modernist environment inhabited for generations by the other major arts" [p.60]. The medium of film is therefore not the camera or celluloid but **"a succession of automatic world projections"** [≈p.72] realized through *media* it discovers. In modernism a **technique becomes an *assertion*** (an acknowledgment) only through the *integrity* of the particular work, never automatically (ch. 18); and the final thing the medium must **acknowledge** is **silence** — the world's silence to us, and the silence native to film that was never really lost when film learned to talk (ch. 19). The book's closing formula identifies the skeptical condition with **immortality**: "a world complete without me which is present to me" [≈p.160].

**One-line core:** *Film is the moving image of skepticism — an automatic projection of the world's presentness that confirms, and consoles us for, our absence from it; its art lies in acknowledging the conditions (automatism, the screen, the type, color, silence) of that absence.*

---

## 2. Orientation — Cavell's method and the shape of the book

**Method 1 — writing "out of memory."** Both texts were written "before I became involved with the apparatus of film study" (moviolas that freeze/repeat a film); Cavell "wrote primarily out of the memory of films" [p.ix]. Hence the book's signature problem of **variable quotability**: "in speaking of a moment … from a film we … cannot quote the thing we are speaking of" [p.ix] — so criticism bears "an immediate and tremendous burden," and one risks not knowing "what we are thinking about" [p.x]. He keeps his factual mis-memories in print on principle: "I am as interested in **how a memory went wrong** as in why the memories that are right occur" [p.xxiv].

**Method 2 — the cinematic circle.** The book's guiding doctrine (stated in the 1979 Foreword): "giving significance to and placing significance in specific possibilities … (or call them **elements**; I sometimes still call them **automatisms**) of the physical medium of film are the fundamental acts of … the director … and the critic"; and "what constitutes an 'element' of the medium of film **is not knowable prior to these discoveries** of direction and of criticism. This reciprocity between element and significance I would like to call **the cinematic circle**" [pp.xiii–xiv]. Corollary: "**the only instruments that could provide data for a theory of film are the procedures of criticism**" [p.12]; "**reading** is not an alternative to seeing but … an effort to detail a way of seeing something more clearly," and is also "an argument, something requiring a response" [pp.xii–xiii].

**On the book's difficulty (the keynote of "More of The World Viewed").** Cavell concedes he wrote "a difficult book, a sometimes incomprehensible book," but holds the obscurity to be "**not external to what I wished most fervently to say about film**" — his prose deliberately interrupts the reader's *standing responses* to film so that film's actual claims on us can surface [pp.162–163].

**The three movements (how to navigate the digest below):**

- **(A) Ontology — chs. 1–4.** Method (ch. 1); the photograph vs. the recording and vs. the painting (ch. 2); the screen, the frame, and "a photograph is of the world" (ch. 3); the audience's mechanical absence and the screen *star/type* (ch. 4).
- **(B) Myths and their end — chs. 5–13.** Types/cycles/genres (ch. 5); origin and the "myth of total cinema" (ch. 6); Baudelaire and film's iconography (chs. 7–9: the Woman, the Military Man, the Dandy/Western hero); the end of the myths and film's entry into modernism (ch. 10); the *medium vs. media* distinction (ch. 11); the world as mortal — age, nostalgia, youth (ch. 12); the long meditation on color and the "world as a whole" (ch. 13).
- **(C) Automatism and modernism — chs. 14–19.** Automatism in its two senses (ch. 14); the Excursus on modernist painting (ch. 15); exhibition, self-reference vs. *acknowledgment* (ch. 16); the camera's implication / outsideness (ch. 17); assertions in techniques (ch. 18); the acknowledgment of silence (ch. 19).
- **(D) The 1979 re-statement — "More of The World Viewed."** A point-by-point defense (vs. Sesonske) that re-grounds reality's role (photographed/projected/screened, not "as such"), distinguishes the *mythological* from the *fictional*, recasts film's *delayed modernism*, and closes with an extended act of criticism on one frame of Renoir's *Rules of the Game*.

---

## 3. Chapter-by-chapter digest

*(Faithful digest of Cavell's moves, in order, with verbatim quotes and page numbers; **[재구성]** = interpretive reconstruction supplied where the text is elliptical.)*

### Foreword to the Enlarged Edition [book pp. ix–xviii]

**Project:** A 1979 retrospective in which Cavell reflects on *The World Viewed* eleven years after its composition — defending its method of writing "out of the memory of films," confessing and re-reading several of his own factual errors of memory, articulating the central methodological doctrine he calls the "cinematic circle," and closing with a programmatic reading of Malick's *Days of Heaven* that leads into a meditation on film's uncertain status as a subject of serious (philosophical, academic) study.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
Cavell is gratified that "More of The World Viewed" (the new material) will share covers with the parent work [p.ix]. He stresses the conditions of composition: both were written before he became involved with "the apparatus of film study" — moviolas slowing, repeating, or freezing a film — so he "wrote primarily out of the memory of films" [p.ix]. Between parent and offspring he had begun taking notes during/after screenings, which altered his preparation and thus what he could write [p.ix]. He was always aware his descriptions of passages were "liable to contain errors, of content and of sequence," and he deliberately declines to correct them in reprinting — he wants neither to disguise the spirit of the work nor to disguise the need for two missing studies: a study of what may be *remembered* in any art, and a study of how using an analyzing machine may *modify* one's experience of a film [p.ix]. The absence of both studies keeps unassessed the cardinal fact: in speaking of a moment from a film, "we... cannot quote the thing we are speaking of" [p.ix]. The danger is not merely that others may not know our reference but that "we ourselves might not know what we are thinking about" [p.x]. This puts an "immediate and tremendous burden" on the capacity for critical description [p.x]. The matter of "variable quotability" raises the question of what constitutes "remembering a work," which raises in turn what constitutes "knowing a work" — and leads to his prior notion (from "The Avoidance of Love") of "the necessity to return to a work, in fact or in memory," which he hits off as "having to remember" [p.x].

He then makes good on the Preface's brave confession ("a few faulty memories will not... shake my conviction... since I am as interested in how a memory went wrong as in why the memories that are right occur") by re-examining specific errors [p.x]. (1) He had described the ending of *Mr. Smith Goes to Washington* with Jean Arthur kneeling beside Mr. Smith on the Senate floor in a "Pietà" posture — but she never leaves the gallery; his words captured a genuine Christ reference in the film, yet he "transposed the imagery" from another film (he guesses Lang's *The Return of Frank James*) [pp.x–xi]. (2) "Self-confident errors" about Katharine Hepburn in *The Philadelphia Story* (the calla-lilies line, which belongs to *Stage Door*) and Cary Grant's line in *His Girl Friday*; he reconstructs plausible *displacements* of real gags, but admits "no explanation short of a dream" accounts for the *Stage Door* reference, and half-seriously wagers the line was left in some prints [pp.xi]. (3) A "more galling error" in *Rules of the Game*: he misremembered which character (the gentle homosexual vs. the heterosexual cad) attempts the final gossip with the general [pp.xi–xii].

These errors, he notes, have "not seemed to vitiate the interpretations based upon them," because while images were tampered with, "the ideas and feelings in them have not been" [p.xii]. **[재구성: 카벨의 암묵적 논리 — 비평적 진실(idea/feeling)과 사실적 기억(image)은 분리 가능하며, 전자가 후자의 오류에도 살아남을 수 있다는 것이 그의 방어선이다.]** A "more problematic case" follows: he twice claimed Schumacher's gun was strapped to his back during the tracking shot of the beaters, and built on this the metaphor of gun-as-camera (Schumacher "not so much guiding the action as following it, tracking it, filming it") — but the gun is in fact *absent*, even conspicuously out of its expected place [p.xii]. An unfriendly critic would conclude we should drop talk of "reading" films and speak only of "seeing" them [p.xii]. Cavell's friendly reply: the mistaken memory was itself a *response* to an assertion about the gun — to its absence/displacement [pp.xii–xiii]. "Reading is not an alternative to seeing but... an effort to detail a way of seeing something more clearly" (he glosses "read" via its root in a word for *advising*) [p.xii]. Indeed, the gun-camera's very absence may declare "all the more emphatically" that it is in its proper place — *shooting* the sequence — consistent with his claim that Octave-Renoir's absence from the concluding scene means he has taken his place behind the camera and "declares his responsibility for what has happened" [p.xiii]. He concedes he was "not then prepared to think its consequences through": that the most human of filmmakers still commands "the murderous power of the movie camera," his humanity measured not only by identification with romance's heroes but with its villains [p.xiii]. A reading is to be checked for accuracy, but "should also be thought of as an argument, something requiring a response" [p.xiii].

He then states the book's guiding theme, now explicit and "guiding in all my subsequent thinking about film": that giving significance to, and placing significance in, "specific possibilities and necessities (or call them elements; I sometimes still call them automatisms) of the physical medium of film are the fundamental acts of, respectively, the director of a film and the critic (or audience) of film"; *and* that what constitutes an "element" of the medium "is not knowable prior to these discoveries of direction and of criticism" [p.xiii]. This reciprocity between element and significance he names "the cinematic circle"; exploring this circle is exploring the medium of film [pp.xiii–xiv]. This specifies the "tremendous burden": critical description must let "the medium of film as such and the events of a given film at each moment... be understood in terms of one another" [p.xiv]. Because in the present pieces he mostly reads only *fragments*, he appends a bibliographical note of later writings that attend to "films as wholes" [p.xiv].

He concludes "not exactly with a reading of a film fragment but rather with a fragmentary reading of a whole film, or rather with a prescription of such a reading": Malick's *Days of Heaven* [p.xiv]. Anyone interested wishes to understand "what its extremities of beauty are in service of" [p.xiv]. No one could explore its subject without confidence that one's capacity "for extracting beauty from nature and from the photographic projection or displacement of nature is inexhaustible" — a confidence at once in nature's and film's capacities; this "ranging of confidence" is itself exhilarating and "must somehow be part of the subject of the film" [pp.xiv–xv]. He proposes the subject as one in which "the works and the emotions and the entanglements of human beings are at every moment reduced to insignificance by the casual rounds of earth and sky" [p.xv]. The film "does indeed contain a metaphysical vision of the world," yet one feels one "has never quite seen the scene of human existence... quite realized this way on film before" [pp.xv]. The images' "formal radiance" realizes sentences from Heidegger's *What Is Called Thinking?* — quoted on the duality of Being and beings, the idea as "the face whereby a given something shows its form, looks at us," participation, and presence as "luminous appearance... radiant self-manifestation" [pp.xv]. (He notes Malick had translated Heidegger's *The Essence of Reasons*) [p.xv]. If Malick transposed such thoughts, he could have done so only by discovering, or discovering how to acknowledge, "a fundamental fact of film's photographic basis: that objects participate in the photographic presence of themselves; they participate in the re-creation of themselves on film; they are essential in the making of their appearances" [pp.xv–xvi]. Objects on a screen are "inherently reflexive... self-referential, reflecting upon their physical origins"; "their presence refers to their absence, their location in another place" [p.xvi]. If human beings are thereby "reduced in significance, or crushed by the fact of beauty left vacant," perhaps it is because in trying to "take dominion over the world, or in aestheticizing it... they are refusing their participation with it" [p.xvi].

He offers this Malick case (right or wrong) not only as an "extreme illustration of the unpredictability of what we may have to count as an element of the medium" but as a case suggesting "the unpredictability of the audience for what may be taken as the study of film" [p.xvi]. The "poignance" of the audience question emerges against the "accelerating professionalization of the study of film" [p.xvi]. To ask insiders (making film academically respectable) to think about Heidegger is to ask them to take on views "inherently embattled within English-speaking intellectual culture"; to ask outsiders is to ask them to grant film "the status of a subject that invites and rewards philosophical speculation, on a par with the great arts" [pp.xvi–xvii]. **[재구성: 양쪽 모두에게 부담이라는 구조 — 카벨은 영미 분석철학 문화 내에서 하이데거(대륙철학)를 끌어들이는 것의 정치적 비용과, 영화에 고급예술 지위를 부여하는 것의 비용을 이중으로 지적한다.]** This is no small matter, for (as Warshow and Benjamin "more or less put it") "to accept film as an art will require a modification of the concept of art" [pp.xvi–xvii]. Even granting film is as brilliant as jazz, jazz can postpone the high-art question because it relates to music as a whole, some of which is "definitively high"; whereas "film has only itself for direct reference" — distinctions of high/low or major/minor must be drawn "within the body of film itself, with no issue postponable and none definitive until someone says otherwise" [p.xvii]. He closes by asking whether this "status of uncertainties" is really "less creative in principle than the status of academic certainties accorded the remaining arts whose names are great" [p.xvii]. A bibliographical note follows (on "Leopards in Connecticut," "Pursuits of Happiness," "What Becomes of Things on Film?," and the Makavejev essay), plus thanks [pp.xvii–xviii].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "I wrote primarily out of the memory of films" [p.ix]
- "in speaking of a moment or sequence from a film we, as we might put it, cannot quote the thing we are speaking of." [p.ix]
- "others might then not be sure what it is we are referring to, but that we ourselves might not know what we are thinking about." [p.x]
- "I am as interested in how a memory went wrong as in why the memories that are right occur when they do." [p.x]
- "while certain images may have been tampered with, the ideas and feelings in them have not been." [p.xii]
- "Reading is not an alternative to seeing but (as its root in a word for advising suggests) an effort to detail a way of seeing something more clearly, an interpretation of how things look and why they appear as, and in the order, they do." [p.xii]
- "A reading, like any recitation, is by all means to be checked for its accuracy. It should also be thought of as an argument, something requiring a response." [p.xiii]
- "giving significance to and placing significance in specific possibilities and necessities (or call them elements; I sometimes still call them automatisms) of the physical medium of film are the fundamental acts of, respectively, the director of a film and the critic (or audience) of film" [p.xiii]
- "what constitutes an 'element' of the medium of film is not knowable prior to these discoveries of direction and of criticism. This reciprocity between element and significance I would like to call the cinematic circle." [pp.xiii–xiv]
- "objects participate in the photographic presence of themselves; they participate in the re-creation of themselves on film; they are essential in the making of their appearances." [pp.xv–xvi]
- "Their presence refers to their absence, their location in another place." [p.xvi]
- "to accept film as an art will require a modification of the concept of art." [p.xvii]
- "film has only itself for direct reference; distinctions between high and low, or between major and minor, if they are to be drawn, must be drawn within the body of film itself" [pp.xvii]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- *Mr. Smith Goes to Washington* (Capra) — site of the Pietà mis-memory (Jean Arthur never on the Senate floor); image transposed, likely from Lang's *The Return of Frank James*.
- *The Philadelphia Story* / *Stage Door* (Hepburn) and *His Girl Friday* / *The Awful Truth* (Cary Grant, Ralph Bellamy) — "self-confident errors" of displaced lines/gags.
- *Rules of the Game* (Renoir) — the Schumacher gun-camera error; Octave-Renoir's absence = responsibility behind the camera; the filmmaker's command of "the murderous power of the movie camera."
- *Days of Heaven* (Malick) — programmatic "prescription of a reading"; objects' participation in their photographic re-creation; beauty crushing human significance.
- Heidegger (*What Is Called Thinking?*, *Being and Time*, *The Essence of Reasons*) — source of the "radiance"/presence vocabulary; Malick as translator.
- Warshow and Benjamin — both held that accepting film as art requires modifying the concept of art.
- Jazz — contrast case: relates to a whole (music) some of which is "definitively high," so can postpone the high-art question, unlike film.

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *cinematic circle* — the reciprocity by which an "element" of the medium and its "significance" are mutually constituted: directors place significance in possibilities of the medium, critics/audiences discover it, and no "element" is knowable prior to these acts.
- *element / automatism* — the "specific possibilities and necessities" of film's physical medium in which significance is placed; Cavell notes he "sometimes still call[s] them automatisms."
- *variable quotability* — the fact that one cannot "quote" a film moment as one quotes a text, generating uncertainty about what one even remembers/knows.
- *having to remember / the necessity to return to a work* — the experience (in fact or memory) that marks genuinely "knowing" a work, distinct from mere recognition.
- *reading (a film)* — not opposed to seeing but a detailing of how to see more clearly (rooted in "advising"); also an *argument* requiring response.
- *objects' participation / reflexivity* — projected objects are self-referential, re-creating themselves on film; presence refers to absence.

---

### Preface [book pp. xix–xxiv]

**Project:** Cavell sets up the book's personal, autobiographical, and explicitly anti-scholarly stance — declaring his real subject to be memory and the conditions his moviegoing satisfied — and narrates the book's intellectual genesis (the failed 1963 seminar, his readings of Rousseau, Heidegger, and Bazin) that authorized him to write "out of memory."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
The Preface opens with the book's keynote: "Memories of movies are strand over strand with memories of my life" [p.xix]. During the quarter-century (~1935–1960) when moviegoing was "a normal part of my week," it would no more have occurred to him to study movies than to write his autobiography [p.xix]. Having finished, he feels he has composed "a kind of metaphysical memoir — not the story of a period of my life but an account of the conditions it has satisfied" [p.xix]. A philosophically motivated book "ought to account philosophically for the motive in writing it" — so he asks: "What broke my natural relation to movies?" [p.xix]. He rejects easy answers: not the arrival of Bergman, Antonioni, Fellini, Godard, Resnais, Truffaut et al. (they invited reflection but achieved a *continuity* with Hollywood that Hollywood itself was losing; "they were no longer foreign") [p.xix]; nor the outgrowing of public entertainment like circus/vaudeville (we haven't outgrown the need for entertainment, and entertainment was never all movies provided) [pp.xix–xx]. He concludes that "to account for the motive in writing this book may be the most accurate description of its motive" [p.xx]. **[재구성: 동기 설명 = 동기 그 자체라는 순환적 정식화 — 책을 쓰는 동기는 별도로 진술 가능한 사실이 아니라, 책 전체가 수행하는 회복/추모 작업 안에서만 드러난다.]**

The compositional history is easier to tell. In 1963 he used the movie as the topic of an aesthetics seminar; its advantage was the absence of "an established canon of criticism," forcing the class "back upon a faithfulness to nothing but our experience" [p.xx]. But "it was a failure" — and what was learned "came from our failures" [p.xx]. Students were assigned to open discussion with "nothing but description" of the shared film; the descriptions "were never quite accurate... often because more was described than had been shown" — e.g., "The car followed her to the hotel," when in viewing they had not known until later the structure was a hotel [p.xx]. He generalized: almost every summary of a movie "contains one or more descriptive inaccuracies"; we would accept such "casual and sometimes hilariously remote" accounts only about operas, never about novels, stories, poems, or plays [p.xx]. The inaccuracy "occurs at the highest level": Truffaut's description of *I Confess* wrongly says Father Michael was being blackmailed (the woman was) and that he was "unable to provide an alibi" (he *refuses* one, to protect the woman) — yet "it feels as if he is, and not merely by Vilette; but that's the movie," and the early refusal "works to prepare the believability" of the confessional premise [pp.xx–xxi].

A second, "more disheartening" failure: the vow to "forgo theory and criticism was too proud," given their inability to discover trustworthy categories or to make weighty comparisons (with novel, plays, painting) [p.xxi]. A frequent reaction was "to start getting technical" (low-angle shots, filters, deep focus, fast cutting) — but this "lost its sense": the relevant technical information exceeds anyone's knowledge, while the only technical matters relevant to the *experience* "are in front of your eyes" [pp.xxi–xxii]. You can see whether a shot is long/middle/close, whether the camera moves; "you may not know how Hitchcock gets the stairwell to distort that particular way in *Vertigo*, but you can see that he got it" [p.xxii]. So: "what is the reality behind the idea that there is always a technical something you don't know that would provide the key to the experience?" [p.xxii].

After the seminar he worked at the questions for three or four years, writing mainly on philosophy of art, the problem of other minds, and two plays [p.xxii]. Questions about movies "kept coming to the surface"; immersion in the idea of theater — "of an audience, of the actor, and of the theater's enclosed and total world" — had its effect, and after the *King Lear* essay he wanted "to extend its thoughts to the work of film" [p.xxii]. Several intellectual discoveries prepared him: (1) Rousseau's *Letter to d'Alembert* — its "accuracies" (often mistaken for paranoia) helped him overcome a "distrust... about movies and about my interest in them"; Rousseau's "obsession with seeing" (going to the theater "to be seen and not to be seen," using tears to "excuse our blindness and coldness to the same situations in the world outside") "guided and confirmed my sense of the level at which viewing, in an audience and out, needs to be followed" [pp.xxii–xxiii]. (2) Heidegger's *Being and Time* — "his philosophical confidence in his native tongue, and his suspicion of it, and his concept of world, and his way of limiting knowing as our access to the world" [p.xxiii]. (3) The "final, or immediate, stimulus" in early 1968: essays by André Bazin [p.xxiii].

On the title: learning of Heidegger's essay "The Age of the World View," the mere words suggested "that ours is an age in which our philosophical grasp of the world fails to reach beyond our taking and holding views of it, and we call these views metaphysics" [p.xxiii]. He "half-deliberately avoided that essay" but wants "the sense of *Weltanschauung*" in his title [p.xxiii]. He read the first five chapters at Illinois (May 1968) and elsewhere [p.xxiii]. He had hoped to "proceed systematically" — to view/review many American talkies and "test the words against them" — but this proved impossible, "not just practically, but because my thoughts would not form around any such procedure"; he was seeing and wanting to see fewer movies, while "memories of old movies, and of the friends I had seen them with, kept on asserting themselves" [pp.xxiii–xxiv]. Hence he often refers to films "seen only once, some as long as thirty years ago," and in no case enough times "to feel I possess it the way it deserves... completely and wordlessly" [p.xxiv]. He issues this as a warning that the book "is not a history of any stretch of the movies," an apology for "off memories," and a confession that "a few faulty memories will not themselves shake my conviction... since I am as interested in how a memory went wrong as in why the memories that are right occur when they do" [p.xxiv]. Acknowledgments follow (Wesleyan's Center for the Humanities; John Hollander, Elisabeth Sifton, Terrence Malick, his wife; and "preeminently but not solely about painting," Michael Fried, whose "writing and conversation" and assigned looking/reading — including Clement Greenberg's criticism — shaped the book: "I would have written a little book about film if Michael Fried and I had never met. But it would not have been this one") [pp.xxiv–xxv]. He closes: "A book is written for two audiences: the one it may create, whose conversation it invites; and the one that has created it, whose conversation it invokes" [p.xxv].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Memories of movies are strand over strand with memories of my life." [p.xix]
- "I feel that I have been composing a kind of metaphysical memoir — not the story of a period of my life but an account of the conditions it has satisfied." [p.xix]
- "What broke my natural relation to movies?" [p.xix]
- "To account for the motive in writing this book may be the most accurate description of its motive." [p.xx]
- "It was a failure. Or rather, what was learned was important enough, but it came from our failures." [p.xx]
- "often because more was described than had been shown." [p.xx]
- "the only technical matters we found ourselves invoking, so far as they were relevant to the experience of particular films... are in front of your eyes." [pp.xxi–xxii]
- "what is the reality behind the idea that there is always a technical something you don't know that would provide the key to the experience?" [p.xxii]
- "guided and confirmed my sense of the level at which viewing, in an audience and out, needs to be followed." [pp.xxii–xxiii]
- "ours is an age in which our philosophical grasp of the world fails to reach beyond our taking and holding views of it, and we call these views metaphysics." [p.xxiii]
- "I am as interested in how a memory went wrong as in why the memories that are right occur when they do." [p.xxiv]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- The 1963 aesthetics seminar — the empirical origin; "more was described than had been shown" (the hotel example).
- *I Confess* (Hitchcock), via Truffaut's *Hitchcock* — inaccuracy "at the highest level"; the blackmail/alibi errors, yet the priest's refusal prepares the confessional premise.
- *Vertigo* (Hitchcock) — the distorted stairwell: you can't know *how* he got it but can see *that* he got it (technique vs. experience).
- Rousseau, *Letter to d'Alembert* — overcame Cavell's distrust of movies; the obsession with "seeing"/being seen sets the level of "viewing."
- Heidegger, *Being and Time* and "The Age of the World View" — confidence in/suspicion of native tongue; concept of world; knowing as access; source of the title's *Weltanschauung* sense.
- Bazin's essays — the immediate stimulus (early 1968) to consecutive writing.
- Michael Fried (and Clement Greenberg) — decisive for the book's thinking, "preeminently but not solely about painting."

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *metaphysical memoir* — Cavell's self-description of the book: not the narrative of a life but "an account of the conditions it has satisfied."
- *viewing* — the activity (in an audience and out) of seeing/being-seen that the book takes as its level of inquiry; calibrated via Rousseau.
- *world / knowing as access to the world* — borrowed from Heidegger: knowing is "limited" as our mode of access to a world (not a relation guaranteeing presence).
- *Weltanschauung / world view* — the sense Cavell wants in his title: an age that grasps the world only by "taking and holding views of it."
- *natural relation to movies* — the pre-reflective, habitual moviegoing whose "breaking" the book must account for.

---

### Ch 1 — An Autobiography of Companions [book pp. 3–15]

**Project:** Cavell establishes his autobiographical method — that his own experience of movies *is* the data the book must account for — and argues for movies' distinctive importance: that their highest and most ordinary instances once attracted the same audience, that liking the great requires liking the typical, and that film is uniquely "the one live traditional art" that has escaped the fate of modernism.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
He opens obliquely with Tolstoy's *What Is Art?* and its "unflinchingness of genius" in dismissing most great art [p.3]. Why care about radical criticism of one's culture (Plato/Rousseau banishing poetry; Arnold; Hegel/Marx on philosophy's end; Wagner, Whitman, Thoreau, Nietzsche)? The trouble is "we are sometimes unsure whether we have survived these prophecies or whether our lives now are realizing their worst fears" [p.3]. His question is "not whether we ought to care about Tolstoy's answer, but whether we can avoid caring" [p.3]. The resolution: Tolstoy was asking "not about the nature of art, but about the nature of the *importance* of art" — and these "are not separate questions": the answer to "What is the importance of art?" is "grammatically related to, or is a way of answering" the question "What is art?" [pp.3–4]. **[재구성: 카벨의 방법론적 전제 — 본성 물음과 중요성 물음의 문법적 동일성. 이것이 책 전체의 정당화다: 영화의 존재론(본성)을 묻는 것은 곧 그것이 우리에게 왜 중요했는가를 묻는 것이다.]**

He then turns to the title question of the chapter's argument: "Why are movies important?" — taking for granted that they are, and posing *that this can be taken for granted* as "the first fact... a distinctive fact about movies" [p.4]. By contrast, the serious modern arts (music, painting, sculpture, poetry, as pursued by artists "of major ambition") "are not generally important, except pretty much for the men and women devoted to creating them"; these artists "have virtually no audiences any longer" [p.4]. But everyone — "rich and poor, those who care about no (other) art and those who live on the promise of art" — "all care about movies, await them, respond to them, remember them, talk about them" [pp.4–5].

This first fact "is paired with a second": the movie "seems naturally to exist in a state in which its highest and its most ordinary instances attract the same audience (anyway until recently)" [p.5]. He illustrates with a long roll of performances anyone "ought to be able to rise to": City Lights' ending, Garbo's moods, Olivier's Richard III, Fonda's Lincoln, Lombard's wit, Jannings' despair, Monroe's "doomed magnetism," Powell and Loy's mutual trust, an Astaire routine's "heartbreaking hesitations"; the "highest sensibility" thrills at Fonda's "Earp. Wyatt Earp," hates Rathbone's/Widmark's/Marvin's villains "perfectly freed of guilt at their lucid and baroque defeats," and savors Bette Davis's entrance in *Jezebel* and her ankles in *Now, Voyager* — "moments [that] provide us with a fair semblance of ecstasy" [pp.5–6]. (He swats away the "camp" objection: anyone who thinks such responses camp "either is camping himself or else grew up in a different world from mine") [p.6].

By contrast: people who attend to serious music "do not attend to light dinner music"; readers of serious novels do not read potboilers (the detective story and science fiction being "sociological curiosities") [p.6]. "My claim is that in the case of films, it is generally true that you do not really like the highest instances unless you also like typical ones. You don't even know what the highest are instances of unless you know the typical as well" [p.6]. This "necessary region of indiscriminateness" creates "three separable nightmares" [pp.6–7]: (1) the conscientious reviewer who must cover every opening; (2) the fastidious writer who takes indiscriminate attention as a sign of bad taste/corruption "rather than as a datum in understanding the appetite for film"; (3) "those trying to awaken both from empty indiscriminateness and from futile discrimination" [pp.6–7].

He treats each. **About (1):** James Agee lived with the nightmare "graciously," needing no "seedy pleasure of feeling superior to drivel"; his gift for "finding and describing something to like, in no matter what yards of junk" adapted the old "praise of beauties" and "established a significant fact about movies: that there is always something to find" [pp.6–7]. This fact is sharpened "against the light of the auteur theory" [p.7]. It was "a clarifying shock to realize that films were directed, that some human being had undertaken to mean... all the angles of a movie" [p.7]; Cavell confesses his "backwardness" in not knowing where movies came from, "ready to admit that I must have had an idea that they sprang full grown from iron-gated sunglassed heads of studios" [p.7]. Yet for most films "the evidence of relation between a film and its director... is often no more than that one can find something to attribute to the man" [p.7]. So the deeper puzzle is "how such a setup could so often have yielded movies worth possessing and questioning" [p.7]. The auteur emphasis turns us from an even more obvious proposition: "that a movie comes from other movies" [p.7]. Each art "knows of this self-generation"; but with movies the idea "looks too good, or bad, to be true," since directors lack an established history reminding them "that a tradition is something in which individuals work out their individuality" [pp.7–8]. An immediate block is that "we know... their origin" — there was a time with no movies, and "the first one surely couldn't have come from others" [p.8]. He concedes the first instance but insists "Whatever the original state of an art, a new work is born in civilization from the powers of the art itself"; "the obscurity about 'the first'... is no more historical than conceptual" [p.8]. Against the picture of Hollywood as "legions of precise slaves, with no leader and no memory but a bank," he sets Bergman's likening of filmmaking to building a medieval cathedral ("a mass of craftsmen... mounting an aspiration no man could achieve alone") — though Bergman was "pretty clearly thinking of himself as the unknown master builder." Neither "a romanticism of anonymity nor a romanticism of individuality" accounts for film's power [p.8].

**About (2):** Some have "stopped worrying and started loving the unminding devotions of the eye and ear," claiming them as natural reaction to "the demise of the printed word" [p.8]. Cavell resists: in his experience "more people read, and read better," and some of the best readers he knows like movies best [pp.8–9]. His real question is "why the standards of rigor and range that we have learned to take for granted... when we give or are given readings of books are ignored or unavailable when we give or are given readings of movies" — while granting "there is a problem about the idea of 'reading a movie'" [p.9]. **About (3):** "A standing discovery of the auteur theory was of the need for a canon of movies to which any remarks about 'the movie' should hold themselves answerable" [p.9]. Without a canon, "the natural circle of theory and evidence will not inscribe the knowledge we want"; the danger is "that there will be evidence for everything and nothing" [p.9]. Organization by directors' oeuvres "is a beginning, but it will include too much and too little" [pp.9–10]. He admits he is "not in a position to establish such a canon," but will "take what bearings I have, trying at each point to meld the ways of thinking that have invited my conviction with the experiences of films that I have cared about" [p.10].

He then thematizes *companionship*. These experiences are "lined with fragments of conversations and responses of friends I have gone to movies with" — and the after-sharings (running across lots as Indians or biplanes; reconstructing score, dialogue, plot; "simply naming moments") [p.10]. He contrasts media of audience: "the audience of a book is essentially solitary, one soul at a time; the audience of music and theater is essentially larger than your immediate acquaintance — a gathering of the city; the crowd at a movie comprises various pools of companions, or scattered souls with someone missing" [p.10]. He "remains faithful to responses I first had to movies, even if I can no longer share them" [p.10]. A long list of films (the Laughton-Gable *Mutiny on the Bounty*, *Captain Blood*, *Algiers*, *Wuthering Heights*, *King's Row*, *Singin' in the Rain*, *White Heat*, etc.) names "hours and days of awe; momentous, but only for the moment; unrecapturable fully except in memory and evocation; gone" — "If you see them now for the first time... you can't know what I know" [p.10].

His difficulty in going to *new* movies has to do partly with anxiousness about new films but "equally with my anxiousness in what I feel to be new audiences" — "people with new reasons for being there" among whom he "cannot locate or remain together with my companions" [p.11]. He diagnoses a historical shift: "movie showings have begun for the first time to be habitually attended by an audience" — people "who arrive and depart at the same time, as at a play" [p.11]. Formerly moviegoing was "casual": one entered "at no matter what point," enjoying later "the return of the exact moment at which one entered," and took "our fantasies and companions and anonymity inside and left with them intact" [p.11]. "Now that there is an audience, a claim is made upon my privacy" — yet "the old casualness of moviegoing has been replaced by a casualness of movie-*viewing*," which he reads as "an inability to tolerate our own fantasies" [p.11]. "I feel I am present at a cult whose members have nothing in common but their presence in the same place" [p.11]. The old casualness "harbored the value of illicitness... part of moviegoing"; the new audience does not dispel it but "is not a gathering of citizens for honest confession and acceptance of one another"; "the demand that I forgo privacy is as illicit as my requirement to preserve it" [pp.11–12].

"The importance of memory goes beyond its housing of knowledge. It arises also in the way movies are remembered or misremembered" — a live topic, since "my way of studying films has been mostly through remembering them, like dreams" [p.12]. He acknowledges "other equally essential ways of getting at movies, like reading their scripts and... counting and timing their shots," but presses his own way, since he "wouldn't at this stage know what further documentation would be documentation *for*"; "My business is to think out the causes of my consciousness of films as it stands" [p.12]. From this "lack of scholarship" he expects three advantages: (i) others will recall what he remembers; (ii) since "my remembering is itself a datum that wants accounting for, and since a book cannot reproduce... the images... I will always be pushed to an account... of the object as a whole" — important because "the only instruments that could provide data for a theory of film are the procedures of criticism"; (iii) "in allowing the thinking to have its head," he avoids "those embarrassed bursts of theory" and gains examples to ask "why movies seem naturally to produce their metaphysical outcries" [p.12]. He names three instances of "humane criticism dealing with whole films" he has learned from: James Kerans on *Grand Illusion*, William Hedges on *Children of Paradise*, Annette Michelson on *2001* [pp.12–13]. Of writing that "has the power of the missing companion," he names Agee, Warshow, Bazin, and also Farber, Kael, Tyler, Sarris [p.13].

He returns to the indiscriminateness requirement, contrasting it with the modern arts. In the established arts, broad cultivation of "current instances of music or painting or theater" actually signals one "does not... know the serious instances" — Fried's account of the modernist condition: an art "leaves no room... for the minor artist"; "the work of the major artist condemns the work of others to artistic nonexistence," and his own work is "condemned to seriousness, to further radical success or to complete failure" [p.13]. This is "sadder, certainly crueler" than Tolstoy's situation: blame now falls "upon the necessities of the separate arts themselves" — music for refusing tonality, sculpture for no longer allowing material to be sculpted, painting for refusing even "evidence of the human hand in its making" — "Or we will have to blame reality for withdrawing itself from our powers" [pp.13–14]. Yet "our state is happier than Tolstoy's," for the community of serious art "is not exclusive — not the way an elite is exclusive. It is esoteric, but the secret is open to anyone. Art now exists in the condition of philosophy" [p.14]. "The good city does not require art to be made for everyone"; its responsibility is "to get out of the way of the artist" and to free citizens to "determine for himself the relevance a serious art may have to his life" [p.14]. "The socialist's demand for a future art is in the end no different from the capitalist's praise of the given: both want art to do, or prove, what can only be done socially" [p.14].

Finally he confronts the claim "that film is *the modern art*, the one to which modern man naturally responds" [p.14]. Two reasons for distrust: (1) it assumes the other arts cannot elicit "the old values of art" (maybe so, but the claimant "is not in a position to recognize the live article"), and it shows "a poor view of what is 'natural,'" since "if there is anything seriously to be called 'the modern man,' one fact about him is that what is natural to him is not natural" [p.14]. (2) If film is seriously an art, "it needs to be explained how it can have avoided the fate of modernism" — how it "maintained its continuities of audiences and genres," "taken seriously without having assumed the burden of seriousness" [pp.14–15]. "For the blatant fact about film is that, if it is art, it is the one live traditional art, the one that can take its tradition for granted" [p.15]. The idea of film's "modernity" is therefore "either empty... or false... or incoherent (suggesting that movies are a new and improved version of art, like a modern detergent or the modern kitchen)" [p.15]. He distinguishes: "The modernist is incomprehensible apart from his questioning of specific traditions... The modernizer is merely blind to the power of tradition, mocking his chains" [p.15]. "The movie's ease within its assumptions and achievements — its conventions remaining convenient... without self-questioning — is central to its pleasure for us" [pp.15]; we may call this "film's naïveté, but that is perhaps a naïve way of looking at the matter," since it merely redescribes rather than explains "film's absorption in its conventions" [p.15]. The chapter ends on the generative puzzle: learning of "the corruptions and stupidities in the industry" (Ben Hecht's memoirs), "the more we are likely to wonder how the films we care about can ever have been made"; "What is the power of film that it could survive (even profit artistically from) so much neglect and ignorant contempt by those in power over it? What is film?" [p.15].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "the answer to the question 'What is the importance of art?' is grammatically related to, or is a way of answering, the question 'What is art?'" [pp.3–4]
- "you do not really like the highest instances unless you also like typical ones. You don't even know what the highest are instances of unless you know the typical as well." [p.6]
- "there is always something to find, often enough to justify a hundred minutes of speculative solitude." [p.7]
- "a movie comes from other movies." [p.7]
- "the audience of a book is essentially solitary, one soul at a time; the audience of music and theater is essentially larger than your immediate acquaintance — a gathering of the city; the crowd at a movie comprises various pools of companions, or scattered souls with someone missing." [p.10]
- "If you see them now for the first time, you may be interested and moved, but you can't know what I know." [p.10]
- "the old casualness of moviegoing has been replaced by a casualness of movie-viewing" [p.11]
- "I feel I am present at a cult whose members have nothing in common but their presence in the same place." [p.11]
- "My business is to think out the causes of my consciousness of films as it stands." [p.12]
- "the only instruments that could provide data for a theory of film are the procedures of criticism." [p.12]
- "Art now exists in the condition of philosophy." [p.14]
- "if it is art, it is the one live traditional art, the one that can take its tradition for granted." [p.15]
- "The modernist is incomprehensible apart from his questioning of specific traditions... The modernizer is merely blind to the power of tradition, mocking his chains." [p.15]
- "What is the power of film that it could survive (even profit artistically from) so much neglect and ignorant contempt by those in power over it? What is film?" [p.15]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- Tolstoy, *What Is Art?* — the model of radical cultural criticism; the pivot from "nature of art" to "importance of art."
- The performance-roll (City Lights, Garbo, Olivier's Richard III, Fonda's Lincoln/"Wyatt Earp," Lombard, Jannings, Monroe, Powell & Loy, Astaire, Rathbone/Widmark/Marvin villains, Bette Davis in *Jezebel* and *Now, Voyager*) — proof that high and ordinary instances draw one response; "semblance of ecstasy."
- James Agee — graciously living the reviewer's "nightmare"; "praise of beauties"; "there is always something to find."
- The auteur theory — the "clarifying shock" that films are *directed*; but it obscures that "a movie comes from other movies."
- Ingmar Bergman — the cathedral analogy (craftsmen + master builder); illustrates the inadequacy of both romanticisms.
- James Kerans (*Grand Illusion*), William Hedges (*Children of Paradise*), Annette Michelson (*2001*) — exemplary "humane criticism dealing with whole films."
- Agee, Warshow, Bazin; Farber, Kael, Tyler, Sarris — critics with "the power of the missing companion."
- Michael Fried — the modernist condition (no room for the minor artist; major work condemns others to nonexistence).
- Ben Hecht's Hollywood memoirs — the industry's corruption sharpening the puzzle of how good films got made.

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *companions / autobiography of companions* — the social texture of moviegoing (pools of companions; the "missing companion"); the experiences the book takes as data are constituted by who one saw films with.
- *the first fact / second fact* — (1) that movies' importance can be taken for granted; (2) that their highest and most ordinary instances drew the same audience.
- *indiscriminateness (necessary region of)* — the condition that liking the great requires liking the typical; distinct from "futile discrimination."
- *a movie comes from other movies* — the thesis of film's internal self-generation / tradition.
- *canon* — the body of films to which claims about "the movie" must hold themselves answerable (a "standing discovery" of auteur theory).
- *the natural circle of theory and evidence* — the mutual dependence of theory and the films adduced as evidence (an early form of the "cinematic circle").
- *casualness of moviegoing vs. casualness of movie-viewing* — old anonymity/illicitness/fantasy vs. new audience that makes a claim on privacy yet cannot tolerate fantasy.
- *humane criticism dealing with whole films* — criticism (vs. technical description) that accounts for a film as a whole.
- *the one live traditional art* — film as the art that escaped modernism's fate, taking its tradition for granted.
- *modernist vs. modernizer* — one defined by questioning specific traditions; the other merely blind to tradition's power.
- *art exists in the condition of philosophy* — serious art is esoteric but non-exclusive: "the secret is open to anyone."

---

### Ch 2 — Sights and Sounds [book pp. 16–22]

**Project:** Cavell begins the ontology proper by asking what film is made of: starting from Panofsky's and Bazin's claim that the medium's basis is photographic and "a photograph is of reality," he reframes the question as "What happens to reality when it is projected and screened?" and works out the ontological difference between photographs and sound-recordings, and between photography and painting — culminating in the thesis that photography overcomes subjectivity by *automatism*, maintaining "the presentness of the world by accepting our absence from it."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
"The beginning of an answer" comes from Panofsky and Bazin [p.16]. Panofsky: "The medium of the movies is physical reality as such"; Bazin: "Cinema is committed to communicate only by way of what is real," and "The cinema [is] of its essence a dramaturgy of Nature" [p.16]. But "'Physical reality as such,' taken literally, is not correct" — that phrase better fits tableaux vivants, formal gardens, or Minimal Art [p.16]. What they have in mind is "that the basis of the medium of movies is photographic, and that a photograph is of reality or nature" [p.16]. Adding that the photographic image "is projected and gathered on a screen," "our question becomes: What happens to reality when it is projected and screened?" [p.16]. **[재구성: 이 한 문장이 책의 존재론적 주문이다 — 파노프스키/바쟁의 "영화=물리적 실재"를 카벨은 '실재가 투사·스크린화될 때 무슨 일이 일어나는가'라는 변형(transformation) 물음으로 재정식화한다.]**

That it is reality "we have to deal with" finds "surprising confirmation in the way movies are remembered, and misremembered" [p.16]. The dream analogy is "not a bad analogy" (you recover a film by finding its "characteristic mood"), but unlike dreams "other people can help you remember"; movies "are hard to remember, the way the actual events of yesterday are," and yet "certain moments... will nag as vividly as moments of childhood" — "as if you had to remember what happened before you slept," which "suggests that film awakens as much as it enfolds you" [pp.16–17].

He anticipates an objection: this starting point seems to "beg the question of the medium" because film "can depict the fantastic as readily as the natural" [p.17]. His reply: "the displacement of objects and persons from their natural sequences and locales is itself an acknowledgment of the physicality of their existence" [p.17]. The antirealist theorists "could not shake the idea that it was essentially a form of painting" — since painting had "visually repudiated... the representation of reality" — which "helped them neglect the differences between representation and projection" [p.17]. "But an immediate fact about the medium of the photograph (still or in motion) is that it is not painting" [p.17].

What does "not painting" mean? "A photograph does not present us with 'likenesses' of things; it presents us, we want to say, with the things themselves. But wanting to say that may well make us ontologically restless" [p.17]. "'Photographs present us with things themselves' sounds, and ought to sound, false or paradoxical" — a photograph of Garbo is not Garbo in the flesh — but that is "not very informative," and it is "no less paradoxical" to hold up the photograph and say "That is not Garbo" [pp.17–18]. "Such troubles... suggest that we do not know what a photograph is; we do not know how to place it ontologically" — "we don't know how to think of the connection between a photograph and what it is a photograph of" [p.18]. "The image is not a likeness; it is not exactly a replica, or a relic, or a shadow, or an apparition either, though all of these natural candidates share a striking feature with photographs — an aura or history of magic surrounding them" [p.18].

Why do "similar questions" not arise about sound recordings? Listening to a record, we are not tempted to say "But I know it's really only a recording" of "That's an English horn" [p.18]. A child might be puzzled by "That's an English horn" (said of a phonograph) just as by "That's your grandmother" (said of a photograph); children stop being puzzled, but "that doesn't mean we know why they were puzzled, or why they no longer are" [pp.18]. He asks whether the difference is that "we are fully accustomed to hearing things that are invisible, not present to us" [p.18]. "It is the nature of hearing that what is heard comes from someplace, whereas what you can see you can look at" — hence "sounds are warnings, or calls"; "our access to another world is normally through voices from it"; "a man can be spoken to by God and survive, but not if he sees God, in which case he is no longer in this world" [pp.18–19]. By contrast "we are not accustomed to seeing things that are invisible, or not present to us... or we are not accustomed to acknowledging that we do (except for dreams). Yet this seems, ontologically, to be what is happening when we look at a photograph: we see things that are not present" [p.19].

He fields the objection that this is "playing with words" — that we are "looking at something perfectly present, namely, a photograph" — and answers that this affirms "something I have not denied": he is "precisely describing... what it means to say that there is this photograph here" [p.19]. He is not making "too great a mystery"; rather "we have forgotten how mysterious these things are, and in general how different different things are from one another, as though we had forgotten how to value them. This is in fact something movies teach us" [p.19]. He tests a deflationary account of recordings ("what I really mean is, 'That's the *sound* of an English horn'... what I hear is exactly the same... whether the thing is present or not"); "What this rigmarole calls attention to is that sounds can be perfectly copied" and "we have various interests in copying them" (else "people would never learn to talk") [pp.19–20]. "It is interesting that there is no comparable rigmarole about visual transcriptions" [p.20]. "The problem is that even if a photograph were a copy of an object... it would not bear the relation to its object that a recording bears to the sound it copies" [p.20]. "We said that the record reproduces its sound, but we cannot say that a photograph reproduces a sight (or a look, or an appearance)" [p.20]. "It can seem that language is missing a word at this place" — but "one doesn't know what to pin the word on" [p.20]. A "sight" is "an object... or an extraordinary happening, like the aurora borealis"; what you see "is an object — anyway, not the *sight* of an object" [p.20]. Nor do the epistemologist's "sense-data" or "surfaces" help: "if the sense-data of photographs were the same as the sense-data of the objects they contain, we couldn't tell a photograph of an object from the object itself"; "surfaces" wrongly "suggests that it emphasizes texture" [pp.20–21]. "What is missing is not a word, but, so to speak, something in nature — the fact that objects don't make sights, or have sights" [p.21]. "Objects are too close to their sights to give them up for reproducing; in order to reproduce the sights they (as it were) make, you have to reproduce them — make a mold, or take an impression" [p.21]. Bazin's notion of a photograph as "a visual mold or a visual impression" dissatisfies Cavell because "physical molds and impressions... have clear procedures for getting rid of their originals, whereas in a photograph, the original is still as present as it ever was. Not present as it once was to the camera; but that is only a mold-machine, not the mold itself" [p.21].

The pivot to *automatism*: "Photographs are not hand-made; they are manufactured. And what is manufactured is an *image of the world*" [p.21]. "The inescapable fact of mechanism or automatism in the making of these images is the feature Bazin points to as '[satisfying], once and for all and in its very essence, our obsession with realism'" [p.21]. "It is essential to get to the right depth of this fact of automatism" [p.21]. It is misleading to say (with Bazin) that "photography has freed the plastic arts from their obsession with likeness," for this implies photography and painting "were in competition" [p.21]. So far as photography satisfied a wish, it satisfied "not... the human wish, intensifying in the West since the Reformation, to escape subjectivity and metaphysical isolation — a wish for the power to reach this world, having for so long tried, at last hopelessly, to manifest fidelity to another" [pp.21]. And painting "was not 'freed' — and not by photography — from its obsession with likeness": "Painting, in Manet, was forced to forgo likeness exactly because of its own obsession with reality, because the illusions it had learned to create did not provide the conviction in reality... that it craved" [p.21]. "One might even say that in withdrawing from likeness, painting freed photography to be invented" [p.21]. Nor did photography free painting "from the idea that a painting had to be a picture": painting maintained objective reference "until long after the establishment of photography," and gave it up not because "it finally dawned on painters that paintings were not pictures, but because that was the way to maintain connection with (the history of) the art of painting" [pp.21]. He asks whether "the final denial of objective reference amounts to a complete yielding of connection with reality" — once we drop the equation of "connection with reality" with "provision of likeness" — and concludes both views (painting "dead without reality" and "dead with it") "are both in need of development": "painting and reality no longer assure one another" [pp.21–22].

He deepens this: "what painting wanted, in wanting connection with reality, was a sense of *presentness* — not exactly a conviction of the world's presence to us, but of our presence to it" [p.22]. "At some point the unhinging of our consciousness from the world interposed our subjectivity between us and our presentness to the world. Then our subjectivity became what is present to us, individuality became isolation" [p.22]. "The route to conviction in reality was through the acknowledgment of that endless presence of self." Expressionism is "one possibility of representing this acknowledgment," but is better understood as "a representation of our response to this new fact of our condition — our terror of ourselves in isolation — rather than as a representation of the world from within the condition of isolation itself"; it is not "a new mastery of fate by creating selfhood" but "the sealing of the self's fate by theatricalizing it" [p.22]. "Apart from the wish for selfhood (hence the always simultaneous granting of otherness as well), I do not understand the value of art. Apart from this wish and its achievement, art is exhibition" [p.22]. To speak of subjectivity "as the route back to our conviction in reality is to speak of *romanticism*," understood as "the natural struggle between the representation and the acknowledgment of our subjectivity" — hence Kant and Hegel; Blake "secreting the world he believes in"; Wordsworth "writing himself back into the world"; Heidegger investigating Being through Dasein (where Being "shows up best, namely as questionable"); Wittgenstein investigating the world by investigating "what we say... in order to wrest the world from our possessions so that we may possess it again" [p.22]. The recent painting Fried describes "as objects of presentness" would be "painting's latest effort to maintain its conviction in its own power to establish connection with reality — by permitting us presentness to ourselves, apart from which there is no hope for a world" [pp.22–23].

The chapter's culmination: "Photography overcame subjectivity in a way undreamed of by painting... one which does not so much defeat the act of painting as escape it altogether: by automatism, by removing the human agent from the task of reproduction" [p.23]. Hence "photography was never in competition with painting"; rather "the quest for visual reality, or the 'memory of the present' (as Baudelaire put it), split apart" [p.23]. The closing formula: "To maintain conviction in our connection with reality, to maintain our presentness, painting accepts the recession of the world. Photography maintains the presentness of the world by accepting our absence from it. The reality in a photograph is present to me while I am not present to it; and a world I know, and see, but to which I am nevertheless not present (through no fault of my subjectivity), is a world past" [p.23].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "the basis of the medium of movies is photographic, and that a photograph is of reality or nature." [p.16]
- "our question becomes: What happens to reality when it is projected and screened?" [p.16]
- "film awakens as much as it enfolds you." [p.17]
- "the displacement of objects and persons from their natural sequences and locales is itself an acknowledgment of the physicality of their existence." [p.17]
- "A photograph does not present us with 'likenesses' of things; it presents us, we want to say, with the things themselves. But wanting to say that may well make us ontologically restless." [p.17]
- "we do not know what a photograph is; we do not know how to place it ontologically." [p.18]
- "It is the nature of hearing that what is heard comes from someplace, whereas what you can see you can look at." [p.19]
- "we see things that are not present." [p.19]
- "we have forgotten how mysterious these things are... This is in fact something movies teach us." [p.19]
- "we cannot say that a photograph reproduces a sight (or a look, or an appearance)." [p.20]
- "Photographs are not hand-made; they are manufactured. And what is manufactured is an image of the world." [p.21]
- "the human wish, intensifying in the West since the Reformation, to escape subjectivity and metaphysical isolation — a wish for the power to reach this world, having for so long tried, at last hopelessly, to manifest fidelity to another." [p.21]
- "in withdrawing from likeness, painting freed photography to be invented." [p.21]
- "what painting wanted, in wanting connection with reality, was a sense of presentness — not exactly a conviction of the world's presence to us, but of our presence to it." [p.22]
- "Apart from the wish for selfhood (hence the always simultaneous granting of otherness as well), I do not understand the value of art. Apart from this wish and its achievement, art is exhibition." [p.22]
- "Photography overcame subjectivity in a way undreamed of by painting... by automatism, by removing the human agent from the task of reproduction." [p.23]
- "Photography maintains the presentness of the world by accepting our absence from it. The reality in a photograph is present to me while I am not present to it; and a world I know, and see, but to which I am nevertheless not present... is a world past." [p.23]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- Panofsky — "The medium of the movies is physical reality as such"; sets the realist starting point.
- Bazin — "communicate only by way of what is real"; "dramaturgy of Nature"; automatism "satisfying our obsession with realism"; the "visual mold/impression" idea Cavell resists.
- Garbo (the photograph of) — the test case for "That is / is not Garbo"; ontological restlessness.
- The English horn / the phonograph — the contrast case: sound *can* be perfectly copied; no comparable "rigmarole" for visual transcription.
- Manet — painting "forced to forgo likeness" by its *own* obsession with reality (not by photography).
- Baudelaire — "memory of the present"; the quest for visual reality that "split apart."
- Blake, Wordsworth, Kant, Hegel — romanticism as the struggle between representation and acknowledgment of subjectivity.
- Heidegger (Being via Dasein), Wittgenstein (world via "what we say") — investigating the world by re-routing through the self/our utterances.
- Michael Fried — recent painting as "objects of presentness."

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *automatism / mechanism* — the "inescapable fact" that photographs are *manufactured*, the human agent removed from reproduction; the depth-feature by which photography "overcame subjectivity."
- *a photograph is of reality (of the world)* — the medium's basis; photographs present "the things themselves," not "likenesses."
- *image of the world* — what is manufactured by the photographic process.
- *sight / look / appearance* — what a photograph does *not* "reproduce" the way a recording reproduces a sound (since "objects don't make sights").
- *presentness* — what painting wants: not the world's presence to us but "our presence to it."
- *the world's presentness / a world past* — photography maintains the world's presentness by accepting our absence; a world present to me to which I am not present is "a world past."
- *subjectivity / metaphysical isolation* — the modern condition (intensifying since the Reformation) that photography "escapes" by automatism and that painting must "acknowledge."
- *romanticism* — the struggle between the representation and the acknowledgment of subjectivity.
- *wish for selfhood / granting of otherness* — the condition of art's value for Cavell; without it "art is exhibition."
- *aura / history of magic* — shared by the "natural candidates" (replica, relic, shadow, apparition) for what a photograph is.

---

### Ch 3 — Photograph and Screen [book pp. 23–24]

**Project:** A short, pivotal chapter that fixes "the specific sense in which photographs are of the world": through the asymmetry between photograph and painting (what lies behind/beyond), the formulas "a painting *is* a world; a photograph *is of* the world," and an analysis of the *screen* as a barrier that "screens" — both hiding the viewer from the world and the world's existence from the viewer.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
"Let us notice the specific sense in which photographs are of the world, of reality as a whole" [p.23]. The defining asymmetry: "You can always ask, pointing to an object in a photograph — a building, say — what lies behind it, totally obscured by it. This only accidentally makes sense when asked of an object in a painting" [p.23]. Likewise: "You can always ask, of an area photographed, what lies adjacent to that area, beyond the frame. This generally makes no sense asked of a painting" [pp.23–24]. The reason: "You can ask these questions of objects in photographs because they have answers in reality. The world of a painting is not continuous with the world of its frame; at its frame, a world finds its limits" [p.24]. Hence the formula: "A painting is a world; a photograph is of the world" [p.24]. **[재구성: 이 대구가 3장의 핵심이자 책 전체의 존재론적 지렛대다 — 회화의 세계는 프레임에서 끝나는 자족적 세계(a world)이고, 사진은 프레임 너머로 연속되는 단일한 현실 전체에 대한(of the world) 것이다. '뒤에/너머에 무엇이 있는가'라는 물음의 유의미성 여부가 이 차이의 시금석이다.]**

"What happens in a photograph is that it comes to an end. A photograph is cropped... by the camera itself" [p.24]. The camera "crops it by predetermining the amount of view it will accept; cutting, masking, enlarging, predetermine the amount after the fact" [p.24]. (He notes this phenomenon "shows up in recent painting," which at "the extremest negation of the photographic" has found "media that achieve the condition of photographs") [p.24]. "The camera, being finite, crops a portion from an indefinitely larger field; continuous portions of that field could be included... in principle, it could all be taken" [p.24]. Hence "objects in photographs that run past the edge do not feel cut; they are aimed at, shot, stopped live. When a photograph is cropped, the rest of the world is cut out" [p.24]. "The implied presence of the rest of the world, and its explicit rejection, are as essential in the experience of a photograph as what it explicitly presents" [p.24]. "A camera is an opening in a box: that is the best emblem of the fact that a camera holding on an object is holding the rest of the world away" [p.24]. He inverts the usual praise: the camera, "praised for extending the senses... may, as the world goes, deserve more praise for confining them, leaving room for thought" [p.24].

The analysis of the *screen*: "The world of a moving picture is screened" [p.24]. "The screen is not a support, not like a canvas; there is nothing to support, that way. It holds a projection, as light as light" [p.24]. "A screen is a barrier. What does the silver screen screen? It screens me from the world it holds — that is, makes me invisible. And it screens that world from me — that is, screens its existence from me" [p.24]. The crucial ontological line: "That the projected world does not exist (now) is its only difference from reality. (There is no feature, or set of features, in which it differs. Existence is not a predicate.)" [p.24]. **[재구성: 카벨은 칸트적 테제 '존재는 술어가 아니다'를 영화 존재론에 적용한다 — 스크린 속 세계와 실재는 어떤 성질에서도 다르지 않고, 오직 '지금 존재함'에서만 다르다. 그래서 차이는 질적(qualitative)이지 않고 양상적(modal)·시제적이다.]** "Because it is the field of a photograph, the screen has no frame; that is to say, no border. Its limits are not so much the edges of a given shape as they are the limitations, or capacity, of a container" [p.24].
