He then re-folds the term: "The screen is a frame; the frame is the whole field of the screen — as a frame of film is the whole field of a photograph, like the frame of a loom or a house. In this sense, the screen-frame is a mold, or form" [p.24].

He closes with the *phenomenological frame*: because successive film frames "are fit flush into the fixed screen frame," the result is "a phenomenological frame that is indefinitely extendible and contractible, limited in the smallness of the object it can grasp only by the state of its technology, and in largeness only by the span of the world" [pp.24–25]. "Drawing the camera back, and panning it, are two ways of extending the frame; a close-up is of a part of the body, or of one object or small set of objects, supported by and reverberating the whole frame of nature" [p.25]. "The altering frame is the image of perfect attention" [p.25]. Early cinema discovered "the possibility of calling attention to persons and parts of persons and objects," but "it is equally a possibility of the medium not to call attention to them but, rather, to let the world happen, to let its parts draw attention to themselves according to their natural weight" — a possibility "less explored than its opposite," whose "masters" are "Dreyer, Flaherty, Vigo, Renoir, and Antonioni" [p.25].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "You can always ask, pointing to an object in a photograph — a building, say — what lies behind it, totally obscured by it. This only accidentally makes sense when asked of an object in a painting." [p.23]
- "You can ask these questions of objects in photographs because they have answers in reality. The world of a painting is not continuous with the world of its frame; at its frame, a world finds its limits." [p.24]
- "A painting is a world; a photograph is of the world." [p.24]
- "objects in photographs that run past the edge do not feel cut; they are aimed at, shot, stopped live. When a photograph is cropped, the rest of the world is cut out." [p.24]
- "The implied presence of the rest of the world, and its explicit rejection, are as essential in the experience of a photograph as what it explicitly presents." [p.24]
- "A camera is an opening in a box: that is the best emblem of the fact that a camera holding on an object is holding the rest of the world away." [p.24]
- "it may, as the world goes, deserve more praise for confining them, leaving room for thought." [p.24]
- "A screen is a barrier. What does the silver screen screen? It screens me from the world it holds — that is, makes me invisible. And it screens that world from me — that is, screens its existence from me." [p.24]
- "That the projected world does not exist (now) is its only difference from reality. (There is no feature, or set of features, in which it differs. Existence is not a predicate.)" [p.24]
- "The altering frame is the image of perfect attention." [p.25]
- "to let the world happen, to let its parts draw attention to themselves according to their natural weight." [p.25]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- A building in a photograph vs. an object in a painting — the test of "what lies behind/beyond"; the photograph's continuity with reality.
- "Recent painting" — at the extreme negation of the photographic, paradoxically "achieve[s] the condition of photographs."
- The camera as "an opening in a box" — the emblem of holding the rest of the world away.
- Dreyer, Flaherty, Vigo, Renoir, Antonioni — masters of the (less-explored) possibility of *not* calling attention, letting the world's parts draw attention "according to their natural weight."

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *a painting is a world / a photograph is of the world* — the governing distinction: the painting's world is self-limiting at its frame; the photograph is of the single continuous reality.
- *cropping* — the camera's predetermination of "the amount of view it will accept"; cutting/masking/enlarging do so "after the fact." Objects running past the edge are "stopped live," not cut.
- *the rest of the world (implied presence and explicit rejection)* — essential to the experience of a photograph; the camera "holds the rest of the world away."
- *screen / to screen* — the screen as barrier: it screens *me* from the world it holds (makes me invisible) and screens *that world's existence* from me.
- *Existence is not a predicate* — the world projected differs from reality in no feature, only in not existing *now*.
- *screen-frame as mold/form* — the screen has no border but is a container; the frame is "the whole field of the screen."
- *phenomenological frame* — the indefinitely extendible/contractible frame produced by fitting film frames flush into the fixed screen frame; "the image of perfect attention."
- *letting the world happen / natural weight* — the medium's possibility of not directing attention, letting objects assert themselves.

---

### Ch 4 — Audience, Actor, and Star [book pp. 25–28]

**Project:** Cavell extends the automatism thesis to the human being on screen: he defines the film audience by its *mechanical absence*, argues against the "real man is there" objection (and Bazin's "relay of presence"), and works out the ontological difference between stage actor and screen performer — that on screen the individual subsumes the role, "the rest is nonexistent," and the photographed human is not an actor but a *star*, a *type*.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
"The depth of the automatism of photography is to be read not alone in its mechanical production of an image of reality, but in its mechanical defeat of our presence to that reality" [p.25]. The audience definition: "The audience in a theater can be defined as those to whom the actors are present while they are not present to the actors. But movies allow the audience to be mechanically absent" [pp.25–26]. The consequence: "The fact that I am invisible and inaudible to the actors, and fixed in position, no longer needs accounting for; it is not part of a convention I have to comply with"; the proceedings "do not have to make good the fact that I do nothing in the face of tragedy, or that I laugh at the follies of others" [p.26]. "In viewing a movie my helplessness is mechanically assured: I am present not at something happening, which I must confirm, but at something that has happened, which I absorb (like a memory). In this, movies resemble novels, a fact mirrored in the sound of narration itself, whose tense is the past" [p.26]. **[재구성: 카벨은 2장의 '과거인 세계(a world past)'를 관객론으로 옮긴다 — 무대 관객의 부재는 따라야 할 관습이지만, 영화 관객의 부재는 기계적으로 보장된 사실이며, 그래서 영화 경험의 시제는 (소설처럼) 과거다.]**

He stages the strongest objection: surely "the obvious difference is that in a theater we are in the presence of an actor, in a movie house we are not... in a theater a real man is there, and in a movie no real man is there" [p.26]. Cavell: "What that means must not be denied; but the fact remains to be understood" [p.26]. Bazin "meets it head on by simply denying that 'the screen is incapable of putting us "in the presence of the actor"'; it... relays his presence to us, as by mirrors" [p.26]. But Cavell objects that "Bazin's idea here really fits the facts of live television, in which the thing we are presented with is happening simultaneously with its presentation" — and even there "what is present to us while it is happening is not the world, but an event standing out from the world. Its point is not to reveal, but to cover (as with a gun), to keep something on view" [p.26].

"It is an incontestable fact that in a motion picture no live human being is up there. But a human *something* is, and something unlike anything else we know" [pp.26–27]. We can keep the "plain description of that human something as 'in our presence while we are not in his'" (present *at* him, "because looking at him, but not present *to* him") and still "account for the difference between his live presence and his photographed presence to us." "We need to consider what is present or, rather, since the topic is the human being, *who* is present" [p.27].

"One's first impulse may be to say that in a play the character is present, whereas in a film the actor is. That sounds phony or false: one wants to say that both are present in both. But there is more to it, ontologically more" [p.27]. He cites Panofsky: "Othello or Nora are definite, substantial figures created by the playwright... they most definitely exist, no matter who plays them or even whether they are played at all. The character in a film, however, lives and dies with the actor... it is the entity 'Greta Garbo' incarnate in a figure called Anna Christie or the entity 'Robert Montgomery' incarnate in a murderer who... will never cease to haunt our memories" [p.27]. Cavell extends it: "If the character lives and dies with the actor, that ought to mean that the actor lives and dies with the character. I think that is correct, but it needs clarification" [p.27].

The stage/screen distinction proper: "For the stage, an actor works himself into a role; for the screen, a performer takes the role onto himself" [p.27]. "The stage actor explores his potentialities and the possibilities of his role simultaneously; in performance these meet at a point in spiritual space — the better the performance, the deeper the point" [pp.27–28]. The baseball analogy: "a role in a play is like a position in a game, say, third base: various people can play it, but the great third baseman... matches [his skills and instincts] most intimately with his discoveries of the possibilities and necessities of third base" [p.28]. By contrast: "The screen performer explores his role like an attic and takes stock of his physical and temperamental endowment; he lends his being to the role and accepts only what fits; the rest is nonexistent" [p.28]. "On the stage there are two beings, and the being of the character assaults the being of the actor; the actor survives only by yielding. A screen performance requires not so much training as planning" [p.28]. The decisive inversion: "the screen performer is essentially not an actor at all: he is the *subject* of study, and a study not his own. (That is what the content of a photograph is — its subject.) On a screen the study is projected; on a stage the actor is the projector" [p.28].

The star: "An exemplary stage performance is one which, for a time, most fully creates a character. After Paul Scofield's performance in King Lear we know who King Lear is" [p.28]. "An exemplary screen performance is one in which, at a time, a star is born. After The Maltese Falcon we know a new star, only distantly a person. 'Bogart' means 'the figure created in a given set of films'" [p.28]. "His presence in those films is who he is, not merely in the sense in which a photograph of an event is that event; but in the sense that if those films did not exist, Bogart would not exist, the name 'Bogart' would not mean what it does" [p.28]. "The figure it names is not only in our presence, we are in his, in the only sense we could ever be. That is all the 'presence' he has" [p.28]. He complicates it: Humphrey Bogart "was a man, and he appeared in movies both before and after the ones that created 'Bogart'" — some created no star (the stable groom in *Dark Victory*), some defined "stars — anyway meteors" possibly "incompatible with Bogart (e.g., Duke Mantee and Fred C. Dobbs)" yet related to the figure [p.28]. He notes that Bogart "was both an accomplished actor and a vivid subject for a camera" — a rare combination ("just as some people are both good pitchers and good hitters") — and that it is "surprising that the word 'actor' keeps on being used in place of the more beautiful and more accurate word 'star'; the stars are only to gaze at, after the fact, and their actions divine our projects" [p.28]. Finally: "we must note the sense in which the creation of a (screen) performer is also the creation of a character — not the kind of character an author creates, but the kind that certain real people are: a *type*" [p.28].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "The audience in a theater can be defined as those to whom the actors are present while they are not present to the actors. But movies allow the audience to be mechanically absent." [pp.25–26]
- "In viewing a movie my helplessness is mechanically assured: I am present not at something happening, which I must confirm, but at something that has happened, which I absorb (like a memory). In this, movies resemble novels, a fact mirrored in the sound of narration itself, whose tense is the past." [p.26]
- "in a theater a real man is there, and in a movie no real man is there." [p.26]
- "What that means must not be denied; but the fact remains to be understood." [p.26]
- "in live television, what is present to us while it is happening is not the world, but an event standing out from the world. Its point is not to reveal, but to cover (as with a gun), to keep something on view." [p.26]
- "But a human something is, and something unlike anything else we know." [pp.26–27]
- "since the topic is the human being, who is present." [p.27]
- "For the stage, an actor works himself into a role; for the screen, a performer takes the role onto himself." [p.27]
- "he lends his being to the role and accepts only what fits; the rest is nonexistent." [p.28]
- "the screen performer is essentially not an actor at all: he is the subject of study, and a study not his own. (That is what the content of a photograph is — its subject.) On a screen the study is projected; on a stage the actor is the projector." [p.28]
- "After The Maltese Falcon we know a new star, only distantly a person. 'Bogart' means 'the figure created in a given set of films.'" [p.28]
- "if those films did not exist, Bogart would not exist, the name 'Bogart' would not mean what it does. The figure it names is not only in our presence, we are in his, in the only sense we could ever be. That is all the 'presence' he has." [p.28]
- "the creation of a (screen) performer is also the creation of a character — not the kind of character an author creates, but the kind that certain real people are: a type." [p.28]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- The theater audience vs. movie audience — present-to/not-present-to; the movie audience is "mechanically absent."
- Bazin's "relay of presence" ("as by mirrors") — refuted: it fits live television, not film; and live TV "covers" rather than "reveals."
- Panofsky on Othello/Nora vs. Garbo-as-Anna-Christie / Robert Montgomery-as-murderer — the character exists independently on stage; on film "lives and dies with the actor."
- Third base / the great third baseman (baseball analogy) — a stage role is a *position* various people can play.
- Paul Scofield in *King Lear* — exemplary stage performance: "we know who King Lear is."
- *The Maltese Falcon* / "Bogart" — exemplary screen performance: "a star is born"; the figure created by a set of films.
- Humphrey Bogart (the man) — *Dark Victory* (stable groom, no star created), Duke Mantee, Fred C. Dobbs (meteors related to "Bogart"); "both an accomplished actor and a vivid subject for a camera" (the pitcher/hitter rarity).

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *mechanical absence (of the audience)* — film mechanically defeats our presence to reality; my invisibility/inaudibility/fixity "no longer needs accounting for," unlike a stage convention.
- *helplessness mechanically assured / the past tense of film* — I am present "not at something happening... but at something that has happened, which I absorb (like a memory)"; film's tense, like the novel's narration, is past.
- *present at / not present to* — the structure of the spectator's relation to the human "something" on screen (looking at him without being present to him).
- *a human something / who is present* — the screen figure is "unlike anything else we know"; the question shifts from *what* to *who* is present.
- *actor (works himself into a role) vs. performer (takes the role onto himself)* — the founding stage/screen distinction.
- *the rest is nonexistent* — the screen performer accepts only what fits his being; whatever does not fit simply does not exist (vs. the stage's "two beings").
- *subject of study vs. projector* — the screen performer is the *subject* (= the content of a photograph); on stage the actor *is* the projector, on screen "the study is projected."
- *star* — the figure created by a set of films; "all the 'presence' he has"; existence-dependent on the films; "more beautiful and more accurate" than "actor."
- *type* — the kind of character the creation of a screen performer also creates: "not the kind an author creates, but the kind that certain real people are."

---

---

### Ch 5 — Types; Cycles as Genres [book pp.29–36]

**Project:** Cavell turns from the physical medium of cinema "in general" to the "specific forms or genres" film has taken historically, and argues that film does not realize itself in individual *works* (as the older arts do) but through *types* — the recurring individualities of star-performers — so that a *cycle* is a *genre* and a *genre* is a *medium*.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell opens by completing the type/star thread from Ch 4: the creation of a screen performer is also the creation of a "character," but not the kind an author creates — rather "the kind that certain real people are: a type" [p.29]. He notes that "star" is more accurate than "actor" because stars are "only to gaze at, after the fact, and their actions divine our projects" [p.29].

He then frames his question against Panofsky and Bazin, both of whom "begin at the beginning," approving that early movies adapted popular/folk arts (farce, melodrama, circus, music hall, romance), and both of whom are "gratifyingly contemptuous of intellectuals who could not come to terms with those facts of life" [pp.29–30]. Cavell's own question is sharper: *why* did these forms and themes and characters "lend themselves to film?" Bazin only expresses gratitude for film "revivifying these ancient forms" and justifies adaptation in general; Hauser gives "wrong answers" with the "baffling" remark that "Only a young art can be popular" (Cavell rebuts: did Verdi, Dickens, Chaplin, Frank Loesser work in young arts?) [p.30]. Cavell insists movies were not "destined to popularity" — at first they were no more popular than other entertainment — and that calling movies "young" means almost nothing, since we don't know the normal life span of an art or its unit of measure [p.30].

He then engages Panofsky's claim about "the unique and specific possibilities of the new medium." Panofsky defines these as "dynamization of space and spatialization of time" — in a movie things move, you can be moved instantaneously from anywhere to anywhere, and witness successive events that happen simultaneously [p.30]. Panofsky calls these "self-evident to the point of triviality." Cavell does not dispute their importance but asks a prior question: *what does it mean to call them possibilities at all?* [p.31].

This is the chapter's pivotal philosophical move. **[재구성: 카벨의 핵심 논점은 매체의 "가능성"이란 단순히 기계적으로 할 수 있는 일(actualities)이 아니라, *의미(significance)*가 부여될 때에야 비로소 가능성이 된다는 것이다.]** He asks why the medium didn't "begin and remain in the condition of home movies" — one shot tacked onto another, cut by subject (newsreels essentially did this, and were valuable enough to justify the invention) [p.31]. The obvious answer — someone "saw the possibilities" of cutting, editing, varying camera distance — fails, because these are "mere actualities of film mechanics: every home movie and newsreel contains them" [p.31]. What makes them *possibilities of the medium* is realizing "what will give them significance" — e.g., "the narrative and physical rhythms of melodrama, farce, American comedy of the 1930s" [p.31]. Crucially, film-makers did not first see possibilities and then look for content; rather "someone with the wish to make a movie saw that certain established forms would give point to certain properties of film" [p.31]. Therefore "the aesthetic possibilities of a medium are not givens" — you can no more deduce what gives significance to projecting photographic images by thinking about them than you can deduce the significance of paint by inspecting paint [p.31]. "Thinking about them" consists in "whatever the useful criticism of an art consists in" [p.31]. His example: painters dripped paint before Pollock, even deliberately, but "Pollock made dripping into a medium of painting" [pp.31–32].

He restates the thesis: the first successful movies "were not applications of a medium that was defined by given possibilities, but the creation of a medium by their giving significance to specific possibilities." Hence "Only the art itself can discover its possibilities, and the discovery of a new possibility is the discovery of a new medium" [p.32]. A medium is "something through which or by means of which something specific gets done or said in particular ways" — forms, like forms of speech, ways "to get through to someone, to make sense" [p.32].

Cavell then attacks Panofsky's claim that early silent film was an "unknown language . . . forced upon a public not yet capable of reading it" [p.32]. He counters that from the beginning the language was *known* — to its creators, "those who found themselves speaking it" — and that at first there was "no 'public' in question; there were just some curious people" [p.32]. The public arrived quickly, "but that just proves how easy the thing was to know." If anything was "unknown," it was "less like a language than like a fact — in particular, the fact that something is intelligible" [p.32]. He dismisses Panofsky's Saxon-peasant analogy (a man pouring water over another's head ≈ baptism): such understanding has "nothing special to do with the problems of a moviegoer," since the meaning of baptism is still hard to grasp for anyone outside the practice [pp.32–33].

He diagnoses *why* Panofsky needed that explanation: to account for the persistence of "fixed iconography" — "the well-remembered types of the Vamp and the Straight Girl . . . the Family Man, and the Villain" — whose conduct was "predetermined accordingly" [p.33]. Panofsky claimed such devices "became gradually less necessary" and were "virtually abolished by the invention of the talking film." Cavell flatly denies this: such devices "persist as long as there are still Westerns and gangster films and comedies and musicals and romances" [p.33]. The *specific* iconography the Villain is given changes with the times, but "that his iconography remains specific . . . seems undeniable: if Jack Palance in Shane is not a Villain, no honest home was ever in danger" [p.33]. Films changed not because we no longer need such explanations "but because we can't accept them" [p.33].

Now the central positive thesis: these facts are "accounted for by the actualities of the film medium itself: types are exactly what carry the forms movies have relied upon" [p.33]. Media of movies "created new types, or combinations and ironic reversals of types; but there they were, and stayed" [p.33]. He confronts the objection — does this mean movies "can never create individuals, only types?" His answer: "this is the movies' way of creating individuals: they create individualities" [p.33]. The decisive claim about *type*: "what makes someone a type is not his similarity with other members of that type but his striking separateness from other people" [p.33].

He develops this through a sustained meditation on black performers as a counter-case. Until recently, types of black human beings "were not created in film: black people were stereotypes — mammies, shiftless servants, loyal retainers, entertainers" [p.33]. Audiences were "not in a position to be given individualities that projected particular ways of inhabiting a social role; we recognized only the role" [p.33]. When humanity broke through the role, the result was "a revelation not of a human individuality, but of an entire realm of humanity becoming visible" [pp.33–34]. He reads two scenes: (1) *Gone With the Wind* — Vivien Leigh slapping Butterfly McQueen exposes the dehumanizing assumption that with "such creatures knowledge of the body comes from nowhere," that they are "to be trusted absolutely or not at all, like lions in a cage" [p.34]; (2) *Breaking Point* (Curtiz) — the camera holding on the waiting black child whose father has been killed "overwhelms the yarn we had been shown," but Cavell judges the effect a failure: it "blot[s] out attention to individual suffering by invoking a massive social evil about which this film has nothing to say" [p.34].

From this he draws the general law: "the individuality captured on film naturally takes precedence over the social role in which that individuality gets expressed" [pp.34–35]. Because on film social role "appears arbitrary or incidental," movies have "an inherent tendency toward the democratic, or anyway the idea of human equality" — though film's "equally natural attraction to crowds" gives it "opposite tendencies toward the fascistic or populistic" [p.35]. This depends on recognizing film types as "inhabited by figures we have met or may well meet in other circumstances": "the recognized recurrence of film performers will become a central idea as we proceed" [p.35]. Black performers, until recently, had "no other place to recur in" except the original role — we wouldn't expect them as parents or siblings; Cavell can't recall a black person in a film "making an ordinary purchase" (Pinky and A Raisin in the Sun "prove the rule": in the former, a purchase is a climactic scene; in the latter, it is the whole subject and structure) [p.35].

He then celebrates the stars who "provided the movie camera with human subjects — individuals capable of filling its need for individualities" [p.35]. One gesture, syllable, or "passing mannerism was enough to single them out from all other creatures." They "realized the myth of singularity — that we can still be found, behind our disguises of bravado and cowardice, by someone, perhaps a god, capable of defeating our self-defeats" [p.35]. This singularity was "always more important than their distinction by beauty"; it "made them more like us," shifting their difference from a "matter of metaphysics, to which we must accede" to "a matter of responsibility, to which we must bend" — which made them "even more glamorous." That they could "stand upon their singularity!" is itself the source of awe: "If one did that, one might be found, and called out, too soon, or at an inconvenient moment" [pp.35–36].

He distinguishes the genuine problem from the false one: what was wrong with type-casting was "not that it displaced some other, better principle of casting, but that factors irrelevant to film-making often influenced the particular figures chosen" [p.36]. Likewise the existence of movie *cycles*, condemned by some theorists as "a mark of unscrupulous commercialism," is instead "a possibility internal to the medium" — "the best emblem of the fact that a medium had been created. For a cycle is a genre (prison movies, Civil War movies, horror movies, etc.); and a genre is a medium" [p.36].

He closes by extending the type-theory: as Hollywood developed, "the original types ramified into individualities as various and subtle . . . as any set of characters who inhabited the great theaters of our world" [p.36]. Where the old theater had Pulcinella, Crispin, Harlequin, Pantaloon, the Doctor, the Captain, Columbine, ours are named "the Public Enemy, the Priest, James Cagney, Pat O'Brien, the Confederate Spy, the Army Scout, Randolph Scott, Gary Cooper, Gable, Paul Muni, the Reporter, the Sergeant, the Sheriff, the Deputy, the D.A., the Quack, the Shyster, the Other Woman, the Fallen Woman, the Moll, the Dance Hall Hostess" — note the seamless mixing of role-names and proper names [p.36]. "Hollywood was the theater in which they appeared, because the films of Hollywood constituted a world" [p.36]. Finally the great comedians — Chaplin, Keaton, W. C. Fields — "form a set of types that could not have been adapted from any other medium" and whose creation depended on what Cavell now calls two *necessities* (not mere possibilities) of the medium [pp.36–37]: **(1) "movie performers cannot project, but are projected"** (projected visibility); **(2) "photographs are of the world, in which human beings are not ontologically favored over the rest of nature, in which objects are not props but natural allies (or enemies) of the human character"** (ontological equality) [p.37]. The first permits "the sublime comprehensibility of Chaplin's natural choreography"; the second permits "his Proustian or Jamesian relationships with Murphy beds and flights of stairs," "the heroism of momentary survival, Nietzsche's man as a tightrope across an abyss" [p.37]. These permit "the philosophical mood of [Keaton's] countenance and the Olympic resourcefulness of his body," making him "perhaps the only constantly beautiful and continuously hilarious man ever seen," and they permit Fields, "heard and seen only by us," to manifest "the psychic brutalities of bourgeois civilization" behind his gentleman's attributes [p.37].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "the creation of a (screen) performer is also the creation of a character—not the kind of character an author creates, but the kind that certain real people are: a type." [p.29]
- "the aesthetic possibilities of a medium are not givens." [p.31]
- "Only the art itself can discover its possibilities, and the discovery of a new possibility is the discovery of a new medium." [p.32]
- "A medium is something through which or by means of which something specific gets done or said in particular ways." [p.32]
- "types are exactly what carry the forms movies have relied upon." [p.33]
- "Does this mean that movies can never create individuals, only types? What it means is that this is the movies' way of creating individuals: they create individualities." [p.33]
- "what makes someone a type is not his similarity with other members of that type but his striking separateness from other people." [p.33]
- "the individuality captured on film naturally takes precedence over the social role in which that individuality gets expressed." [pp.34–35]
- "movies have an inherent tendency toward the democratic, or anyway the idea of human equality." [p.35]
- "The recognized recurrence of film performers will become a central idea as we proceed." [p.35]
- "They realized the myth of singularity—that we can still be found, behind our disguises of bravado and cowardice, by someone, perhaps a god, capable of defeating our self-defeats." [p.35]
- "For a cycle is a genre (prison movies, Civil War movies, horror movies, etc.); and a genre is a medium." [p.36]
- "First, movie performers cannot project, but are projected. Second, photographs are of the world, in which human beings are not ontologically favored over the rest of nature, in which objects are not props but natural allies (or enemies) of the human character." [p.37]

*(Note: the system-prompt phrase "the camera has been said to provide the actor with his immortality" is Panofsky's, quoted by Cavell in Ch 4 just before p.27, not within pp.29–36; in this section the corresponding motif appears as the "myth of singularity" [p.35] and, negatively, in Ch 9 as Wilde "forgoing immortality" [p.56].)*

**Examples / films / persons / star-types invoked:**
- Humphrey Bogart — both "an accomplished actor and a vivid subject for a camera"; the rare double talent (like a player who is both good pitcher and good hitter) [p.29].
- Panofsky / Bazin / Hauser — theorists Cavell argues against on origins, possibilities, and "young art" [pp.29–32].
- Jackson Pollock — "made dripping into a medium of painting": the paradigm of giving significance to a possibility [pp.31–32].
- Vamp, Straight Girl, Family Man, Villain — Panofsky's "fixed iconography" types whose persistence Cavell defends [p.33].
- Jack Palance in *Shane* — proof the Villain's iconography remains specific [p.33].
- Butterfly McQueen / Vivien Leigh in *Gone With the Wind* — the slap scene exposing the dehumanizing assumption about blackness [p.34].
- John Garfield / Patricia Neal in *Breaking Point* (Curtiz) — the waiting black child; a misfire that "blots out individual suffering" [p.34].
- *Pinky*; *A Raisin in the Sun* — "prove the rule" that black figures had no ordinary place to recur (the purchase as climax / whole structure) [p.35].
- Commedia-style types (Pulcinella, Crispin, Harlequin, Pantaloon, the Doctor, the Captain, Columbine) vs. Hollywood types (Cagney, Pat O'Brien, Randolph Scott, Cooper, Gable, Paul Muni, the Public Enemy, the Reporter, the Sergeant, the Moll, the Fallen Woman, etc.) — the recurrent "world" of Hollywood [p.36].
- Chaplin, Keaton, W. C. Fields — a set of types "that could not have been adapted from any other medium," illustrating the two *necessities*; Chaplin's choreography, Keaton's body/countenance, Fields's gentleman-mask over bourgeois brutality [pp.36–37].

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **type** — a recurrent screen individuality; defined not by similarity to others of its kind but by "striking separateness from other people" [p.33]; the unit through which film creates meaning.
- **individuality** — the movies' way of "creating individuals"; the projected separateness that "takes precedence over the social role" [pp.33–35].
- **stereotype** — a degraded, merely-role recognition (e.g., early black figures) lacking projected individuality [p.33].
- **possibilities of a medium** — not "givens" or mechanical actualities, but properties to which significance has been given through criticism/use [pp.31–32].
- **necessities of the medium** — two conditions stronger than possibilities: projected visibility ("performers cannot project, but are projected") and ontological equality ("human beings are not ontologically favored over the rest of nature") [p.37].
- **cycle / genre / medium** — the identity chain: "a cycle is a genre; and a genre is a medium" [p.36].
- **myth of singularity** — that we "can still be found, behind our disguises," by someone capable of "defeating our self-defeats" [p.35].
- **recurrence (recognized recurrence of film performers)** — the structural fact that we meet star-figures again across films, grounding type [p.35].

---

### Ch 6 — Ideas of Origin [book pp.37–40]

**Project:** Cavell speculates about film's *origin*, arguing that the invention of the photographic picture is not the same as the creation of photography "as a medium for making sense," and that film satisfies, *magically* and *automatically*, the old "myth of total cinema" — the wish for the world re-created in its own image — by letting us "view it unseen."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell observes it is "inevitable" to speculate about film's origin because, "despite its recentness, its origin remains obscure" [p.37]. The technical facts (inventors, cameras, fixing and moving the image) are known; the problem is that "the invention of the photographic picture is not the same thing as the creation of photography as a medium for making sense" [pp.37–38]. A chronicle of preceding facts "does not explain why it happened when and as it did" [p.38].

He attacks Panofsky's opening formula: "It was not an artistic urge that gave rise to the discovery . . . of a new technique; it was a technical invention that gave rise to the discovery . . . of a new art" [p.38]. We "seem to understand this, but do we understand it?" An "artistic urge" is no explanation — "about as useful as explaining the rise of modern science by appealing to 'a scientific urge'"; such urges "are themselves rather badly in need of explanation" [p.38]. Moreover "the motion picture is not a new technique, any more than the airplane is" — and he asks the deflationary question "What did we use to do that such a thing enables us to do better?" [p.38]. Yet "some idea of flying, and an urge to do it, preceded the mechanical invention of the airplane" [p.38]. **[재구성: 카벨의 함의는, 매체의 발생은 단순한 기술 발명으로 설명되지 않으며, 그 기술이 충족시키는 *관념·소망*(idea/wish)이 먼저 있었다는 것이다 — 따라서 origin의 문제는 기술사가 아니라 관념사의 문제다.]**

He takes Bazin to reverse "the apparent relation between the relevant technology and the idea of cinema," emphasizing that "the idea preceded the technology, parts of it by centuries, and that parts of the technology preceded the invention of movies, some of it by centuries" [p.38]. So what must be explained is "not merely how the feat was technically accomplished but . . . what stood in the way of its happening earlier" [pp.38–39]. Cavell faults Bazin for omitting "the contemporary condition of the related arts as a part of the ideological superstructure that elicited the new material basis of film" [p.39]: it is "certainly relevant that the burning issue during the latter half of the nineteenth century, in painting and in the novel and in the theater, was realism." Unless film "captured possibilities opened up by the arts themselves, it is hard to imagine that its possibilities as an artistic medium would have shown up as, and as suddenly as, they did" [p.39].

He then names the founding wish: "The idea of and wish for the world re-created in its own image was satisfied at last by cinema. Bazin calls this the myth of total cinema" [p.39]. But this "had always been one of the myths of art; each of the arts had satisfied it in its own way. The mirror was in various hands held up to nature" — "in some ways it was more fully satisfied in theater" [p.39]. (An aside: because theater is "on the whole not now a major art for us," it "no longer makes contact with its historical and psychological sources," so we are rarely gripped by the "trauma" once felt when the chorus-leader turned to face the others, "suffering incarnation" [p.39].)

The key question: "What is cinema's way of satisfying the myth? Automatically, we said." But what does "automatically" mean "mythically"? "It means satisfying it without my having to do anything, satisfying it by wishing. In a word, magically" [p.39]. This yields the famous answer to his own earlier puzzle: "How could film be art, since all the major arts arise in some way out of religion? Now I can answer: Because movies arise out of magic; from below the world" [p.39].

He develops the magic thesis: "The better a film, the more it makes contact with this source of its inspiration; it never wholly loses touch with the magic lantern behind it" [p.39]. This explains why films *of* the fantastic (*The Cabinet of Dr. Caligari*, *Blood of a Poet*) and filmed *scenes* of magic (materialization/dematerialization), though they yield moods and devices, "have never established themselves as cinematic media": they are "technically and psychologically trivial compared with the medium of magic itself" [pp.39–40]. It is otherwise when presented magic is "itself made technically or physically interesting" (*The Invisible Man*, *Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde*, *Frankenstein*, *2001*), but then "that becomes another way of confirming the physicality of our world" [p.40]. "Science presents itself, in movies, as magic, which was indeed one source of science"; "projected science retains magic's mystery and forbiddenness" [p.40]. Science-fiction exploits adventure and special-effects physicality but also "the terrific mumbo-jumbo of hearsay science" (he mock-quotes: "the thing is impervious to the negative beta ray! We must reverse the atom recalcitration spatter") whose dialogue has "the surface of those tinbox-and-lever contraptions . . . convincing in prime Flash Gordon" [p.40]. Such films are carried by "the immediacy of the fantasy that motivates them" plus "the myth of the one way and last chance" of averting external danger [p.40]. The beauty of forms in Frankenstein's laboratory is essential to the film's success (computers "seem primitive in comparison"): "It always made more sense to steal from God than to try to outwit him" [p.40].

The chapter culminates in the definitive account of *how* film reproduces the world magically: "Not by literally presenting us with the world, but by permitting us to view it unseen" [p.40]. This is "not a wish for power over creation (as Pygmalion's was), but a wish not to need power, not to have to bear its burdens" — "the reverse of the myth of Faust" [pp.40–41]. "The wish for invisibility is old enough" — gods profit from it; Plato tells it as the Myth of the Ring of Gyges in *Republic* Book II [p.40]. "In viewing films, the sense of invisibility is an expression of modern privacy or anonymity" [p.40]. The world's projection "explains our forms of unknownness and of our inability to know": "we are displaced from our natural habitation within it, placed at a distance from it. The screen overcomes our fixed distance; it makes displacement appear as our natural condition" [pp.40–41]. He closes by posing the bridge to Ch 7: "What do we wish to view in this way? What specific forms discover this fundamental condition of the medium of film?" [p.41].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "the invention of the photographic picture is not the same thing as the creation of photography as a medium for making sense." [pp.37–38]
- "the motion picture is not a new technique, any more than the airplane is." [p.38]
- "The idea of and wish for the world re-created in its own image was satisfied at last by cinema. Bazin calls this the myth of total cinema." [p.39]
- "It means satisfying it without my having to do anything, satisfying it by wishing. In a word, magically" [p.39]
- "How could film be art, since all the major arts arise in some way out of religion? Now I can answer: Because movies arise out of magic; from below the world." [p.39]
- "it never wholly loses touch with the magic lantern behind it." [p.39]
- "It always made more sense to steal from God than to try to outwit him." [p.40]
- "How do movies reproduce the world magically? Not by literally presenting us with the world, but by permitting us to view it unseen." [p.40]
- "This is not a wish for power over creation (as Pygmalion's was), but a wish not to need power, not to have to bear its burdens. It is, in this sense, the reverse of the myth of Faust." [pp.40–41]
- "The screen overcomes our fixed distance; it makes displacement appear as our natural condition." [pp.40–41]

**Examples / films / persons / star-types invoked:**
- Panofsky — the "technical invention → new art" formula Cavell rejects [p.38].
- The airplane / the idea of flying — analogy: invention follows a prior idea/wish [p.38].
- Bazin's "myth of total cinema" — the wish for "the world re-created in its own image," older than film [p.39].
- *The Cabinet of Dr. Caligari*; *Blood of a Poet* — films of the fantastic that never became cinematic media (trivial vs. real magic) [pp.39–40].
- *The Invisible Man*; *Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde*; *Frankenstein*; *2001: A Space Odyssey* — presented magic made "physically interesting," confirming the world's physicality [p.40].
- *Flash Gordon* — "tinbox-and-lever" sci-fi whose mumbo-jumbo dialogue is "convincing" within its fantasy [p.40].
- Pygmalion / Faust — contrast myths: film's wish is *not* power over creation but freedom from its burdens [pp.40–41].
- Plato, *Republic* Book II, Ring of Gyges — the ancient myth of invisibility, glossing the film-viewer's condition [p.40].

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **myth of total cinema** (Bazin's) — the perennial wish for "the world re-created in its own image," satisfied "at last" by film [p.39].
- **automatically / magically** — film satisfies the myth "without my having to do anything," "by wishing," i.e., magically; the photographic/automatic basis read as *magic* [p.39].
- **from below the world / magic (vs. religion)** — film's source: not religion (origin of the other arts) but magic, "from below the world" [p.39].
- **viewing the world unseen** — film's specific mode of magical reproduction; grounds the viewer's invisibility [p.40].
- **invisibility / displacement** — the viewer's ontological status; "modern privacy or anonymity"; the screen makes "displacement appear as our natural condition" [pp.40–41].
- **myth (general sense)** — an inherited wish/idea an art satisfies; arts as the historical satisfiers of myths [p.39].

---

### Ch 7 — Baudelaire and the Myths of Film [book pp.41–46]

**Project:** Cavell reads Baudelaire's *The Painter of Modern Life* as an unwitting *anticipation of film*: Baudelaire's wish for an art of "modernity" and "presentness," and his catalogue of iconographical items (carriages, cosmetics, fashion, dandies, women, the military man), are really "the wish for photography, in particular for motion pictures" — "stores of cinematic obsession" — and they reveal film's inherently *pornographic* relation between clothing and the body.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell announces that the wish and forms posed at the end of Ch 6 "can be glimpsed in a famous but puzzling text": Baudelaire's *The Painter of Modern Life* [p.41]. It is "puzzling" because Baudelaire, "in so many ways the prophet of the modern, has seemed to me merely perverse or superficial in this most ambitious of his theoretical statements about art" — a perversity "fated partly by his lung-bursting inflation of Delacroix, partly by his comparative casualness about what his friends Courbet and Manet were accomplishing" [p.41]. Baudelaire called for "a realization of the other half of art whose first half is 'the eternal and unchangeable'": the half he wanted he named "modernity," that which is "ephemeral, fugitive, contingent upon the occasion," the "description of contemporary life," "the nature of beauty in the present time" [p.41]. Baudelaire: "The pleasure we derive from the depiction of the present arises not only from the beauty in which it can be attired, but also from its essential quality of being the present" [pp.41–42].

Cavell's counter: "the quality of presentness is exactly what Courbet and Manet themselves craved, and in establishing it as it could be established in painting, they were establishing modernist painting" [p.42]. So Baudelaire's iconographic "paraphernalia" (Dandies, Cosmetics, Uniforms) prove nothing about painting's possibilities — being "forms of contemporary happiness" and "our adornments" is no argument for serious art [p.42]. (Aside: "Baudelaire is a progenitor not only of modernists but also of their parodies, the modernizers" [p.42].) Cavell grants Baudelaire's deeper insight — "Nothing less than everything is new in a new period—not merely attirements, but physiques and postures and gaits, and nudity itself. Courbet's and Manet's nudes are as different from their ancestors as a Dandy is from a Magus" [p.42] — but presses the puzzle: why did Baudelaire "succumb to the idea that these natural and historical and phenomenological transformations were something artists should *make happen*"? And why "forgo serious art in favor of modest draftsmanship"? Why "that special set of iconographical items"? [p.42].

His answer is the chapter's thesis: out of Baudelaire's "despair of happiness," "disgust with its official made-up substitutes," "knowledge of his isolation and estrangement from the present and the foreignness of the past (and, I believe, in his experiments with hashish), he found the wish for photography, in particular for motion pictures — the wish for that specific simultaneity of presence and absence which only the cinema will satisfy" [p.42]. **[재구성: 카벨은 보들레르의 "모더니티/현재성"에의 갈망을 회화로는 충족 불가능한 소망으로 보고, 그 소망의 진짜 충족처는 영화 — 곧 *현존과 부재의 동시성*(simultaneity of presence and absence) — 라고 재배치한다.]**

Why did actual photographs not satisfy the wish? Cavell answers via Baudelaire's *Salon* of the same year: "Photographs did not look like photographs to him; they looked like imitation paintings, which is more or less what they were" [p.42]. Photography was praised as "the royal road to drawing" [p.42]. "Hindsight reveals that photography would soon stop imitating paintings and invent itself. But hindsight is of its essence blind to the past, a way of looking away from the present" [pp.42–43]. He distinguishes *overview* from *perspective*: "an overview is of its essence a losing substitute for perspective." From the height of overview "you couldn't tell a painting from a photograph"; the truer claim is that painting affected photography "only when painting had already affected itself and was good and ready" [p.43].

Cavell then proposes to "read as an anticipation of film" by simply reciting Baudelaire's chapter titles "against our knowledge of cinema": Fashion, The Man of the World, Crowds, The Child, War-Sketches, Pomps and Ceremonies, The Military Man, The Dandy, Cosmetics, Women and Courtesans, Carriages — "Here are stores of cinematic obsession" [p.43].

He works examples. **Carriages:** Baudelaire — "a carriage, like a ship, is lent by its movement a mysterious and complex grace which it is very difficult to note down in shorthand" [p.43]. Cavell: such grace is "impossible to imagine its being seen on paper; but it is the very grain of moving pictures." Carriages "carry the weight of all those conveyances and machines whose movements are so lovingly studied in the film — not . . . because 'we like to see things move,' but because their characteristic movements have that 'mysterious and complex grace' divined by Baudelaire" [p.43]. Film "returns to us and extends our first fascination with objects, with their inner and fixed lives," and studies "what is done in and with them" — contrivances with "a drama of its own, its unique logic of beginning, middle, and end," which "create the kind of creature who may use them" [pp.43–44]. Baudelaire's praise of "the series of geometrical figures that the object . . . successively and rapidly creates in space" is "not describing anything a draftsman showed him; he is having a prophetic hallucination" [p.44].

**Cosmetics:** Baudelaire's praise extends beyond make-up to "fashion generally and beyond that to the artifices necessary to civilized life as a whole: its streets, parks, buildings, furnishings, commodities — the secretions and scaffoldings of our forms of life" [p.44]. For Baudelaire these "take on the value of specific settings — which is their indispensable value in film, where sets must be in reality, whether genuine or counterfeit. They have to have all of the surfaces and sides of reality, for a film takes place wholly behind the scenes" [p.44].

**Fashion / clothing and the body** — the chapter's deepest move. Baudelaire: fashions "must be seen as vitalized and vivified by the lively woman who wore them" [p.44]. Cavell reads this not as the cliché that worn clothes "take on a life of their own" but as a response "to the fact that they simultaneously conceal and reveal the body" [p.44]. He then draws the painting/theater vs. film contrast: "In paintings and in the theater, clothes reveal a person's character and his station, also his body and its attitudes. The clothes are the body, as the expression is the face" [p.44]. But "In movies, clothes conceal; hence they conceal something separate from them; the something is therefore empirically there to be unconcealed. A woman in a movie is dressed (as she is, when she is, in reality), hence potentially undressed" [pp.44–45]. Baudelaire's earlier line ("a woman and her garb [are] an indivisible whole") "strikes one as uncharacteristically pious" [p.45]. Cavell distinguishes *nudity* from *being undressed*: "A nude is a fine enough thing in itself, and no reason is required to explain nakedness . . . 'the human body is the best picture of the human soul.' But to be undressed is something else, and it does require a reason; in seeing a film of a desirable woman we are looking for a reason" [p.45]. Joined to "our ontological status — invisibility — it is inevitable that we should expect to find a reason . . . no matter how consistently our expectancy is frustrated" [p.45]. Hence the famous claim: "The ontological conditions of the motion picture reveal it as inherently pornographic (though not of course inveterately so)" [p.45]. The "million times" a shot ended as the zipper finished its course, or the robe fell behind a shower door, "were not sudden enticements or pornographic asides; they were satisfactions, however partial, of an inescapable demand. All the Hays Offices in the world could not prevent that; they could merely enforce the interruptions. A woman and her garb are a divisible whole" [p.45].

Cavell adds a contemporary coda (written "while I was completing this book"): the new audience-classification system made "nude scenes mandatory in adult films, like blood" [p.45]. He withholds judgment but notes "genitalia seem to be as difficult either to follow around or to ignore as the microphone was in early talkies," and that "the new half-freedom unhinges the camera; it either becomes fixated or skittish-lyrical. The interruption occurs at a more painful point" [pp.45–46]. He judges cases where screen sexuality *works*: *Belle de Jour* (extends Buñuel's knowledge of sexuality's "crude beauty and privacy"); *Women in Love* (serious, stylish, set in the past, nudity recurs enough "to keep your attitude from wobbling," occasions "righter than usual"); *The Prime of Miss Jean Brodie* (a self-possessed girl "dressing from scratch" yields "an unpredictably tender and eventful plash of motions") [p.46].

He ends with a polemic on *pornography*: "Straight pornography is not a problem: a drug is not a food" [p.46]. Against liberals who say pornography's moral problem is "its invasion of the last corner of privacy," Cavell counters that from the performers' side this is "ludicrous — like saying that the moral problem about child labor is that the kids worked in poor light," and from the audience's side "it is bullying; pornography is more likely to be privacy's last proof" [p.46]. An audience at a pornographic film is "more respectful of one another's needfulness"; though each is "penetrated with the knowledge the others must be having, no one supposes that this lessens his privacy" [p.46]. He notes "the absolute attention, the common awe, when all holds are unbarred. An artist must envy that power" [p.46]. That such an experience is for many "confined to the occasion of viewing a pornographic film, deprived of what it analogues in serious art or in actual sex, is an indictment of society's invasion and enforcement of privacy" [p.46].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "the quality of presentness is exactly what Courbet and Manet themselves craved, and in establishing it as it could be established in painting, they were establishing modernist painting." [p.42]
- "Nothing less than everything is new in a new period—not merely attirements, but physiques and postures and gaits, and nudity itself." [p.42]
- "he found the wish for photography, in particular for motion pictures—the wish for that specific simultaneity of presence and absence which only the cinema will satisfy." [p.42]
- "an overview is of its essence a losing substitute for perspective." [p.43]
- "Here are stores of cinematic obsession" [p.43]
- "It is impossible to imagine its being seen on paper; but it is the very grain of moving pictures." [p.43]
- "he is not describing anything a draftsman showed him; he is having a prophetic hallucination." [p.44]
- "a film takes place wholly behind the scenes." [p.44]
- "In paintings and in the theater . . . The clothes are the body, as the expression is the face." [p.44]
- "A woman in a movie is dressed . . . hence potentially undressed." [pp.44–45]
- "But to be undressed is something else, and it does require a reason; in seeing a film of a desirable woman we are looking for a reason." [p.45]
- "The ontological conditions of the motion picture reveal it as inherently pornographic (though not of course inveterately so)." [p.45]
- "A woman and her garb are a divisible whole." [p.45]
- "Straight pornography is not a problem: a drug is not a food." [p.46]

**Examples / films / persons / star-types invoked:**
- Baudelaire, *The Painter of Modern Life* (and his *Salon* of the same year) — the central anticipatory text; "modernity"/"presentness"; chapter-title catalogue [pp.41–43].
- Delacroix — Baudelaire's "lung-bursting inflation" of him, marking his blindness to his own moment [p.41].
- Courbet and Manet — Baudelaire's friends actually achieving "presentness"/modernist painting that he undervalued [pp.41–42].
- Hays Offices — censorship that could only "enforce the interruptions," not abolish the pornographic demand [p.45].
- *Belle de Jour* (Buñuel); *Women in Love*; *The Prime of Miss Jean Brodie* — instances where screen sexuality "works well" [p.46].
- (Microphone in early talkies) — analogy for how genitalia "unhinge the camera" once mandatory [pp.45–46].

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **modernity / presentness** — Baudelaire's "ephemeral, fugitive, contingent" half of art, "the nature of beauty in the present time"; the quality Courbet/Manet truly secured in painting [pp.41–42].
- **simultaneity of presence and absence** — the wish "only the cinema will satisfy"; the real object of Baudelaire's longing [p.42].
- **overview vs. perspective** — overview as "a losing substitute for perspective," blind to past and present [p.43].
- **the grain of moving pictures** — the medium's native register for "mysterious and complex grace" of movement (carriages, machines) [p.43].
- **specific settings / "behind the scenes"** — film's requirement that sets "must be in reality" with "all of the surfaces and sides of reality"; "a film takes place wholly behind the scenes" [p.44].
- **conceal/reveal; dressed → potentially undressed** — film's clothing logic (vs. painting/theater where "the clothes are the body") [pp.44–45].
- **nudity vs. being undressed** — nakedness needs no reason; being undressed "does require a reason," which the invisible viewer perpetually seeks [p.45].
- **inherently pornographic** — the ontological condition (not inveterate fact) of motion pictures, given invisibility + the dressed/undressed structure [p.45].

---

### Ch 8 — The Military Man and the Woman [book pp.47–54]

**Project:** Cavell takes two of Baudelaire's three "presiding genius" types — the Military Man and the Woman — and shows what each *myth* means for film: the Military Man asserts "the myth of community" (society as man's natural state), while the Woman is film's supreme individuating subject; he then probes how movies handle marriage, community, families, and the false hope that "explicitness" solves film's evasions.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell sets the three Baudelairean types — "the Dandy, the Woman, and the Military Man" — apart from the iconographic paraphernalia: these "are not merely further items in cinema's daily sustenance; they have been movies' presiding geniuses" [p.47].

**The Military Man** "represents man in uniform, which is to say, men doing the work of the world, in consort, each exercising the virtues of his position or failing them; each bearing the marks of his condition. This figure asserts the myth of community, the idea that society is man's natural state" [p.47]. He notes there are whole "genres of film — with nothing but men in them, or with just one or two peripheral women" [p.47].

**The Woman:** Cavell quotes Baudelaire at length — Woman as "the source of the most vivid and also . . . the most lasting delights," the Being toward whom "all the efforts of most men are directed," "as terrible and incommunicable as God" (but differing in that the Infinite cannot communicate because it would "blind and overwhelm the Finite," whereas Woman is "perhaps incomprehensible only because she has nothing to communicate"), the Being "through whom . . . artists and poets create their finest jewels," "the source of the most enervating pleasures and the most fruitful griefs," "a ray of light, a look, an invitation to happiness, sometimes a watchword" [pp.47–48]. Cavell glosses: "how much of the history of film is a history of the firmament of individual women established there. Individual men, even the greatest, with few exceptions are fads or conveniences by comparison" [p.48]. "Remarkable directors have existed solely to examine the same woman over and over through film." In many Garbo or Dietrich films "next to nothing may be memorable, or even tolerable, but these women themselves. The miracle is that they are enough" [p.48]. He illustrates the asymmetry with the *pas de deux* analogy: you can make satisfying films where "Bette Davis is supported (if that is the word) by George Brent; but reverse the qualities and the thing would be impossible" [p.48]. "The most durable male star cannot survive a cipher as his object," and if one is "repelled by a given woman, no man can rescue the film." (He can tolerate Fred MacMurray for the films and the women around him — e.g., Barbara Stanwyck — but "even Tyrone Power will have trouble getting me back to see a film with Alice Faye in it") [p.48].

Cavell then advances a structural sociology of movie love and community. *If* movies assert community "typically through male comradeship (rather than, as in novels and the theater, through families)"; and *if* "women are anti-community (because they interfere with comradeship)"; and *if* "neither the woman outside (i.e., sexuality) nor the movie wife (i.e., frigidity) are reproductive, except accidentally, which further interferes with community" — "then we had better stop drawing the morals of movies too hastily" [p.48]. **[재구성: 카벨은 영화의 공동체가 가족이 아니라 남성 동지애로 성립하며, 여성은 — 외부의 성적 여성이든 불감의 아내든 — 재생산하지 않기에 공동체를 교란한다는 가설을 세우고, 이로부터 "선은 보상받고 악은 패배한다"는 통념적 도덕 독해를 의심한다.]**

He tests the cliché "that virtue is always rewarded and vice vanquished" [p.48]. The "woman outside" may not get the man, "so she suffers for her acceptance of sexuality. Civilization exacts its discontents from us all. But her recognition of sexuality permits her intelligence and depth and independence." At those prices, "what is virtue rewarded with, and who wants marriage?" (the question peaks in *Children of Paradise*) [pp.48–49]. He catalogs the recurrent narrative logics by which films keep marriage *out of view* or treat it as "as illicit as sexuality outside":
- When the man "goes home to his wife, his life is over" (*Intermezzo*; *Now, Voyager*; *The Man in the Gray Flannel Suit*) [p.49];
- Or "the woman outside suffers a death or withdrawal in time for the man's incorporation of her into his marriage" (*Under Two Flags*; *Destry Rides Again*; *High Noon*; *A Stolen Life*, where good/bad mothers-lovers are literal twins) [p.49];
- Or "the man's identity is doubled so that it is half of him who withdraws or is killed by the other half" (*The Corsican Brothers*; *The Prisoner of Zenda*; *The Man in the Iron Mask*; *Shane*; *The Man Who Shot Liberty Valance*) [p.49].
He reads further variants: *The Lady Eve* (comic — the man thinks they aren't married); *The Great Lie* (hysterical — the man "only thinks his wife is his child's mother"); *The Postman Always Rings Twice* and *Double Indemnity* (the woman "seduces her seducer and sanctions the killing of her husband," understood as "his test of love"; the lovers die because they killed, but "also . . . because they transgress the deeper law against combining sex and marriage") [p.49]. "In a thousand other instances the marriage must not be seen, and the walk into the sunset is into a dying star: they live happily ever after — as long as they keep walking" [pp.49–50]. Where the marriage *is* established and worth keeping, "the drama turning upon getting it back together" (*Philadelphia Story*; *Woman of the Year*), "it is the man whose feeling is constant and predominant"; where the woman works the reconciliation (*The Awful Truth*) "she is made to use wiles and finally requires the aid of a door ex machina" [p.50].

He treats Bergman's *Smiles of a Summer Night* as "close enough to the surface of this tradition for its brilliance to remember and confirm that old resourcefulness" (with *Lesson in Love* as "a set of sketches for the later masterpiece") [pp.50–51]. Here the woman effects the reconciliation "through a calculated set of arrangements," yet "her efforts are not wiles" — she gives the man "more than he asked . . . in contriving for him to discover the truth of his desires"; "The passiveness of her man is neither contemptible nor stimulating to the woman, but a fact within her acceptance of him" [p.51]. He praises its "self-confident wittiness of its references to *A Midsummer Night's Dream*," and ranks it (after *Rules of the Game*) "about as close as we've got to another *Marriage of Figaro*. Alas" [p.51].

On **families:** "A believable family has seemed as hard to get on film as a believable intellectual or university classroom" [p.51]. The best film families (*Pride and Prejudice*, *How Green Was My Valley*, *The Little Foxes*, *Meet Me in St. Louis*, *I Remember Mama*, *To Kill a Mockingbird*, *Splendor in the Grass*) "are set in the elegiac past," and succeed by extraordinary child actors, electric unifying performances (Olivier and Greer Garson), or exceptional ensembles "who play together throughout" (he defends Warren Beatty, Natalie Wood, Pat Hingle, Zohra Lampert as "as good together" as he claims) [pp.51–52].

Cavell then mounts an extended argument that the cure for film's "sexual or intellectual or political simplicities and evasions" is *not* "verbal or visual explicitness": "No one will deny that inexplicitness can be abused, but so can explicitness. An avoided subject is not confronted by calling it names" [p.52]. His central case is the *These Three* (1936) / *The Children's Hour* (1962) pair (both Wyler). The later film "is evidently proud of its new freedom to mention explicitly the homosexual attachment" the earlier had to recast as heterosexual; Cavell finds the pride "misplaced; the earlier version is psychologically deeper and more adult" — its talents "stifled by a structure that depends upon halfhearted explicitness to guarantee sincerity and depth of feeling" [p.52]. He reads the women's "world of women" as a strategy to "reproduce their world of women by starting their own school for girls," depending "upon a pact to expel desire and fruitfulness," where "Heterosexuality is as direct a threat to it as homosexuality" — "What is monstrous to it is not merely overt perversion, but desire itself" [pp.52–53]. He reads the male's entrance (Joel McCrea) "through the rafters of the decaying house . . . enveloped in beekeeper's clothing" as a valid insight: he "presents maleness as the source of stings, and as impervious to their repulsions" [p.53]; in such an environment, introducing homosexuality "merely shows it as a deeper strategy to avoid the intrusive company of males," which the events "can merely purvey . . . with no idea . . . about why such a choice should ever be made" [p.53]. The later version's "external, peeping treatment" shows in "the mechanical dramaturgy of the suicide" — the close-up premonition that comes "From her wish?", and the question "Must she kill herself because she cannot live with the knowledge that she is homosexual? That destroys our sense of the moving confession of love she has just given" [p.53].

He analyzes the one shared "piece of business" — "a hand of one covers the mouth of the other to stop a question" — as "the point at which the films declare themselves as studies of explicitness" [pp.52–53]. In *These Three* it is "a moment of courtesy and tenderness": the man stops "the explicit question that would mean her distrust of him" (a "simple question of fact" — betrayal with his best friend — "more humiliating to ask than to answer; but he answers"), leaving her to ask "what has brought her to this question," so she "can accommodate her love" [p.53]. In *The Children's Hour* "the point is lost": now the *woman* stops the *man's* question, and it is "no longer a question of external fact . . . but, do you return her love or do you not?" — "painful, but . . . not humiliating," and "he has a right to know" (James Garner is "not capable of that knowledge"; imagine "the young Joel McCrea" to see "its wrongness"). Her stopping it "is therefore an evasion, and her explicit denial of it is a further falseness"; at the end "we read her isolation not as ostracism but as the sealing of a secret victory: she was loved, someone died for love of her . . . but now it will never need to show; vulnerability is bypassed" [p.53]. The general moral: "If you understand homosexuality as an avoidance of consequential love or as the theft of love, you have yet to distinguish it from the avoidance and thefts in heterosexual, or parental, or any other love" [p.53]. The unhappy ending "stops at what the surviving woman wishes to be accepted as the truth"; the happy ending of *These Three* "is right: it shows that happy endings require trust and a faithfulness to one's desire which overcomes what faithfulness must always overcome — the meanness and maliciousness of opinion" [pp.53–54].

He closes with two further studies of explicitness vs. its region. *Mr. Smith Goes to Washington* "is not explicit about the causes of political corruption; it suggests, populistically," that they are "matters of personal greed . . . curable by the individual or mass goodness of the little people," yet "the image of its abrupt close marks out the region which will have to be explored" — Jean Arthur kneeling beside Mr. Smith "in the ambiguous birth-death posture of a Pieta," the corrupted senator (Claude Rains) confirming the truth "like Liberty's ambiguous upraised, threatening arm." His questions: "Whom does the truth kill? Who is in a position to speak it, or to hear it, or to act upon it if he knows he has heard?" [p.54]. Finally, on D. H. Lawrence: if you are Blake you find visual equivalents of "The head Sublime, the heart Pathos, the genitals Beauty"; but if you are "merely D. H. Lawrence" sickened by "the world's furtiveness," you write *Lady Chatterley's Lover* "and try to exorcize the bad magic of certain words and deeds with a magic of repetition and literalness," which leaves "the importance of the subject incomprehensible," whereas in *Women in Love* "moon and water and meadows and beasts and snow peaks are not symbolic evasions but tracings of that universe of feeling in which each of us finds or loses the space of our desire" [pp.54–55]. The lesson: "From the fact that the birds and the bees become insufficient it doesn't follow that all we ever need are the ABC's. What you need is the tact in each case to be specific enough. (Pornography, so far at least, combines the absolutely explicit with the completely unspecific.)" [p.55].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "This figure asserts the myth of community, the idea that society is man's natural state." [p.47]
- "how much of the history of film is a history of the firmament of individual women established there. Individual men, even the greatest, with few exceptions are fads or conveniences by comparison." [p.48]
- "The distribution of emphasis between a man and a woman in a film is like its distribution in a pas de deux." [p.48]
- "women are anti-community (because they interfere with comradeship)" [p.48]
- "her recognition of sexuality permits her intelligence and depth and independence." [pp.48–49]
- "In a thousand other instances the marriage must not be seen, and the walk into the sunset is into a dying star: they live happily ever after—as long as they keep walking." [pp.49–50]
- "A believable family has seemed as hard to get on film as a believable intellectual or university classroom" [p.51]
- "An avoided subject is not confronted by calling it names." [p.52]
- "What is monstrous to it is not merely overt perversion, but desire itself." [pp.52–53]
- "happy endings require trust and a faithfulness to one's desire which overcomes what faithfulness must always overcome—the meanness and maliciousness of opinion." [pp.53–54]
- "From the fact that the birds and the bees become insufficient it doesn't follow that all we ever need are the ABC's. What you need is the tact in each case to be specific enough." [p.55]

**Examples / films / persons / star-types invoked:**
- Garbo, Dietrich — women who alone justify "the making and preserving of countless films"; "the miracle is that they are enough" [p.48].
- Bette Davis / George Brent; Fred MacMurray / Barbara Stanwyck; Tyrone Power / Alice Faye — the male/female asymmetry (*pas de deux*) [p.48].
- *Children of Paradise* — where "who wants marriage?" reaches "its highest range and assessment" [p.49].
- *Intermezzo*; *Now, Voyager*; *The Man in the Gray Flannel Suit* — "man goes home to his wife, his life is over" [p.49].
- *Under Two Flags*; *Destry Rides Again*; *High Noon*; *A Stolen Life* — "the woman outside" dies/withdraws for marriage (twins in *A Stolen Life*) [p.49].
- *The Corsican Brothers*; *The Prisoner of Zenda*; *The Man in the Iron Mask*; *Shane*; *The Man Who Shot Liberty Valance* — doubled male identity, half withdrawn/killed [p.49].
- *The Lady Eve*; *The Great Lie*; *The Postman Always Rings Twice*; *Double Indemnity* — comic/hysterical/criminal handlings of the sex-marriage law [p.49].
- *Philadelphia Story*; *Woman of the Year*; *The Awful Truth* — reconciliation dramas (man's constant feeling; woman's "wiles" + "door ex machina") [p.50].
- Bergman, *Smiles of a Summer Night* (and *Lesson in Love*) — modern confirmation of the tradition; "another *Marriage of Figaro*" [pp.50–51].
- *Pride and Prejudice*, *How Green Was My Valley*, *The Little Foxes*, *Meet Me in St. Louis*, *I Remember Mama*, *To Kill a Mockingbird*, *Splendor in the Grass* — believable film families, all "set in the elegiac past" [pp.51–52].
- *These Three* (1936) vs. *The Children's Hour* (1962), both Wyler — the core case study in explicitness; Joel McCrea / James Garner; Bonita Granville as "the fascist child"; the hand-over-mouth gesture [pp.52–53].
- *Mr. Smith Goes to Washington* — Jean Arthur as Pieta, Claude Rains as Liberty's arm; populist inexplicitness marking a "region" [p.54].
- Blake vs. D. H. Lawrence (*Lady Chatterley's Lover*, *Women in Love*) — explicitness/literalness vs. "tracings" of the "universe of feeling" [pp.54–55].

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **myth of community** — the Military Man's assertion "that society is man's natural state"; in film realized "through male comradeship" rather than families [pp.47–48].
- **the Woman (as type)** — film's supreme individuating subject; "incommunicable as God"; the firmament around which films are built [pp.47–48].
- **the woman outside / the movie wife** — sexuality (non-reproductive, intelligent, independent, punished) vs. frigidity; both "anti-community" [p.48].
- **the law against combining sex and marriage** — the "deeper law" whose transgression dooms lovers (*Postman*, *Double Indemnity*); marriage "as illicit as sexuality outside" [p.49].
- **doubling of identity** — the device by which "half" the man withdraws or is killed (Westerns, swashbucklers) [p.49].
- **wiles / "door ex machina"** — the constrained means by which the *woman* (vs. the man) is permitted to work reconciliation [p.50].
- **explicitness vs. inexplicitness** — the false antithesis: "An avoided subject is not confronted by calling it names"; the need for "tact . . . to be specific enough" [pp.52–55].
- **studies of explicitness** — Cavell's name for the *These Three* / *Children's Hour* pair at the hand-over-mouth gesture [p.53].

---

### Ch 9 — The Dandy [book pp.55–59]

**Project:** Cavell analyzes the third Baudelairean type, the Dandy, as the deep myth behind the Western hero and the Bogart figure — a hero of "passive potency," "hidden fire," and *privacy* — and uses *The Maltese Falcon*, *Shane*, and especially *The Man Who Shot Liberty Valance* to read the Western's "play of fate" and the "traumatic births of law in the new world."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell opens with Baudelaire's descriptions of the Dandy: "Dandyism is an institution as strange and obscure as the duel," "an institution above laws, [that] has laws to which all its representatives . . . are subject"; the dandy "does not make love his special aim," is "free from the need to follow any profession," for whom personal appearance and material elegance are "symbols of the aristocratic superiority of his personality"; whose "ruling passion" is "a burning need to acquire originality, within the apparent bounds of convention," "the delight in causing astonishment, and the proud satisfaction of never oneself being astonished"; whose characteristic beauty "consists, above all, in his air of reserve, which in turn arises from his unshakeable resolve not to feel any emotion," likened to "a hidden fire whose presence can be guessed at; a fire that could blaze up, but does not wish to do so" [p.55].

Cavell: "We still feel the power of this myth, of this figure still staking himself upon a passive potency" [pp.55–56]. (Aside on Oscar Wilde — "My life is my art . . . That's two ways in one of forgoing immortality. One ought to get something for that" [p.56].) "Our most brilliant representatives of the type are the Western hero and Bogart," extending to "the smaller and more jaded detectives and private eyes" and to "the elegant nonprofessional solver of mysteries (Warren William . . . William Powell is the transcendent figure here)," a branch "inspired by Sherlock Holmes . . . and exhausted in James Bond's greasy taste for the refined" [p.56]. Crucially, "Such heroes are not outside society because they have been pushed out . . . they have stayed out" — hence the suspicion that "their freedom is never more than fantasy, like Huckleberry Finn's — that while their profession is risks, their selves are never chanced" [p.56]. The "latest avatar, self-proclaimed and claiming to be the last," is Peter Fonda in *Easy Rider*: "The equation of the motorcycle with the horse is worked into the ground" (the motorcycle iconography "comes from Brando in *The Wild Ones*"), and Peter Fonda's "perfect manifestation of Henry Fonda brings an entire history deep into the present" [p.56].

The "hidden fire" feature is "essential": "Our conviction in the strength of the hero depends upon our conviction in the strength and purity of character he has formed to keep his fires banked. Otherwise he is merely physically indomitable, and no man is; in that case, his success over evil would be arbitrary, an aesthetic and moral cheat" [p.56]. "He does not know he will succeed; what he knows is himself, his readiness." The compressed formula: **"The private hero must be a hero of privacy"** [p.56].

He turns to the **Villain as double**: "The Villain of the piece may either be a lout or a swell — generally, some combination of the two. In each, the hero faces one of his doubles and comes to his kind of terms with it" [pp.56–57]. Two cases: "in the first case he kills brutishness, false masculinity (*Stagecoach*, *My Darling Clementine*, *Hombre*); in the second he kills vanity, false dandyism (*Destry Rides Again*, *Shane*, *The Appaloosa*)" [p.57]. **[재구성: 카벨에게 악당은 영웅의 *분신*(double)이며, 영웅이 악당을 죽이는 것은 자기 안의 거짓 남성성 또는 거짓 댄디즘을 죽이는 일이다 — 따라서 결투는 외부의 선악 대결이 아니라 자기 정체성의 한 절반을 제거하는 행위다.]** In *The Maltese Falcon* "the Villain is the woman, another range of the dandy's double," who is "both brutish (hence a fake man) and so wholly caught up in appearances that she can't tell a dandy from a brute" [p.57].
