"But one's love must not wait for justification by its object, and Bogart-Spade loves her." He sends her "over" "for the way she is," "but in finishing her he does not finish his love for her, and this makes him true to type (his feeling is not dead, but banked); and because that shows the depth of his strength, he is the greatest instance of the type" [p.57].

Cavell catalogs the moments when "the hero's emotion blazes up, showing that what is happening takes place over fire" — "the meaning of Bogart's familiar mannerisms (the lips caught back, the distracted tug at the ear lobe)" — and the Bogart character's "fixed attribute": "the occasion usually found for him, when he is alone, to laugh at himself, as if at all types and their confinement, putting him and them in perspective." He locates this moment in *The Maltese Falcon* (after the first Fat Man interview), *The Big Sleep* (before the "queer act" in the rare-books shop), and *To Have and Have Not* (the "You know how to whistle, don't you?" lines) [p.57].

He then gives an extended reading of ***The Man Who Shot Liberty Valance***. The John Wayne character "knows that when he kills the brute (and Lee Marvin's brute is also a false dandy, with his silver-handled whip) he is killing half of himself" [pp.57–58]. He must do it because "the James Stewart character is incapable of preserving himself either from the brute or from true or false dandies," and Stewart "must survive, because he is the only real man around who has left room for the woman in himself (Wayne calls him 'tenderfoot,' and he is frequently shown in an apron and with books of law); hence only he is capable of preserving civilization, marrying privacy with society" [pp.57–58]. So Wayne "breaks not only society's law but his own, murdering in secret in order to establish justice. This is the best that can be done so long as men go on believing that the force of force is greater than the power of justice, and that justice comes from nowhere" [p.58]. He judges: "*Liberty Valance* is the fullest expression of the knowledge of the cost of civilization to be found in this genre of film, and therefore it is the greatest instance of it." (Wordplay: "The valence of liberty: the power with which one man's freedom combines with, or shuns, the freedom of another.") "In so fully opening the legend of the West, it ends it" [p.58].

He contrasts *Shane*: "Shane's beauty is of a different valence. What Shane knows, as he rides back out of the valley, is only that he is not made for civilization, which he finally learned, as we did, when after his climactic duel he twirled his brilliant pistol . . . back into its holster. His satisfaction pins him to his fate; he recognizes that he cannot forgo his mark of mastery, his taste for distinction, the privilege in his autonomy" [p.58].

Returning to *Liberty Valance*, he reads the **transfer of identity**: "The man who shoots Liberty Valance in that act also kills the identity of the tenderfoot, because the secret transforms the tenderfoot into 'the man who shot Liberty Valance.'" This "establishes the limits of the identities: power may prevent chaos, but it is impotent to establish the order of community; powerlessness may attract destruction, but accepting the limitation of one's individual power can attract higher power to one's aid" [p.58]. The "stern moral": **"justice, to be done, must be seen to be done; but justice, to be established, must not be seen to be established. No single man can establish it, only men together, each granting the other a certain right over his own autonomy"** [p.58]. He invokes a mythic absence: "Were there still a place beyond our lives from which Athena could appear, the just city could be seen to be established"; "Such are the traumatic births of law in the new world." In "the old world . . . the homologous trauma lay in the deaths of kings, not in the establishment but in the replacement of legitimacy" [pp.58–59].

The chapter closes by re-deriving the Western's deep structure from the bad-man/good-man relation: "both are outside the law, the one because he's strong enough to get away with it, the other because he's strong enough to impose his own code upon himself and have it respected, hence . . . each denies the other's existence and is fated to try to rid his outer realm of the other" [p.59]. From this it "cannot be true that the satisfaction of powerful Westerns consists merely in viewing again and again the triumph of good over evil"; if it were, "the arbitrariness of victory would have only the anxious pleasure in watching a game of chance" [p.59]. Instead: "The anxiety in the Western is a deeper one, watching the play of fate. The victory is almost arbitrary, and the hair's-breadth lets in the question: What is the fate that chooses the stronger to defend the good?" [p.59]. "Evil is always victorious in the short run, why not forever? Why is it the fate of good in an evil world ever to attract strength in its behalf, and strengthen it? Because God is a mighty fortress? So is a mighty fortress; and it is very hard to tell one from another" [p.59]. He invokes Plato: "Plato asked it first, and while he undertook to prove that justice brings happiness to the just, he did not claim that justice would in fact prevail; he predicted the opposite." The real question "is not, Why should I be moral?" but "whether the fate of goodness will be to lose its power to attract, whether all men and women will despair of happiness" [p.59].

The chapter ends with a coda on the Western landscape (carrying into Ch 10): "In the world of the Western there is only land, dotted here and there with shelters and now and then with a dash of fronts that men there call a city. The gorgeous, suspended skies achieved in the works of, say, John Ford, are as vacant as the land. When the Indians are gone, they will take with them whatever gods inhabited those places, leaving the beautiful names we do not understand (Iroquois, Shenandoah, Mississippi, Cheyenne) in place of those places we will not understand. So our slaughtered beauty mocks us, and gods become legends" [pp.59–60].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "We still feel the power of this myth, of this figure still staking himself upon a passive potency." [pp.55–56]
- "Our most brilliant representatives of the type are the Western hero and Bogart" [p.56]
- "they have stayed out. There is therefore about them the suspicion that their freedom is never more than fantasy, like Huckleberry Finn's—that while their profession is risks, their selves are never chanced." [p.56]
- "The private hero must be a hero of privacy." [p.56]
- "In each, the hero faces one of his doubles and comes to his kind of terms with it." [pp.56–57]
- "in finishing her he does not finish his love for her, and this makes him true to type (his feeling is not dead, but banked)" [p.57]
- "he is the only real man around who has left room for the woman in himself . . . hence only he is capable of preserving civilization, marrying privacy with society." [pp.57–58]
- "Liberty Valance is the fullest expression of the knowledge of the cost of civilization to be found in this genre of film, and therefore it is the greatest instance of it." [p.58]
- "His satisfaction pins him to his fate; he recognizes that he cannot forgo his mark of mastery, his taste for distinction, the privilege in his autonomy." [p.58]
- "justice, to be done, must be seen to be done; but justice, to be established, must not be seen to be established. No single man can establish it, only men together, each granting the other a certain right over his own autonomy." [p.58]
- "Such are the traumatic births of law in the new world." [pp.58–59]
- "The anxiety in the Western is a deeper one, watching the play of fate." [p.59]
- "Because God is a mighty fortress? So is a mighty fortress; and it is very hard to tell one from another." [p.59]
- "So our slaughtered beauty mocks us, and gods become legends." [p.60]

**Examples / films / persons / star-types invoked:**
- The Western hero and Bogart — "our most brilliant representatives" of the Dandy [p.56].
- Warren William / William Powell — the "elegant nonprofessional solver of mysteries" ("Powell the transcendent figure"); Sherlock Holmes (origin) → James Bond ("exhausted") [p.56].
- Oscar Wilde — "My life is my art": "two ways in one of forgoing immortality" [p.56].
- Huckleberry Finn — emblem of the hero whose freedom "is never more than fantasy," "selves never chanced" [p.56].
- Peter Fonda in *Easy Rider* (motorcycle iconography from Brando in *The Wild Ones*) — "the latest avatar," manifesting Henry Fonda [p.56].
- *Stagecoach*, *My Darling Clementine*, *Hombre* — hero kills "brutishness, false masculinity" [p.57].
- *Destry Rides Again*, *Shane*, *The Appaloosa* — hero kills "vanity, false dandyism" [p.57].
- *The Maltese Falcon* — Bogart-Spade and the woman-Villain (a "fake man" caught in appearances); the greatest instance of the type [p.57].
- *The Big Sleep*; *To Have and Have Not* — the Bogart character's fixed attribute (laughing at himself, alone) [p.57].
- *The Man Who Shot Liberty Valance* — Wayne (kills "half of himself"), James Stewart (the "tenderfoot" with apron and law books, "room for the woman in himself"), Lee Marvin (brute / "false dandy" with silver-handled whip); "the greatest instance" of the Western genre [pp.57–58].
- Athena — the mythic "place beyond our lives" from which the just city could be seen established (now absent) [pp.58–59].
- Plato — "asked it first"; proved justice brings happiness to the just but predicted justice would *not* prevail [p.59].
- John Ford — the "gorgeous, suspended skies . . . as vacant as the land" [p.59].

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **the Dandy** — Baudelaire's type of "passive potency," "air of reserve," "resolve not to feel any emotion"; the deep myth of the Western hero and Bogart [pp.55–56].
- **hidden fire / banked fires** — the hero's emotion withheld but "guessed at"; his strength depends on the "purity of character he has formed to keep his fires banked"; emotion "blazes up" only at crucial moments [pp.55–57].
- **passive potency** — the dandy's mode of self-staking: power held in reserve, not exercised [pp.55–56].
- **hero of privacy / "they have stayed out"** — the hero is outside society by choice, not expulsion; "The private hero must be a hero of privacy" [p.56].
- **the Villain as double** — bad man as the hero's other half (lout/swell; false masculinity / false dandyism); killing him kills "half of himself" [pp.56–58].
- **marrying privacy with society** — the achievement (in *Liberty Valance*, the Stewart figure) of joining individual privacy to communal order; "the cost of civilization" [pp.57–58].
- **transfer of identity** — the secret act that converts the "tenderfoot" into "the man who shot Liberty Valance," fixing "the limits of the identities" [p.58].
- **valence (of liberty)** — Cavell's pun: "the power with which one man's freedom combines with, or shuns, the freedom of another"; *Shane*'s beauty is "of a different valence" [p.58].
- **play of fate (vs. game of chance)** — the Western's deep "anxiety": not the arbitrary triumph of good over evil but "watching the play of fate" — "What is the fate that chooses the stronger to defend the good?" [p.59].
- **traumatic births of law** — the founding of legitimacy "in the new world" (vs. the "deaths of kings" / replacement of legitimacy in the old) [pp.58–59].

---

### Ch 10 — End of the Myths [book pp.60–67]

**Project:** Cavell argues that the classical Hollywood "myths" — the genres, cyclic plots, and projected human "types" that once secured film's conviction — have broken down, and that film has, within the last decade, entered the "modernist environment" the other major arts have long inhabited, where each art must fight to justify its own existence.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
Cavell opens by taking it as "sufficiently obvious" that the established "ways of giving significance to the possibilities of film" — i.e., "the media of movies exemplified by familiar Hollywood cycles and plots that justify the projection of types" — are "drawing to an end." In his developed terms, this means these media "no longer naturally establish conviction in our presentness to the world" [p.60]. Why this happened is, he says, "another story, as complex as the comparable events in other arts"; he explicitly refuses the explanation that "the conventions have become exhausted," which "merely restates the historical fact" rather than explaining it [p.60].

He then states the central historical thesis: "Within the last decade film has been moving into the modernist environment inhabited for generations by the other major arts, within which each art has had to fight for its survival, to justify its existence in its own way" [p.60]. Traditional genres (detective movies, romances, war films) are still made, but there is "no longer the same continuity between these movies and the films we take seriously, or between the audiences awaiting each" [pp.60–61]. He warns that the sociological/economic split between movie palaces and art houses is "not an accurate guide to the division between the commodity and the serious work" [p.61]. The continued production of traditional movies is double-edged evidence: both of "their completion as media of seriousness" and of an occasion to examine what "cinematic" and "exploration of the possibilities of the medium itself" really mean [p.61].

He distinguishes mere modernization from genuine modernist exploration. Traditional movies "move with the times" — dressed up "with fancier cutting and dreamier color and extremer angles and more explicit dialogue" — and this "dressing up" is "sometimes taken as an investigation of new possibilities of the cinematic medium," but it is not [p.61]. It "is hardly news" that the camera can move, vary distance, or that a story can be told by abrupt editing; treating "mechanical extensions of these known quantities" as explorations of the cinematic presupposes that those quantities already constitute the significance of "cinematic" [p.61]. **[재구성: 카벨의 암묵적 논점은 — "cinematic"이라는 개념 자체가 무엇을 의미하는지가 아직 미결정인데, 알려진 기술적 변수의 기계적 확장을 "탐구"라 부르는 것은 결론을 선취하는 순환이라는 것이다.]** He offers the analogy: these extensions bear roughly the relation to cinema's history that "late-nineteenth-century chromaticism bears to the history of music" — after Wagner, composers had unlimited chromatic alteration available, but "practically no one knew to what point these new 'possibilities' should be employed," because "altered" tones "were no longer alterations of anything": "Everything was altered. Alteration became everything" — until "a few strokes of genius" (Mahler, Schönberg, Debussy) supplied "new sources of organization" [p.61].

Cavell concedes that "certain possibilities, particular directions within the medium, came to an end," noting real physical limits on camera speed, scene change, and lens-blur compatible with depicting human action to "the aided eye" [pp.61–62]. (He flags that forgoing the depiction of human action would be one escape, "a solution not unheard of elsewhere in the arts," but sets it aside.) He poses the modernist question — why directors are "led to push possibilities (or why are they pushed by them) to their limits" — answering only that this would belong to "an investigation of modernism" [p.62].

One answer is that mere extensions were filmmakers' effort "to retain interest in movies." But "interest is not enough to keep an art alive," any more than interest in children suffices to motivate their care; one must "get beyond the fear of boredom." What art requires is "belief, relation to one's past, conviction that one's words and conduct express oneself" [p.62]. To intensify the known quantities mechanically is therefore "a familiar direction of artistic — of religious, of human — desperation: the recourse to rhetoric as a cover for the absence of conviction rather than as a mode for its release" [p.62]. In film specifically this means that "conviction in the movies' originating myths and geniuses — in the public world of men, the private company of woman, the secret isolation of the dandy — has been lost, or baffled" [p.62]. (These three name the myths developed in earlier chapters.)

Cavell then narrates the dissolution of each myth via the recurring formula "We no longer grant, or take it for granted…":

1. *The public world of men.* "We no longer grant… that men doing the work of the world together are working for the world's good, or that if they are working for the world's harm they can be stopped" [pp.62–63]. These beliefs "flowered last in our films about the imminence and the experience of the Second World War," then "began withering in its aftermath" [p.63]. He launches an extended Cold War indictment (atomic guilt, containment, "massive retaliation, moon races, yellow perils, red conspiracies"; "ceding to Stalin and Hitler the permanent victories of the war"), arguing that we "lash ourselves to these ideas" in fixed fury "in order not to see the injury we have done, and do" — "So the mind tears itself apart trying to pull free" [p.63].

2. *The private company of woman.* "We no longer grant… that stylish dumb women are as interesting as stylish intelligent ones" [p.63]. He distinguishes mere beauty (Madeleine Carroll, Anita Louise, Hedy Lamarr) from beauty "animated" by an intelligence that "animates their presence" (Lombard, the young Rosalind Russell, Katharine and Audrey Hepburn) [p.63]. Marilyn Monroe's complexity turns on body plus "the compassion that was lost and confusing within it" and a "capacity for wit and laughter" [p.63]. The Bogart leading women (Mary Astor, Ingrid Bergman, Lauren Bacall) possess "an intelligence that gives them an independence from men, hence makes them worth winning, worth yielding independence for" [p.63]. He adds reflections on French leading ladies (Darrieux, Morgan, Feuillère, Arletty) who needn't "pretend that their favors were won rather than conferred"; on Garbo and Dietrich "suppressing a running giggle at their lover's ignorance"; on the Ninotchka myth (Melvyn Douglas wins by "making an ass of himself"), the folktale of the Princess who could not laugh, and *Smiles of a Summer Night*. A "private theory" explains failed careers (Frances Farmer, Ann Dvorak, Ann Sheridan, Jane Greer) by their not being "given contexts in which their intelligence could operate freely" [p.64].

   He extends this to the cold/demonstrative axis. "Just as we no longer take it for granted that a demonstrative woman is capable of intimacy, we no longer grant that a superficially cold one probably is" [p.64]. The Hitchcock heroine (Grace Kelly, Kim Novak, Tippi Hedren) is meant to "excite a promise of passion by the very coldness of her surface," but also carries "a certain denseness that lends the additional promise of vulgarity" [p.64]. In *To Catch a Thief* the necklace/fireworks cliché first prompts a laugh, but "Hitchcock too is grinning": "here the conventional symbol exposes a conventional imagination of the deed" [pp.64–65]. The heroine's imaginative "stricture" is "reassuring" — "it pulls the teeth of the vampire" — because she "can be won by satisfying her crudity… She does not want to incorporate her man but to be absorbed into his fantasy," a parody of "the sharing of fantasy required in the marriage of true minds" [p.65]. A "terrible psychic price" is paid for this reassurance. He reads *Marnie* (the "world of stolen love"), *Psycho* (the "brutal rationality of the 'psychiatrist'" exemplifying feeling elicited "immediately by the theories we give ourselves of love and hate") — yielding the formula: "knowledge has not replaced love as our address to the world, but knowledge has replaced the world as the object of our passion. So science turns back into magic, theory becomes incantation, and intellectual caution produces psychic promiscuity" [p.65]. *The Birds* takes "a new dialectical step": explanation is "stifled," "the universe now sees to it that the consequences of our actions hunt us to a conclusion"; nature, when "love is teased," is "not amused, but aroused" [pp.65–66]. He reads the final attack on Tippi Hedren as a mythic ravishing ("No single creature can penetrate this Leda. The sky itself must empty into her lap"), contrasting it with *Notorious*, where the Bergman lady "will stand again on her own feet" [pp.66–67]. The "new lady will not be recovered; she has flown into her own imagination… they move in different elements" [p.66]. The lesson: modern love is "perverse, because now tangential to the circling of society," and its promise "requires the strength to share privacy, to cohabit in one element, unsponsored by society" — which this "fancy lady" cannot do ("no man is a bird of her feather") [pp.66–67]. Here Hitchcock "earns his title from Greek comedy." A coda warns against equating films with dreams: most dreams are "boring narratives," and to call film adventures dreams "is a dream of dreams… merely the wish to have interesting dreams"; but horror films "specifically do infuse boring narratives with the skin-shrinking haunts of dreams" [p.67].

3. *The secret isolation of the dandy.* "We no longer grant… that a man who expresses no feeling has fires banked within him"; if we grant him depth we now suspect him "banking destructive feeling" (Antonioni's, Bergman's, Godard's leading men) [p.67]. Within Hollywood, the later cool men (Yul Brynner, James Coburn, Sean Connery, Michael Caine) "measure the distance we have come, along the line of silent strength," from Gary Cooper, Fonda, Stewart, Bogart, Alan Ladd [p.67]. "The rebirth of unexpressed masculine depth required the rebound of culture that created the new possibility of the cool" — Clift and Brando as early instances, Dean, Newman, McQueen counting on it, Belmondo "its latest original" [pp.67–68]. Conviction in their depth "depends upon their being young, upon the natural accuracy of their physical movements (like athletes between plays)"; here "the body is not unhinged from the mind, as in the brute; it is the expression of selfhood, of the ability to originate one's actions" (citing Spinoza on "wisdom as aptness of the body") [p.68]. The young man's "careless slouch" expresses not "the vanity of personal appearance or fashion, but the vanity of personal freedom: of distinctness, not of distinction" — "the democratic equivalent of the dandy." Its "guiding myth is the myth of youth itself, that life has not yet begun irretrievably," that when "the time comes to declare oneself, one will be recognized." Under social pressure for uniformity ("consensus crowding out the claims of consent"), individuality's strategy becomes "to become identifiable within uniform — not by it… but despite it" [p.68].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "they no longer naturally establish conviction in our presentness to the world." [p.60]
- "Within the last decade film has been moving into the modernist environment inhabited for generations by the other major arts, within which each art has had to fight for its survival, to justify its existence in its own way." [p.60]
- "To say that the 'conventions have become exhausted' is no explanation; if that means anything, it merely restates the historical fact." [p.60]
- "Everything was altered. Alteration became everything—but only after a few strokes of genius permitted new sources of organization… to manifest themselves (Mahler, Schonberg, Debussy)." [p.61]
- "the recourse to rhetoric as a cover for the absence of conviction rather than as a mode for its release." [p.62]
- "conviction in the movies' originating myths and geniuses—in the public world of men, the private company of woman, the secret isolation of the dandy—has been lost, or baffled." [p.62]
- "knowledge has not replaced love as our address to the world, but knowledge has replaced the world as the object of our passion. So science turns back into magic, theory becomes incantation, and intellectual caution produces psychic promiscuity." [p.65]
- "the vanity of personal freedom: of distinctness, not of distinction. It is the democratic equivalent of the dandy." [p.68]
- "Its guiding myth is the myth of youth itself, that life has not yet begun irretrievably… and that when the time comes to declare oneself, one will be recognized." [p.68]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- Wagner / Mahler, Schönberg, Debussy — chromaticism analogy: exhausted directional possibilities resolved only by genius supplying new organization.
- John Ford — the "gorgeous, suspended skies… as vacant as the land" (carryover image opening the page).
- WWII films (e.g. *Notorious*) — last flowering, then withering, of the "public world of men" myth.
- Madeleine Carroll, Anita Louise, Hedy Lamarr vs. Lombard, R. Russell, K. Hepburn, A. Hepburn — beauty without vs. with animating intelligence.
- Marilyn Monroe; Mary Astor, Bergman, Bacall (Bogart's women) — intelligence yielding independence "worth winning."
- Garbo, Dietrich; *Ninotchka* (Melvyn Douglas); *Smiles of a Summer Night* — the laughing/conferring woman.
- Frances Farmer, Ann Dvorak, Ann Sheridan, Jane Greer — careers failed for lack of contexts for intelligence.
- *To Catch a Thief*, *Marnie*, *Psycho*, *The Birds*, *Notorious* (Grace Kelly, Kim Novak, Tippi Hedren, Cary Grant, Ingrid Bergman) — the cold-surface heroine, the conventional imagination of the deed, knowledge displacing world, nature aroused, the unrecoverable "fancy lady."
- Antonioni / Bergman / Godard leading men; Brynner, Coburn, Connery, Caine vs. Cooper, Fonda, Stewart, Bogart, Ladd; Clift, Brando, Dean, Newman, McQueen, Belmondo — the loss of "banked fires" and the rise of "the cool."
- Spinoza (wisdom as aptness of body); Yeats ("bodily decrepitude," dismissed as "his problem") — the body as expression of selfhood.

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *myth(s)* — the originating organizing fantasies of classical film (the public world of men, the private company of woman, the secret isolation of the dandy) that grounded conviction in genres and types.
- *media of movies* — the specific Hollywood cycles, plots, genres, and projected types through which film's possibilities were given significance (distinguished from "the medium" in Ch 11).
- *presentness to the world* — the conviction, secured by classical media, of our being present to the reality film projects.
- *the modernist environment / modernism* — the predicament, long faced by other arts, in which an art must "fight for its survival" and justify its existence; an art's exploring its medium becomes exploring "the conditions of its existence."
- *known quantities* — the already-familiar technical capacities (camera movement, varying distance, abrupt editing) whose "mechanical extension" is mistaken for genuine exploration.
- *rhetoric (as desperation)* — intensification of technique used to cover an absence of conviction rather than to release it.
- *the cool* — the modern, democratic transformation of the dandy: unexpressed masculine depth vouched for by youth and bodily accuracy, expressing "the vanity of personal freedom."

---

### Ch 11 — The Medium and Media of Film [book pp.68–73]

**Project:** Cavell draws his central distinction between *a medium* (here, film) and its *media* (the specific genres/types/individualities through which it has been realized), and proposes that the *material basis* of film's media is "a succession of automatic world projections" — unpacking each word — so as to reframe (and dissolve) the standard aesthetic debates about what is "cinematic."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
The chapter opens conditionally: if "defining 'the medium of an art'" helps us understand "what has in fact been achieved and accepted within the various physical bases of an art," and if film's physical basis, "being irreducibly photographic, necessitates or makes possible the meaningful use of human reality and of nature as such," then "the genres and types and individualities that have constituted the media of movies" are "fixed in that specific collection of human beings with which movies have been made" — in their "utterly specific rhythms of voice and gesture and posture," and the "particular streets and carriages and chambers" in which they had their being [pp.68–69]. These "are now gone or old." Crucially: "When they and their particular locales are gone, Hollywood is ended. Its ending is the end of its media, of those arrangements whose significance was unquestioned, conviction in which was immediate" [p.69]. This poses the chapter's guiding questions: "What, then, are the media of movies? What possibilities are now to be explored?" [p.69]

Cavell catalogues what traditional movies can still do well: "new wrinkles to old formats" — race-recast genre pictures (*For Love of Ivy*, *In the Heat of the Night*, *Guess Who's Coming to Dinner*); "more convincing and luxurious props" (the James Bond series dressing up Saturday serials, *From Russia with Love* with "four complete climaxes"); and "at the highest level of taste and craftsmanship, well-made adventures or struggles in completely realized exotic settings" giving room to "an actor of traditional grandeur" (Kurosawa with Mifune; Arthur Penn choreographing Bancroft and Patty Duke in *The Miracle Worker*; Brando in *Sayonara*; comparisons with Olivier and George C. Scott) [pp.69–70].

He then identifies "a problem quite unforeseen": when a film "deliberately avoids cinematic traditions in an effort to modernize its looks," it tends to produce performers Cavell cannot remember — "I am simply being unable to recall the names or faces or presences" of recent American screen people [p.70]. Their "featurelessness" is really a sameness: "many of them look alike to me," like "composite drawings… of the Presidents of the United States" or a barber-shop face-chart [p.70]. He develops the Baudelaire point: "Baudelaire's perception of union between a woman and her cosmetics is here realized with perfect literality: the new actresses tend to be cosmetics, or prosthetics" — the adornment "has moved from its position as live landscape into the soul of the action" [p.70]. Samuel Fuller "thrive[s] on this indistinctness," forgoing characterization, plot, and symbolism, preserving "only the blocks of crude emotion that all humans share," thereby "achieving old-fashioned movies" [pp.70–71].

Exceptions: Lana Turner "missed the fate of indistinctness because she was one of its originals" (*The Bad and the Beautiful*, where starlet and star are "all but indistinguishable except in age and spatial location"); Natalie Wood and Tuesday Weld miss it because their "nervous awkwardness… is out of register with the evenness of their looks." Anthony Perkins "was headed for this indistinctness until Hitchcock discovered a natural meaning for it in the split, or theft of personality; psychological vampirism" — his later roles "trade on" *Psycho*'s prior meaning [pp.71]. This illustrates "the kind of history… the natural result of a star's successive incarnations": the interpenetration of lawman and outlaw (Bogart, Cagney, Lloyd Nolan, Howard Duff) becomes "part of what the movies they are in are about," so that "an account of the paths of stars across their various films must form part of the internal history of the world of cinema" [p.71].

Cavell then names the ontological stakes of the new interchangeability. The interchangeability of new performers — of "their demeanor and posture and cadences, and the way they inhabit their trappings" — is "a perfect negation of that condition of movies… in which an individuality is the subject of film." "To impersonate one is to impersonate all; their personalities are already impersonations." It further "negates or literalizes the condition I characterized as the ontological equality of objects and human subjects in photographs; for these figures are no longer the human part of nature" [p.72]. What needs accounting for is the simultaneity of two facts: the tradition "is still available to current successful films," yet serious works "are moving into the modernist predicament in which an art has lost its natural relation to its history," in which the artist, precisely because devoted to making an object that "will bear the same weight of experience" as the historical objects of his art, "is compelled to find unheard-of structures that define themselves and their history against one another" [p.72]. He notes that history's meaningfulness depends on "the constancy of human experience"; "the history of an art is a human history" [p.72]. Hence: "When in such a state an art explores its medium, it is exploring the conditions of its existence; it is asking exactly whether, and under what conditions, it can survive" [p.72]. This reposes the questions: "What conditions of movie-making are to be explored? What 'possibilities' of the medium of movies are now given significance?" [p.72]

He then gives the chapter's pivotal definition. "The material basis of the media of movies (as paint on a flat, delimited support is the material basis of the media of painting) is… a succession of automatic world projections" [pp.72–73]. He glosses each term:
- "*Succession* includes the various degrees of motion in moving pictures: the motion depicted; the current of successive frames in depicting it; the juxtapositions of cutting." [p.73]
- "*Automatic* emphasizes the mechanical fact of photography, in particular the absence of the human hand in forming these objects and the absence of its creatures in their screening." [p.73]
- "*World* covers the ontological facts of photography and its subjects." [p.73]
- "*Projection* points to the phenomenological facts of viewing, and to the continuity of the camera's motion as it ingests the world." [p.73]

He observes that succession and projection cover the topics most emphasized in film aesthetics — in particular "the basic question" of whether the essence of the cinematic is cutting from view to view (succession) or "continuous projection of an altering view" (projection, via depth of focus or camera movement). He declines to adjudicate the Eisenstein-montage vs. continuity dispute, offering instead "a hypothesis about it, namely, that it is not a question," which he can only cash out "after developing the categories of world and automatism" [p.73]. **[재구성: 카벨의 함의는 — 매체의 "가능성"들은 개별적으로 의미를 갖지 않고 오직 서로의 관점에서만 의미를 가지므로, 컷팅 대 연속성 중 어느 하나를 "본질"로 고립시켜 묻는 물음 자체가 잘못 구성되었다는 것이다.]** He concludes: a discovery never "shows the significance of an isolated possibility," for "each is what it is only in view of the others" [p.73]. This is why the right answer to "In what ways do movies differ from novels or from theater?" is "In every way," and why "the idea that a movie should be 'cinematic' is either as bad or as special as the idea that a poem should be poetic, or as empty" [p.73].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "When they and their particular locales are gone, Hollywood is ended. Its ending is the end of its media, of those arrangements whose significance was unquestioned, conviction in which was immediate." [p.69]
- "the new actresses tend to be cosmetics, or prosthetics; the adornment that was the whole point of the old chorus line has moved from its position as live landscape into the soul of the action." [p.70]
- "To impersonate one is to impersonate all; their personalities are already impersonations." [p.72]
- "this… negates or literalizes the condition I characterized as the ontological equality of objects and human subjects in photographs; for these figures are no longer the human part of nature." [p.72]
- "When in such a state an art explores its medium, it is exploring the conditions of its existence; it is asking exactly whether, and under what conditions, it can survive." [p.72]
- "The material basis of the media of movies… is… a succession of automatic world projections." [pp.72–73]
- "'Automatic' emphasizes the mechanical fact of photography, in particular the absence of the human hand in forming these objects and the absence of its creatures in their screening." [p.73]
- "'World' covers the ontological facts of photography and its subjects. 'Projection' points to the phenomenological facts of viewing, and to the continuity of the camera's motion as it ingests the world." [p.73]
- "For whatever is meant by a medium's 'possibilities,' each is what it is only in view of the others." [p.73]
- "It is why the idea that a movie should be 'cinematic' is either as bad or as special as the idea that a poem should be poetic, or as empty." [p.73]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- *For Love of Ivy*, *In the Heat of the Night*, *Guess Who's Coming to Dinner* — "new wrinkles" (racial recasting) on old genre formats.
- James Bond series / *From Russia with Love* — luxurious-prop dressing-up of the Saturday serial.
- Kurosawa & Mifune; Arthur Penn / Bancroft / Patty Duke (*The Miracle Worker*); Brando (*Sayonara*); Olivier (*Spartacus*, *Bunny Lake Is Missing*); George C. Scott (*The Hustler*, *Dr. Strangelove*) — the actor of traditional grandeur in realized settings; gradations of "natural subject of film."
- Baudelaire — woman/cosmetics union, now "realized with perfect literality" in featureless new actresses.
- Samuel Fuller — thriving on indistinctness by forgoing characterization/plot/symbolism for "blocks of crude emotion."
- Lana Turner (*The Bad and the Beautiful*), Natalie Wood, Tuesday Weld, Zohra Lampert, Joan Lorring — exceptions to indistinctness.
- Anthony Perkins (*Psycho*, *Pretty Poison*, *The Champagne Murders*) — Hitchcock turning indistinctness into "theft of personality"; the dialectic innocence→guilt→ironic innocence.
- Bogart, Cagney, Lloyd Nolan, Howard Duff — lawman/outlaw interpenetration becoming "the internal history of the world of cinema."
- Eisenstein (montage) vs. Bazin (continuity) — the montage/continuity dispute Cavell declines to adjudicate, hypothesizing "it is not a question."

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *a medium* vs. *the media (of a medium)* — the medium of film is its material basis as such (succession of automatic world projections); its *media* are the discovered, accepted arrangements — genres, types, individualities — through which the medium has actually been realized. The media are not given but achieved; their loss is the "end of Hollywood."
- *material basis* — the medium's underlying material constitution (paint-on-support for painting; "a succession of automatic world projections" for film), the analysis of which grounds claims about possibilities.
- *succession* — the temporal/motion dimension: motion depicted, the current of frames, the juxtapositions of cutting.
- *automatic / automatism* — the mechanical fact of photography: absence of the human hand in forming the objects and absence of human creatures in their screening (developed further in later chapters).
- *world* — the ontological dimension: photography's subjects are the world itself.
- *projection* — the phenomenological dimension of viewing: the continuity of the camera's motion "as it ingests the world."
- *ontological equality of objects and human subjects in photographs* — the prior condition (from earlier chapters) that photography places objects and persons on equal ontological footing; the new interchangeability "negates or literalizes" it.
- *individuality as the subject of film* — the classical condition (a star's self-identity across recurrent incarnations) negated by interchangeable performers.

---

### Ch 12 — The World as Mortal: Absolute Age and Youth [book pp.74–79]

**Project:** Cavell reads two developments in recent filmmaking as acknowledgments of film's a priori condition — that its medium is photographic and its subject is reality — focusing on how the *absolute age* of the movie (its mortal dependence on real living people who age and die) gets registered as nostalgia, and how, against this, the *myth of youth* and modern romance flare up.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
Cavell announces "two recent developments in movie-making" that "acknowledge the a priori condition that its medium is photographic and its subject reality" [p.74]. (The first occupies this chapter; the second — color — is Ch 13.) The first development concerns the previously noted fact that "the particular human beings whose characteristics helped to fix the media of movies are now old or gone." Their children are now in films, "the latest class with hereditary privilege," and like "all second generations… most of them look and feel alike," sharing "the same past, or lack of past, somewhere within the even heat of West Los Angeles" [p.74]. He distinguishes the *artistic age* of the movie (unclear) from its *absolute age*, which "is acknowledged in the use of these familiar faces explicitly as familiar ancients": Buster Keaton in *Sunset Boulevard*, Andy Devine in *Liberty Valance*, Bette Davis in *The Star* [p.74]. Such films suit this aging effect because "they are about returning, about making a comeback, or going back to beginnings"; we are "meant to remember their stars as having been there… when they and we were young." Watching Gloria Swanson "watching her young films, the juxtaposition of the phases of her appearance cuts the knowledge into us, of the movie's aging and ours, with every frame" [p.74].

He universalizes this into a meditation on mortality and photography. "The nostalgia of old photographs is the perception that mortality is at some point to be stopped in its tracks. The figures in them seem so vulnerable, so unknowing of what we know about them" [p.75]. We could turn nostalgia "toward an anticipation of the fact that every moment is always stopped from every other"; in the vulnerable details (the "embarrassed laugh," "the tilt of the hat," "the dust that lies on the shoe," "the crook of the arm where the baby nods") "the knowledge of mortality has room to live, even jauntily" — "There is no better place for the knowledge" [p.75]. He contrasts the misleading "roundness of clocks" (whose hands "repossess their old positions every day") with "the reels on a projector, like the bulbs of an hourglass," which figure irreversible time: "as the past fills up, the future thins; and… the end, already there against the axle… seems to pick up speed" [p.75]. **[재구성: 카벨이 시계의 순환성과 영사기 릴/모래시계의 비가역성을 대비시키는 요점은 — 영화의 물질적 장치 자체(필름이 한쪽 릴에서 다른 쪽으로 옮겨가며 한 번 지나간 프레임은 되돌아오지 않는 구조)가 인간 유한성의 시간 구조를 그 형식 안에 새기고 있다는 것이다.]**

He returns to specific cases. In *Liberty Valance*, John Wayne "is not seen old," and Stewart's old-man performance "is felt as play-acting because it contrasts with his youth *in this film*… rather than with the free accumulation of memories of him from film worlds of an earlier time" [p.75]. In *Ride the High Country*, the pathos of aging cowboys (Randolph Scott, Joel McCrea) "depends upon their being enacted by aging men whom we can remember as young cowboys"; Cavell judges the film "overrated… because it taps this particular source of feeling" [p.75]. The aging actor "declares a condition that was naturally registered in the fact of recurrence itself: that movies depend upon the appearance in them of living men and women, mortals." "The individuality of stars was defined by their self-identity through repeated incarnations" [p.75].

He then theorizes the *stock company*. European directors of the past decade-and-a-half wanted "the depth of reference and range of inflection the use of a stock company of subjects brings." "Hollywood was an enormous stock company, America's only successful one, its State Theater" [pp.75–76]. The recurring figures in Bergman, Godard, Resnais, Antonioni, Fellini "were part of what made them acceptable as movies, not merely as foreign films"; they "count on the power of recurrence" that Hollywood, having achieved it, "had to declare… by the assertion of age — that is, by nostalgia. (Hollywood, like America, finally pays for its invasions.)" [p.76]. *Beat the Devil* (Huston, 1954) "self-consciously exploited the knowledge that Bogart adventures could no longer inspire their old conviction," but, instead of facing the loss (as in *The Treasure of the Sierra Madre*) and its released nostalgia (as in *The African Queen*), "he pretends that nothing of value has passed. I find it about as funny as a mustache on the Mona Lisa" [p.76].

He extends recurrence to supporting players: their "distinctness assured through recurrence" was "equally obvious." One may forgivably forget a bit player's name but "not to remember their faces and temperaments is unthinkable" [p.76]. Yet he cannot recall the minor leads of recent neo-Hollywood films (*In the Heat of the Night*, *The Detective*, *Petulia*, *Pretty Poison*, *Bullitt*). More strongly: "they could not become memorable," because he has "no sense of the range of role or temperament they may occupy" — "But without that, there is no world before us. Neo-Hollywood is not a world" [p.76]. *Bonnie and Clyde* is a "remarkable achievement" for establishing "at least three supporting players, enough to survey a whole and specific world" [p.76]. A "failure of *The Graduate*" is its supporting players' failure "to accept and fill and specify the types they project," so their characters are "thin and imposed — stereotypes" [pp.76–77]. He dwells on Anne Bancroft's "elaborated arbitrariness" (incoherent motivations), concluding the problem is not insufficient information about her character "but because we aren't convinced that we have been given a character to respond to" [p.77]. He grants that the film may intend "a series of projections from the graduate's point of view," well-realized in the "punning cuts" radiating his new bodily experience across hotel/parents' house/swimming pool — "the way his body has become marked and charted with the knowledge of hers" [p.77]. But "the primacy of his private life, which wants to be the subject of the film, is also denied by the film, as firmly as by his parents." The hotel sequence is "lobby-to-bedroom farce," and "the film's talented director takes revenge on all sides responsible for his success — on his audience… and on his hero" [pp.77–78].

He pivots to praise. "Miracles are likely to be costly, and Mike Nichols' and Dustin Hoffman's graduate is a miraculous appearance of the myth of youth itself," soliciting feeling "out of all proportion to any feeling of his own" [p.78]. The investment is "fully justified in the final act": Hoffman "takes upon himself the Belmondo figure created in *Breathless*… a real modern hero, equal to his demand for happiness," "assailing daughters without fathers, or with defeated fathers." Such women "need to be won without anyone to award them. They have to award themselves" — and crucially "these tests of love cannot be passed": "We are not swans, not showers of gold, neither real beasts nor real princes… We win only by overcoming the woman's power of testing altogether, by showing her that her destructiveness has not destroyed us" [p.78]. The closing bus image (the "spotless and prepared bride" with "her exhausted and disheveled and victorious lover") "calls upon the great movie image of leave-taking in wedding: Vigo's bride in *L'Atalante*," drifting "into the unknown territory of marriage." "What garb is appropriate to the unknown, if not the garb of unknownness?" — "we do not know that they will not survive together" [pp.78–79].

This reinstates "the myth of modern marriage, which is the modern myth of romance." In classical comedy "the stage at the end is littered with marriages… families are present to celebrate the continuance of their order"; but "at some point, perhaps when the world went to war, society stopped believing in its ability to provide that continuity." Modern romance asserts "that one couple can make it alone, unsponsored" — "The great movie genres of this idea are thirties comedies and musicals," in which big-city survival "requires wit, invention, good spirits, the capacity to entertain" [p.79]. The community needed to know not merely "that its legitimacy is being acknowledged, but that it is worth acknowledging" — that it "still allows those meant for one another to find one another." Inverting the formula: "it has allowed people who have found one another to be meant for one another" [p.79]. The chapter closes (carrying into the p.80 hinge) noting "the myth has died a thousand deaths, but each is equivocal" (*Holiday Inn*, *Damsel in Distress*, *Sabrina*), and ties the graduate-as-track-man to Lévi-Strauss/Thoreau on walking as "a gait natural only to man" — "it is analytically true that men do not inhabit the moon" [p.80].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "two recent developments in movie-making seem to me to acknowledge the a priori condition that its medium is photographic and its subject reality." [p.74]
- "however unclear the issue of the artistic age of the movie, its absolute age is acknowledged in the use of these familiar faces explicitly as familiar ancients." [p.74]
- "The nostalgia of old photographs is the perception that mortality is at some point to be stopped in its tracks." [p.75]
- "as the past fills up, the future thins; and that the end, already there against the axle, when the time comes for its running, seems to pick up speed." [p.75]
- "movies depend upon the appearance in them of living men and women, mortals. The individuality of stars was defined by their self-identity through repeated incarnations." [p.75]
- "Hollywood was an enormous stock company, America's only successful one, its State Theater." [pp.75–76]
- "But without that, there is no world before us. Neo-Hollywood is not a world." [p.76]
- "these tests of love cannot be passed. We are not swans, not showers of gold, neither real beasts nor real princes, and mountains of glassy courtesies will roll us back." [p.78]
- "the myth of modern marriage, which is the modern myth of romance." [p.79]
- "it has allowed people who have found one another to be meant for one another." [p.79]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- Buster Keaton & Stroheim in *Sunset Boulevard*; Andy Devine in *Liberty Valance*; Bette Davis in *The Star*; Gloria Swanson watching her young films — "familiar ancients" registering the movie's absolute age/mortality.
- *Liberty Valance* (Wayne/Stewart) — youth-vs.-present contrast read as "play-acting," not earned nostalgia.
- *Ride the High Country* (Randolph Scott, Joel McCrea) — pathos tapping aging-actor recurrence; judged overrated.
- Bergman, Godard, Resnais, Antonioni, Fellini — European directors' use of recurring "stock company" subjects.
- *Beat the Devil* vs. *The Treasure of the Sierra Madre* / *The African Queen* (Huston, Bogart) — denying vs. facing the loss of conviction; the "mustache on the Mona Lisa."
- Neo-Hollywood minor leads (*The Detective*, *Petulia*, *Pretty Poison*, *Bullitt*, *In the Heat of the Night*) — unmemorable, hence "Neo-Hollywood is not a world."
- *Bonnie and Clyde* — establishing three supporting players, "a whole and specific world."
- *The Graduate* (Mike Nichols, Dustin Hoffman, Anne Bancroft) — failed/stereotyped supporting players, but Hoffman as the "miraculous appearance of the myth of youth."
- Belmondo in *Breathless* / *That Man from Rio* — the modern hero whose figure Hoffman assumes.
- *L'Atalante* (Vigo's bride) — the great image of bridal leave-taking "into the unknown territory of marriage."
- Thirties comedies & musicals; *Holiday Inn*, *Damsel in Distress*, *Sabrina* (Astaire, Crosby, Joan Fontaine, Audrey Hepburn, Bogart) — the unsponsored-couple myth and its equivocal deaths.
- Lévi-Strauss / Thoreau — walking/running as the human gait; "men do not inhabit the moon."

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *the a priori condition (of film's medium)* — that its medium is photographic and its subject is reality; the two developments (age, color) "acknowledge" it.
- *absolute age* (of the movie) vs. *artistic age* — the absolute age is the literal mortal aging of the real persons photographed (registered by "familiar ancients"); the artistic age is the unsettled question of film's maturity as an art.
- *the world as mortal / mortality* — film's dependence on real living, aging, dying human beings; photography stops the moment, and so carries "the knowledge of mortality" within its form.
- *nostalgia* — the perception in old photographs that mortality "is at some point to be stopped in its tracks"; Hollywood, having lost immediate conviction, can declare recurrence "only by the assertion of age — that is, by nostalgia."
- *recurrence / self-identity through repeated incarnations* — the source of a star's individuality and a film world's distinctness; its loss means "no world before us."
- *stock company* — a recurring ensemble of subjects ("Hollywood was an enormous stock company… its State Theater") whose recurrence gives depth of reference.
- *the myth of youth* — that "life has not yet begun irretrievably"; the graduate as its "miraculous appearance."
- *the myth of modern marriage / modern romance* — that "one couple can make it alone, unsponsored," replacing classical comedy's family-sponsored continuity.

---

### Ch 13 — The World as a Whole: Color [book pp.80–100]

**Project:** Cavell's long, central meditation on color as the *second* property by which film "declare[s] its recording of a total world." He argues that color, when it becomes "a declared condition of a medium," neutralizes the black-and-white "drama" through which we comprehended human personality and event — thereby de-psychologizing/un-theatricalizing film's subjects and projecting a *world of futurity* — and he distinguishes this from color's other uses (packaging, make-believe, private-fantasy symbolism), culminating in an extended critique of Godard.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**
Cavell introduces color as "a second major property of film which can serve to declare its recording of a total world" [p.80]. Its commonest use is "merely… a new form of packaging," which "market research… has convinced me is profitable"; this is not the whole point even in *Gone With the Wind*, whose success depended "upon its appearance as a luxury item" — Technicolor as "the natural visual equivalent of the Scott-descended prose" of historical romance, glamour being "the content of that myth" [pp.80–81].

He distinguishes a first serious use — color creating a *world of make-believe*. *The Wizard of Oz* "really defined or declared color as such" (ruby slippers, yellow brick road, Emerald City, "the horse of a different color," and the framing device of black-and-white Kansas), but "exploiting" these "is not merely a matter of painting props to match some names": it required great comic performances (Lahr, Haley, Bolger), inspired casting (Burke, Hamilton), and an inventive score [p.81]. *The Adventures of Robin Hood* (Errol Flynn) likewise recalls "the shiny color illustrations in lost childhood books… veils of magic" [p.81]. These films "discovered that color can serve to unify the projected world in another way than by direct reliance upon… the spatial-temporal consistency of the real world." The world so unified "is obviously not the real past world of photography, but a consistent region of make-believe," so "it is essential to their rightness that these films are children's tales" (Olivier's *Henry V* "works this possibility to its greatest elegance") [p.81]. He contrasts *The Cabinet of Dr. Caligari*, which creates an artificially unified environment but "competes with reality by opposing it… with images that compose a conventional expression of madness, not by filtering reality through a normal stage of fantasy"; its "feeling of constriction… is a function of its existence in black and white" [pp.81–82].

He then names the chapter's chief thesis about *recent* serious color film. When color "becomes a declared condition of a medium" it also "create[s] a world. But the world created is neither a world just past nor a world of make-believe. It is a world of an immediate future" [p.82]. *Red Desert* and *Fahrenheit 451* explicitly carry "the premonition that the world we inhabit is already the world of the future" [pp.82]. Two neo-Hollywood films "achieve their power through this quality of futurity": *Petulia* (settings "among the outsized machines of advanced medical physics," the motel of "absolute privacy — just at the moment that experience is ceasing… to be one's own") and *Bullitt* (a world "not different from ours… yet the simplifications and opacities of the lives it presents are what ours may fully become"; it "forms a complete and abstract world," its narrative mode "not 'Once upon a time…' but 'What if one day…'") [pp.82–83]. He credits Hitchcock as "the master of this effect" — "the perception of a familiar locale as a natural setting for a hitherto unrelated individual task or predicament" (tennis match in *Strangers on a Train*, cymbal crash in *The Man Who Knew Too Much*, crop-duster in *North by Northwest*): "what is revealed is the radical contingency of convention" — "society's web of expectancies may at any moment be torn… to span the abyss of the unexpected (that is to say, the future)" [pp.83–84].

He treats two "apparent exceptions." Godard's *Alphaville* holds "that the cities we now inhabit are the future" yet is in black and white — but there "the black and white are made to function like colors" (bright metallic interiors, night exteriors, plus Godard's "de-psychologizing or un-theatricalizing the characters") [p.84]. *Rosemary's Baby*, in color but "firmly rooted in the immediate past," uses color "to establish a world of private fantasy," not "to unify a world" but "to juxtapose opposing moods and to symbolize mutually exclusive environments" [p.84]. The "great example of this combination of fantasy and color symbolism is Hitchcock's *Vertigo*," which "establishes the moment of moving from one color space into another as one of moving from one world into another" (Stewart's storage-room door opening into "a florist shop alive with bright flowers, predominantly red"); he traces the *Rosemary's Baby* allusions to *Vertigo*'s red/green traffic lights and Hitchcock's "smoke and dust… as signals of evil" [pp.84–85].

This launches the chapter's central reflection on *fantasy*. Each of these films "is about the power of fantasy… its power to survive every inroad of science and civility intact, and to direct the destiny of its subject with, finally, his active cooperation" [p.85]. *Vertigo* "seems at first to be about a man's impotence" but "turns out to be about the specific power of a man's fantasy" not merely "to forgo reality… but to gear every instant of his energy toward a private alteration of reality" [p.85]. Cavell then states a key positive doctrine: "It is a poor idea of fantasy which takes it to be a world apart from reality, a world clearly showing its unreality. Fantasy is precisely what reality can be confused with. It is through fantasy that our conviction of the worth of reality is established; to forgo our fantasies would be to forgo our touch with the world" [pp.85–86]. He invokes Freud ("To retrieve stifled fantasy so that its annihilating power can command the self's self-esteem… to insist upon a world with room in it for fruitful work and love") and faults external "analyses of ideology" that "fail to honor… the ineradicable weave of fantasy within ideology" — Marx "thought it was separable and called it Utopianism, not seeing his own"; politics and fantasy now "try to devour the other," "two directions of nihilism," politely called "the failure of our institutions" [p.86].

He resumes *Vertigo* as the purest conveyance of "the sealing of a mind within a scorching fantasy" — "the modern Pygmalion reverses his exemplar's handling of his desire, and turns his woman to stone" — praising Kim Novak's casting ("the featurelessness of her presence and nonexistence of her acting allow full play to one's perversity… declaims perfect pliancy") [pp.86]. Hitchcock "trades on an idea that even Freud could not stomach — that the fantasy of a transcendent God… is the product of adults… whose knowledge is of childhood past" [pp.86–87]. This frames Hitchcock's "preoccupation with nuns and churches," a zone "in which superstition, expectancy, explanation, and obsession cross one another"; the Hitchcock heroine is "a defrocked nun." Voyeurism — "explicit in *Rear Window* and *Psycho*" but "a dominant mood of his narration as a whole" — "is a retracted edge of fantasy; its requirement of privacy shows its perversity. Modern advertising is its offspring." Decisively: "Narrative voyeurism is Hitchcock's way of declaring the medium of film, a condition of which is that its subjects are viewed from an invisible state" [p.87].

He develops *Rosemary's Baby* as "about the power of fantasy" of motherhood. Rather than mere ambiguity ("What Rosemary comes to think may be happening, is happening"), "the cause of hell's power is not her husband's pact with it. Her own succumbing to the fantasy of motherhood produces her world" [pp.87–88]. The Time magazine "Is God Dead?" cover is "an annunciation": "In the absence of God, it is up to her to create God. And what is thus created, in isolation, is not God" [p.88]. (He adds the Nietzsche gloss: the news of God's death "has come as news, i.e., as gossip," in which form "the knowledge Nietzsche speaks of cannot come to us at all… the absence of conscience, of so much as the possibility of self-knowledge" [p.88].) "Rosemary's is the original sin of mothers, to regard what they give birth to as their own" [p.88]; he reads Kant on the responsibility to children as "logically indifferent to mother love" — care "is not automatically satisfied by liking them" [pp.88–89]. The Devil "deals in pacts and bargains, and we must call him up; God's medium is the covenant, or promise, and he calls us… unbidden" [p.89].

Cavell now states the **theoretical core** connecting color to futurity. "I have recorded my experience of the work of color in serious films as a de-psychologizing or un-theatricalizing of their subjects. My hypothesis is that, correctly understood, this would account for the feel of futurity" [p.89]. The argument: "Black and white was the natural medium of visual drama. This is painting's underlying legacy to photography: value contrast in painterly modeling is a means by which depicted gesture and posture takes on individual (as opposed to hieratic or symbolic) significance and in so doing becomes humanly dramatic" [pp.89–90]. Hence "theatricalism and realism are not… opposed; rather, the acceptance of theatricalism is then a condition of our accepting a work as the depiction of human reality" [p.90]. "One impulse of photography… is to theatricalize its subjects"; "the photographer's command, 'Watch the birdie!' is essentially a stage direction" — the deep question being "why the impression of naturalness is conveyed by an essentially theatrical technique" [p.90]. "The inherent drama of black and white film" clarifies "what it is about film that invited the outline clarity of types" and justified the genres: "In accepting these works as movies we accepted what they depicted as reality… but reality… dramatized." Crucially, film did not create this: "the ease with which we accepted film reality came from our having already taken reality dramatically. The movie merely confirmed what the nineteenth century completed" [p.90].

He retells the autobiographical anecdote (c.1940): told that color "made [movies] unrealistic," the young "already a philosopher" Cavell refuted it (the real world is not black and white) and prophesied all films would be in color — but "I now have an explanation of the truth of his idea." It is not that film colors were inaccurate nor that color stories were unrealistic: "It was that film color masked the black and white axis of brilliance, and the drama of characters and contexts supported by it, along which our comprehensibility of personality and event were secured. Movies in color seemed unrealistic because they were undramatic" [pp.90–91].

Then comes the long historical excursus (pp.91–94) reconstructing how *drama* itself migrated out of art into society — and thus why dramatic comprehension is failing. **[재구성: 이 장의 가장 난해한 구간으로, 카벨의 논증 사슬은 다음과 같이 재구성된다 — (i) 종교가 천국을 떠나 드라마가 되고, 드라마가 예술을 떠나 정치가 되고, 정치가 사회·자아 전체에 스며들어 다시 종교가 되었다. (ii) 이성이 모든 사회적 세부와 개인적 관계에 주의를 돌리자(Hegel의 전체적 차원만이 아니라 Bentham·William Morris의 친밀한 "design" 차원까지) 신비에 맡겨질 영역이 사라졌으나, 신비는 사라진 게 아니라 단지 마음과 도시의 더 깊은 곳으로 밀려나 퍼졌다. (iii) 사회가 이제 이론적으로 변경 가능한 것으로 제시되자, 사회를 바꾸지 못함이 "무능(impotence)"으로 경험되고, 따라서 사회적 행동에 대한 이론적 설명의 필요가 무한해졌다 — "모든 것이 변하고 있으나, 아무것도 변할 수 없으므로, 모든 것이 해석을 요구한다." (iv) 그러므로 우리 조건에 대한 "총체적 설명은, 설득력을 가지려면, 극적(dramatic)이어야 했다."]** Key moves verbatim: when "princes and kings were thrown from the stage… it came to encompass social intercourse as a whole; you could not tell the stage from the house" [p.91]; "If religion, as it left heaven, became drama, then drama, as it left art, became politics; and politics… became religion" [p.91]; "Everything was changing, but nothing can be changed; so everything… stands in need of interpretation" [pp.91–92]; "our total explanation of our condition, to be convincing, had to be dramatic" [p.92]. He reads Marx's "first time as tragedy, the second time as farce" as expressing "both the genius that set him apart from his age and the genius that placed him inside it"; "class conflict" and "classlessness" are "inherently liable… to theatrical employment" [p.92]. "When everyman becomes his own philosopher, he simultaneously becomes his own sophist," so philosophy "no longer knows to whom to direct its voice… a message in a bottle" cast on "darkening straits" — and Cavell adds, "I am without the authority to excuse myself either for, or from, that position" [p.92]. He glosses "Victorian" as "the insane and independent energy of reason" and "the moralization of morality"; "Society and the perception of society move past drama into melodrama. Movies begin as Victorian theater" [pp.92–93]. Machiavelli "first described the theatricalization of politics"; Marx "first planned the recovery from this mortal slapstick," "class consciousness" standing "as a permanent goal of social epistemology" — the claims being "that our place in society has become unknown to us," that self-knowledge comes "only together with knowledge of the self's society," "as though what is 'unconscious' in an adult is not merely his psychic past but his social present" [p.93].

He continues: "self-consciousness, for the foreseeable future, will start, and mostly continue, as embarrassment," because "the discovery of modern society… is the discovery of modern individuality, whether as isolated, as homeless, or as dispossessed" [pp.93–94]. Periodizing discoveries (Greeks/the self; 18th–19th c./childhood), "our recent accomplishment has been the discovery of adolescence… and our task is to discover the existence of community" [pp.93–94]. "Revolution… is society's self-dramatization; as romanticism… is the self's self-dramatization. But then drama was still a form of acknowledgment" [p.94].

Now the **payoff** linking the excursus back to color: "When dramatic explanations cease to be our natural mode of understanding one another's behavior… black and white ceases to be the mode in which our lives are convincingly portrayed." But "since until yesterday dramatic modeling was the mode in which the human appeared… its surcease must seem to us the vanishing of the human as such" [p.94]. Painting and sculpture "found ways to cede human portrayal" (e.g., paintings "without value contrast among their hues") "in favor of the unappeasable human wish for presentness and beauty"; "but movies cannot cede human figuration or reference." Therefore: "Movies in color cede our recently natural (dramatic) grasp of those figures, not by denying so much as by neutralizing our connection with the world so filmed." And the crucial inference — "since it is after all our world that is presented to us, and since those figures… do after all resemble us, but since nevertheless they are no longer psychically present to us, we read them as de-psychologized, which, for us, means untheatricalized. And from there it is only logical to project them as inhabiting the future, a mutation away from the past we know" [p.94].

Cavell then carefully limits his claims (pp.94–95): he has described color "as packaging, as unifying the worlds of make-believe and of fantasy, and as projecting a future," but not claimed "these are all its uses." Counter-instances: in *Contempt*, Bardot "flooded in changing centerfold or calendar hues" registers that "our tastes and convictions in love have become pornographized, which above all means publicized, externalized" [p.95]. Nor is futurity projectable "only through color": "In Bergman's harsh black and white mysteries, the future began a long time ago; it is already old" — the melodrama being "whether we will arrive to ourselves in time to remove its sting" [p.95]. Nor must all directors pursue futurity: "The greatest will probably resist it, for the future has replaced the past as the object of timely elegy" [p.95].

He devotes a sustained analysis to **Antonioni** as an innovator who "gets" futurity "with his spacing of film time, in particular by his fermata over single shots, which enclose an air of presentiment" — "an autograph emotion of surrealist painting," making Antonioni "the inheritor of surrealism from painting" [pp.95–96]. His obsession with façades of uninhabited new buildings (the *L'Avventura* town development "a transcription of Chirico") — they "are not haunted, we know nothing is present inside them, they have no past"; his juxtaposition of old and new; his making "his people, humanity as a whole, seem archaic"; "Absence is obviously a root topic in Antonioni, as it was in surrealism" [p.96]. In the Monica Vitti trilogy there is "the absence of not merely feeling, but of so much as the effort to explain that absence"; "Male impotence is no longer a personal problem… but merely a portion of the new human landscape." The final shot of *L'Avventura* (the woman's hand on the man's shoulder) expresses acceptance "that there is nothing to forgive… no new place to be won on the other side of this moment" — faithfulness as accepting "a world of uneventful adventure" [pp.96–97].

He turns to the extended **critique of Godard** (pp.96–100). For Godard's post-*Breathless* characters "there is no longer any problem of ending or change. They are somewhere else, already in a future," established "not by altering the psychology of his characters… but by depersonalizing them from the start" [pp.96–97]. Color-neutralization and make-believe/fantasy worlds are "antithetical" to Godard's effort; "his people are without fantasy (hence pastless and futureless, hence presentless)," and his depersonalization "depends both upon our responding to these characters as persons and upon our continuously failing to read their motions within… ordinary human emotion" [p.97]. Against critics who liken Godard to Brecht's distancing theater, Cavell objects that "'Actor' and 'audience' lack clear application to film," so the distance "one reads… as one does in reality, as the inability to feel," and we attribute our distance "to Godard's position"; because the films "do not themselves justify or clarify his position, it remains arbitrary," depriving him of "a Brechtian justification" [pp.97–98]. He insists an artist "does have a position and does have his reasons" — "What entitles him to our attention is precisely his responsibility to this condition" — and that when "an art demands that its disciple call its existence into question and then affirm it, his responsibility is also in question" [p.98]. "In art — as now in politics, as formerly in religion… finding the right to speak the truth is as difficult as finding the truth"; "Where the silence of attention is not maintained by force, it is gathered through personal standing. When three stand on the same ground, there is a community" [p.98]. Godard's admirers read his "withdrawal of feeling as a combination of knowingness and objectivity," but "objectivity is a spiritual achievement, and apart from it knowingness is only a sentiment" [p.98].

He contrasts the Belmondo figure in *Breathless*, who "has achieved objectivity by winning his subjectivity": "There is no unhappy love" turns "an apparently empirical claim into a definition of his world"; "You are a coward" is "not an accusation but… an observation" [p.98]. "After that, Godard seems to have regarded such achievement as impossible or unnecessary… Yet without it, his claims to serious criticism of the world are empty" [pp.98–99]. The decisive challenge: pouring "further slogans into that world" ("People speak in slogans," "Women have become objects") has no critical value; "how do you distinguish the world's dehumanizing of its inhabitants from your depersonalizing of them?… your disapproval of the world's pleasures… is not criticism (the negation of advertisement) but censoriousness (negative advertising)" [p.99]. He analyzes Godard's "eyes-on interview" with subjects like Anna Karina: achieved "not by justifying a subject's acceptance of the camera… but by taking a subject with no character, from whose person he has removed personhood… That is the condition of prostitution, and of advertisement. And Godard has created it, not captured it" [pp.99–100]. Finally, Godard's "use of the sound of philosophy": a "good perception" that "the sound of sense still falls sweetly upon the human ear," but "Godard hasn't seen it through, because he does not care whether what the philosopher says is valid or not." Cavell develops the Platonic theme that "sexual attractiveness has been an open motive to philosophy" — "Where philosophy is foreplay, that at least refuses intellectuality as a substitute for sexuality"; "The love that philosophy can teach is the power to accept intimacy without taking it personally. Its opposite is vanity" [p.100]. Godard's girls "walk away intact"; but "they are seduced — by slogans, advertisements, and illicitness" [p.100]. (The Godard critique runs to the end of the chapter, p.100; the *La Chinoise* coda spills onto p.101 under the next chapter heading "Automatism," where Cavell notes "the color suggests make-believe and so provides the out that the whole thing is child's play.")

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "A second major property of film which can serve to declare its recording of a total world is color." [p.80]
- "These films discovered that color can serve to unify the projected world in another way than by direct reliance upon… the spatial-temporal consistency of the real world. The world so unified is obviously not the real past world of photography, but a consistent region of make-believe." [p.81]
- "the world created is neither a world just past nor a world of make-believe. It is a world of an immediate future." [p.82]
- "The narrative mode is not 'Once upon a time…' but 'What if one day…'" [p.83]
- "Fantasy is precisely what reality can be confused with. It is through fantasy that our conviction of the worth of reality is established; to forgo our fantasies would be to forgo our touch with the world." [pp.85–86]
- "Narrative voyeurism is Hitchcock's way of declaring the medium of film, a condition of which is that its subjects are viewed from an invisible state." [p.87]
- "In the absence of God, it is up to her to create God. And what is thus created, in isolation, is not God." [p.88]
- "Black and white was the natural medium of visual drama. This is painting's underlying legacy to photography: value contrast in painterly modeling is a means by which depicted gesture and posture takes on individual… significance and in so doing becomes humanly dramatic." [pp.89–90]
- "the acceptance of theatricalism is then a condition of our accepting a work as the depiction of human reality." [p.90]
- "It was that film color masked the black and white axis of brilliance… along which our comprehensibility of personality and event were secured. Movies in color seemed unrealistic because they were undramatic." [pp.90–91]
- "If religion, as it left heaven, became drama, then drama, as it left art, became politics; and politics, no longer confined to one realm, pervaded society and the self; it became religion." [p.91]
- "Everything was changing, but nothing can be changed; so everything… stands in need of interpretation." [pp.91–92]
- "our total explanation of our condition, to be convincing, had to be dramatic." [p.92]
- "When everyman becomes his own philosopher, he simultaneously becomes his own sophist." [p.92]
- "Movies begin as Victorian theater." [p.93]
- "Revolution… is society's self-dramatization; as romanticism… is the self's self-dramatization. But then drama was still a form of acknowledgment." [p.94]
- "since nevertheless they are no longer psychically present to us, we read them as de-psychologized, which, for us, means untheatricalized. And from there it is only logical to project them as inhabiting the future, a mutation away from the past we know." [p.94]
- "In Bergman's harsh black and white mysteries, the future began a long time ago; it is already old." [p.95]
- "the future has replaced the past as the object of timely elegy." [p.95]
- "objectivity is a spiritual achievement, and apart from it knowingness is only a sentiment." [p.98]
- "how do you distinguish the world's dehumanizing of its inhabitants from your depersonalizing of them?" [p.99]
- "That is the condition of prostitution, and of advertisement. And Godard has created it, not captured it." [pp.99–100]
- "The love that philosophy can teach is the power to accept intimacy without taking it personally. Its opposite is vanity, which takes every attention personally and none intimately." [p.100]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:**
- *Gone With the Wind* / Technicolor — color as luxury packaging; "the natural visual equivalent of the Scott-descended prose."
- *The Wizard of Oz* (Lahr, Haley, Bolger, Burke, Hamilton); *The Adventures of Robin Hood* (Errol Flynn); Olivier's *Henry V* — color unifying a "consistent region of make-believe"; children's tales.
- *The Cabinet of Dr. Caligari* — artificially unified environment that "opposes" reality (madness, constriction), its black-and-whiteness essential.
- *Red Desert*, *Fahrenheit 451*; *Petulia*; *Bullitt* — color declaring "a world of an immediate future" ("What if one day…").
- Hitchcock: *Strangers on a Train* (tennis), *The Man Who Knew Too Much* (cymbal), *North by Northwest* (crop-duster) — familiar locale as natural site of the unexpected; "the radical contingency of convention."
- *Alphaville* — black-and-white "made to function like colors," city-as-future exception.
- *Rosemary's Baby* (Polanski) — color as private-fantasy symbolism; motherhood-fantasy producing her world; the "Is God Dead?" annunciation.
- *Vertigo* (Stewart, Kim Novak) — moving between color spaces = moving between worlds; the "modern Pygmalion" who "turns his woman to stone"; fantasy of a transcendent God.
- *Rear Window*, *Psycho*, *The Lady Vanishes* (Tippi Hedren films) — Hitchcockian voyeurism as "declaring the medium of film."
