- Freud, Marx, Nietzsche, Kant, Hegel, Machiavelli, Rousseau, Bentham, William Morris, Empson, Kleist (*The Marquise of O.*), Thoreau, Lévi-Strauss — the historical/philosophical excursus on fantasy, ideology, the theatricalization of politics, drama→melodrama, and the "discovery" of modern individuality.
- *Contempt* (Bardot) — color registering love "pornographized… publicized, externalized."
- Bergman's black-and-white mysteries — futurity ("already old") without color.
- Antonioni — *L'Avventura* (Chirico "transcription"; final hand-on-shoulder shot), the Monica Vitti trilogy; surrealist inheritance, "fermata," presentiment, "absence."
- Chirico / surrealist painting — source of Antonioni's "autograph emotion."
- Godard — *Breathless* (the Belmondo figure: objectivity by winning subjectivity), the Anna Karina "eyes-on interviews," the "sound of philosophy" dialogues, *La Chinoise*; the sustained critique of depersonalization, arbitrary position, censoriousness, and false Brechtian/objectivity credentials.
- Manet's *Olympia* — the model who can accept "her own self-awareness," contrasted with Godard's character-less subjects.
- Plato — sexual attractiveness as "an open motive to philosophy"; "platonic" as foreplay.

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *the world as a whole / a total world* — what color, like the earlier-noted age, can "declare film's recording of"; color unifies the projected world independently of the real world's spatial-temporal consistency.
- *color (as a declared condition of a medium)* — color used not as packaging/make-believe/fantasy-symbolism but as a constitutive condition; it then "neutralizes" the dramatic black-and-white axis and projects futurity.
- *world of make-believe* vs. *world of private fantasy* vs. *world of an immediate future* — three world-creating functions of color (children's-tale unification; mood/environment symbolism; futurity).
- *futurity / world of the future* — the temporal mode of recent serious color film: "What if one day…" rather than "Once upon a time…"; the future as the new object of elegy.
- *value contrast / the black and white axis of brilliance* — painting's legacy to photography: tonal modeling by which gesture/posture gains "individual (as opposed to hieratic or symbolic)" and "humanly dramatic" significance; color "masks" it.
- *drama / dramatic modeling / theatricalism* — the mode in which "the human appeared" and personality/event became comprehensible; "theatricalism and realism are not opposed" — accepting theatricalism is a condition of accepting a work as human reality.
- *de-psychologizing / un-theatricalizing* — the effect of serious color (and of Godard's depersonalization): subjects resemble us and present our world yet are "no longer psychically present," so are read as de-dramatized — hence projected into the future.
- *fantasy* — not "a world apart from reality" but "precisely what reality can be confused with"; the medium through which "our conviction of the worth of reality is established"; woven ineradicably into ideology.
- *(narrative) voyeurism* — viewing "from an invisible state," a "retracted edge of fantasy"; Hitchcock's mode of "declaring the medium of film."
- *presentness / presentness and beauty* — the "unappeasable human wish" that painting/sculpture serve by ceding human portrayal; counterposed to the loss of psychic presence in color film.
- *position (of the artist) / objectivity* — the artist's responsibility-grounded stance toward what he shows; "objectivity is a spiritual achievement," distinguished from mere "knowingness"; its absence makes criticism into "censoriousness (negative advertising)."
- *acknowledgment* — what drama (revolution as society's, romanticism as the self's, self-dramatization) "still was" before dramatic comprehension lapsed.

---

---

### Ch 14 — Automatism [book pp. 101–107]

**Project:** Cavell introduces "automatism" as his master concept for the medium of film and, more broadly, for any artistic medium. He works to hold together two senses of the word — (a) the *photographic* automatism by which film mechanically reproduces the world, and (b) the *modernist* sense of an automatism as an inherited convention/medium "a something" the artist relies on — and to show why film, having been *given* its automatism rather than having to invent one, only now (in modernism) faces the task of establishing new ones.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell resumes the thread from earlier chapters: film satisfies "the wish for the magical reproduction of the world by enabling us to view it unseen" [p.101], and what we wish to see this way is "the world itself—that is to say, everything" [pp.101–102]. He immediately ties this to modern philosophy: "everything" is precisely what modern philosophy (Kant, Locke, Hume — and on the other side Hegel, Marx, Kierkegaard, Nietzsche) has told us is "metaphysically beyond our reach or... beyond our reach metaphysically" [p.102]. So the wish to view the world itself is "wishing for the condition of viewing as such" [p.102] — viewing has become our way of establishing connection with a world from which we are separated.

He then gives the famous phenomenology of the modern self: "our natural mode of perception is to view, feeling unseen. We do not so much look at the world as look out at it, from behind the self" [p.102]. Our fantasies are "unseen and must be kept unseen," and we are less than ever able "to marry them to the world" [p.102]. **[재구성: 이 단락은 회의주의의 사적·심리적 판본이다 — 자아가 세계로부터 물러나 '뒤에서 내다보는' 조건이 곧 세계의 현전을 직접 소유하지 못하는 회의주의적 조건이며, 영화는 이 물러섬을 기계화함으로써 거기에 응답한다.]** Viewing a movie "makes this condition automatic, takes the responsibility for it out of our hands" [p.102] — hence movies "seem more natural than reality," not as escapes into fantasy but as "reliefs from private fantasy and its responsibilities" [p.102]. Movies convince us of the world's reality "in the only way we have to be convinced... by taking views of it" [p.102].

Cavell now turns to the first sense of automatism. What enables moving pictures to satisfy the wish is "the automatism of photography" [p.102]. He carefully restricts the claim: film not used photographically (to reproduce the world) is "not being used in its power of automatism" [pp.102–103]. The crucial line: "Reproducing the world is the only thing film does automatically" [p.103]. Art can be made without this power — but *movies* cannot. What "made the movie a candidate for art is its natural relation to its traditions of automatism" [p.103]. And here the hinge to modernism: "The lapse of conviction in its traditional uses of its automatism forces it into modernism; its potentiality for acknowledging that lapse in ways that will redeem its power makes modernism an option for it" [p.103].

He glosses film's "natural relation to its traditions of automatism": a given movie "can naturally tap the source of the movie medium as such," and "the medium is profounder than any of its instances" [p.103]. This was *always* true of art: the power of a sonnet, rondo, or portrait "was its power to stand for the form it took and thence to invoke the power of poetry or music or painting as such" [p.103]. He then states the thesis about modernism that governs the rest: "Modernism signifies not that the powers of the arts are exhausted, but on the contrary that it has become the immediate task of the artist to achieve in his art the muse of the art itself—to declare, from itself, the art as a whole for which it speaks, to become a present of that art" [p.103]. Restated: "the task is no longer to produce another instance of an art but a new medium within it" [p.103]. (He flags "the relevance of series in modern painting and sculpture, and of cycles in movies, and of the quest for a 'sound' in jazz and rock" [p.103].) Consequence: in this predicament "media are not given a priori," and "the failure to establish a medium is a new depth, an absoluteness, of artistic failure" [p.103].

Cavell next revisits a doubt: whether the movie is an art at all, "its effects being too powerful or immediate to count as the effects of art" [p.103]. He notes (recalling Agee) that in any film "some moment of interest, even beauty, is likely to appear" — that is "what the camera, left to itself, is like: the objects it manufactures have for us the same natural interest... as the world itself" [p.104]. So the puzzle: how, while the other arts "folded themselves double and risked absolute failure," could movie-makers "tap the source of their art so innocently?" — i.e., how could the movie remain *traditional* for "these sixty years" [p.104].

He now restates the modernist task as "establishing a new automatism" [p.104], and clarifies the second (modernist) sense of the term. The word fits both (i) "the broad genres or forms in which an art organizes itself (e.g., the fugue, the dance forms, blues)" and (ii) "those local events or topoi around which a genre precipitates itself (e.g., modulations, inversions, cadences)" [p.104]. Calling these "automatisms" does *not* mean they "automatically ensure artistic success or depth," but that "in mastering a tradition one masters a range of automatisms upon which the tradition maintains itself, and in deploying them one's work is assured of a place in that tradition" [p.104].

To pose the category accurately, he says he must first develop it in modernist painting (the program for Ch 15). This will "neutralize the presence of the physical mechanisms of camera and projector" [pp.104–105]. Those mechanisms "produce the physical or material basis of the medium of film, which I am articulating as successions of automatic world projections" [p.105]. What gives the physical basis significance are "artistic discoveries of form and genre and type and technique, which I have begun calling automatisms" [p.105]. He concedes the apparent perversity of using "automatism"/"automatic" both for film's physical basis and for these artistic achievements, but insists "I do not take the perverseness here to be of my making" [p.105]: it belongs (a) to the identity of film — "its medium just does have this manufacturing mechanism at its basis" — and (b) to "the fate of modernist art generally—that its awareness and responsibility for the physical basis of its art compel it at once to assert and deny the control of its art by that basis" [p.105]. This is why he keeps one word for both: "confusion here is caused by precisely the fact that this concept is justified in both places," and "modernist art is itself an investigation of this confusion" [p.105].

The complexities, he says, "are at once historical and ontological" [p.105]. When one says philosophically that the medium of an art is its physical basis (paint, words, sound), one may be suppressing/assuming knowledge of the history of forms, or recognizing that established genres are "'merely' 'conventional,'... cannot be taken for granted any longer," each placing "demands which constrict as well as release a subject" [pp.105–106]. He brings in Lessing's *Laocoön*: there the search for the limits of the individual arts assumes the arts are "different genres... of a total something called the Arts," sharing the obvious common task of narrating/picturing events, each faithful "to its own way, dictated by the nature of its medium" [p.106]. But "what modernist painting proves is that we do not know a priori what painting has to do or be faithful to in order to remain painting" [p.106]. Hence there is no common "thing" done differently by different arts: "Its mode of existence is different" [p.106]. Modernism "seems to break down the concept of genre altogether: what a painter or poet or composer has to achieve... is not a landscape or sonnet or fugue, but the idea of his art as such" [p.106].

He sharpens the ontological problem. A description of the styles/genres of classical music "would be a description of the media of classical music" [p.106]; but in modernist art, where style/genre lack clear application, "the concept of a medium loses touch with ideas of manner and ordonnance, and seems to separate out for denotation the physical materials of the art as such" [p.106]. So: what is the medium of painting/poetry/music as such? Two responses, "neither... an answer." (1) "The medium of music as such is sound as such; the medium of painting is paint as such" — but this implies "all sound is music, all areas of color are paintings," which says nothing about the nature of the art; it is rather "a claim about... humankind's experience of the world, e.g., that nature... is for certain creatures a medium of expression" [p.106] (the claim romanticism took over from religion). (2) "Nothing is the medium of, say, painting as such. A medium of painting is whatever way or ways paint is managed so as to create objects we accept as paintings. Only an art can define its media" [p.107]. A modernist art, "investigating its own physical basis, searching out its own conditions of existence, rediscovers the fact that its existence as an art is not physically assured" [p.107]. It "gracefully accepts our condemnation to meaning—that for separate creatures of sense and soul, for earthlings, meaning is a matter of expression; and that expressionlessness is not a reprieve from meaning, but a particular mode of it; and that the arrival of an understanding is a question of acknowledgment" [p.107]. **[재구성: 여기서 카벨은 매체 개념을 물리적 기체로부터 떼어내, 매체란 '예술로 인정받는 대상을 만들어내는 방식'이라고 재정의한다 — 즉 매체는 예술에 선행해 주어지는 것이 아니라 예술이 사후적으로 정의하는 것이며, 따라서 모더니즘은 매체의 존재 자체가 보증되지 않는 사태를 발견한 예술이다.]**

He closes with three "impulses" behind calling an artistic medium an "automatism." *First:* when such a medium is discovered "it generates new instances: not merely makes them possible, but calls for them, as if to attest that what has been discovered is indeed something more than a single work could convey" [p.107]. *Second:* "the notion of automatism codes the experience of the work of art as 'happening of itself'" [p.107]. In a tradition the great figure "knows best how to activate its automatisms, and how best to entice the muse to do most of the work"; in a modernist situation "there is no such help: your work is all your own, there is no longer a natural relation between your work and its results, you are looking for what works (happens of itself)" [p.107]. Crucially: "The automatisms of a tradition are given to the traditional artist, prior to any instance he adds to it; the master explores and extends them. The modernist artist has to explore the fact of automatism itself" [p.107] — investigating "what it is at any time that has provided a given work of art with the power of its art as such" [p.107]. *Third:* calling the creation of a medium the creation of an automatism registers that the point is "to free me not merely from my confinement in automatisms that I can no longer acknowledge as mine (this was the point of the explicit efforts at automatic painting and writing early in the century), but to free the object from me, to give new ground for its autonomy" [pp.107–108]. (Note: the assigned "movies are a moving image of skepticism" formulation does *not* appear within pp.101–125; the closest move here is the account of viewing-unseen as the mechanized form of the skeptical condition.)

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Reproducing the world is the only thing film does automatically." [p.103]
- "what has made the movie a candidate for art is its natural relation to its traditions of automatism. The lapse of conviction in its traditional uses of its automatism forces it into modernism; its potentiality for acknowledging that lapse in ways that will redeem its power makes modernism an option for it." [p.103]
- "the medium is profounder than any of its instances." [p.103]
- "Modernism signifies not that the powers of the arts are exhausted, but on the contrary that it has become the immediate task of the artist to achieve in his art the muse of the art itself—to declare, from itself, the art as a whole for which it speaks, to become a present of that art." [p.103]
- "the task is no longer to produce another instance of an art but a new medium within it.... media are not given a priori. The failure to establish a medium is a new depth, an absoluteness, of artistic failure." [p.103]
- "What modernist painting proves is that we do not know a priori what painting has to do or be faithful to in order to remain painting." [p.106]
- "Only an art can define its media. A modernist art, investigating its own physical basis, searching out its own conditions of existence, rediscovers the fact that its existence as an art is not physically assured." [p.107]
- "the arrival of an understanding is a question of acknowledgment." [p.107]
- "The automatisms of a tradition are given to the traditional artist, prior to any instance he adds to it; the master explores and extends them. The modernist artist has to explore the fact of automatism itself." [p.107]
- "to free the object from me, to give new ground for its autonomy." [pp.107–108]

**Examples / films / artists / works invoked:**
- *La Chinoise* (Godard) — carried over from p.101's opening (the train dialogue / make-believe color); not central to the automatism argument.
- The sonnet, rondo, portrait — illustrate that any instance's power is its power to "stand for the form it took" and invoke the art as such.
- The fugue, dance forms, blues — broad genres/forms as automatisms.
- Modulations, inversions, cadences — local *topoi* as automatisms.
- James Agee — recalled for the observation that any film yields some moment of interest/beauty ("the camera, left to itself").
- Lessing's *Laocoön* — the pre-modernist assumption that the arts are genres of one "total something called the Arts," each faithful to its medium's nature; foil for modernism's breakdown of genre.
- Northrop Frye — cited for the term "genre" as an alternative Cavell declines.
- Early-century "automatic painting and writing" — the Surrealist/Dada efforts; contrasted as freeing the self vs. (his point) freeing the object.

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **Automatism (photographic sense)** — film's mechanical, hand-independent power to reproduce the world; "the only thing film does automatically."
- **Automatism (modernist sense)** — an inherited convention/form/medium ("a something") on which a tradition maintains itself and which the artist relies on; both broad genres and local *topoi*. The medium of film *is* a set of such automatisms, not the camera or celluloid.
- **Medium** — not the physical basis but "whatever way or ways" a material is managed so as to create objects accepted as that art; "only an art can define its media."
- **Successions of automatic world projections** — Cavell's articulation of film's physical/material basis (the camera/projector mechanism producing it).
- **Muse of the art itself** — the modernist task of achieving, in a single work, the whole art for which it speaks; "to become a present of that art."
- **Acknowledgment** — the mode in which understanding arrives; meaning as expression that must be owned; the home of knowledge (developed in Ch 15).
- **"Happening of itself"** — the experiential core of automatism: the work seems to occur on its own; the traditional artist entices the muse, the modernist has lost the "natural relation between your work and its results."
- **Condemnation to meaning** — for "earthlings," meaning is unavoidably a matter of expression; expressionlessness is itself a mode of meaning, not an escape from it.

---

### Ch 15 — Excursus: Some Modernist Painting [book pp. 108–117]

**Project:** Cavell develops the concept of automatism (and "medium," "acknowledgment," "presentness") through modernist painting — Pollock, Louis, Noland, Olitski, Stella — using Greenberg's and Fried's terms (flatness, the support, acknowledgment of conditions) to show how modernism makes the *conditions* of an art into the art's subject, and to ground his later claims about film.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell begins from William Rubin's monograph *Dada, Surrealism and Their Heritage*, which relates Pollock to automatism: "What Pollock took from Surrealism was an idea—automatism—rather than a manner" [p.108]. Cavell sharpens *which* idea: "The surrealists looked for automatisms which would create images; Pollock looked for an automatism with which to create paintings" [p.108]. Using automatism to make paintings "is what painters have always done"; so for a new automatism to make *paintings*, Pollock "had newly to consider what constitutes a painting... and, in particular, to discover what would give any automatism of his the significance of painting" [p.108].

The third feature of automatism (its claim for the *autonomy* of the art object, from Ch 14) shows why it is wrong to call Pollock's major work "action painting" [p.108]. The "action" was a discovery that "precisely evaded the traditional actions of painters, which he had found no longer made paintings," and it "would have absolutely no artistic relevance unless it produced entities we accepted as paintings" [pp.108–109]. (Analogy: calling a Pollock an "action painting" because the painter moved in new ways is "about as useful as calling a treaty a piece of paper for the reason that it is written on paper" [p.109].)

So what *was* the discovery — the all-over line? What makes that "a discovery, or an automatism"? Not merely its being "a way of laying on paint quickly, with repeated gestures," since repetitive gestures and quickness "are in themselves not news" [p.109]; and it "would have been no discovery at all had not his all-over line... been accepted as painting" [p.109]. The right question: "Since it makes paintings, what does his all-over line discover?" [p.109]. Cavell's answer: a primitive fact of painting — "not exactly that a painting is flat, but that its flatness, together with its being of a limited extent, means that it is totally there, wholly open to you, absolutely in front of your senses... as no other form of art is" [p.109]. He names this **total thereness**, and distinguishes it from the old spatial/temporal classification of the arts: total thereness "can be taken as a denial of (physical) spatiality," since "what is in three-dimensional space is not all there to the eyes" [p.109].

He then asks the epistemological question this raises: what does it mean that a painter "discovers, by painting, something true of all paintings, something that everybody has always known"? Not the unconscious becoming conscious, but "like something hidden in consciousness declaring itself. The mode is revelation" [p.109]. Following Michael Fried, he calls this "an acknowledging of [painting's] conditions" [pp.109–110]. The decisive distinction: "Any painting might teach you what is true of all painting. A modernist painting teaches you this by acknowledgment—which means that responding to it must itself have the form of accepting it as a painting, or rejecting it" [p.110]. He registers the paradox that ordinary acknowledgment concerns "only what I know to be true of me," whereas this painting "is supposed to be speaking for all painting" — and answers: "that is where the art comes in"; there have always been "men who have been able to acknowledge something that other men accept as true also of them, or that they have to deny" [p.110].

He compresses the epistemology into a chain: "Painting, being art, is revelation; it is revelation because it is acknowledgment; being acknowledgment, it is knowledge, of itself and of its world" [p.110]. Modernism "did not invent this situation; it merely lives upon nothing else" — "reasserting that acknowledgment is the home of knowledge," recalling "what the remainder of culture is at pains to forget" [p.110]. He rejects the "avant-garde" picture of modernism (a coherent culture vs. a massed enemy) in favor of "an effort, along blocked paths and hysterical turnings, to hang on to a thread that leads from a lost center to a world lost" [p.110]. **[재구성: 카벨에게 '인정(acknowledgment)'은 단순 인식(recognition)이 아니라, 대상을 회화로서 받아들이거나 거부하는 행위를 통해서만 그 진리에 접근하는 응답의 양식이다 — 따라서 모더니즘 회화의 진리는 관조자의 능동적 응답 없이는 성립하지 않는다.]**

He multiplies the ways a painting can acknowledge total thereness: its "frontedness," "finitude," or "specific thereness—that is, its presentness," so that accepting it means acknowledging one's own frontedness/directionality/verticality and "your presentness, in its aspect of absolute hereness and of nowness" [p.110]; or a painting may declare "that a painting, like nature, is of more than one color, and that its colors occur simultaneously" — "not a simultaneity of events but of the reaches of a world" [p.110]. These "possibilities," he says, are "historical unfoldings" declared in the post-Pollock paintings he responds to — "especially those of Morris Louis, Kenneth Noland, Jules Olitski, Frank Stella" [pp.110–111].

He isolates a quality: "openness achieved through instantaneousness—which is a way of characterizing the candid" [p.111]. **The candid** has "a reverse feature": it "must occur independently of me or any audience, that it must be complete without me, in that sense closed to me" [p.111]. This is why candidness in acting required "the actor's complete concentration within the character," denying any control of the audience's awareness over him; and when theatrical conventions "lost their naturalness and became matters of mutual complicity," serious drama had "to deny my control openly"—by removing any "character" to disappear into (Beckett) or "explicitly wedging the mutual consciousness of actor and audience between the actor and his character (Brecht)" [p.111].

Openness and closedness "do not describe particular forms within paintings, but conditions of painting as a form of art" [p.111]. Their declaration is "most palpable in the work of Louis"—especially the *Unfurled* series—because of "the frankness that leaves individual colors not merely separate but separated," and because "one sees how little may be required in order to make the object complete, to close it" [p.111]. The point is not the amount of work ("great masters can afford to finish a work without giving it a finish"): rather these paintings "acknowledge that no matter how much work goes into the making of a work, at some point the work must be done, given over, the object declared separate from its maker, autonomous; that he has seen it, that it is good" [p.111]. ("'Finish' becomes a way of avoiding that renunciation and judgment.") They acknowledge "not merely what paintings are, but what the painting of them is" [pp.111–112] — two primitive features: first, "the responsibility for making them is more significant than the manner in which they are made" (they may even have to be made "without painting"—opaquing an expanse); second, "the painting of them must get over and done with, come to a form, be brought into the open and to a close" [p.112].

He connects this to closure as a *spiritual* requirement, via Kierkegaard's criticism of "premise-authors," "helpless to draw conclusions"; "writing requires the moral stamina to conclude, to achieve resolution, in the self and of the self's work" [p.112]. He recalls his earlier claim that abstract painting achieves "a condition of photographs by their use of (the 'possibility' of) cropping," "predetermining the amount after the fact" [p.112]. In *traditional* painting cropping seems superficial (like mounting or framing), but "the nature of these new paintings gives significance to the fact of framing; they create a way of acknowledging finality as a specific spiritual step," so that "the exactness of the cropping is essential to the entire achievement" [p.112].

The "recession of manner (in the face... of the presentness of the painting itself)" explains why "the concept of style has no clear application to these modernist works" [p.112]: "the 'manner in which they paint' refers merely to the technical procedures they use, and there is no established connection between those procedures and the responses they yield. Nothing comes between the idea and its realization" [pp.112–113] (Croce: intuition and expression; Greenberg found this "patently correct for these artists"). The candidness of Louis's *Unfurleds* lies in "the vast expanse of canvas he has, in candor, left white"—the effect of "the side falls of colors, which bring the vastness to uncanny incandescence" [p.113]; achieved "without the trace of hands or wrists or arms, without muscle—the idea realizing itself—an automatism of canvas and paint... is set in motion, admitting an overpowering beauty" [p.113]. He qualifies (only *Sigma* strikes him as of overpowering beauty among the *Unfurleds*) and notes that "one automatism may not be so deep or fertile as another," that a transition *between* automatisms can be "a larger moment than a transition from one to another instance of a single automatism" (instancing Louis's *High*, between the *Veils* and the *Unfurleds*) [p.113]. "But to speak of an automatism which admits a sometimes overpowering beauty is a way of characterizing nature" [p.113].

This launches the chapter's romantic-ontological climax. The works of Pollock, Louis, Noland, Olitski "achieve in unforeseen paths an old wish of romanticism—to imitate not the look of nature, but its conditions, the possibilities of knowing nature at all and of locating ourselves in a world" [p.113]. For the old romanticist those conditions appeared as nature's power of destruction/healing/fertility; for these modernists they "present themselves as nature's autonomy, self-sufficiency, laws unto themselves" — quoting Wittgenstein: "'Not how the world is, but that it is, is the Mystical'" [p.113]. This is "not a return to nature but the return of it, as of the repressed... the release of nature from our private holds" [pp.113–114]. Such art "reasserts that... nature is held—we are held by it—only in common" [p.114]; "its declaration of my absence and of nature's survival of me puts me in mind of origins, and shows me that I am astray" [p.114]. It "faces me, draws my limits, and discovers my scale... gives me horizon and gravity," reasserting that "in whatever locale I find myself, I am to locate myself" [p.114]. It "speaks of terror, but suggests elation," its faith being "that we can still be moved to move, that we are free, if we will, to step upon our transport"; nature's absence is "only the history of our turnings from it" [p.114]. (Hegel "rose without arising, harped upon movement without readying us to take steps; that is why Marx and Kierkegaard... turned him over.")

Cavell then defends abstraction epistemologically. We no longer "trust our representations" such that "the absence of them must mean... the absence of the things represented" [p.114]; art in the absence of representation can declare "that those earlier approaches to nature had indeed been the making of representations—not merely of the world, but to it, as appeals or protests" [p.114]. There are as many causes of painting the world as reasons for wording it ("to appeal, protest, state, claim, assert, judge, comment, notice, witness, accuse, praise, interrogate, pledge, remember, behold...") [p.114]; but "at some point in the last century, such appeals and protests were found to be made on behalf of nobody, to nothing. At such a juncture, art, if it is to survive, ends" [p.114]. He dismisses the sophomore's "discovery" that pictures were never objective because never perfect replicas: as silly "as to say that people can never be represented because their representatives are other people" [p.115]. What matters is faithfulness and objectivity — "what you are being faithful to," "on behalf of whom," and "why and when an objective representation... fails to capture your interest" [p.115]. Perhaps "what we must be faithful to is our knowledge that distance from nature is no longer represented by perspective, which places us in relation to it... and falsifies our knowledge that we are lost to nature" [p.115]; then "an art that reveals without representation may give us perspective" — e.g., showing "that a painting must be viewed alone, from the one place one occupies at any time," an acknowledgment "that one must take them one at a time" [p.115].

This grounds **the fact of series**: "a new medium establishes and is established by a series. Each instance of the medium is an absolute realization of it; each totally eclipses the others" [p.115]. Unlike old masterpieces (a fugue "might have worked out differently," so calling it "inevitable" praises it), with a Pollock all-over line, a Louis stripe, a Noland chevron, or a Stella Z-form, "any change... would simply create a new instance, an absolutely new painting" [pp.115–116]; here "inevitable" is "no longer a term of praise, but a statement of its existence" [p.116]. "These are works every one of whose properties is essential to them. This is the definition of a Leibnizian monad" — "like the world there is, the only fact about these paintings that does not follow analytically from a complete idea of them is that they exist in space and time," which is "the only contingent fact about them"; "they are themselves... contingencies, realizations" [p.116].

"Nothing but our acceptance of an instance determines whether its series is worth realizing... when we find that a series is exhausted, it is absolutely past, over" [p.116]. As instances "they declare the evanescence of existence in space and time" — whereas "Literal or material objects do not declare evanescence... Metaphysically a pyramid is as evanescent as wrappings are, or as a flash of neon" [p.116]. To the charge that no object "the actual making of which is so unimportant" could bear the importance attached to art, he concedes their existence-as-instances "is carried on their face; labor is not in them; they look as if they might... have been made instantaneously" — but "the fact about an instance, when it happens, is that it poses a permanent beauty, if we are capable of it" [p.116]. That simultaneity proffers beauty is "a declaration about beauty: that it is no more temporary than the world is; that there is no physical assurance of its permanence; that it is momentary only the way time is, a regime of moments; and that no moment is to dictate its significance to us, if we are to claim autonomy" [p.116].

Acceptance of such objects "achieves the absolute acceptance of the moment, by defeating the sway of the momentous" [pp.116–117] — "an ambition worthy of the highest art" [p.117]. "Nothing is of greater moment than the knowledge that the choice of one moment excludes another... that the significance of one moment is the cost of what it forgoes. That is refinement" [p.117]. "Beauty and significance, except in youth, are born of loss" [p.117]. The "idea of infinite possibility is the pain, and the balm, of adolescence"; the only return on adulthood is "the reception of actuality—the pain and balm in the truth of the only world: that it exists, and I in it" [p.117].

He closes with **haecceity**: in its "absolute difference and absolute connection with others, each instance of a series maintains the haecceity (the sheer that-ness) of a material object, without the need of its substance" [p.117]. (Perhaps minimal art "wants to convey" this; but modernist painting *acknowledges* it.) So "I must respond to it... by acknowledging my own haecceity, that my existence is inescapable from my presentness" [p.117]. He contrasts the two: "In response to minimal art I am deployed, dematerialized, unidentifiable... In response to modernist painting, I am concentrated, finitized, incarnate" [p.117]. These abstractions, retaining "the power of art" after representation's failure, "have overcome the representativeness which came between our reality and our art... by abstraction, abstracting us from the recognitions and engagements and complicities... which distracted us from one another and from the world we have constructed" [p.117] — the dictum: "Attracted from distraction by abstraction. Not catching our attention yet again, but forming it again" [pp.117–118]. They "exist as abstracts of intimacy—declaring our common capacity and need for presentness, for clear separateness and singleness and connection... for the simultaneity of a world, for openness and resolution" [p.118]; they "represent existence without assertion; authority without authorization; truth without claim, which you can walk in" [p.118] — out of which vision "Thoreau in *Walden*... speaks of nature as silent" [p.118]. He ends with the open question whether representation is "otherwise irretrievable" — whether one can "declare again the *content* of nature, not merely its conditions" — quoting Thoreau: "'Who knows what the human body would expand and flow out to under a more genial heaven?'" [p.118].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "The surrealists looked for automatisms which would create images; Pollock looked for an automatism with which to create paintings." [p.108]
- "Since it makes paintings, what does his all-over line discover?" [p.109]
- "not exactly that a painting is flat, but that its flatness, together with its being of a limited extent, means that it is totally there, wholly open to you, absolutely in front of your senses... as no other form of art is." [p.109]
- "Any painting might teach you what is true of all painting. A modernist painting teaches you this by acknowledgment—which means that responding to it must itself have the form of accepting it as a painting, or rejecting it." [p.110]
- "Painting, being art, is revelation; it is revelation because it is acknowledgment; being acknowledgment, it is knowledge, of itself and of its world." [p.110]
- "openness achieved through instantaneousness—which is a way of characterizing the candid." [p.111]
- "no matter how much work goes into the making of a work, at some point the work must be done, given over, the object declared separate from its maker, autonomous; that he has seen it, that it is good." [p.111]
- "they acknowledge not merely what paintings are, but what the painting of them is." [pp.111–112]
- "to imitate not the look of nature, but its conditions, the possibilities of knowing nature at all and of locating ourselves in a world." [p.113]
- "'Not how the world is, but that it is, is the Mystical.'" [p.113]
- "This is the definition of a Leibnizian monad.... Existence in this world, like the existence of the world itself, is the only contingent fact about them." [p.116]
- "Acceptance of such objects achieves the absolute acceptance of the moment, by defeating the sway of the momentous." [pp.116–117]
- "Beauty and significance, except in youth, are born of loss." [p.117]
- "In response to minimal art I am deployed, dematerialized, unidentifiable... In response to modernist painting, I am concentrated, finitized, incarnate." [p.117]
- "Attracted from distraction by abstraction. Not catching our attention yet again, but forming it again." [pp.117–118]
- "They represent existence without assertion; authority without authorization; truth without claim, which you can walk in." [p.118]

**Examples / films / artists / works invoked:**
- Jackson Pollock; the **all-over line** — the paradigm modernist automatism; his "action" evaded traditional painters' actions yet still made paintings; discovers painting's "total thereness."
- William Rubin, *Dada, Surrealism and Their Heritage* — source for the Pollock/automatism link Cavell refines.
- Morris Louis; the *Unfurled* series (esp. *Sigma*), the *Veils* series, *High* — declare openness/closedness, candor, the white expanse, "an automatism of canvas and paint"; the transition between series as a larger moment.
- Kenneth Noland (the chevron), Jules Olitski, Frank Stella (the Z-form) — post-Pollock painters declaring the "possibilities" of painting; each change yields "an absolutely new painting" (the series/monad point).
- Michael Fried — source for "acknowledgment" of a painting's conditions.
- Clement Greenberg — found the "nothing comes between idea and realization" point "patently correct" for these artists; the medium-as-flatness lineage.
- Croce — intuition and its expression; "nothing comes between the idea and its realization."
- Beckett; Brecht — two ways serious drama denies the audience's control once theatrical conventions lose naturalness (no character to disappear into; wedging actor/audience consciousness).
- Kierkegaard — "premise-authors" helpless to conclude; closure as moral stamina; (with Marx) "turned over" Hegel.
- Hegel; Marx — Hegel "rose without arising"; the romantics' shift between paralysis and omnipotence.
- Wittgenstein (*Tractatus*) — "Not how the world is, but that it is, is the Mystical."
- Leibniz — the monad: a work each of whose properties is essential, contingent only in existing in space/time.
- Minimal art — wants to convey haecceity but only *conveys* (vs. acknowledges) it; leaves the viewer "dematerialized."
- Thoreau, *Walden* ("The Pond in Winter") — nature as silent; the closing questions about nature's content.

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **Total thereness** — painting's condition (from flatness + limited extent) of being wholly open, absolutely in front of the senses, as no other art is; a denial of ordinary three-dimensional spatiality.
- **Acknowledgment (of conditions)** — (after Fried) the modernist mode in which a painting reveals what is true of all painting such that responding *must* take the form of accepting or rejecting it as a painting; "the home of knowledge."
- **Revelation** — the mode of modernist painting: "something hidden in consciousness declaring itself" (not the unconscious made conscious).
- **The candid / candidness** — openness achieved through instantaneousness; with its reverse feature: completeness/closedness independent of any audience.
- **Openness and closedness** — not forms within paintings but *conditions* of painting as art: the work's openness to the viewer and its closure/autonomy from its maker.
- **Presentness** — a painting's specific thereness in its aspect of "absolute hereness and of nowness," which the viewer must answer with his own presentness.
- **Series** — the form in which a new medium establishes/is established by its instances; each instance an absolute realization that eclipses the others; any change makes a new painting.
- **Leibnizian monad** — model for such works: every property essential, the only contingent fact being existence in space and time.
- **Haecceity** — "the sheer that-ness" of a material object, which each instance maintains "without the need of its substance"; the viewer answers it by acknowledging his own haecceity (existence inescapable from presentness).
- **Evanescence** — instances "declare the evanescence of existence in space and time" (which literal objects, however transitory, do not declare).
- **"Acknowledging finality as a specific spiritual step"** — the new significance modernist painting gives to cropping/framing/closure.

---

### Ch 16 — Exhibition and Self-Reference [book pp. 118–125 (extending into p.126)]

**Project:** Cavell contrasts film's situation with the modernist plight just described — film "avoided" (did not answer) modernism's perplexities because it is *screened*, not *exhibited* — and then redeems what is valid in the popular notion of modernist "self-reference" by re-routing it through his concept of *acknowledgment*, distinguishing genuine self-acknowledgment from self-reference as mere "exhibiting of self," with film's early (comic) self-reference as his test case.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

The chapter opens by stating film's exemption: "Movies from their beginning avoided (I do not say answered) modernism's perplexities of consciousness, its absolute condemnation to seriousness" [p.118]. Media "based upon successions of automatic world projections" do not have to do the three things modernist arts must do: they "do not... have to establish presentness to and of the world: the world is there"; "do not have to deny or confront their audiences: they are screened"; "do not have to defeat or declare the artist's presence: the object was always out of his hands" [p.118]. **[재구성: 14·15장에서 모더니즘 회화의 세 과제 — 현전 확립, 관객과의 대면, 작가 현존의 처리 — 가 자동적 세계-투영이라는 영화의 물리적 기체 자체에 의해 이미 해결되어 있다는 것이 핵심이다; 영화는 이 과제들을 '회피'한 것이지 '해결'한 것이 아니므로, 모더니즘에 진입할 때 비로소 그것들이 문제로 되돌아온다.]**

He then traces a finer dialectic of theatricality. "Setting pictures to motion mechanically overcame... the inherent theatricality of the (still) photograph" [pp.118–119]; fast film let subjects "be caught unawares," yet "they are nevertheless caught; the camera holds the last lanyard of control we would forgo" [p.119]. Freed from "the painter's formalisms of composition," photography "rebounds to the draftsman's formalisms of caricature" [p.119]; artistry must "explore the condition of capture itself" to discover "what will register as candor and what instantaneousness can reveal of character and of the relation of character to its locale" [p.119]. He probes the "eyes-on pose" as a sign of candor: in animal photographs it "tends to deprive them of their innerness, to anthropomorphize them" (do we project "a human prospect of self-consciousness"?), and photographs "tend to anthropomorphize nature itself" [p.119]. The "primitives" who fear photographs "are not wrong to be terrorized": we laugh at the idea that their souls are captured, "pleased with that respect for our power," but "it may be that they see their bodies being given a foreign animation"; "We are too used to what happens to us" [p.119].

In motion, "the photographic subject is released again," and the viewer is released "from the links of nostalgia"; but "another region of theatricality overtakes the image: the presenting of the past world becomes a presentation of it. Instead of being caught in a pose, the subject is cast in a type" [p.119]. So "the maker of films has then to find media within which types can project the world's presentness" [p.119]. Film "takes our very distance and powerlessness over the world as the condition of the world's natural appearance"; it "promises the exhibition of the world in itself. This is its promise of candor: that what it reveals is entirely what is revealed to it, that nothing revealed by the world in its presence is lost" [pp.119–120].

He guards against over-claiming. Does this mean "everything revealed by film is true"? Then why does Steichen take "a thousand various pictures of the same cup and saucer"? Because we cannot "assume that we know beforehand how few or many revelations the truth will take" [p.120]. "Call truth infinite"; the facts are unlimited and "all are compatible (compossible)" [p.120]. But it is "also a fact that the world has to be told not to dictate what we are to say about it" — and he runs a quick philosophical history of the will and error: Descartes (error in "the will's freedom to overreach our position"; freedom as the price of knowledge), Milton (error as the price of freedom), Kant (keeping "nature from swamping" the will), Hegel (riding the will "on the back of history"), the Romantics ("still shift between paralysis and omnipotence") [p.120].

He returns to the modernist paintings: they "seek to refuse knowledge that is not a mode of acknowledgment." Their "achievement of certainty (through the candor of their total presentness, leaving no room for will), together with silence (through the naturalness of their beauty, leaving no need for will), works out an environment in which conscience is stirred" [p.120]. Against the complacent — someone who, "rebuked by modernism's custody of seriousness," declares as a "new freedom" that "now anything can be exhibited and so tried as art" — Cavell answers: "that is just the problem, that perhaps all you can do with your work and works is to exhibit them, that all hope for acknowledgment by and of the self is to be forgone... stares of amusement or boredom replacing all acceptance and real rejection"; such people "will be amused by the plight of the Hunger Artist" [p.120].

He turns to **exhibition** as a historical-ontological condition. "Like religion, art had to learn to defeat theater, to close the remove of our vision from its object, to stand the self within the self again so that it may bear again the world's gaze" [p.121]. If modernism's quest for presentness "arises with the growing autonomy of art (from religious and political and class service; from altars and halls and walls), then that quest is set by the increasing nakedness of exhibition as the condition for viewing a work of art"; so "the object itself must account for the viewer's presenting of himself to it and for the artist's authorization of his right to such attendance" [p.121]. He fends off the objection that this just restates that "art provides pleasure and entertainment and amusement and recreation": such an explanation "further denatures pleasure" [p.121]; given "the meanness of the beliefs we hold and... our disgust with the work we do," how can our after-hours capacities "have remained intact"? [p.121]. He praises Nietzsche on Luther — "that he had the courage of his sensuality... to marry his desire" [p.121] — and concludes "The boast of pleasure is the modern hypocrisy" [p.121]. Dada "burlesques the condition of high art... It mimics the condition in which the fact of exhibition takes precedence over the quality and meaning of the thing exhibited"; vaudeville "makes that condition laughable" [pp.121–122].

Now the central contrast: "Movies from their beginning avoided the shadowing of seriousness by exhibition, because they are simply not exhibited (or performed), but distributed and screened and viewed" [p.122]. "One print of a movie is as full and authentic an instance of it as any other... It is not a substitute for an original, but its manifestation" [p.122]. Photographs differ — they can have "many originals" (those the photographer accepts as realizations), each print "vies with every other for that acceptance" — whereas with a movie "the craft of its maker does not extend through to each instance; when his work is done, he releases it to multiply itself. It is everything a commodity should be: equal instances available to all, regardless of position" [p.122]. **[재구성: 여기서 카벨은 영화의 존재론을 회화·사진의 '원본/실현' 논리와 구별한다 — 영화의 각 프린트는 원본의 대체물이 아니라 동등한 '현현(manifestation)'이며, 이 비위계적 복수성이 영화가 '전시'의 곤경을 면제받는 존재론적 근거다.]**

He reflects on novelty: people "amazed to see the motion in motion" responded "as if by the novelty," but "what movies did at first they can do at last: spare our attention wholly for that thing now, in the frame of nature, the world moving in the branch" [p.122]; "It is not novelty that has worn off, but our interest in our own experience" — "Our experience is so coarsened that we can be moved by it only through novelty, and that has lost interest" [p.122]. "Because movies can still work their original fortune... most good movies are still largely traditional" — but the traditions "are borne uneasily" [p.122]. The "sudden storms of flash insets and freeze frames and slow-motions and telescopic-lens shots and fast cuts and negative printing and blurred focusings" — unlike early-period experiments — "are responses to an altered sense of film, a sense that film has brought itself into question and must be questioned and openly confessed" [pp.122–123].

This sets the chapter's titular problem: "How, specifically, are movies questioning themselves, and what specifically requires acknowledgment in their making?" [p.123]. The "habitual answer" begins from the idea "that the way for a work to acknowledge itself is to refer to itself," underwritten by "an indefinite... notion that modern art is 'about' itself, painting about painting, music about music, the novel about the novel" [p.123]. Cavell rejects this bluntly: "Stranded upon such a notion, you couldn't tell the difference between Mallarmé and Joyce Kilmer" [p.123]. What is *valid* in "self-reference" is that "the artist's self-consciousness has come between his conviction and his work, between himself and the conventions (automatisms) he relied upon, forcing him to justify his works even as he performs them. This is the Protestant malady, and legacy" [p.123]. It means "the modernist artist is first of all a modern man: his art is his way of questioning his humanity without forfeiting it—to taste, to appetite, to irony, to boredom... to halfhearted skepticism, to halfhearted belief" [p.123].

He then re-grounds self-reference in **acknowledgment** as a speech-act. "My harping on acknowledgment is meant to net what is valid in the notion of self-reference" [p.123]. The "explicit form of an acknowledgment is 'I know I [promised; am withdrawn; let you down]...'" — but "that is not the only form it can take," and "it is not clear why this form functions as it does" [p.123]. Crucially: "We should not assume that the point of the personal pronoun here is to refer to the self, for an acknowledgment is an act of the self (if it is one of recognition, then it is not like recognizing a place but like recognizing a government)" [p.123]; it is inseparable from "an admission of the existence of others (denial of which made the acknowledgment necessary)" and from "an expression of one's aliveness to that denial (the revelation in acknowledgment)" [pp.123–124]. So "self-reference is no more an assurance of candor in movies than in any other human undertaking. It is merely a stronger and more dangerous claim, a further opportunity for the exhibiting of self" [p.124]. **[재구성: 카벨의 결정적 수는 모더니즘의 '자기-지시(self-reference)'를 '자기-인정(self-acknowledgment)'으로 재서술하는 것이다 — 인칭대명사 '나'는 자아를 *지시*하는 것이 아니라 *타자의 존재를 인정*하는 자아의 *행위*이므로, 단순한 자기 언급은 진정성을 보증하기는커녕 자아를 *전시*할 또 하나의 위험한 기회일 뿐이다.]**

He charges the popular notion with "forgetfulness of the film's early capacities for self-reference, both by alluding to other movies and by calling attention to the camera at hand" [p.124], and gives instances: Groucho Marx finding a sled marked "Rosebud" and saying "I thought they burned that"; Katharine Hepburn in *The Philadelphia Story* saying "The calla lilies are in bloom again" (her line from *Stage Door*); Cary Grant on meeting Ralph Bellamy in *His Girl Friday*: "Haven't we met someplace before?" (they had, in *The Awful Truth*) [p.124]. These "inside joke[s]" "confirm for the insiders a strong sophistication in moviegoing, a proof that their increasing consciousness of movie-making routines will not jeopardize the film's strength" [p.124]; so French New Wave allusions to Hollywood "are not simple acts of piety... but claims to be a continuation of that tradition" [p.124].

He then distinguishes good from bad self-reference. Instead of confirming conviction "by a gesture of self-awareness whose self-confidence inspires conviction, the gesture may attempt to distract us from our lack of conviction"; instead of "laughing at himself with a well-deserved appreciation of self, an author... may wish to steal our laughter in order to cover his embarrassment" [pp.124–125]. His instance of *bad* self-reference is *Tom Jones*: Albert Finney, having rescued the half-naked widow, "uses his cloak to cover the camera instead of shielding her from its gaze" [p.125]. It might have worked had Finney's character been "innocently capable of that confusion of courtesy," but "Finney's reflex is to shield himself" — since Tom's "natural confusions of nature and convention never lead him to protect himself at the cost of another," we read it "not as Tom's sparing the widow... but as Finney's or Richardson's... cloaking his own" [p.125]. (He notes "an old conspiracy being called upon," as when Groucho "looks into the audience and asks... 'How did I ever wind up in this lousy picture?'") [p.125].

He observes "A striking feature of these self-referential instances is that their effect is comic" [p.125], confirmed in "three extendedly reflexive movies": Fields's *Never Give a Sucker an Even Break* (it dawns on us that "what Fields has in his hands is the whole film we are seeing"); Olsen and Johnson's *Hellzapoppin'* (a character "caught... by an off-sprocket film alignment... hoists himself over the frame separation"); Keaton's *Sherlock Jr.* (the dreamer "repeatedly undone by shifting scene placements") [pp.125–126]. The comedy "arises first from a transgression of mechanical or conventional conditions upon which coherent narration in film has depended" [p.126]; reversing the old idea that laughter is "a burst of danger miraculously escaped," *Sherlock Jr.* and *Hellzapoppin'* "project dangers which the new art miraculously creates" [p.126]. Keaton uses "the fact that in film anything can follow anything... to depict our lack of control over both fantasy and reality—above all, our helplessness to align them" [p.126]; *Never Give a Sucker* shows "there is nothing beyond the power of film to make us accept: man's adaptability, his suckerhood, is his greatest trick and his greatest threat" [p.126]. The closing thesis: "We are at the mercy of what the medium captures of us, and of what it chooses, or refuses, to hold for us. This comedy of self-reference satirizes the effort to escape the self by viewing it, the thought that there is a position from which to rest assured once and for all of the truth of your views" [p.126].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Movies from their beginning avoided (I do not say answered) modernism's perplexities of consciousness, its absolute condemnation to seriousness." [p.118]
- "they do not... have to establish presentness to and of the world: the world is there. They do not have to deny or confront their audiences: they are screened. And they do not have to defeat or declare the artist's presence: the object was always out of his hands." [p.118]
- "the presenting of the past world becomes a presentation of it. Instead of being caught in a pose, the subject is cast in a type." [p.119]
- "It promises the exhibition of the world in itself. This is its promise of candor: that what it reveals is entirely what is revealed to it, that nothing revealed by the world in its presence is lost." [pp.119–120]
- "One print of a movie is as full and authentic an instance of it as any other... It is not a substitute for an original, but its manifestation." [p.122]
- "It is not novelty that has worn off, but our interest in our own experience." [p.122]
- "Stranded upon such a notion, you couldn't tell the difference between Mallarmé and Joyce Kilmer." [p.123]
- "the artist's self-consciousness has come between his conviction and his work, between himself and the conventions (automatisms) he relied upon, forcing him to justify his works even as he performs them. This is the Protestant malady, and legacy." [p.123]
- "We should not assume that the point of the personal pronoun here is to refer to the self, for an acknowledgment is an act of the self." [p.123]
- "self-reference is no more an assurance of candor in movies than in any other human undertaking. It is merely a stronger and more dangerous claim, a further opportunity for the exhibiting of self." [p.124]
- "We are at the mercy of what the medium captures of us, and of what it chooses, or refuses, to hold for us. This comedy of self-reference satirizes the effort to escape the self by viewing it, the thought that there is a position from which to rest assured once and for all of the truth of your views." [p.126]

**Examples / films / artists / works invoked:**
- Steichen's "thousand various pictures of the same cup and saucer" — truth is infinite/compossible; we don't know in advance how many revelations it takes.
- Kafka's *Hunger Artist* — the figure whose plight (the collapse of acceptance/real rejection into amusement/boredom) the complacent will find exaggerated.
- Dada / vaudeville — burlesque/mimic the condition in which "the fact of exhibition takes precedence over the quality and meaning of the thing exhibited."
- Groucho Marx — "I thought they burned that" (the "Rosebud" sled); and "How did I ever wind up in this lousy picture?" — self-reference by allusion and by addressing the audience.
- *The Philadelphia Story* / *Stage Door* (Katharine Hepburn, "The calla lilies are in bloom again") — inside-joke allusion to another film.
- *His Girl Friday* / *The Awful Truth* (Cary Grant, Ralph Bellamy, "Haven't we met someplace before?") — allusion to a prior role-juxtaposition.
- French New Wave directors — Hollywood allusions as *claims to continue* a tradition, not mere piety.
- *Tom Jones* (Albert Finney; dir. Tony Richardson) — the cloak-over-the-camera gag: Cavell's case of *failed/bad* self-reference (Finney shielding himself, not the widow).
- *Never Give a Sucker an Even Break* (W. C. Fields, with Franklin Pangborn) — extended reflexivity: Fields holds "the whole film we are seeing"; man's "suckerhood."
- *Hellzapoppin'* (Olsen and Johnson) — the off-sprocket frame-separation gag; danger the new art creates.
- *Sherlock Jr.* (Buster Keaton) — the dreamer "undone by shifting scene placements"; film's "anything can follow anything" and our helplessness to align fantasy and reality.
- Nietzsche on Luther — "the courage of his sensuality," "to marry his desire"; against "the boast of pleasure."
- Descartes, Milton, Kant, Hegel, the Romantics — the history of the will and the possibility of error (freedom as price of knowledge / error as price of freedom / keeping nature from swamping the will / riding it on history / paralysis vs. omnipotence).

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- **Exhibition** — the condition of art's "increasing nakedness" before a viewer once art loses religious/political/class service; the modernist object must "account for the viewer's presenting of himself to it" and for the artist's authorization. Film *escapes* this because it is not exhibited but distributed/screened/viewed.
- **Self-reference (the notion criticized)** — the popular idea that a work acknowledges itself by referring to itself / modern art being "about" itself; Cavell holds this can't distinguish genuine from empty modernism, and is "a further opportunity for the exhibiting of self."
- **Acknowledgment (as speech-act)** — the corrective: an *act* of the self (not a *reference* to it) that admits the existence of others and expresses "one's aliveness to that denial"; "recognizing a government," not "a place."
- **Manifestation (vs. substitute/original)** — each print of a movie is the film's manifestation, an equal authentic instance; grounds film's commodity-like non-hierarchical multiplicity (contrast: photographs have "many originals" competing for the photographer's acceptance).
- **Casting in a type (vs. caught in a pose)** — film's specific theatricality: in motion the subject is no longer "caught in a pose" (still-photographic theatricality) but "cast in a type"; the filmmaker must find "media within which types can project the world's presentness."
- **Candor / promise of candor (in film)** — film's promise that "what it reveals is entirely what is revealed to it," that nothing of the world's presence is lost.
- **The condition of capture** — the photographic subject is "caught," "the camera holds the last lanyard of control"; artistry must explore this to discover what registers as candor.
- **Compossible / truth as infinite** — the facts film can reveal are unlimited and mutually compatible; hence many true pictures of one cup and saucer.
- **The Protestant malady (and legacy)** — the modernist artist's self-consciousness intruding between conviction and work, forcing self-justification; modernism as the condition of "a modern man."
- **Comedy of self-reference** — the comic effect of transgressing "the mechanical or conventional conditions upon which coherent narration in film has depended"; it satirizes "the effort to escape the self by viewing it."

---

### Ch 17 — The Camera's Implication [book pp.126–132]

**Project:** Cavell asks what it now means for the camera (and the film-maker) to be *implicated in* the world it photographs while remaining ontologically *outside* it, and argues that modernist film must move from the easy "narcissistic honesty" of self-reference to the harder acknowledgment of the camera's outsideness to its world and the viewer's absence from that world. The chapter binds the book's skepticism theme — the world is present to me but I am not present to it — to the problem of how a camera can honestly *declare* its presence.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell opens (still trailing the prior chapter on film comedy of self-reference, *Sherlock Jr.*, *Hellzapoppin'*, *Never Give a Sucker an Even Break*) by registering a metaphysical discomfort that frames the whole chapter: "One can feel that there is always a camera left out of the picture: the one working now" [p.126]. He transposes this into a first-person predicament — when my limitation *to* myself feels like a limitation *of* myself, "the saying is left out," and human existence comes to seem "metaphysically dishonest" [p.126]. **[재구성: 카벨은 카메라가 자기가 찍는 그림에는 결코 들어가지 못한다는 사실을, '나'가 내 발화의 진실성을 발화 행위만으로는 세계에 결박할 수 없다는 회의주의적 곤경의 영화적 형상으로 제시한다. 즉 카메라의 외부성은 인간 주체의 외부성의 알레고리다.]** He glosses Berkeley's metaphysics as an attempt "to keep the world honest" by taking it that you cannot imagine a situation without imagining yourself present in it [p.127].

The core problem: there *should* be "some stronger connection between an assertion and the world it asserts than my asserting of it is empowered to make" [p.127], but there is not. Prefacing an assertion with "I assert…" is "just a shift on the same plane of assertion." One imagines catching the connection "in the act, by turning the camera on it" — even putting a camera and crew into the picture — but "that just changes the subject." The camera can photograph itself in a mirror, "but that gets it no further into itself than I get into my subjectivity by saying 'I'm speaking these words now'" [p.127]. He states the governing analogy: "The camera is outside its subject as I am outside my language" [p.127]. This outsideness is "the abyss of ready insincerity," but it is precisely what makes truthfulness possible and virtuous. He invokes Wittgenstein: famous for the *publicness* of language, but his emphasis "falls equally upon the absoluteness of my responsibility for the meaning I attach to my words" [p.127] — publicness is a *shared* responsibility, and the superficiality of what we share is also our responsibility.

Cavell turns to documentary, where the problem arises most explicitly, though "the distinction between documenting and narrating blurs" [p.127]. The documentary film-maker feels an impulse to make his presence known, as if to justify his intrusion — but this is a "guilty impulse," produced by his denial of "the only thing that really matters: that the subject be allowed to reveal itself" [p.127]. The denial is an unwillingness to see what is revealed or an inability to wait for its revelation. He states a moral epistemology: "the only justification for the knowledge of others is the willingness for complete knowledge. That is the justice of knowledge" [p.128], and your position in this is "no more localizable beforehand than the knowledge itself is."

He poses the pivotal question: if the camera's presence is to be made known, "it has to be acknowledged in the work it does" [p.128] — this is the seriousness of the recent fashion for "shakings and turnings and zoomings and reinings and unkind cuts." But why isn't the projected image itself a sufficient acknowledgment, given that we are not in doubt that it comes by way of a camera? "But what is this certainty, this not being in doubt?" [p.128]. Cavell unfolds this through his model of *acknowledgment* (drawn from his work on other minds): mutual knowledge of presence is not yet acknowledgment — "I know I am here; you know I am here… Why isn't that enough?" [p.128]. Because what must be acknowledged "is always something specifically done or not done; the exact instance of my denial of you" [p.128]. The "particular hurt or crudity or selfishness or needfulness or hatred or longing" must be given leave to declare our separateness, hence the possibility of our connection — "It is balm, but it must still touch the wound" [p.128].

Applied to film: the notion of self-reference suggests the camera need only "tip its hand," not hide. That is sensible, and it honors "the hard Berkeleyan-Kantian truth that an event in which we participate is not knowable apart from our knowledge of our participation in it" [p.128]. But mere self-declaration neglects "the Hegelian truth that the actual consciousness of our participation is not assured a priori, but can be won only through the paths over which we have arrived at this event, and at this specific placement toward it" [p.128]. Therefore "the camera cannot in general merely declare itself; it must give at least the illusion of saying something" [p.129] — paired with an analytic-philosophy analogue: you cannot merely *say* something; there is always a reason for saying just that just then, and saying it implies the reason [p.129].

He reads two camera movements. Godard's "long, slow swings" for the conversation piece in *Contempt* are "an original and deep statement of the camera's presence," but by that very fact also "a statement *from* the camera *about* its subjects" — their simultaneous distance and connection, "the sweeping desert of weary familiarity" [p.129]. Against the cliché that the camera is "an extension of the eye" — which would imply that placing it at a character's physical point of view objectively reveals what the character sees — Cavell shows from *Paths of Glory* (Kirk Douglas in the trenches before a doomed attack) that when the camera "moves to a place behind his eyes, we do not gain but forgo an objective view of what he sees; we are given a vision constricted by his mood of numb and helpless rage" [p.129]. The moral has two parts: the camera "will have its assertion," and "a narrator cannot cede his position to his protagonist" — "the protagonist's point of view is not the same as the placement and angle of the camera which records it" [p.129].

He then tests whether the camera's situation is really one of *concealment* or *withdrawal* (the two cases where mere declaration of presence is what's called for). It cannot straightforwardly be concealment, since "there is no more complete evidence of its presence than the shown film itself" [p.130]. Is the difficulty that the camera cannot be present at the same time the film is shown? Live television "overcomes that dishonesty, but only… by giving up connection with the world as such" [p.130]. Is the concealment directed at the *subjects* rather than the audience? Then why not have them glance at the lens? But that misses the problem: the subjects "still would not know the secret of the camera's presence, the foreign animation it imposes upon them" [p.130]; even projecting the footage for them as it is shot would just impose a new animation on their response. And for *spiritual withdrawal*, acknowledgment cannot help — "If you or your camera suddenly swing into life, that is not an acknowledgment but a denial of your withdrawal. You cannot sidestep the claims of a position with a trick. The question is whether you can choose to occupy any, and do it honor" [p.130].

This drives the chapter's thesis: all these questions "prepare for an idea that the camera must now, in candor, acknowledge not its being present in the world but its being outside its world" [p.130]. Cavell states the skeptical reversal at full pitch: "The world's presence to me is no longer assured by my mechanical absence from it, for the screen no longer naturally holds a coherent world from which I am absent" [p.130]. **[재구성: 고전 영화의 '기적'은 내가 기계적으로 부재함으로써 세계가 내게 현존했다는 것이었다. 모더니즘 국면에서 이 보증이 무너지면서, 카메라가 인정해야 할 것은 '내가 세계 안에 있음'이 아니라 '내가 세계 밖에 있음'으로 역전된다.]**

He develops this loss phenomenologically through color. The screen "has darkened, as if in fury at its lost power to enclose its content"; "the opacity of color masks the old brilliance, the answering glow of the black and white screen"; light becomes "indiscriminate, promiscuous" [pp.130–131] — which is why good color films restrict and simplify their groupings (Kubrick's *2001* is "a full if eccentric beneficiary of this fact"; the blurred foreground flowers in *Red Desert* produced "the clearest cases of color shock I've witnessed in an audience") [p.131]. "The images keep staying back, as in photographs they will" [p.131]; feeling this as a loss of connection underlies "the camera's efforts to engulf its subjects" — widening reach, staying close enough to hold their scent, freezing or slowing them "as if to glimpse them before they vanish" (the massive sounds of *Point Blank*, *The Dirty Dozen*) [p.131].

Cavell names this loss "a new theatricalizing of its images… another replacement of the intensity of mystery with the intensity of mechanism" [p.131], a response to the draining of film's original myths of their power to hold conviction in film's characters. The conventions film relied on "have come to seem conspiracies": the close-up, which "used to admit the mysteriousness of the human face, now winks a penny-ante explanation at us." Hitchcock parodies this with "the long final close shot of Tony Perkins in *Psycho*, making a mystery of himself" [p.131]; the vulgar limit is *Ship of Fools*, where Kramer's camera pushes into the dwarf's face (with a "musical thunderclap") to register "at a glance an explicit vision and comprehension of the following decade of German history" [p.131]. Staying "nose-close to the body" finally serves "to stupefy it beyond all expression" [p.132]. He resists attributing the camera's "bear-hugging" merely to television's influence — that "would not account for the movies' preparation to receive it" [p.132] — and praises the "limited ambition of *Mississippi Mermaid*" for "recalling the sweetness of the human body going its ways" [p.132].

He concludes the diagnostic arc: film exhibits "a growing doubt of its ability to allow the world to exhibit itself, and instead its taking over the task of exhibition, against its nature" [p.132]. Crucially, "the same techniques which serve to betray it can also be used, and seen, to keep faith with its nature" — there are "serious uses of these devices, something beyond chic" [p.132]. Against those who dismiss them as "mod," he insists the live question is not whether seriousness will be debased ("Of course the Beethoven Ninth will be used to throw out the news"), but, "in a world beset by seriousness… whether room continues to be made for the genuine article, and whether we will know it when we see it" [p.132]. A film-maker may begin by using a device "merely to embarrass the hateful complacency of studio production technique, in which every question is academic… every triviality is given the same unloving polish" [pp.132–133]; what is peculiar to movies is "their way of allowing new procedures and formats to continue film's particular capacity to reveal only and all of what is revealed to it, to let the world and its children achieve their candidness" [p.133]. The chapter's closing sentence states the trajectory of the book's last movement: "From the narcissistic honesty of self-reference there is opened the harder acknowledgments of the camera's outsideness to its world and my absence from it" [p.133].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "One can feel that there is always a camera left out of the picture: the one working now." [p.126]
- "The camera is outside its subject as I am outside my language. The abyss of ready insincerity is fixed, but that is what makes truthfulness possible—and virtuous." [p.127]
- "But this is a guilty impulse, produced, it may be, by the film-maker's denial of the only thing that really matters: that the subject be allowed to reveal itself." [p.127]
- "the only justification for the knowledge of others is the willingness for complete knowledge. That is the justice of knowledge." [pp.127–128]
- "what is to be acknowledged is always something specifically done or not done; the exact instance of my denial of you… It is balm, but it must still touch the wound." [p.128]
- "Yet it neglects the Hegelian truth that the actual consciousness of our participation is not assured a priori, but can be won only through the paths over which we have arrived at this event, and at this specific placement toward it." [p.128]
- "the camera cannot in general merely declare itself; it must give at least the illusion of saying something." [pp.128–129]
- "When the camera then moves to a place behind his eyes, we do not gain but forgo an objective view of what he sees; we are given a vision constricted by his mood of numb and helpless rage." [p.129]
- "You cannot sidestep the claims of a position with a trick. The question is whether you can choose to occupy any, and do it honor." [p.130]
- "the camera must now, in candor, acknowledge not its being present in the world but its being outside its world." [p.130]
- "The world's presence to me is no longer assured by my mechanical absence from it, for the screen no longer naturally holds a coherent world from which I am absent." [p.130]
- "close-up, which used to admit the mysteriousness of the human face, now winks a penny-ante explanation at us." [p.131]
- "the same techniques which serve to betray it can also be used, and seen, to keep faith with its nature." [p.132]
- "From the narcissistic honesty of self-reference there is opened the harder acknowledgments of the camera's outsideness to its world and my absence from it." [p.133]

**Examples / films / directors / works invoked:**
- Berkeley — metaphysics as "keeping the world honest" by requiring you to imagine yourself present in any imagined situation.
- Wittgenstein — publicness of language *and* the absoluteness of one's responsibility for word-meaning.
- Documentary film (genre) — the locus where the camera's-implication problem arises most explicitly; the film-maker's "guilty impulse" to announce his presence.
- Godard, *Contempt* — the long slow swings as "a statement from the camera about its subjects."
- Kubrick, *Paths of Glory* — the camera moving "behind the eyes" of Kirk Douglas's officer; proof that POV placement yields mood, not objective vision.
- Live television — "overcomes that dishonesty" of the camera's temporal absence but at the cost of "connection with the world as such."
- Kubrick, *2001: A Space Odyssey* — beneficiary of color films' need to restrict groupings.
- Antonioni, *Red Desert* — blurred foreground flowers; "the clearest cases of color shock."
- *Point Blank*, *The Dirty Dozen* — massive sound/amplification as vengeful response to nature's withdrawal.
- Hitchcock, *Psycho* — the long final close shot of Tony (Anthony) Perkins, parody of the explanatory close-up.
- Stanley Kramer, *Ship of Fools* — camera pushing into the dwarf's face; the vulgar limit of the explanatory close-up.
- *A Married Woman* (Godard) — the fragmenting "sexual passage" close to the body.
- Truffaut, *Mississippi Mermaid* — its "limited ambition" recalls "the sweetness of the human body going its ways."
- Beethoven's Ninth — emblem of seriousness inevitably debased ("used to throw out the news").

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *The camera's implication* — the camera's being caught up in / answerable to the world it films, while remaining outside it; "the one working now" that is always left out of the picture.
