- *Acknowledgment* — not mere mutual knowledge of presence but the specific declaration of "what is specifically done or not done," the exact instance of one's denial of the other; "it must still touch the wound."
- *Self-reference / narcissistic honesty* — the (insufficient) modernist gesture of the camera merely "tipping its hand" / declaring itself.
- *Outsideness* — the camera's ontological position *outside* its world, modeled on "I am outside my language"; what modernist film must now acknowledge.
- *Theatricalizing* — film's turn to "exhibiting" its images itself, replacing "the intensity of mystery with the intensity of mechanism."
- *Foreign animation* — the alien life the camera imposes on its subjects, unknown to them.
- *Promiscuous / indiscriminate light* — Cavell's name for the way color (vs. black-and-white's "answering glow") fails to enclose a coherent world.

---

### Ch 18 — Assertions in Techniques [book pp.133–145]

**Project:** Cavell assembles a set of close readings of specific film *techniques* (slow motion, freeze frames, flash insets, newsreel footage, reverse shots, split screen, selective focus) to show that in modernist film a technical device becomes an *assertion* or acknowledgment only through the *integrity* of the particular work — never automatically, never by being "integrated." The chapter operationalizes the principle that "nothing is a 'possibility of a medium' unless its use gives it significance."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell announces a survey: "Let me collect, not quite at random, some uses of the devices I have mentioned" [p.133], and lays down the governing rule: "nothing is a 'possibility of a medium' unless its use gives it significance. And 'the' significance of a possibility is as worth looking for as 'the' meaning of a word" [p.133]. **[재구성: 어떤 기법도 그 자체로 매체의 가능성이 아니다 — 특정 작품에서 의미를 부여받을 때만 가능성이 된다. '그' 의미(정관사)를 찾는 일이 헛되듯, 슬로모션·프리즈 등의 '본질적' 의미를 찾는 일도 헛되다. 의미는 오직 작품의 통합성(integrity) 속에서만 결정된다.]**

*Slow motion.* Riefenstahl (her diving sequences, cuts "syncopated on the rising arc of many dives, against the sun") found "the sheer objective beauty in drifting a diver through thin air" [p.133]. The cliché — "for a fast touch of lyricism, throw in a slow-motion shot of a body in free fall" — is now everywhere (*The World of Henry Orient*, *Popi*, feminine-hygiene commercials). But *Goodbye, Columbus* "blew" the trick by slowing Ali MacGraw's swimming: "ordinary swimming is already in slow motion, and to slow it further and indiscriminately only thickens it… about the only thing you can do to good swimming to make it ugly" [pp.133–134]. By contrast: Mamoulian's *Love Me Tonight* reads a slow-motion field of horses "as silent" [p.134]; Kurosawa counts on this in the slow-motion duel-death of *Seven Samurai* — "the instant of a mortal thrust followed by the long drop into endlessness," capping "the nations of dyings" in which men freeze, double, sink, or topple; "Kurosawa waits out the bounce, illuminated by the shifting flow of the hair, as the body lands" [p.134]. *Point Blank* invites but fails this possibility, yet earns "one lyrical passage from slow motion, in a close-up of bullet shells falling" — lyricism from the counterpoint of identical objects in different attitudes, plus slow motion's intensification of "the reflecting color in the metal" [p.134].

The freeze *finish.* At the end of *Bonnie and Clyde* the film "persists in an elegy of bullets long after the pair are dead"; "Society is making sure of itself. But art is not so satisfied. The camera is at once confessing its invasion of their existences and its impotence to preserve them… at once taking vengeance on them for their absence and accompanying them across the line of death" [p.134]. *Butch Cassidy and the Sundance Kid* does it differently: "the ending freeze of the two figures… instantly translates them to immortality" [p.134]. Cavell diagnoses "the uncontrollable tic of the freeze finish" (contractual in TV drama) as mimicking two old ending topoi: (1) the *comic epilogue* that lets society "breathe a sigh of relief," now degraded into "freezing a face at a point of grimace, a comic 'take'—as if the film couldn't afford to let the subject do his own acting"; and (2) the *outside pull-away* "up from the house or neighborhood," now replaced by stopping departing subjects in their tracks — "unable really to let them go, since otherwise you wouldn't be convinced they had ever appeared" [pp.134–135].

*Flash insets.* In *Hiroshima, Mon Amour* (the first use Cavell recalls) they signify "the presence of the past, the pastness of the past" [p.135] — moments in the consciousness of a woman whose life is an effort "to keep past and present from suffocating one another, and to keep her present and the world's present from betraying one another." It is essential that we feel "it is her consciousness breaking in upon itself" [p.135]: many in Nevers saw the dead German soldier, "but only she bears in a wall of her memory the impression of the curve his fingers made as his body clotted in the roadbed" [p.135]. *The Pawnbroker*'s "imitative use" fails: it "does not accept this burden of significance but expects the device to provide its own… to the film's damnation" [p.135], showing memories anyone could imagine "looking through a grisly picture book of the camp," so "the flash device deprives this bereft man even of his own memories" [p.135]. Cavell runs a relentless series of disqualifying questions about why the black woman's breasts flash the pawnbroker's wife's suffering — each candidate reading either denies the women's humanity, equates incommensurable crimes, or collapses — concluding: "The thing is hopeless. It is only another instance of the appeal to a favorite speechless horror as a cover for the inability to respond" [p.136]. In *La Guerre est Finie*, Resnais uses flash insets (held longer, "because containing internal motion") to mean "the outsideness and insideness not of one time to another, but of one place to another" [p.136] — exploiting the suspense properties of cross-cutting: because Resnais "does not always cut to the flash inset from Montand's face," we do not read it as Montand's imagining; "his friend is there, something specific is happening to him now. This is the fact upon which Montand is acting" [p.136]. "The very objectivity and banality of the flashes… intensify the character's unknownness to us—his privacy, rather, though we know everything" [p.136]. The film "does not… rise to its idea, but it is the idea of a genuine mind," motivating all Resnais' work: "an idea of the simultaneous gravity and weightlessness of the past, as though our present is merely a shifting orbiting of our memories" [pp.136–137].

*Freeze frames* (the chapter's central case). In *Darling* they "show what the world sees of a darling," and the perversity of getting to "pick what the world will see"; in *Jules and Jim* they "show what Jules and Jim see together in the woman of their world" [p.137]. Pairing the two films should "prevent our taking any listing of rhetorical devices for more or less than it is worth": *Darling* is "a clever film," *Jules and Jim* "a masterpiece" [p.137]. One might think the freeze is well-*integrated* in *Darling* (a film about "the shallowness and the depth of appearances, about fashion" — what better motive than "a fashion photographer's hopeful catchings"?), whereas in *Jules and Jim* the device "is used out of the blue" [p.137]. Cavell resists this: "How an automatism is motivated and justified is no simpler a question than how a number is motivated in an opera or musical comedy" [p.137]. *Darling*'s literal justification is "comparable to justifying Astaire's dancing by its being a dance in the world depicted" — but Astaire also transforms offices, skating rinks, golf ranges "into a setting for his dance, letting his wish provide the occasion… making the world dance to his music" [pp.137–138], where resourcefulness with setting is integral to the routine.

He then reads *Jules and Jim* at length to demonstrate that significance flows from integrity, not motivation. The freeze "materialized by their desire… freezes her at the height of laughter" confirms her identity with "the statue they had gone in search of come to life," and that "she is their creation, their greatest work as artists, the one work on which they could stake their lives" — hence "their proof that they are artists… also to be their proof as men." But "this proof fails. Or maybe we fail to know success when we meet with it" [p.138]. The film is "a story of fidelity": its question is "to what degree the human frame can house the unaligned fidelities which human nature requires" — they will not let "comradeship and desire and art and politics destroy the place of each in each"; "the surprise is not that in the end they fail but that they get so far" [p.138]. He insists, against reductive psychologizing/politicizing, that "the relation between self and community (because they are composed of one another) is an undying dialectic" — "you cannot know beforehand whether a given contradiction requires a revolution of self or an adaptation of community" [p.138]. The woman ("She was all women") "will not be the statue, the totem of their community, but she will be watched, and not merely when they want to see" [pp.138–139] — "She is the answer to a prayer, not the servant of wishes" [p.139]. Her two leaps into water are keyed to the men's exclusions of her (Baudelaire's remark on women in church; later, Nazis burning books), each treated as a supposed unity among the men that erases real difference [p.139]. Her "final solution" — taking Jim "for that ride off the earth" and saying again to Jules, "Watch!" Cavell makes the viewer adjudicate: vanity/neurosis, or "loyalty to the three of them"? On his reading, she sees "not that the men have failed but that they have broken faith with the original vows of their community" [p.139]. The book-burning is decisive for Jim: "at the end not even a serious journalist… defeated by the book-burning before he has acted against it," so his conversation with Jules "is a substitute for leading his life… It makes a sentiment of horror" [pp.139–140].

Within this reading Cavell elevates *Jules and Jim*'s *newsreel footage* to a separate technical claim. The book-burning newsreel is "ontologically the negation of the newsreels used earlier in the film" [p.140]: the earlier clips set the little community's life in a real Paris and "halving the film as the First World War halved the characters' lives," placing the friends "in those battles on opposite sides" — where the terror is "the knowledge that the friend, the essence of his community, may be there, unknown to him, as he advances against fire, firing" [p.140]. Truffaut uses the nostalgia and "heartbreaking beauty and terror of the soldiers rising like flowers from their fields, to open us to the knowledge that these mortals… were there, in the only world we inhabit" — "the most elegant, direct, and sustained use of the familiar device of newsreel clips in movies, a possibility of the medium which declares that the world of movies is an extension of the world of news" [p.140]. The book-burning clip, by contrast, "shows us an event taking place at a time the men are not there," serving "to rebuke their absence, or to state that the world has passed them by" [pp.140–141]. He completes the Jules reading: Jules "has not failed, as anyone might, to write a serious novel" but cheats his ambition (the insect novel), turning his forgoing of his wife from "the natural exclusion there is in separateness" into "a denial of his responsibility with her" [p.141]; "Watch!" means "see that your ability to watch and to wait, which was your strength, has become your destructiveness, our destruction" [p.141]. After cremation "the bones of love remain, the genders indistinguishable except in size"; Jules is "relieved" — not of fidelity "but of making room for incompatible faiths," because "with the child, Jules knows he can keep faith" — "He gave birth to the mother of his child, who therefore is the grandchild of his friendship… since all is reflected in each, it is enough to keep the idea alive" [p.141].

Cavell extracts the chapter's central methodological thesis from the *Darling/Jules and Jim* comparison: the point is **not** that one has a simple meaning and the other complex, nor that one is crudely and the other subtly motivated. "It is to show that integrating a device is not the artistic issue, because integration can itself be a device (like motivic unity, or an organization 'based' on a lump of mathematics). There is no substitute for integrity. And it is to show that only the integrity of a given work can make out the significance of a given possibility. If the device is integral to what makes a work convincing, it has full importance; without the conviction it has any and none" [pp.141–142].

He generalizes to the *physical base* of film: "One necessity of movies is that the thread of film itself be drawn across light." Is *that* a possibility of some medium? It is noticed in experimental films "meant to hurt the eyes," and in the epilogue of *Eclipse*, "where the motionlessness within each shot acknowledges the continuous current of film necessary to hold one view in view" — which is why Antonioni's idea "does not read to me… as more do-it-yourself nihilism" [p.142]. He draws a key distinction: "depicted motionlessness feels and looks different from motionless depiction" [p.142]. The *Eclipse* epilogue is "an environment in which something more happens… in which strange gods are readied," reminding us "that half the world is always eclipsed by the world itself" [pp.142–143]. (He notes Monica Vitti's "capacity for fun" as "a factor of her independence, hence of her desirability" [p.143].) The most beautiful declaration of "the difference between motionless and moving depiction" is in a Chris Marker science-fiction film [*La Jetée*] "told in stills except for one late shot of eyes opening" [p.143].

*Camera placement, reverse shots, split screen, focus.* "The camera has to be somewhere; it can be anywhere" [p.143] — are placement and angle aesthetic possibilities even when no significance is made of them? In repeated reverse shots following dialogue, the listener's demeanor at the cut is often "not continuous… like the necktie in old continuity" [p.143]; technically this is a mismatch, but "the discontinuous, unnaturally altered expression of the listener could be intended; e.g., to convey lack of communication" — "some films we care about might undertake to mean something by it. But if no film does… is it so much as a possibility of the medium of film?" [p.143]. *Split screen* "does not overcome, but multiplies, or fractures, the somewhere of the camera; it might thereby declare it" [pp.143–144]. *Selective focus* (blurring foreground or background, "as in an intention of the eye") shows that "after the choice of the somewhere, and of horizon, and of distance and tilt, there is still a choice in seeing; resolution is still to be made, which steadies and excludes." (Masking is "a different mode of focusing, as in an intention of the ear.") [p.144].

He closes the survey with the contrast of two endings in snow that turns on the camera's *refusal to assert its placement*. *Grand Illusion* closes on "the two figures bobbing through a field of snow, away from us. Somewhere under that one white is a mathematical line, a fiction that men call a border" [p.144] — the film "is about borders… and the grand illusion that they are not"; that final view "carries this weight exactly because Renoir's camera does not take advantage of its possibility to move all the way up to the figures, but asserts its power to remain, accepting some position as its last, its own" [p.144]. Compare Borzage's *The Mortal Storm* (the German-Swiss border): "the camera cannot resist a last close look at James Stewart's and Margaret Sullavan's faces," turning a history of broken loves "into a private tragedy of a failed escape attempt," its privacy "heightened by the melodramatic irony of the last words" given to the ski patrol [pp.144–145]. In *Grand Illusion* "the very success of the escape is a metaphysical irony… That such an action should constitute an escape and that there should have to be such things in our world are facts from which there is nothing like an escape" [p.145]. "The reticence of Renoir's camera is more than beautiful tact. It is the refusal to assert what no one is in a position to assert for us: where it is that one man's life ends and another's begins" — the refusal to "manufacture a response… which covers our complicity in the metaphysics of exclusions we have willed for our world, which in turn covers the ontological facts of our separateness and commonality, which we will not will" [p.145].

Cavell ends the chapter on the *technique vs. achievement* distinction that motivated the whole survey: "I wanted to specify the difference between a condition of the physical bases of a medium and an achievement of art by means of them" [p.145]. This is familiar in traditional art (he credits "Dewey's treatment of it in *Art as Experience*"), "but in modernism, when the physical basis is itself the subject of acknowledgment, the distinction between technique and victory is not familiar and is likely to go unnoticed" [p.145]. The reason not to keep listing significances is that it "suggests a foolish idea of 'technique': that its mastery is something other than the endless acquisition of an art" [p.145]. He reworks the "great technician, but no soul" formula: the great technician has "not too much technique, but too little; he can do only what his technique dictates, not what his art demands" [p.145]. Wagner's Beckmesser exemplifies "the absurdity of the critic putting on the laurels of the artist" [p.145], not slavery to technique (which "is not absurd, but the condition any serious artist… pits his character against") [p.145]. Nor is technique "attending to details," "because the moral of art, as of a life, is that you do not know in advance what may arise as a significant detail" [p.145] — and movies "all but deliberately mislead about this," seeming "to promise that the magic of its results can be had by anyone who rubs its lamp" [p.145]. The chapter's last word: the purest statement of the moral (besides Bach's *Art of the Fugue*) is the Chopin Études, "in which atoms of technique are shown to contain their own skies, and in which it is declared that there is no preparation for art which is not already art" [pp.145–146].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "As always, nothing is a 'possibility of a medium' unless its use gives it significance. And 'the' significance of a possibility is as worth looking for as 'the' meaning of a word." [p.133]
- "At the finish of *Bonnie and Clyde* the film persists in an elegy of bullets long after the pair are dead. Society is making sure of itself. But art is not so satisfied." [p.134]
- "It is only another instance of the appeal to a favorite speechless horror as a cover for the inability to respond." [p.136]
- "an idea of the simultaneous gravity and weightlessness of the past, as though our present is merely a shifting orbiting of our memories" [pp.136–137]
- "integrating a device is not the artistic issue, because integration can itself be a device… There is no substitute for integrity. And… only the integrity of a given work can make out the significance of a given possibility." [pp.141–142]
- "depicted motionlessness feels and looks different from motionless depiction." [p.142]
- "The camera has to be somewhere; it can be anywhere." [p.143]
- "Renoir's camera does not take advantage of its possibility to move all the way up to the figures, but asserts its power to remain, accepting some position as its last, its own." [p.144]
- "It is the refusal to assert what no one is in a position to assert for us: where it is that one man's life ends and another's begins." [p.145]
- "the distinction between technique and victory is not familiar and is likely to go unnoticed" [p.145]
- "a great technician… can do only what his technique dictates, not what his art demands." [p.145]
- "you do not know in advance what may arise as a significant detail." [p.145]
- "atoms of technique are shown to contain their own skies… there is no preparation for art which is not already art." [pp.145–146]

**Examples / films / directors / works invoked:**
- Leni Riefenstahl — slow-motion diving (*Olympia*); "the sheer objective beauty in drifting a diver through thin air."
- *The World of Henry Orient*, *Popi* — clichéd lyrical slow motion (high-jumping/floating children).
- *Goodbye, Columbus* — Ali MacGraw's slow-motion swimming; the trick "blown."
- Rouben Mamoulian, *Love Me Tonight* — slow-motion horses "read as silent."
- Kurosawa, *Seven Samurai* — slow-motion duel-death; the body landing, "illuminated by the shifting flow of the hair."
- *Point Blank* — fails the slow-motion death, but earns the lyrical close-up of falling bullet shells.
- *Bonnie and Clyde* — freeze/elegy of bullets after death; "art is not so satisfied."
- *Butch Cassidy and the Sundance Kid* — ending freeze translating the pair "to immortality."
- Resnais, *Hiroshima, Mon Amour* — flash insets as "the pastness of the past," the woman's consciousness breaking in on itself.
- *The Pawnbroker* — failed imitative flash insets; "the appeal to a favorite speechless horror."
- Resnais, *La Guerre est Finie* (with Montand) — flash insets for outsideness/insideness of *place*; exploiting cross-cutting suspense.
- John Schlesinger, *Darling* — freeze frames as "what the world sees of a darling"; a "clever film."
- Truffaut, *Jules and Jim* — freeze frame of Jeanne Moreau; the central reading; "a masterpiece."
- Fred Astaire — dance that transforms ordinary settings; "making the world dance to his music."
- Truffaut, *Jules and Jim* (newsreel footage) — Paris/WWI clips vs. book-burning clip; "the world of movies is an extension of the world of news."
- Antonioni, *Eclipse* (*L'Eclisse*) — epilogue's "depicted motionlessness" acknowledging the film-thread; not nihilism. Monica Vitti's "capacity for fun."
- Chris Marker, *La Jetée* — sci-fi film told in stills "except for one late shot of eyes opening."
- Renoir, *Grand Illusion* — closing shot of figures crossing the snow-buried border; camera's refusal to advance.
- Frank Borzage, *The Mortal Storm* (James Stewart, Margaret Sullavan) — contrast case; the camera's "last close look" privatizes the tragedy.
- Dewey, *Art as Experience* — locus classicus of the technique/achievement distinction.
- Wagner, *Die Meistersinger* (Beckmesser) — "the critic putting on the laurels of the artist."
- Bach, *The Art of the Fugue*; Chopin, *Études* — purest statements that "there is no preparation for art which is not already art."

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *Assertion (in/of a technique)* — a device's becoming a meaningful claim/acknowledgment within a work; the chapter's titular concept.
- *Possibility of a medium* — only constituted when a use *gives it significance*; not a property the device has in itself.
- *Integrity vs. integration* — significance is fixed by the *integrity* (conviction) of the whole work, not by whether a device is technically *integrated*/motivated; "integration can itself be a device."
- *Automatism* — Cavell's term (here, the freeze frame and like devices) for a self-sustaining procedure of the medium whose justification is at issue.
- *Depicted motionlessness vs. motionless depiction* — a still rendered *within* the moving film (acknowledging the running thread) vs. a static image; phenomenologically distinct.
- *Flash inset* — a brief intercut image (memory/elsewhere); signifies "the pastness of the past" or the outsideness/insideness of place.
- *Freeze finish / freeze frame* — the stilled ending image; mimics the comic epilogue and the outside pull-away; can confer "immortality" or merely fail to let figures go.
- *Newsreel clip* — documentary footage embedded in fiction; declares "the world of movies is an extension of the world of news."
- *Technique vs. victory (achievement)* — the condition of the physical base vs. the artistic accomplishment by means of it; in modernism, easily conflated because the base *is* the subject of acknowledgment.
- *Reticence / refusal to assert (of the camera)* — the camera's declining to move closer or manufacture a response; an ethical-ontological act (e.g., Renoir refusing to say "where one man's life ends and another's begins").

---

### Ch 19 — The Acknowledgment of Silence [book pp.146–161 (text ends p.160)]

**Project:** The culminating chapter argues that what film's modernist condition must acknowledge is *silence* — the silence of the world to us, and the silence native to film that was never truly lost when film acquired speech. Cavell reconstructs why the coming of sound was traumatic, what synchronization of speech is, what "the poetry of speech" can be, and how film's relation to the human voice, to the body's motion, to theater, and to our own contingency and immortality together close the book's ontology.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):**

Cavell restates his modernist method and recapitulates the book's argument. A possibility of a medium "can be made known only by successful works that define its media; in modernism, a medium is explored by discovering possibilities that declare its necessary conditions, its limits" [p.146]. He recalls the path: from "an initial characterization of the medium of the moving picture as a succession of automatic world projections" [p.146], through the detour into modernist painting "as a means for developing the idea of acknowledgment as modernism's faithfulness to its art and the history of its art," to the question of what movies specifically must acknowledge — "what it is that would not exist (for it) unless admitted by it" [p.146]. His answer: film "must acknowledge… its own limits: in this case, its outsideness to its world, and my absence from it," because "these limits were always the conditions of its candor, of its fate to reveal all and only what is revealed to it, and of its fortune in letting the world exhibit itself" [p.146]. The recent rush of technical assertions, "insofar as they are serious, [are] responses to a sense of withdrawing candor" [pp.146–147], and listing them, he finds, has made him emphasize "silence, isolation in fantasy, the mysteries of human motion and separateness: such are the conditions of existence that film, in its magical reproduction of the world, tries and tries not to transgress" [p.147]. His montage/continuity hypothesis comes "just to this: that any such procedures will find their significance within these tasks of acknowledgment" [p.147].

He pivots to the *sound* question by returning "to beginnings" [p.147]. We still do not know why "the loss of silence was traumatic for so many who cared about film"; they may have mis-explained the loss, "but that would not mean that their trauma was not an accurate measure of it" [p.147]. He sharpens the question: "What was given up in giving up the silence of film, in particular the silence of the voice?… For the voice has spells of its own" [p.147]. The deep issue "now underlies all the explorations in film" he has discussed: sound-recording technology soon freed the camera from "the actor's stiff bondage to the microphone," but not from "a deeper source of bondage, in the idea of synchronization itself" [p.147]. The very power "of following an actor anywhere with both eye and ear seemed to make their binding necessary," rooted in "the absolute satisfaction of a craving for realism, for the absolute reproduction of the world—as though we might yet be present at its beginning" [p.147]. **[재구성: 동기화(synchronization)는 단순한 기술적 사실이 아니라 형이상학적 결박이다 — 눈과 귀로 인물을 어디든 따라갈 수 있다는 가능성 자체가 소리와 영상의 결박을 필연으로 만들었고, 이는 세계를 그 시작에서부터 절대적으로 재생하려는 욕망과 맞물려 있다.]**

But film pursues "a further reality… in which the words we need are not synchronized with the occasions of their need or in which their occasions flee them" [pp.147–148]. Cavell carefully delimits what he means: *not* the various ways "dialogue can stand at an angle to the life that produces it," nor the missed occasion, nor speech blocked by fear or "pitched by grief or joy" [p.148] — but "the pulsing air of incommunicability which may nudge the edge of any experience and placement: the curve of fingers that day, a mouth… laughing all afternoon mostly about nothing… spools of history that have unwound only to me now, occasions which will not reach words for me now, and if not now, never" [p.148]. This is not a call for "more stream-of-consciousness" (which "floats the time of action in order to give space for the words"); he is "asking for the ground of consciousness, upon which I cannot but move" [p.148].

He locates the *sense of the unsayable*: "From where the sense of the unsayable?—time's answer to the ineffable" [p.148]. "Out of my obscurity I reach the wish for total intelligibility, as though my words should form for you but remain part of our breaths" [p.148]. "Poetry is so out of the ordinary that it could not appear unless the world itself wished for it" — "Not alone the poetry of poetry, but the poetry of prose—wherever the time of saying and the time of meaning are synchronized" [p.148]. **[재구성: 카벨은 '동기화'를 시(詩)의 기준으로 재정의한다. 시란 발화의 시간과 의미의 시간이 일치하는 곳에서 일어난다. 영화가 음성-영상 동기화에서 풀려나는 것은, 역설적으로 이 더 깊은 (말할 수 없는 것의) 동기화를 드러낼 기회다.]**

He connects this to film technique: "This reality of the unsayable is what I see in film's new release from the synchronization of speech with the speaker, or rather in its presenting of the speaker in forms in which there can be no speech" [p.148]. Speech slowed to slow motion "sinks into moan and grunt"; speeded actions become machine-like, "speech matched to them rises to blurts of twittering"; "you cannot flash a word into a phrase without altering the phrase; you cannot freeze a word without losing it. The tempo and progression of spoken intelligibility are inexorable" [pp.148–149]. The poetry of poetry "finds new breath for the world within that inexorability; it does not escape and does not want to escape it" [p.149]. Music's paces have their own inexorability: "melisma puts the word in slow motion but continues the surrounding world as its rod and staff; the soprano's high dominant freezes her word and her world for their descent into one another" [p.149]. The summary claim: "The possibilities of moving pictures speak of a comprehensibility of the body under conditions which destroy the comprehensibility of speech. It is the talkie itself that is now exploring the silence of movies" [p.149].

The chapter's most striking ontological thesis follows: "A silent movie has never been made" [p.149]. We called films silent retroactively, "to register the satisfaction of the world's reproduction," as if the actors "had been inaudible. But they were no more inaudible than the characters in radio were invisible" [p.149]. The Lone Ranger "was no more invisible than his horse or gun." Crucially: "No word we could hear could be the word spoken by that figure of silence" [pp.149–150]. He demolishes the legend that a few silent stars "crossed the boundary intact" when sound came: "No; no one did; all were replaced, some by themselves. We were universally disappointed. The new creations of synchronized sight and sound were merely powerful enough to distract us from the disappointment… Now the disappointment is waking again" [p.150]. **[재구성: '무성영화'라는 개념 자체가 사후적 착시다. 무성영화는 소리가 결여된 것이 아니라 *침묵의 세계*를 투사했다 — 마치 라디오가 *소리의 세계*를 투사했듯. 유성 전환은 결핍을 채운 것이 아니라, 그 침묵의 인물을 다른 (목소리 가진) 창조물로 대체한 것이며, 우리는 보편적으로 실망했다.]**

He contrasts the two worlds: "Movies, before they spoke, projected a world of silence, as the radio beamed a world of sound" [p.150]. Against the praise of radio for "leaving room for the imagination," he calls this "a wrong praise of imagination, which is ordinarily the laziest, if potentially the most precious, of human faculties": "A world of sound is a world of immediate conviction; a world of sight is a world of immediate intelligibility. In neither is imagination called upon" [p.150]. With talkies "we got back the clumsiness of speech, the dumbness and duplicities and concealments of assertion, the bafflement of soul and body by their inarticulateness and by their terror of articulateness" — "ontological facts" that "technical improvements will not overcome… they only magnify them" [p.150]. "The advent of sound broke the spell of immediate intelligibility—a realistic renunciation, given the growing obscurity of the world. Then the task is to discover the poetry in speech" [p.150].

This poetry "will not be the poetry of poetry" [p.150]; one expected that filmed speech "would have the same absolute intelligibility as its exhibition to sight," but "every art wants the expression of the world… and none can simply hand its own powers to the others" [pp.150–151]. "The poetry of synchronized speech arises from the fact that just that creature, in just those surroundings, is saying just that, just now" [p.151]. Best film dialogue "has so far been the witty and the hard-nosed… because the lines are fast, or laconic" — but wit and clip work "because they provide natural occasions on which silence is broken, and in which words do not go beyond their moment of saying; hence occasions on which silence naturally reasserts itself" [p.151]. The governing ontological sentence: "For the world is silent to us; the silence is merely forever broken. Poems and music incorporate that silence when they speak the world. Film speech can merely imitate it, backed by the world" [pp.151]. Film's imitation relies on its long dialogue-free stretches "in which the characters can be present without having to say anything (not: without having anything to say)" [p.151], and on "some conventionalized sense of the best distance at which dialogue can be overheard" [p.151].

He praises Orson Welles's speech tracks as "a class almost by themselves" [p.151] — not merely the overlapping dialogue (which on second thought seemed "a simple trick," yet "I have never seen the trick successfully duplicated"): for Welles "each of the different origins of speech attempts to maintain its individual meaning and coherence, so that they vie for significance" [p.151], and beyond that "Welles explores and declares the fact and variation of *distance* between the speaker and the hearer of a line. Here he justifies the sound of high theater for film" [pp.151–152]. Examples: the motorboat-circling razzes in *The Lady from Shanghai*; in *Citizen Kane*, "Kane's repetition of the name 'Geddes' down a lengthening stairwell," and the transition from the campaign speech read to a small gathering to its delivery at a mass meeting, "so shocking and yet smooth that one recognizes the platform as an inner distance from which Kane always speaks" [p.152] — his money, "fallen upon him from an incomprehensible distance," causing "his distance from individual others, and equally from the reality of his own inner life… on some isolated platform he cannot mount" [p.152].

"How hard it is to let silence resume, how frightening it is" [p.152] is registered early in *King Kong*: arty words and "continuous Wagnerama and almost equally continuous screams; you finally feel the film is more afraid of silence than Fay Wray is of the beast" [p.152]. The film is "an artless confession of film: film-makers on location discover that a thing of nature is more wondrous than any film; and when they trap this nature and bring it back, it is displayed crucified" [p.152]. Movie music "remained in genuine continuity with its soundless era… continued to cartoon the emotions it could accompany, and continued to use that as an excuse for the general deadening of the pain of silence" [p.152]. The "most perfect provisions for the breaking of silence by speech" occur in two of the greatest films: *Children of Paradise*, where Arletty "breaks the silent conventions of mime with Baptiste's name," and again speaks his name as a window curtain is ripped aside — "The speaking of the word in those times and at those places collects to itself the fantasies it expresses and shatters them against the reality it shatters" [p.152]; and *Rules of the Game*, where the aviator's first words "over the public radio, to a watching world, but addressed to the absent woman" give his words "the power of Shakespearean soliloquy" [p.152].

Cavell turns to "another half to the idea of conveying the unsayable": "freeing the motion of the body for its own lucidity" [pp.152–153]. The body's lucidity does not depend on slowing/flashing/freezing it; "it was always part of the grain of film that, however studied the lines and set the business, the movement of the actors was essentially improvised" — like everyday actions (walking through a new room, lifting a cup, greeting a friend): "They could all go one way or another… an improvisation of meaning, out of the present" [p.153]. These facts return him to *acting* on film: earlier he objected to calling film's subjects "actors," "But obviously they are actors the way any human being is." The ontological fact "that actions move within a dark and shifting circle of intention and consequence, that their limits are our own, that the individual significance of an act (like that of a word) arises in its being this one rather than every other that might have been said or done here and now, that their fate (like the fate of words) is to be taken out of our control—this is the natural vision of film" [p.153].

He links this to the modern wish "To act without performing, to allow action all and only the significance of its specific traces, the wound embracing the arrow and no self-consciousness to blunt or disperse that knowledge" — "the explicit wish of human action since Kierkegaard and Nietzsche summed up Protestantism and Stanislavsky brought theater into line" [p.153]. Brecht "automatically gets an unanticipated version of his wish": not the dissociation of actor and character "but their total coalescence, allowing a dissociation or freeing of action from speech" [p.153]; he does not get "detached lucidity directly issuing in effective action for change," but "the turning of the spectator into somebody who just looks on" without "forcing him to make decisions" [pp.153–154]. Cavell objects: "art alone is not going to achieve the changes of consciousness which its own reception also requires" [p.154]; epic theater ought to contain "the confrontation of its own continuous failing—every night our knowing the truth of our condition and every day dawning just the same" so as to make this failing "something we can live with faithfully, in consciousness, and with readiness for the significant detail when it really is ours to act upon" [p.154]. And Brecht "gets the vision that everything in the world other than nature is a human construction, humanly open to change" [p.154].

Cavell now turns to the *power* of movies over the self. "The impact of movies is too massive… to speak politely of involvement. We involve the movies in us. They become further fragments of what happens to me, further cards in the shuffle of my memory" [p.154] — like childhood memories "whose content is nothing compared with their unspeakable importance for me" (St. Augustine's stolen pear, Rousseau's spanking) [p.154]. Parents "do not know what is important to the child," and their amusement at the child's odd memories means "they are not going to try to learn" [p.154]. The movie reaches this level partly through "the gigantism of its figures, making me small again" — illustrated (the one acknowledged instance of the watching-a-movie motif) by *Saboteur*, where a man chased across the stage in front of the screen at Radio City Music Hall appears, beside the screen character, "about the size of Fay Wray wriggling in the sky-blown hand" [pp.154–155] — and partly because "the world it screens [is] literally of my world" [p.155].

This launches the chapter's theater/film comparison. Earlier he said "in a theater we do not occupy the same space as the actors… but… we do occupy the same time" — "That is rather superficial" [p.155]; the objection that we obviously *are* in the same room at a theater "comes from wrong pictures of how the spaces can be entered" [p.155]. Crossing the footlights would only "stop the performance"; breaking the screen "is of no use… you are headed in the wrong direction. (As in Plato's Cave, reality is behind you. It will become visible when you have made yourself visible to it, presented yourself.)" [p.155]. The decisive claim: "The actors are there, all right, in your world, but to get to them you have to go where they are, and in fact, as things stand, you cannot go there now. Their space is not metaphysically different; it is the same human space mine is… In a movie house, the barrier to the stars is time" [p.155]. **[재구성: 영화와 연극의 차이는 공간의 형이상학적 차이가 아니라 *시간*의 차이다. 스크린 속 배우들은 나와 같은 인간적 공간에 있으나, 나는 지금 그곳에 갈 수 없다 — 가로막는 것은 시간이다. 이것이 책 전체의 '세계는 내게 현존하나 나는 세계에 부재한다'는 회의주의 테제의 최종 정식화다.]**

By contrast "the sacredness of the stage still holds," now "not because certain figures are authorized to be there, but because the characters in a play have a past and future that you could never have been part of" [p.155]. The "world filmed is all profane, all outside. They and we could have been anywhere, may be anywhere next" [pp.155–156]. He refines the ontology of cinematic discontinuity: "The discontinuities in the environment of a film are discontinuities not of space but of places. You do not discontinuously go from one place to another… The discontinuities are those of attention. You are given bits of the world, and you must put them together into those lives, one way or another, as you have yours" [p.156].

He develops *contingency and placement* as the underpinning of all film genres. "The fact that we are in a given place on earth is as utterly contingent as the fact that we are on earth. The fact that we are in one place at any given time is as necessary as the fact that, once on earth, we are until the end earthling" [p.156]. Everyone knows "odd moments in which it seems uncanny that one should find oneself just here now… The uncanny is normal experience of film" [p.156]. "Escape, rescue, the metamorphosis of a life by a chance encounter… underpin all the genres of film, from the Keaton and Chaplin figures who know nothing of the abyss they skirt, to the men who know too much" [p.156]. Because "the absolute reality and placement of these people are conditions of their appearance to us, film has an absolute freedom of narrative. It escapes Aristotelian limits according to which the possible has to be made probable" [p.156]. He contrasts realistic theater: Ibsen's achievement of a suicide "so improbable and in the character so right" that he could rebuke the curtain line "But people just don't do things like that" "with the force of fact" [pp.156–157] — "That is the ordinary condition of the audience of film": things that don't happen in our world ("your father does not turn out to be a foreign spy," the Statue of Liberty ledge, werewolves) — "But there they are" [pp.156–157]. This is "the knowledge which makes acceptable film's absolute control of our attention" [p.157], and, with "the visible incarnation of character into star and of star into successive characters," the knowledge "that we exist in the condition of myth: we do not require the gods to show that our lives illustrate a story which escapes us… Any life may illustrate any; any change may bring it home" [p.157].

Cavell then unfolds *physiognomy* and *seeing-as* via Wittgenstein. "The knowledge of the unsayable is the study of what Wittgenstein means by physiognomy" [p.157], his sketches occurring "in Part II of the *Philosophical Investigations*… beginning with an investigation of the concept of 'seeing as'" [p.157]. He warns against "a false idea of its importance—namely the idea that 'seeing as' is some fancy species of seeing" [p.157]; rather, Wittgenstein's ideas "have to do with my relation to my own words and with the point at which my knowledge of others depends upon the concepts of truthfulness and interpretation" [p.157]. He draws his signature distinction: "Empirical statements that claim truth depend upon evidence; statements that claim truthfulness depend upon our acceptance of them"; "a true statement is something we know or do not know; a truthful statement is one we must acknowledge or fail or refuse to acknowledge" [p.157].

He applies this to the *Kuleshov experiment* (the same face altered by the preceding image), via Wittgenstein's examples (duck-rabbit; the acute triangle read as "fallen over"; line-drawings of faces in which "different aspects… can dawn, and in dawning strike us") [p.158]. He reports Bazin's reading — that the experiment's "demonstrated power of montage [shows] its weakness, because the inherent ambiguity or mystery of the human face is denied in presenting a context which forces one definite interpretation upon us" [p.158] — and then offers his own finer analysis: shown a human face, I might be struck "right off" with one aspect (like the duck-rabbit); to read the triangle as "fallen over" I must "surround it with a fiction"; but the face is unique — "unlike the triangle and the duck-rabbit and all other optical illusions, I must surround the face with a *reality*—as though the seeing of a reality is the imagining of it; and it may itself either dictate or absorb the reality with which I must surround it, or fascinate me exactly because it calls incompatible realities to itself which vie for my imagination" [p.158]. **[재구성: 카벨은 'seeing-as'를 인식론적 트릭이 아니라 *진실성(truthfulness)*의 윤리로 재배치한다. 얼굴을 본다는 것은 (착시와 달리) 그것을 *현실*로 둘러싸는 일이며, 이 둘러쌈은 임의적 해석이 아니라 인정(acknowledgment)의 문제다.]**

The recurrence of an actor "in his type both limits his range of expression, the physiognomical aspects which may dawn… and also threatens the limit. It can be internal to a character that he threaten his own limit" — without the explicitness of *Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde*: "The threat is present when Harpo inevitably finds his harp," the usual "frenzy of his information and gluttony and satyromania… becalmed in the angelic sounds… the camera doting on the innerness of that face" [pp.158–159]. The "special use of unknown faces for their sheer impression upon us (as… in Dreyer and Fellini) serves both to invite and to refuse the imposition of imagination" [p.159]: "The inflection of meaning available to a type is the background against which the inflexibility of a face commands its power of mystery. Film's promise of the world's exhibition is the background against which it registers absolute isolation: its rooms and cells and pinions hold out the world itself" [p.159]. The fullest image of absolute isolation is in Dreyer's *Joan of Arc*: "Falconetti at the stake looks up to see a flight of birds wheel over her with the sun in their wings. They, there, are free… But first there is this body to be gone through utterly" [p.159].

The chapter rises to its existential-ontological close through three "willingnesses" keyed to Heidegger. "To satisfy the wish for the world's exhibition we must be willing to let the world as such appear" — which "according to Heidegger" means "we must be willing for anxiety, to which alone the world as world, into which we are thrown, can manifest itself; and it is through that willingness that the possibility of one's own existence begins or ends" [p.159]. "To satisfy the wish to act without performing… we must be willing to allow the self to exhibit itself without the self's intervention" [pp.159]. And "The wish for total intelligibility is a terrible one. It means that we are willing to reveal ourselves through the self's betrayal of itself" [p.159]. The woman in *Hiroshima* "is almost there": "'I betrayed you tonight,' she says in a monologue to her dead lover, looking at herself in the mirror, confessing her new lover" [pp.159–160]; her old lover's death does not mitigate "the need for acknowledgment, because what she has betrayed is her love for him, which is not dead" [p.160]. The generalized law of self-knowledge: "love is always the betrayal of love, if it is honest… The knowledge of the self as it is always takes place in the betrayal of the self as it was. That is the form of self-revelation, until the self is wholly won" [p.160] — "Until then… our loyalty to ourselves is in doubt, and our loyalty to others is in partialness" [p.160].

The book's closing paragraph fuses film, skepticism, and immortality: "A world complete without me which is present to me is the world of my immortality. This is an importance of film—and a danger" [p.160]. It takes my life "as my haunting of the world, either because I left it unloved (the Flying Dutchman) or because I left unfinished business (Hamlet)" [p.160]. Hence the double wish: "there is reason for me to want the camera to deny the coherence of the world… to deny that the world is complete without me. But there is equal reason to want it affirmed that the world is coherent without me. That is essential to what I want of immortality: nature's survival of me. It will mean that the present judgment upon me is not yet the last" [p.160]. **[재구성: 책은 '나 없이도 완결되어 있으면서 내게 현존하는 세계'라는 회의주의의 정식을 *불멸성*으로 전환하며 닫힌다. 카메라가 세계의 완결성을 부정하길 바라는 동시에 긍정하길 바라는 이 이중 욕망이, '세계는 내게 현존하나 나는 부재한다'는 테제의 마지막 형상이다 — 자연이 나를 넘어 존속한다는 것, 따라서 현재의 심판이 최후의 심판은 아니라는 것.]**

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "in modernism, a medium is explored by discovering possibilities that declare its necessary conditions, its limits." [p.146]
- "What was given up in giving up the silence of film, in particular the silence of the voice?… For the voice has spells of its own." [p.147]
- "the possibility of following an actor anywhere with both eye and ear seemed to make their binding necessary." [p.147]
- "Poetry is so out of the ordinary that it could not appear unless the world itself wished for it. Not alone the poetry of poetry, but the poetry of prose—wherever the time of saying and the time of meaning are synchronized." [p.148]
- "you cannot flash a word into a phrase without altering the phrase; you cannot freeze a word without losing it. The tempo and progression of spoken intelligibility are inexorable." [pp.148–149]
- "It is the talkie itself that is now exploring the silence of movies." [p.149]
- "A silent movie has never been made." [p.149]
- "No; no one did; all were replaced, some by themselves. We were universally disappointed." [p.150]
- "A world of sound is a world of immediate conviction; a world of sight is a world of immediate intelligibility. In neither is imagination called upon." [p.150]
- "The poetry of synchronized speech arises from the fact that just that creature, in just those surroundings, is saying just that, just now." [p.151]
- "For the world is silent to us; the silence is merely forever broken. Poems and music incorporate that silence when they speak the world. Film speech can merely imitate it, backed by the world." [p.151]
- "you finally feel the film is more afraid of silence than Fay Wray is of the beast." [p.152]
- "the wound embracing the arrow and no self-consciousness to blunt or disperse that knowledge" [p.153]
- "The actors are there, all right, in your world, but to get to them you have to go where they are, and in fact, as things stand, you cannot go there now… In a movie house, the barrier to the stars is time." [p.155]
- "The discontinuities in the environment of a film are discontinuities not of space but of places… The discontinuities are those of attention." [p.156]
- "The uncanny is normal experience of film." [p.156]
- "film has an absolute freedom of narrative. It escapes Aristotelian limits according to which the possible has to be made probable." [p.156]
- "we exist in the condition of myth… Any life may illustrate any; any change may bring it home." [p.157]
- "a true statement is something we know or do not know; a truthful statement is one we must acknowledge or fail or refuse to acknowledge." [p.157]
- "unlike the triangle and the duck-rabbit and all other optical illusions, I must surround the face with a reality—as though the seeing of a reality is the imagining of it." [p.158]
- "Film's promise of the world's exhibition is the background against which it registers absolute isolation: its rooms and cells and pinions hold out the world itself." [p.159]
- "we must be willing for anxiety, to which alone the world as world, into which we are thrown, can manifest itself." [p.159]
- "love is always the betrayal of love, if it is honest… The knowledge of the self as it is always takes place in the betrayal of the self as it was." [p.160]
- "A world complete without me which is present to me is the world of my immortality. This is an importance of film—and a danger." [p.160]
- "there is equal reason to want it affirmed that the world is coherent without me… nature's survival of me. It will mean that the present judgment upon me is not yet the last." [p.160]

**Examples / films / directors / works invoked:**
- Radio / The Lone Ranger — a "world of sound"; foil to film's "world of silence"; the sound-figure no more invisible than film's silent figure is inaudible.
- Orson Welles, *The Lady from Shanghai* — the motorboat-circling "loony singsong razzes"; declaring distance between speaker and hearer.
- Orson Welles, *Citizen Kane* — "Geddes" down the lengthening stairwell; the campaign-speech transition revealing the "inner distance" platform Kane speaks from.
- *King Kong* (with Fay Wray) — "Wagnerama" and screams; "more afraid of silence than Fay Wray is of the beast"; nature "displayed crucified."
- Marcel Carné, *Children of Paradise* (Arletty / Baptiste) — speaking the name that breaks mime's silence; word shattering fantasy against reality.
- Renoir, *Rules of the Game* — the aviator's radioed first words addressed to the absent woman; "the power of Shakespearean soliloquy."
- Kierkegaard, Nietzsche, Stanislavsky — the genealogy of the wish "to act without performing."
- Brecht (epic theater / alienation) — gets actor-character *coalescence* and "the spectator who just looks on," but not action issuing in change; critiqued.
- St. Augustine (stolen pear), Rousseau (the spanking) — childhood memories whose importance exceeds their content; analogue to movies "involved in us."
- Hitchcock, *Saboteur* — the chase across the Radio City stage; the only acknowledged use of the watching-a-movie motif; gigantism (cf. Fay Wray "in the sky-blown hand").
- Plato's Cave — "reality is behind you… when you have made yourself visible to it."
- Ibsen — realistic theater's improbable-but-right suicide, rebuking "people just don't do things like that."
- Keaton, Chaplin — figures "who know nothing of the abyss they skirt"; contingency/placement underpinning genre.
- Wittgenstein, *Philosophical Investigations* Part II — physiognomy, "seeing as," the duck-rabbit, the "fallen over" triangle; truthfulness vs. truth.
- Kuleshov experiment — the same face altered by the preceding image; read (after Bazin) as montage's *weakness*, denying the face's mystery.
- Bazin — cited as turning the Kuleshov demonstration against montage's claimed omnipotence.
- *Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde* — the (over-explicit) figure of a character threatening his own limit.
- Harpo Marx — the type "threatening its own limit"; frenzy "becalmed" when he finds his harp; camera "doting on the innerness of that face."
- Dreyer, Fellini — special use of "unknown faces" to invite and refuse imagination.
- Dreyer, *The Passion of Joan of Arc* (Falconetti) — the birds wheeling with the sun; "the fullest image of absolute isolation."
- Heidegger — willingness *for anxiety* as the condition for the world as world (thrownness) to manifest.
- Resnais, *Hiroshima, Mon Amour* — the mirror monologue "I betrayed you tonight"; the woman "almost there" at total intelligibility.
- The Flying Dutchman; Hamlet — figures of haunting (life left "unloved" / "unfinished business") in the closing meditation on immortality.

**Technical vocabulary introduced/used:**
- *The acknowledgment of silence* — film's modernist task of admitting silence (of the world, of the voice, native to film) as a condition of its medium; the chapter title.
- *Synchronization* — the binding of speech to speaker; not merely technical but a metaphysical "bondage" tied to the craving for absolute reproduction; redefined as the criterion of *poetry* (saying-time = meaning-time).
- *The unsayable / "pulsing air of incommunicability"* — experience at the edge of any placement that "will not reach words… and if not now, never"; what film conveys by presenting speakers "in forms in which there can be no speech."
- *Poetry of poetry vs. poetry of prose vs. poetry of (synchronized) speech* — three modes; film achieves the last, "just that creature… saying just that, just now," by breaking and letting silence reassert itself.
- *"A silent movie has never been made"* — the thesis that silent film projected *a world of silence* (not a deficient sound film), parallel to radio's *world of sound*.
- *World of sound / world of sight* — radio gives "immediate conviction," film "immediate intelligibility"; in neither is imagination "called upon."
- *Distance (of speaker from hearer)* — a declared dimension of film speech (Welles), "the sound of high theater for film."
- *Improvisation of meaning* — the everyday, essentially improvised quality of bodily action that is "the grain of film"; basis for film's *natural vision* of acting.
- *To act without performing* — the modern wish (Kierkegaard/Nietzsche/Stanislavsky) to let action carry "all and only the significance of its specific traces."
- *Discontinuities of places (not space) / of attention* — cinematic editing joins *places* and reapportions *attention*, not metaphysically distinct spaces; "you are given bits of the world."
- *Contingency and placement* — the utter contingency of *which* place I occupy vs. the necessity of occupying *some* place; ground of the "uncanny" and of film's genres.
- *Absolute freedom of narrative* — film's escape from "Aristotelian limits" (possible → probable), because "absolute reality and placement" are conditions of appearance; "There they are."
- *The condition of myth* — our existing such that "any life may illustrate any," with star/character incarnation, without needing gods.
- *Physiognomy / "seeing as"* — Wittgenstein's region (PI II); not "a fancy species of seeing" but the site where knowledge of others turns on *truthfulness* and *interpretation*.
- *Truth vs. truthfulness* — truth depends on evidence and is *known*; truthfulness depends on *acceptance* and must be *acknowledged* (or refused).
- *Surrounding the face with a reality* — unlike optical illusions (fiction-surrounded), reading a human face requires surrounding it with a *reality*; "the seeing of a reality is the imagining of it."
- *Threatening one's own limit* — a type/star's recurrence both limits and (internally) threatens the physiognomical aspects that can "dawn" (Harpo, Jekyll/Hyde).
- *Willingness for anxiety* (Heidegger) — the condition under which "the world as world, into which we are thrown," can appear; tied to the possibility of one's own existence.
- *Total intelligibility / the self's betrayal of itself* — the "terrible" wish to reveal oneself; self-knowledge takes place "in the betrayal of the self as it was."
- *World complete without me which is present to me* — the book's final formula identifying the skeptical condition (world present to me, I absent from it) with *immortality* ("nature's survival of me"); the closing ontological statement.

---

---

### "More of The World Viewed" — the 1979 essay [book pp. 162–230, with substantive Notes pp. 231–246]

> Continuous (un-chaptered) essay added to the Enlarged Edition; orig. *Georgia Review* 1974, centrally a reply to Alexander Sesonske. Digested below as thematic segments. The main prose ends p.230; the endnotes pp.231–246 contain several load-bearing philosophical excurses.

---

*Cavell's 1979 reply to critics (orig. Georgia Review 1974), centrally answering Alexander Sesonske's Sarasota symposium objections. Citations by book page.*

---

#### Segment 1: Apologia for the book's difficulty — method and its reversal of his philosophical prose [pp. 162–163]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell opens by conceding to sympathetic friends that he wrote "a difficult book, a sometimes incomprehensible book," and accounts for that difficulty as internal to his subject rather than a failure of craft.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** He reaffirms the Preface's claim that the difficulty lies "as much in the obscurity of its promptings as in its particular surfacings of expression" [p.162]. Crucially, the obscurity of prompting is "not external to what I wished most fervently to say about film" — it could not be cleared up before writing, nor even by the time the writing was over. He therefore set himself two commitments: first, to let obscurities "express themselves as clearly and as fervently" as he could; second, to organize and clarify "just these obscurities and just this fervor" across the book's progression [pp.162–163]. These procedures presuppose that his obscurities were "accurate responses to the nature of film" and that his expressions were accurate to those responses. He then notes these procedures "almost reverse" those of his previous philosophical writing. In philosophy he sought conviction by *motivating* assertions so the reader felt he "was himself thinking them, had been about to have said them" — not approximately, but finding himself thinking *those specific words* just as they appeared. (He parenthetically grants this "need not... occur on a first perusal," asking otherwise "what in a first should encourage going back?" [p.163]) In writing about film, by contrast, he felt called upon to voice responses "with their privacy, their argumentativeness, even their intellectual perverseness, on their face"; often "to refuse that thought, to break into the thought, as if our standing responses to film are themselves standing between us and the responses that film is made to elicit and to satisfy" [p.163].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "its difficulty lies as much in the obscurity of its promptings as in its particular surfacings of expression" [p.162]
- "to allow obscurities to express themselves as clearly and as fervently as I could say" [p.162]
- "as if our standing responses to film are themselves standing between us and the responses that film is made to elicit and to satisfy" [p.163]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** None (programmatic/methodological).

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *obscurity of promptings* — the obscurity belongs to the source/motive of the writing, not merely its surface; it cannot be dissolved in advance because it is faithful to film's own nature.
- *standing responses* — habitual, ready-made reactions to film that block the responses film is actually made to elicit; his prose deliberately interrupts these.

---

#### Segment 2: Why obvious points needed making — three issues known from the outset (worth, "formal devices," "essence") [pp. 163–165]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell restates the plainest intention of the book — that movies "may be written about... with the same seriousness that any work of art deserves" [p.163] — and enumerates three issues he knew awaited him, each of which forces him to belabor the apparently obvious.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** **(1)** He must say why "so grotesquely obvious a point as the worth of certain movies is worth the making" [p.163]. It is worth making because asserting worth commits one "to specify the grounds of worth in particular cases," because not all movies are in fact worth this attention, and because there is "next to no agreement" over a canon of movies against which standards of worth could be set, nor over "the cinematic grounds" for arguing worth or worthlessness [p.164]. These lacks of agreement he attributes "less [to] the comparative 'youth' of the art of film than [to] the sociology of film criticism and theory" [p.164] — comparable to literary criticism before "the professionalization of scholarship and the academicizing of criticism" [p.164]. **(2)** He must specify what counts as "formal devices" of film, and "Why a rehearsal of the technological properties of lenses, filters, shutters, film exposures, etc., does not in itself constitute specifications of aesthetic properties of film" [p.164]. He shares the sense that "the sheer power of film is unlike the powers of the other arts" and agrees this power "is essentially related to film's technology," but the aesthetic *role* of the technology is no more specified by studying it apart from its achievements in significant films than the role of electronic amplification in rock is specified apart from its results in actual rock sounds — "apart from its role in determining what counts as a 'sound'" [p.164]. **(3)** He must say why a general declaration of cinematic "essence" "was apt to be called for and likely to be fruitless"; whatever the "essence" of an artistic medium means, "its sense can only be specified by the achievements of that art itself" [p.164].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "movies may be written about, and that some are worth thinking and writing about, with the same seriousness that any work of art deserves" [p.163]
- "a rehearsal of the technological properties of lenses, filters, shutters, film exposures, etc., does not in itself constitute specifications of aesthetic properties of film" [p.164]
- "Whatever one may mean by the 'essence' of a medium of art, its sense can only be specified by the achievements of that art itself." [p.164]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Electronic amplification in rock music — analogy: technology is aesthetically specified only through its concrete results, not in isolation.

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *formal devices* — the aesthetically significant features of film; not identical to its technological properties, which only become aesthetic through specific achievements.
- *sociology of film criticism* — Cavell's diagnosis: the disagreement over film's canon stems from the institutional/critical situation, not from film's youth.

---

#### Segment 3: "Light and movement" rejected; reality as the entry-point; skepticism and the resistance to reality [pp. 165–166]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell explains why he rejected the standard answer to "the essence of film" ("light and movement") and instead began from the role of *reality*, situating that choice against the contemporary intellectual fashion of denying reality.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** The most frequent declaration of film's essence he had heard was that it consists of "light and movement" — "the natural, the only, answer to the isolated and persistent question" — which is exactly why he takes that question to be "more or less the wrong question to ask" [p.165]. He has seen no objects "consisting essentially of light and movement (and essentially of nothing else)" that struck him as having "the force of art"; and since the film objects that *do* strike him as art "all incontestably use moving pictures of live persons and real things in actual spaces," he began "by asking what role reality plays in this art" [p.165]. He identifies "a double cause" for the contemporary urge to "resist the pressure of reality upon art": (a) a "vague and pervasive intellectual fashion, apparently sanctioned by the history of epistemology and the rise of modern science, according to which we never really, and never really can, see reality as it is"; and (b) an interpretation of the history of the representative arts (painting and the novel, contemporaneous with photography and motion pictures) "according to which art had been withdrawing from the representation of reality as from a hopeless, but always unnecessary, task" [p.165]. Cavell counts on its being equally natural to *resist this resistance*: a general dismissal of reality "depends upon theories... whose power to convince is hardly greater than reality's own" [p.165]. He acknowledges his resistance is "colored by my readings of Wittgenstein and of Heidegger," each understood "to be writing within the threat of skepticism, out of a philosophical necessity not so much to deny its conclusion... but to determine the place of skepticism's inspiration, as though its threat is as revelatory of human thinking as science itself is" [pp.165–166]. He hopes his reading is "not overly idiosyncratic" and that he kept these philosophers "from overlying what I had it in mind to say about film" [p.166].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Since the answer seems to me more or less empty, I take the question to be more or less the wrong question to ask." [p.165]
- "the objects of film I have seen which do strike me as having the force of art all incontestably use moving pictures of live persons and real things in actual spaces" [p.165]
- "writing within the threat of skepticism, out of a philosophical necessity not so much to deny its conclusion... but to determine the place of skepticism's inspiration, as though its threat is as revelatory of human thinking as science itself is" [pp.165–166]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Wittgenstein, Heidegger — both read as writing "within the threat of skepticism," the model for Cavell's own stance toward reality.

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *the pressure of reality upon art* — reality's claim on representation, which modern fashion resists on two grounds (epistemological-scientific and art-historical).
- *the place of skepticism's inspiration* — not refuting skepticism's conclusion but locating *why* it arises, treating its threat as revelatory of human thought.

---

#### Segment 4: Correcting Panofsky and Bazin — from "reality as such" to *projections of reality* and our absence [pp. 166–167]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell stages his core ontological move as an emendation of Panofsky and Bazin, whose appeals to "nature" and "reality" he honors but finds overstated, and introduces the thesis of presentness/absence.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** The issue of reality "was, in any case, forced upon me by the experience of Panofsky and of Andre Bazin" [p.166]. Whatever his discomfort with "their unabashed appeals to nature and to reality," the accuracy of their remarks about particular films, genres, and figures "could not, or ought not, be dissociated from their conviction that film bears a relation to reality unprecedented in the other arts" [p.166]. Yet they "significantly misspoke themselves, or spoke unnecessarily," in calling film "a dramaturgy of Nature" and "the medium of movies [as] reality as such." They rightly wish to emphasize that on film reality "is not merely described or merely represented." But "obviously it is not actually present to us either (anyway, obviously not present *with* us) when it appears to us on the screen" [p.166]. This leads Cavell to his thesis: "what makes the physical medium of film unlike anything else on earth lies in the absence of what it causes to appear to us; that is to say, in the nature of our absence from it; in its fate to reveal reality and fantasy (not by reality as such, but) by projections of reality, projections in which... reality is freed to exhibit itself" [p.166]. He notes wryly that "my emphasis on reality has caused a certain amount of unhappiness among readers" [p.166], and that at the Sarasota symposium Alexander Sesonske "raised questions about this topic of reality" intended to specify causes of unhappiness "likely to be shared by others" [pp.166–167]. Cavell will organize his reply "along a series of such causes," accounting for "the way in which, at each point, the issue of reality poses itself at once as too obvious to mention and too obscure, or false, to argue" [p.167]. He flags two further suspicions about his canon: over-indulgence of Hollywood (thought to distort his account of "traditional" movies) and near-complete neglect of contemporary experimental film (thought to falsify his account of film's relation to modernism, since "the real modernism of the art of film is spoken for by its experimentalists") [p.167].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "obviously it is not actually present to us either (anyway, obviously not present *with* us) when it appears to us on the screen" [p.166]
- "in its fate to reveal reality and fantasy (not by reality as such, but) by projections of reality, projections in which... reality is freed to exhibit itself" [p.166]
- "the issue of reality poses itself at once as too obvious to mention and too obscure, or false, to argue" [p.167]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Erwin Panofsky ("a dramaturgy of Nature") and André Bazin ("the medium of movies [as] reality as such") — the two critics whose reality-thesis Cavell honors but corrects; Alexander Sesonske — the symposium critic whose objections structure the reply.

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *projections of reality* — film reveals reality not "as such" but as projected; the world appears without being present *with* us.
- *our absence from it* — the defining feature of film's medium: what it causes to appear is absent, and we are absent from it.
- *reality freed to exhibit itself* — in projection reality displays itself of its own accord, not under our presence or control.

---

#### Segment 5: Cartoons are not movies — Sesonske's objection and Cavell's "reflections on cartoons" [pp. 167–173]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Sesonske argues that animated cartoons (Disney) satisfy Cavell's claims about film yet have nothing to do with projections of the real world — so if cartoons are movies, the reality-thesis collapses. Cavell concedes the point is "merely logic" but offers extended phenomenological reflections to dislodge the conviction that cartoons are movies.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Cavell recalls his summary characterization of the "material basis of the media of movies" as "a succession of automatic world projections" (book p.72) [p.167]. Cartoons threaten this because they "explicitly [have] nothing to do with projections of the real world"; if they count against his insistence on projection of reality, "it counters it completely" [p.167]. He quotes Sesonske's description: of cartoon creatures "neither these lively creatures nor their actions ever existed until they were projected on the screen... when we ask if there is any feature in which it differs from reality, the answer is, 'Yes, every feature'"; it is "a world I know and see but to which I am nevertheless not present, yet not a world past," with "no access to its space from ours except through vision" [pp.167–168]. Cavell observes each remark is "the negation or parody of something I claim for the experience of movies," but they disprove his claims only "on the assumption that cartoons are movies" [p.168]. On his own assumption — "which I should no doubt have made explicit" — that cartoons are *not* movies, "these remarks about their conditions of existence constitute some explanation about why they are not" [p.168]. The moral: a good case can be made, "using the very terms of *The World Viewed*," that cartoons *are* movies, so it would not surprise him if someone thinks so. But there is an "asymmetry": his opponent "has no theory which his taste contradicts" and can simply say "the two are not that different," whereas Cavell "apparently, [has] to show that cartoons are not movies" [p.168]. He cannot *prove* this "any more than I can show that a robot is not a creature or that a human is not a mouse or a dog or a duck" [p.169]; all he can do is "provide some reflections on cartoons."

The reflections [pp.169–172]: The chief inhabitants are "talking animals — anthropomorphic... in everything but form" [p.169]; human figures "seem... out of place or in the way" because there is "less room... in these human figures for our fulfillment of their animation, i.e., for our anthropomorphizing of them" [p.169]. The cartoon *world* is itself animated — "animistic" — tapping "perhaps the most primitive of our convictions about the world"; "essentially a child's world," which he insists is not a belittling but "an ineluctable substratum of our own" [pp.169–170]. The difference from our world is not that its laws differ but that "we are uncertain when or to what extent our laws and limits do and do not apply (which suggests that there are no real laws at all)" [p.170]. The clearest abrogations concern "physical identity or destruction": metamorphosis is familiar, and gravity is suspended — though "what is abrogated is not gravity... but corporeality" [p.170]. Cartoon bodies "never get in their way," are "indestructible... immortal... totally subject to will, and perfectly expressive"; reversing "the economy of human expressiveness," their bodies bear "the brunt of meaningfulness" while faces stay fixed (capturing "the poignance... of real animals"); they are "animations, disembodiments, pure spirits" [p.170]. Animation favors *small* animals (they convey "animatedness, quickness" and take upright posture without seeming "grotesque or parodistic of the human") [pp.170–171]. Being "pure spirits," these beasts "avoid, or deny, the metaphysical fact of human beings, that they are condemned to both souls and bodies"; "a world devoid of sex and death," hence "either very sad or very happy," eliciting "a painful tenderness" [p.171].

He then locates moods "natural to (narrative) animation," stressing *natural* means historically/culturally variable, not exclusive: cartoon violence "can be funny because while it is very brutal it cannot be bloody" [p.171] (contrasted with silent slapstick, which is under-cranked, "more imminent than actual," often between humans and objects); "Cartoon tenderness and loss is tenderness and loss maximized, or purified"; "Cartoon terror is absolute, because since the body is not destructible, the threat is to the soul itself" — distinguished from horror movies, where the threat is "isolation through unacknowledgeable disfigurement," the fear "that cartoons laugh at: irreversible metamorphosis" [p.171]. He asks "Does Popeye have a soul?" — his body "survives, or ignores, everything that brute human strength can deliberately inflict upon it," provided "its magic infusion of canned fuel"; it is "stoked," and "acts on its own," face "preternaturally fixed," voice a "continuous static of undecipherable commentary" [pp.171–172]. In cartoons "sexuality is apt to be either epicene or caricatured," because cartoons, "being fleshless, do not veer toward the pornographic" though they "may naturally veer toward the obscene" [p.172]. A cartoon of Garbo, Dietrich, or Monroe "will miss what is truly attractive about them — not simply their specific physical presences but the animation of those presences by the human female's exemplification of independence and profundity and wit"; but a cartoon of Mae West "may really capture her genius, because she is already a caricature of sexuality" — "the woman of no mystery," her invitations "not suggestive, but epicene" [p.172].
