He returns to whether he missed Sesonske's point: if the charge is that his characterization "rules out... cartoons altogether as a species of film — that... according to me they ought not to exist," "That would be bad," and he could simply amend it to "cartoons are successions of *animated* world projections" — a characterization "no more helpful, and no emptier" than what it summarizes [pp.172–173]. He wonders if "perhaps I am unduly solemn," and that Sesonske's joke ("the two are not that different") may really mean "that Hollywood feature films are mostly mickey mouse" — on which "our experiences may really disagree," and which "I cannot afford to laugh... off" [p.173].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "this basis is 'a succession of automatic world projections.'" [p.167]
- "He does not have to show that cartoons are movies because he has no theory which his taste contradicts." [p.168]
- "what is abrogated is not gravity... but corporeality. The bodies of these creatures never get in their way." [p.170]
- "They are animations, disembodiments, pure spirits." [p.170]
- "A world whose creatures are incorporeal is a world devoid of sex and death, hence a world apt to be either very sad or very happy." [p.171]
- "Cartoon terror is absolute, because since the body is not destructible, the threat is to the soul itself." [p.171]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Disney (the obvious case, "containing stars"); Bambi (unseen by Cavell) and Dumbo (exceptions — both children, the latter "redeemed" by flight); Popeye (body "stoked" by canned fuel; "mythical children") and Wimpy ("timid acquaintance" tied to a "childish food, ground meat"); Garbo / Dietrich / Marilyn Monroe (what cartoons of them *miss* — animation by independence, profundity, wit); Mae West ("already a caricature of sexuality" — what a cartoon *can* capture); horror movies (contrast: "unacknowledgeable disfigurement" vs. cartoon's reversible metamorphosis).

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *automatic world projections* — the material basis of movies (book p.72); cartoons are at most "*animated* world projections," diagnosing why they differ.
- *animism / the child's world* — the cartoon world is itself alive, tapping primitive conviction; an "ineluctable substratum" of our world, not beneath dignity.
- *corporeality (vs. gravity)* — what cartoons truly abrogate: bodies that "never get in their way," indestructible, "subject to will."
- *epicene* — sexuality in cartoons (and in Mae West) that is neither pornographic nor truly suggestive; "nothing permanent ever happens."
- *anthropomorphizing as "fulfillment of animation"* — the viewer's act of animating the figures; human cartoon figures leave "less room" for it.

---

#### Segment 6: Defending Hollywood — genres, happy endings, and America's place [pp. 173–174]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell answers the charge that his over-indulgence of Hollywood distorts his account of "traditional" movies, arguing his praise is fully compatible with conceding most Hollywood films are worthless, and that Hollywood's "limitations" are no worse than other traditions'.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** "Everything I wrote... about Hollywood films is perfectly compatible with a claim that most of them are childish or worthless" — though he bets the worthless proportion is no greater "than of, say, French or Russian films, a part of whose prestige comes from our knowing so few of them" [p.173]. His real problem was "to understand, if the accidents and stringencies of Hollywood production were the wholly baneful things that devotees of non-Hollywood films suggest, how so many of them could have been so good" [p.173]. He rejects the alibi that good films are good "in spite of Hollywood's bosses and agents": does it "make any sense at all to think of figures like John Ford or Howard Hawks or Frank Capra or Preston Sturges as limited by Hollywood?" — "theirs are perfect of their kind" [pp.173–174]. On happy endings: blaming the moguls "will not account for the good films which call for, and earn, their happy endings"; the prevalence of the happy ending shows only "that Hollywood did its best work in genres which call for happy endings" [p.174]. One *could* argue the genres and conventions are "themselves the essential limitation," but then one must show either that other traditions have "no comparable limitations" or that *theirs* are "less limiting" — e.g., "a Russian tendency toward the monumental and operatic, or a French tendency toward the private and provincial, or a German tendency toward the theatrical and claustrophobic" [p.174]. He concludes it is "not clear to me that American films occupy a less honorable place among the films of the world than American fiction occupies in world literature" [p.174].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Everything I wrote in *The World Viewed* about Hollywood films is perfectly compatible with a claim that most of them are childish or worthless." [p.173]
- "how so many of them could have been so good" [p.173]
- "Hollywood did its best work in genres which call for happy endings." [p.174]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** John Ford, Howard Hawks, Frank Capra, Preston Sturges — directors "perfect of their kind," not "limited by Hollywood"; Russian/French/German national tendencies — each its own "limitation," no less limiting than Hollywood's genres.

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *genres which "call for, and earn," their endings* — Cavell's defense of the happy ending as an achievement of genre, not a commercial imposition.

---

#### Segment 7: Types, not characters — the actor as type; the mythical in the typical [pp. 174–179]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Sesonske claims European directors and sound film deal in individual *characters* whereas Hollywood/silent film deals in *types*. Cavell defends his book's thesis that on film the type is primarily the *actor*, and that the deepest films explore types, not "well-rounded characters" — reframing the typical as the *mythical*.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Cavell describes his method: "to invite words or concepts which are common, all but unavoidable, in speaking about film, and then try to discover what there is in these words and in my experience of these objects that they should go together" [p.174] — risking either not being credited for, or not actually, saying something new ("Neither seems to me unbearable") [p.174]. Sensing "an accuracy in the idea of a type," he traced the intuition "to the fact that on film the type is not primarily the character but primarily the actor" [pp.174–175]. The "fixed attitudes and attributes" defining types are, on film, "established by the individual and total physiognomy (of face, of figure, of gait, of temperament)" of the human beings in the drama [p.175]. Hence "a type on the screen need not be established in theatrical tradition... it can be established by the recurrence of an actor in a corpus of films" — which is why his list of Hollywood types (book p.36) "mixed together the names of characters and of stars" [p.175]. This explains why "the distinction between actor and character is broken up on the screen," leading him to speak of screened individuals as "individualities" and "to deny in the experiencing of them the distinction between types and well-rounded characters" [p.175]. So he is "not likely to be much impressed by the statement that European directors (Sesonske mentions Renoir, Vigo, and Pagnol)... concern themselves with characters and not types" [p.175]; he grants the Europeans made "different sorts of movies" and that "their films are on the whole better," but their differences "are not touched, or they are obscured, by saying that the American keep to a concern with types and the European do not" [p.175].

Cavell then argues the great European films are "explorations of types rather than... characters" [p.175]. The figures in both Renoir films "are insistently labelled for us: the Aristocrat, the Jew, the Officer, the Professor, the Good Guy, the Poacher, the Wronged Wife, the Impetuous Lover" [pp.175–176], and both films are "about the arbitrariness and the inevitability of labels," hence "the human need for society and the equal human need to escape it," hence "human privacy and unknownness" — "the search for society, or community, outside, or within, society at large" [p.176]. "If four of the greatest films ever made are about the same subject, it gives one to think that film has here found one of its great subjects" — the same subject he found in the Western and in thirties comedies/musicals; his terms there were "individual autonomy and its resemblance to outlawry, and... the claims placed upon autonomy by society and by love" [p.176]. He develops *L'Atalante* as "the best film ever made about the idea of marriage" — its richness "has nothing to do with anything I understand as a study of character"; compared to Desdemona and Othello, "Vigo's bride and groom are, well, mere types" — "We know and learn next to nothing about them" [pp.176–177]. The old man is more developed but "this is a function of his actions rather than of his character" — he holds "the key to the plot," for "if this marriage is to take effect... then the old man will have to effect it," i.e., "give the bride," having authority because "we have seen him win her for himself" [p.177]. From "this nice little yarn" we learn (by "the resonance that Vigo's camera... elicits") how wedding processions "can feel like funeral processions" (commemorating "the dying of the bride to her past"), "the daze and remoteness of brides," and that "a man wins a beautiful young girl only when he wins her imagination"; above all "that one's responsibility to one's desire is to acknowledge it, and acknowledge its object, i.e., acknowledge its object's separateness from you" [p.177] — power "as inexplicable as... a phrase of music or of poetry." "Of course there is a sense of character explored. It is our own." [p.177]

He invokes Thomas Mann's essay on Freud — "the point at which the psychological interest passes over into the mythical," and "the typical is actually the mythical" [pp.177–178] — Mann's move as a novelist "from the bourgeois and individual to the mythical and typical," where "the mythical knowledge resides in the gazer and not in that at which he gazes" [p.178]. "What [Mann] finds to be an achievement for the novel I find to be the natural mode of revelation for film"; "That gaze of knowledge is the province of camera and screen" [p.178]. The mythical-in-the-typical also yields "the right level of explanation for the familiar fact that mediocre novels often make good film scripts and that great novels almost never do" [p.178]. Answering Sesonske's claim that silent film was confined to "mere types" because "speech is so deeply implicated in the formation and expression of our character," Cavell makes two remarks: (1) speech immediately created a new way of defining a type "through his particular attitude and attribute of speech" — Groucho and Harpo (whose "muteness... most brilliantly declares the new medium"), Mae West, W. C. Fields, "characters unthinkable apart from their singular voices" [p.178]; (2) Sesonske's "impatience" must have led him to overlook Chaplin — "the Little Man, the Tramp, who is a type, and reenacts types, as deep as comedy" [pp.178–179]. He notes Enid Welsford's study of folly emphasizing "particular actors in the development of comic stage types," suggesting "the early affinity of film with comedy is an expression of their respective latitudes and appetites for the actor's individuality" [p.179].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "on film the type is not primarily the character but primarily the actor" [pp.174–175]
- "the distinction between actor and character is broken up on the screen" [p.175]
- "Compared to Desdemona and Othello, Vigo's bride and groom are, well, mere types." [p.177]
- "one's responsibility to one's desire is to acknowledge it, and acknowledge its object, i.e., acknowledge its object's separateness from you." [p.177]
- "Of course there is a sense of character explored. It is our own." [p.177]
- "What he finds to be an achievement for the novel I find to be the natural mode of revelation for film." [p.178]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** *Grand Illusion* & *Rules of the Game* (Renoir — figures "insistently labelled," films "about the arbitrariness... of labels"); *Zero for Conduct* & *L'Atalante* (Vigo); *L'Atalante* (the great study of marriage as exploration of types, not character; the old man "gives the bride"); Desdemona/Othello (theatrical *characters*, contrast to Vigo's "mere types"); the Western and thirties comedies/musicals (same "great subject" — autonomy, outlawry, claims of society and love); Thomas Mann's essay on Freud ("the typical is actually the mythical"; "knowledge resides in the gazer"); Groucho, Harpo (muteness "declares the new medium"), Mae West, W. C. Fields (types defined by "singular voices"); Chaplin (the Tramp — type "as deep as comedy," Sesonske's omission); Enid Welsford (folly study — actors in comic-type development); Pagnol (named among Sesonske's "European directors").

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *type (on film) = the actor* — types are constituted by an actor's "total physiognomy" recurring across a corpus of films, not by a role recurring across plays.
- *individualities* — Cavell's term for screened persons, in whom the type/well-rounded-character distinction dissolves.
- *the mythical in the typical* (Mann) — the typical, deeply taken, is mythical; the "gaze of knowledge" belongs to camera and screen.
- *acknowledgment of separateness* — responsibility to desire = acknowledging the object's separateness from oneself (the *L'Atalante* lesson).

---

#### Segment 8: Acting and presence — concessions on slips; "actors the way any human being is"; unknown physiognomies [pp. 179–182]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell concedes several of Sesonske's specific criticisms of his weak remarks on acting, restates his account of the screen actor's non-presence, and defends his treatment of "unknown faces" against the charge that it conflicts with his emphasis on actor-recurrence.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Recurring to "the topic of acting and performance for film," Cavell "agree[s] with Sesonske that a number of my remarks on this topic are weak and unconvincing" and that Sesonske "called me on a couple of stupid slips" [p.179]. First slip: he had written, "The audience in a theater can be defined as those to whom the *actors* are present while they are not present to the *actors*" — "a conflation of two ideas": the first (from earlier essays) defines the theater audience as those to whom the *characters* are present while not present to the characters; the second "contrasts the audience in a movie house as those present neither to the characters nor the actors" [p.179]. Second concession: he "was wrong... to put this as though it was always the actor who did the exploring and not the director," since his point "does not depend on any such general division of labor" [p.179]. Answering Sesonske ("De Sica and Bresson have not shown that film does not require actors but rather that there is something of the actor in us all"), Cavell points to his own book (p.153): "But obviously they are actors the way any human being is" — which says "that we are all ineluctably actors," leaving open "how far that means we theatricalize ourselves and how far we are naturalized, fated both to motion and to motive"; and it suggests our condition as actors "is shown not particularly by De Sica and Bresson, but by film itself" [pp.179–180]. What those directors showed, rather, is "that there is something of the *type* in us all, something of the singular and the mythical" — that we are "set apart or singled out for sometimes incomprehensible reasons, for rewards or punishments out of all proportion... as though our lives are the enactments of some tale whose words continuously escape us" [p.180]. "The mismatch of intention and consequence was the natural province of tragedy in theater. On film it is the natural province of comedy, and of something that is neither tragedy nor comedy" — exemplified by *Bicycle Thief*, *Nights of Cabiria*, *The Diary of a Country Priest* — where the mismatch is "the distance between the depth to which an ordinary human life requires expression, and the surface of ordinary means through which that life must, if it will, express itself." "One may call such forms secular mysteries." [p.180]

On unknown faces: De Sica, Bresson, Dreyer, Fellini are "famous for their interest in unknown physiognomies" — seemingly incompatible with actor-recurrence, "But why?" [p.180]. The use of unknown faces is "a special possibility" with "different powers... [from] recurrence" (book p.159) [pp.180–181]. Compared to casting unknowns in theater — where "the fact of unknownness is artistically more or less irrelevant" — a film director "sometimes... require[s] physiognomies... whose point, whose essence, is that they are unknown" [p.181]. "Not just any unknown face will do; it must be one which, when screened, conveys unknownness; and this first of all means that it conveys privacy — an individual soul's aliveness or deadness to itself" [p.181]. The natural reason is "that his film is itself about unknownness, about the fact and causes of separateness or isolation or integrity or outlawry" [p.181]. "The greatest use of this quality, in my experience, is found in Dreyer's *Passion of St. Joan*" — and "a film of such depth must be giving deep significance to conditions of the medium of film itself. (This is, or ought to be, the meaning of 'cinematic.')" Dreyer's film "declares at once the power of the camera to interrogate its subjects and... its final impotence to penetrate the mystery of the individual human face" [p.181]. This is measured against "the use of known faces, *as known*, to the same effect, which generally means placing them in unanticipated environments" — film's tendency "to discover at any moment the endless contingency of the individual human's placement in the world, as though nothing could be more unanticipated than one's existence itself, always in placement" [p.181]. Chaplin, Keaton, Garbo, "perhaps Gary Cooper" "consistently explore this experience; they are at home everywhere and nowhere" [pp.181–182], and "the mystery of the familiar" is caught in a list of known faces in unanticipated environments [p.182].

He then poses the underlying question: stage actors are "present, live," screen actors not — "Is this important, or trivial? Mostly it is obscure" [p.182]. "What is the screen actor not, if he is not live? Is he dead? Not necessarily, but he could be. He could also be sitting next to me, which he could not be if he were on stage" [p.182]. These "seem stupid mysteries": "Of course it's not him on the screen. Nothing is on the screen, but, at best, moving light and shadows. It's the performance which is live" [p.182] — but live "As contrasted with what?" Since sound transcription and videotape, "'live' performances contrast with recorded performances," and the same contrast seems to hold between stage and screen. "Then what is a movie a recording of?; to which the answer is apt to be, Nothing" [p.182]. From this it may seem to follow "that the issue of reality is settled, that movies are 'something on their own'" — the only thing they could record, "real events happening as they are transcribed on the screen — have simply never taken place" — so the Panofsky/Bazin issue "is a false issue." "With this last assertion I shall hardly disagree. What are they?" [pp.182–183]

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "But obviously they are actors the way any human being is" [p.180, quoting book p.153]
- "we are all ineluctably actors... fated both to motion and to motive" [p.180]
- "One may call such forms secular mysteries." [p.180]
- "it must be one which, when screened, conveys unknownness; and this first of all means that it conveys privacy — an individual soul's aliveness or deadness to itself." [p.181]
- "the power of the camera to interrogate its subjects and... its final impotence to penetrate the mystery of the individual human face." [p.181]
- "Nothing is on the screen, but, at best, moving light and shadows. It's the performance which is live." [p.182]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** De Sica & Bresson (Sesonske's "something of the actor in us all" — Cavell: rather "something of the type in us all"); *Bicycle Thief*, *Nights of Cabiria* (Fellini), *The Diary of a Country Priest* (Bresson) — "secular mysteries," mismatch of depth and ordinary means; Dreyer, Fellini (interest in unknown physiognomies); Dreyer's *Passion of St. Joan* (supreme use of unknownness; camera's power to interrogate and impotence to penetrate the face); Chaplin, Keaton, Garbo, Gary Cooper ("at home everywhere and nowhere"); the known-faces list — Henry Fonda (*The Wrong Man*), Cary Grant (*Suspicion*), Ginger Rogers (*Roxie Hart*), Joan Crawford (*A Woman's Face*), Billie Burke (*The Wizard of Oz*), Joe E. Brown (*A Midsummer Night's Dream*), Rita Hayworth (*The Lady from Shanghai*), Montgomery Clift (*From Here to Eternity*), Burt Lancaster (*The Leopard*), Clark Gable (*The Misfits*), John Garfield (*Gentleman's Agreement*), Kirk Douglas (*The List of Adrian Messenger*) — "the mystery of the familiar."

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *present / live (vs. screen actor)* — stage actors are present to an audience; screen actors are not present, though "the performance... is live"; the contrast is "obscure," not trivial.
- *secular mysteries* — film forms (neither tragedy nor comedy) staging the distance between the depth a life requires and the ordinary surface available to express it.
- *unknownness / privacy* — a screened face that conveys "an individual soul's aliveness or deadness to itself"; the essence of certain casting, tied to films "about... separateness or isolation."
- *"cinematic"* — properly: a film of depth "giving deep significance to conditions of the medium of film itself."
- *contingency of placement* — film's discovery of "the endless contingency of the individual human's placement in the world."

---

#### Segment 9: Movies are not recordings — reality's role is photographed, not recorded [pp. 183–184]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell takes the concession "movies are not recordings of anything" and shows that, far from settling the reality question against him, it leaves untouched what reality's *actual* role is — which his book answers as: photographed, projected, screened, exhibited, viewed.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** "First of all, movies are not recordings. It is worth seeing why they are not" [p.183]. Calling something a recording invokes two criteria: "(1) there is an original, separate event of which this is a recording and to which one can be present directly... apart from the recording; (2) the recording is in principle aurally indistinguishable from that event," where the recording's "essential virtue... is fidelity" [p.183]. Now when one says we cannot have been present at screened events apart from the projection, "nothing follows about what is apart from that projection itself"; in particular "it does not follow that reality has played no essential role in the origin of that projection." "All that follows is that any role reality has played is not that of having been recorded" [p.183]. Reality "is not so much as a candidate for that role," because film projections "are not in principle aurally or visually indistinguishable from the events of which they are projections — what could be more distinguishable?" [p.183]. Therefore "to say we cannot have been present apart from the projection does not settle the question of reality; it does not so much as raise it" [p.183]. (He flags two motives behind "can't have been present": some say it because "the issue oughtn't so much as be raised"; some say it to him "because they imagine that I have asserted that one can have been present" — both to be examined.) The state of the question: "If reality does play an essential role in understanding the medium of film, and if its role is not that of being recorded, then what is its role?" [pp.183–184]. His book's series of answers: "I describe the role of reality as one of being photographed, projected, screened, exhibited, and viewed" — "the paths of my differences from Panofsky's and Bazin's emphasis on 'reality itself'" — whose significance "can be assessed... by nothing short of my book as a whole" [p.184].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "movies are not recordings. It is worth seeing why they are not." [p.183]
- "All that follows is that any role reality has played is not that of having been recorded." [p.183]
- "to say we cannot have been present apart from the projection does not settle the question of reality; it does not so much as raise it." [p.183]
- "I describe the role of reality as one of being photographed, projected, screened, exhibited, and viewed." [p.184]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Newsreels (the limit case — film "used as a sort of recording device"; but ordinary movie events "we can never be... present at apart from the movie itself"); Panofsky and Bazin ("reality itself," from which Cavell's verb-series differs).

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *recording (two criteria)* — (1) a separate original to which one can be present apart from the recording; (2) fidelity / in-principle indistinguishability. Film meets neither; hence it is not a recording.
- *photographed, projected, screened, exhibited, viewed* — Cavell's series of verbs naming reality's actual role in film, replacing "reality itself."

---

#### Segment 10: The myth of the camera — "all and only what is revealed to it"; sterility not chastity; the abyss between exposure and exhibition [pp. 184–186]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Sesonske called Cavell's image of the camera "revealing all and only what is revealed to it" a "myth of immaculate conception" denying the human role in conceiving. Cavell defends and reinterprets the mythological description, distinguishing the camera's *sterility* from purity and locating the photograph's mystery between exposure and exhibition.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Cavell concedes "My description certainly was mythological, as the terms 'fate' and 'revelation'... declare," but "the mythological point... was not exactly to describe purity" [p.184]. He first clarifies that "camera" stands for "the entire physical apparatus which comes between what is before the camera and what results after it, on the screen" — camera plus lenses, filters, film, light, and "the physical procedures of preparing the film for projection" (defining "camera" "in terms of its final cause") [p.184]. When the camera so understood reveals "all and only what is revealed to it," he does *not* limit what is revealed "merely to the subject which has passed before it": "The revelation can, as it were, come from inside" [p.184]. Before the camera stands a white building; if the screen shows "a shaky white building, or a blurred or deformed one, or two identical ones moving past one another, this does not prove unclean conception — unless... it prompts us to believe that the building itself is shaking, or is subject to deformation as in a cartoon, or is capable of self-locomotion and spontaneous self-duplication" [p.184]. The mythological point "is twofold: first, that the camera has no choice either over what is revealed to it nor over what it reveals; and second, that what is revealed to and by it can only be known by what appears upon the print or screen" [pp.184–185].

The *first* fold "is not an emphasis on its chastity but on its sterility": "To say it has no choice is to say that it is a species of machine, a contrivance, and a reasonably simple one at that, compared, say, with a TV set" [p.185]. Its work is "immaculate" not because produced "inhumanly (i.e., automatically)" — though its automatism "is an essential fact about its work" — "but because it has no conception whatever of its own. (A camera is a kind of room, not a kind of womb.)" [p.185]. "It does all and only what it is made to do. It tells no lies, any more than a typewriter tells lies; not because it is perfectly honest but because it is perfectly dumb" [p.185]. But unlike a typewriter, "you cannot know what you have made the camera do, what is revealed to it, until its results have appeared" — which is the *second* fold: "the mysteriousness of the photograph lies not in the machinery which produces it, but in the unfathomable abyss between what it captures (its subject) and what is captured for us (this fixing of the subject), the metaphysical wait between exposure and exhibition, the absolute authority or finality of the fixed image" [pp.185]. This weight "is not reduced if some director is in fact able to predict his results with assured accuracy. Hitchcock is famous for this" — "the gift of photographic prophecy" — though one could explore "that he limits beforehand what he shoots to what he can thus assuredly foretell," and the "consequences upon his style" [p.185]. Cavell crystallizes the epistemic reversal: "Film turns our epistemological convictions inside out: reality is known before its appearances are known. The epistemological mystery is whether, and how, you can predict the existence of the one from the knowledge of the other. The photographic mystery is that you can know both the appearance and the reality, but that nevertheless the one is unpredictable from the other" [pp.185–186]. He recalls Edward Steichen's "thousand shots of the same cup and saucer": each different, "a different physiognomy," yet "it is still the same cup and saucer" — "Therein lies the fascination of the thing of photography, like the fascination in faces"; "The art of the thing lies in the capacity for finding what will cause what, and for finding what is wanted" [p.186].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "The revelation can, as it were, come from inside." [p.184]
- "the camera has no choice either over what is revealed to it nor over what it reveals; and second, that what is revealed to and by it can only be known by what appears upon the print or screen." [pp.184–185]
- "It is not an emphasis on its chastity but on its sterility... A camera is a kind of room, not a kind of womb." [p.185]
- "It tells no lies, any more than a typewriter tells lies; not because it is perfectly honest but because it is perfectly dumb." [p.185]
- "the unfathomable abyss between what it captures (its subject) and what is captured for us (this fixing of the subject), the metaphysical wait between exposure and exhibition" [p.185]
- "reality is known before its appearances are known." [p.185]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** The white building (thought experiment — internal "revelation" of distortion does not impugn the camera's veracity); Hitchcock ("photographic prophecy" — predicting results, possibly by limiting what he shoots; consequences for style); Edward Steichen's thousand shots of one cup and saucer (each a different physiognomy, yet the same object — "the fascination of the thing of photography").

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *"camera" (defined by final cause)* — the entire apparatus between subject and screen (lenses, filters, film, light, processing), not just the box.
- *sterility (not chastity)* — the camera "has no conception whatever of its own"; "a kind of room, not a kind of womb"; "perfectly dumb," not "perfectly honest."
- *automatism (here, the camera's)* — its working "automatically" / inhumanly is "an essential fact," but does not by itself make its work "immaculate."
- *the abyss / metaphysical wait between exposure and exhibition* — the photograph's mystery: the gap between subject and fixed image, the "absolute authority or finality of the fixed image."
- *epistemological reversal* — film knows reality before its appearances; you can know both appearance and reality yet not predict one from the other.

---

#### Segment 11: Automatisms — the camera's life of its own; significance of a possibility; intention and responsibility [pp. 186–189]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell defines **automatism** as the term for film's significance-bearing possibilities, distinguishes the camera (which "leads a life of its own") from a mere recorder, and develops the critical method that follows — culminating in the artist's absolute responsibility and film as "a moving image of skepticism."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Though the camera "has no conception of its own," it can, "unlike a recording apparatus, be said to lead a life of its own, with its own possibilities of variation and inflection" [p.186] — these are "original with it" and "create its particular temptations, but they are not its sins. They are either its stupidities or its glories, depending upon who is using it. Here is where sin gets a foothold" [pp.186–187]. He cites Descartes: the contents of the mind "may be distorted, but in themselves they are not erroneous. Error arises when you make judgments on the basis of them" — this being "the specific topic of the sections... called 'The Camera's Implication' and 'Assertions in Techniques'... of giving significance to a possibility of film" [p.187]. He states the thesis: "any and every gesture of the camera may or may not mean something, and every cut and every rhythm of cuts, and every framing and every inflection within a frame — something determined by the nature of film and by the specific context in which the gesture occurs in a particular film. I call such possibilities of the physical medium of film its *automatisms*" [pp.186–187]. "They are the bearers of the film maker's intentions — like syntactical and lexical elements of a language," but with a difference: "Unlike speakers of a language, film makers can construct not merely... new sentences, but new elements of sentences" [p.187]. This "intentionality of film's automatisms dictates the perspective from which a critical understanding of a film must proceed" — a perspective serving "the question, 'Why is this as it is?' — the critical question" [p.187].

He illustrates with "a shot of a stairway": this description "may or may not have a point." Calling it "a 'point of view shot'" — established e.g. "by cutting to it from the face of a character and cutting from it back to that face" — can seem "a sort of syntactic remark," though "The analogy with syntax is extremely thin" (the term "verb" carries systematic implications, unlike "travelling shot," "medium shot," "point of view") [pp.187–188]. Only if you say "why this way of establishing a point of view is used, and why here, and why with respect to this character, and why by way of this content" are you "proposing a critical understanding of this passage," whose interest "will depend upon its faithfulness to the intention of this work" [p.188]. Merely naming the shot, with nothing further, is "at most the uttering of a name, which, as Wittgenstein puts it, is a preparation for going on to say something" [p.188]. He gives a positive case: in *Grand Illusion*, "a film obsessed with the entering and leaving of rooms... with the impassability of sills and frames," the commonest of devices (cutting on a character entering/leaving) "is actively avoided until the beginning of the last act, at the farm," when "we cut from Gabin entering a doorway away from us... to Gabin entering through that doorway towards us, into a bright kitchen" — "The very commonness of the gesture yields to, and acknowledges, the commonness, the anonymity, of the need for warmth and food" [p.188].

He grades directors: "Good directors know how to mean everything they do. Great directors mean more... and they discover how to do everything they mean. The gestures of bad directors are empty — they speak... nonsense" [p.188]. The implication "is the absolute responsibility of the artist for the actions and assertions in his work" — "an instance of the human being's absolute responsibility for the intentions and consequences of his actions, and a kind of solace for it. (The human condemnation to intention and consequence is the sequel, if not the meaning, of original sin.)" [p.188]. His impatience with the idea "that photographs and paintings never really project or represent reality (when... they obviously do) expresses my sense that... a fake skepticism is being used to deny that human responsibility" [p.188].

Then the skepticism thesis: "We may need freedom from this responsibility, but the denial of its claims is no route to that freedom, except within the bounds of comedy or of religion. Film is a moving image of skepticism: not only is there a reasonable possibility, it is a fact that here our normal senses are satisfied of reality while reality does not exist — even, alarmingly, because it does not exist, because viewing it is all it takes" [pp.188–189]. To deny "just this satisfaction — to deny that it is ever reality which film projects and screens — is a farce of skepticism": it "remember[s] that skepticism concludes against our conviction in the existence of the external world, but... forget[s] that skepticism begins in an effort to justify that conviction" [p.189]. "The basis of film's drama, or the latent anxiety in viewing its drama, lies in its persistent demonstration that we do not know what our conviction in reality turns upon." To speak of film as an "illusion of reality" would "disguise this latent anxiety — as does the conclusion of philosophical skepticism itself," dimming "the differences in the role of reality posed in painting, in theater, and in film," and closing out "the wish of art to address reality in order to combat, or suspend, our illusions of it." The sense of reality "is neither enforced nor escaped through film; one might say that it is there *entertained*" [p.189].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "I call such possibilities of the physical medium of film its *automatisms*. They are the bearers of the film maker's intentions — like syntactical and lexical elements of a language." [p.187]
- "film makers can construct not merely, as it were, new sentences, but new elements of sentences." [p.187]
- "Good directors know how to mean everything they do. Great directors mean more... and they discover how to do everything they mean." [p.188]
- "Film is a moving image of skepticism: not only is there a reasonable possibility, it is a fact that here our normal senses are satisfied of reality while reality does not exist — even, alarmingly, because it does not exist, because viewing it is all it takes." [pp.188–189]
- "it seems to remember that skepticism concludes against our conviction in the existence of the external world, but it seems to forget that skepticism begins in an effort to justify that conviction." [p.189]
- "The basis of film's drama, or the latent anxiety in viewing its drama, lies in its persistent demonstration that we do not know what our conviction in reality turns upon." [p.189]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Descartes (mental contents "in themselves... not erroneous"; error in judgment — model for the camera's possibilities as not yet "sins"); Wittgenstein (naming as "a preparation for going on to say something"); *Grand Illusion* (the Gabin doorway cut — the commonest device, withheld then deployed to "acknowledge... the need for warmth and food").

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *automatisms* — the significance-bearing possibilities of film's physical medium (every gesture, cut, framing, inflection); "bearers of the film maker's intentions," analogous to (but thinner than) linguistic syntax/lexicon; film makers can invent "new elements of sentences."
- *"Why is this as it is?"* — the critical question that the intentionality of automatisms dictates; mere naming of a device is only "a preparation" for it.
- *absolute responsibility of the artist* — the corollary of automatism: the artist is fully responsible for every assertion in the work, an instance (and "solace") of the human "condemnation to intention and consequence."
- *fake / farce of skepticism* — denying that film projects reality misuses skepticism: it forgets that skepticism *begins* in an effort to justify our conviction in the external world.
- *film as a moving image of skepticism* — our senses are "satisfied of reality while reality does not exist," because "viewing it is all it takes"; the sense of reality is "entertained," neither enforced nor escaped.
- *illusion of reality (rejected)* — a phrase that "disguise[s]" film's latent anxiety and flattens the differing roles of reality in painting, theater, film.

---

#### Segment 12: Screening reality — withholding; nature vs. society; sentimentality (It's a Wonderful Life) [pp. 189–191]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell unfolds the double sense of "screening" (revealing and concealing), then turns to whether the reality at issue is *natural* or *social*, illustrating film's projection of community and its sentimentality with Capra.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** "The moral of film's image of skepticism is not that reality is a dream and not that reality confines our dreams. In screening reality, film *screens* its givenness from us; it holds reality *from* us, it holds reality *before* us, i.e., *withholds* reality before us" [p.189]. "We are tantalized at once by our subjection to it and by its subjection to our views of it." Yet "while reality is the bearer of our intentions it is possible... to refuse to allow it to dictate what shall be said about it (p. 120)"; "Flanked by its claims to speak for us, it is still open to us in moments to withhold it before ourselves, so that we may see for ourselves and may gladly grant that we are somewhat spoken for" [p.189]. "To know how far reality is open to our dreams would be to know how far reality is confined by our dreams of it" [p.189].

He anticipates a wish "to distinguish between natural and social reality (or between epistemological and political 'givenness')," but doubts "we yet know what becomes, on film, of the distinction, hence the relation, between nature and culture, or between individual and society" [pp.189–190]. "Nature is naturally projected as the scene of specific human occupation; contrariwise, society tends to be projected in human relations which are either wholly personal or wholly anonymous" [p.190]. He recalls the book's claim (p.48) that film projects community "in the form of male comradeship — as though the sense of belonging together as citizens could only appear as an intimacy of discipline, and only after the nation had been threatened (as by war) or exercised its rejection (as by prisons)" [p.190]. Work can yield "pools of comradeship if it has an internal drama and involves the adventure of danger — rigging and drilling for oil comes to mind"; but factory comradeship "seems available only for propaganda... or for the explicitly fantastic" [p.190]. "It is common enough for social evil to be projected as the deliberate product of melodramatic aging villains" [p.190]. Capra's *It's a Wonderful Life* "most innocently reveals a further range of this fantasy": the hero's ruin is averted "when the good people, the little people, band together with their individual contributions, returning personal favors" [pp.190]. "The sentiment in the scene is very deep. It has been constructed as cunningly as a Keaton gag; what caps it, finally bursting the dam of tears, is the crowding of this band of goodness into this hero's house, each member testifying individually to his or her affection for him; so that the good society... is pictured as this man's family (personally sponsored... by a denizen of heaven). This justice, hence this society, is poetic or nothing" [p.190]. The film "adopts the terms, and at least equals the effect, of *A Christmas Carol*, told as it were from Cratchit's point of view." He would "forego its effect if we had something to equal, say, *Bleak House*. But in that case the effect would not be so worrisome" [pp.190–191].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "In screening reality, film screens its givenness from us; it holds reality from us, it holds reality before us, i.e., withholds reality before us." [p.189]
- "To know how far reality is open to our dreams would be to know how far reality is confined by our dreams of it." [p.189]
- "Nature is naturally projected as the scene of specific human occupation; contrariwise, society tends to be projected in human relations which are either wholly personal or wholly anonymous." [p.190]
- "the sense of belonging together as citizens could only appear as an intimacy of discipline, and only after the nation had been threatened... or exercised its rejection" [p.190]
- "This justice, hence this society, is poetic or nothing." [p.190]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** *It's a Wonderful Life* (Capra — "most innocently reveals" the fantasy of the good society as the hero's family, "personally sponsored... by a denizen of heaven"; sentiment "constructed as cunningly as a Keaton gag"); Keaton gag (the standard of construction); *A Christmas Carol* (the model "told... from Cratchit's point of view"); *Bleak House* (Dickens — the unmatched standard that would make the sentimental effect "not so worrisome"); rigging/drilling for oil (work with "internal drama" and "danger" yielding comradeship).

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *screening (double sense)* — film at once reveals and *withholds* reality's givenness; "holds reality from us... before us."
- *withholding reality before ourselves* — the viewer's freedom not to let reality "dictate what shall be said about it"; seeing "for ourselves" while granting we are "somewhat spoken for."
- *natural vs. social (epistemological vs. political) givenness* — a distinction Cavell raises but suspends; film's handling of nature/culture is not yet understood.
- *community as male comradeship* — film's characteristic projection of belonging, requiring threat (war) or rejection (prison); a diagnosis of film's *sentimentality* about society.

---

#### Segment 13: Why "noncompeting emphases" fail — emphasis as everything in philosophizing; the fixed point of reality [pp. 191–193]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Against the "sensible" compromise that one may *equally* emphasize either film's manipulation/arrangement or its being-of-reality, Cavell argues there is no such neutral parity: in philosophizing emphasis is decisive, and there is no "sensible alternative" to grounding arrangements in reality's fixed point.

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** The compromise proposal: "Let us agree that movies are made of incisions, ingestions, projections... of reality. Then it is just a matter of emphasis whether you go on to study the significance of the fact that the ingestions and projections are manipulated and arranged (e.g., edited) or whether you go on to study the fact that the manipulated and arranged projections are *of reality*" [p.191]. Cavell rejects "this idea of alternative and noncompeting emphases" for two reasons. **(1) General:** "a difference of emphasis may, in philosophizing, make all the difference in the world" [p.191]: emphasizing nature's likenesses vs. unlikenesses to an artifact "may determine whether you wind up with a proof for the existence of God"; emphasizing likenesses vs. unlikenesses between men and women "may determine what you take their relationship to promise"; emphasizing liberty vs. equality "may determine whether you are a liberal or a socialist." "The question is why your emphasis falls where it does, and whether it makes sufficient and exact room for the competing emphasis" — "a theoretical... philosophical question, which only means that it is not going to be settled by any given set of instances alone" [pp.191–192]. He takes Dziga Vertov's *Man With a Movie Camera*, "at the least a brilliant anthology of the tricks a loaded camera and fixed and refixed film can play" — "a natural example for someone... who wishes to emphasize film's independence of reality." But "it is fully open to me to say that what this film shows is precisely the inescapability of reality, its fixed point within every brilliance of technique"; indeed it would not be "so good an instance for my emphasis unless its technique were an extreme of brilliance, for only then would it suggest that the extremest bound of technique is still leashed to reality" [p.192]. Only "within this theoretical issue" can "a simpler fact about this film's declaration of reality" be felt: "all virtuosity of technique is quite overwhelmed, or silenced, by a shot of childbirth" [p.192].

**(2) Specific:** "there is no sensible alternative of the kind proposed. To speak of reality photographed, projected, screened, exhibited, and viewed, just *is* to speak of the specific ways in which — arrangements in which and successions by which — reality is projected, screened, etc." — another way of "giving significance to specific possibilities and necessities of the physical medium" [p.192]. Conversely, "to speak of the arrangements and successions of projections apart from the fixed point of reality is to block comprehension of why the arrangements and successions are as they are" [p.192]. This "can only be proven in the analysis and criticism of particular films; that is the only source of evidence... for any theory of film — but as usual only the theory can tell you what to look for, or what counts, as evidence" [p.192]. If someone holds "that we never see reality as it is, with or without a camera, on the basis of some epistemological or scientific theory of vision, then immediately he owes us a presentation of this theory" [p.192]. (And belief "that projection inevitably distorts what is seen" must be compared "with cases in which... projection alone can reveal what is seen — as... in a slow-motion shot of a moment of impact, or a time-lapse sequence of a gathering storm" [p.192].) Whether or not such a theory is "forthcoming," Cavell is "content to proceed on the assumption that the camera is no better off epistemologically or scientifically than the naked eye — that the camera provides views of reality only on the assumption that we normally do, apart from the camera, see reality, i.e., see live persons and real things in actual spaces" [pp.192–193]. He defuses the Steichen point: that one cup and saucer "can appear in continuous variations and inflections of light and of angle" may feed the conclusion that "we never see objects as they are," but this fact "is as unexceptional as the fact that you could not see the cup and saucer were they in total darkness. What else would you expect?" [p.193]. He adds a contrast with words: "There is also no end to the ways these objects may be picked out in words; but variations and inflections among alternative words are not naturally continuous" — a difference that affects "what counts 'as possibilities' in photographing and in writing" [p.193].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "a difference of emphasis may, in philosophizing, make all the difference in the world." [p.191]
- "it is fully open to me to say that what this film shows is precisely the inescapability of reality, its fixed point within every brilliance of technique." [p.192]
- "all virtuosity of technique is quite overwhelmed, or silenced, by a shot of childbirth." [p.192]
- "To speak of reality photographed, projected, screened, exhibited, and viewed, just is to speak of the specific ways in which... reality is projected, screened, etc." [p.192]
- "to speak of the arrangements and successions of projections apart from the fixed point of reality is to block comprehension of why the arrangements and successions are as they are." [p.192]
- "the camera is no better off epistemologically or scientifically than the naked eye" [p.192]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Dziga Vertov's *Man With a Movie Camera* (the would-be proof of film's independence from reality, turned by Cavell into proof of reality's "fixed point" — its technique "leashed to reality," "silenced... by a shot of childbirth"); proof of God / men-and-women / liberty-vs-equality (analogies showing emphasis is decisive in philosophy); slow-motion impact, time-lapse storm (cases where "projection alone can reveal what is seen"); Steichen's cup and saucer (variation of appearances does not show "we never see objects as they are").

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *the fixed point of reality* — reality as the inescapable anchor "within every brilliance of technique"; arrangements/successions are unintelligible apart from it.
- *emphasis (philosophical)* — not a neutral choice but potentially "all the difference in the world"; the real question is *why* it falls where it does and whether it makes room for the competing emphasis.
- *theory and evidence (circularity)* — particular films are the only evidence for film theory, but "only the theory can tell you what to look for, or what counts, as evidence."
- *camera = naked eye (epistemically)* — the camera gives views of reality only on the assumption that we normally see reality apart from it; no special epistemic deficit.
- *continuous variation (photography) vs. discrete words* — photographic appearances vary continuously; verbal descriptions do not — bearing on "what counts as possibilities" in each medium.

---

#### Segment 14: Autonomy of the medium; photographic *inception*; the instrument vs. the tool; modernism, realism, and "viewing unseen" [pp. 193–196]

**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** Cavell answers the objection that, because spatial/temporal relations in projection differ from those in reality, it is not *reality* that is projected — and the worry that the "fixed point of reality" leaves no room for the *art* of movies. He grants the medium's autonomy, but reframes film's photographic basis as the medium's *inception*, leading back to modernism, realism, and the thesis of "viewing unseen."

**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** If one denies that reality is projected "on the ground that spatial or temporal relations within and among projections are not the same as... within and among real things and persons," Cavell "may not be much moved to disagree — as long... as one sees that such a remark says either practically nothing or practically everything ambiguously" [p.193]. The remark says "at least two things": (a) it "repeat[s] and begin[s] to specify the point that projections are not recordings, they do not reproduce what they are of" — as a statue's reproduction reproduces its dimensionality (is another statue), or a painting's reproduction its dimensionality [pp.193–194]; (b) it "stress[es] the fact that the physical medium of film, like the physical medium of painting (paint on a two-dimensional surface), or like an aesthetic medium of literature (e.g., one of its genres), obeys its own laws, has its own autonomy — even when it is also a representation of reality" [p.194]. Hence "you can compose a slow-moving narrative of a fast-moving horse race, and you can represent a horse rearing in a painting which itself does not rear. You merely have to know... how to do it; and your audience merely has to know how to read what you have done" [pp.193–194].

Having stressed autonomy, one might "go on to discover and publish what those laws... are" — perhaps "syntactical laws... governing this medium's power of narration" (requiring one to know "its lexicon"), or "particular ways in which the two-dimensionality of the projection, or of the screen, is organized in order to capture certain dramatic or fictional qualities of the three-dimensional space it projects" [p.194]. Such laws "I could only welcome," taking "their place in position with the other facts about this medium... e.g., with the fact that it is photographic" [p.194]. From this Cavell draws "one further inference": "the general relation between a projection and what it projects is not an achievement *within* the medium (as in the relation between a visual representation and what it represents) but constitutes the *inception* of the medium altogether" — so "exploring the physical basis of the medium is not, as in the case of painting, like exploring and mastering the uses of *tools* but rather like exploring and mastering the powers of an *instrument*, such as the Hammond organ" [p.194]. The danger: "to become a virtuoso of the instrument without... being able to say anything upon it of any significance." "The moral is merely that if one is to discover laws for the artistic medium which takes the medium of film as its physical basis, one will first of all have to know which players of the instrument have been able to speak significantly upon it, and what it is they have been saying" [p.194].

He turns to the worry "that this leaves no room for the art of movies." A "poor reason" is that it "seems to leave no room for the work of the cinematographer," picturing him "in terms of his manipulations of his equipment and images"; whereas Cavell insists on "his, in principle, incessant exercise of insight and choice — for example, upon whether to manipulate, and how, and how much" — beginning "before there is anything to cut or pan or freeze," continuing "until he has the insight, and the choice, to stop" [pp.194–195]. A "better reason" is historical: "realism had become, at the time of the advent of photography and continuing through the advent of the motion picture, the central issue in the practice of the other major representative arts" [pp.195]. This is why Cavell "felt drawn into a discussion of modernist painting" — "as a history of responses to the loss of connection with reality and a consequent history of painting's ways of reestablishing this connection, which in practice required a self-conscious reestablishing of its connection with the past of its art" [p.195]. He restates film's artistic significance: "its apparent and unpredictable solution of the problem of reality at a stroke, by its miraculous neutralizing of the need to connect with reality through representing it, by its stroke of acquiring this connection through successive projections of reality itself" [p.195]. But this "had also not solved the problem of reality but brought it to some ultimate head, since the connection is established by putting us in the condition of 'viewing unseen,' which establishes the connection only at the price of establishing our absolute distance and isolation. And this is exactly the price of skepticism" [p.195]. He closes the move with a chiasmus: "My complaint against the complaint against me to the effect that I am naive about reality is that it is naive about reality" [pp.195–196]. Finally he names "more obvious reasons for taking my emphasis on reality to be misguided," introducing the next stretch (handled by the second reader): "(1) My insistence seems just to deny the obvious fact that what appears on the screen may not be real from the beginning; the props may be fake and the backgrounds painted. (2) It seems to neglect the decisive role... of composition within the frame" [pp.195–196], beginning to distinguish "the counterfeit and the artificial" [p.196].

**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "the physical medium of film... obeys its own laws, has its own autonomy — even when it is also a representation of reality." [p.194]
- "the general relation between a projection and what it projects is not an achievement within the medium... but constitutes the inception of the medium altogether" [p.194]
- "like exploring and mastering the powers of an instrument, such as the Hammond organ." [p.194]
- "its miraculous neutralizing of the need to connect with reality through representing it, by its stroke of acquiring this connection through successive projections of reality itself." [p.195]
- "the connection is established by putting us in the condition of 'viewing unseen,' which establishes the connection only at the price of establishing our absolute distance and isolation. And this is exactly the price of skepticism." [p.195]
- "My complaint against the complaint against me to the effect that I am naive about reality is that it is naive about reality." [pp.195–196]

**Examples / films / persons invoked:** The slow-moving narrative of a fast horse race / a painting of a rearing horse that does not rear (the medium's autonomy — representation obeying its own laws); the Hammond organ (analogy: film's physical basis is an *instrument* whose powers are mastered, not a kit of *tools*); modernist painting (the history of responses to "the loss of connection with reality," requiring reconnection with the art's past — the context that drew Cavell in).

**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:**
- *autonomy of the medium* — film (like painting's two-dimensionality or a literary genre) "obeys its own laws" even while representing reality; spatial/temporal relations need not match reality's.
- *inception (vs. achievement within the medium)* — film's photographic basis is not something achieved inside the medium (as a painting achieves representation) but constitutes the medium's very starting condition.
- *instrument vs. tool* — exploring film's physical basis is like mastering an instrument (Hammond organ), not like mastering the use of painters' tools; one can be a "virtuoso" yet "say nothing of significance."
- *realism as the central modern problem* — at photography's advent, realism was the crux for the representative arts; film "solved" it "at a stroke" by acquiring connection to reality through projecting it — yet thereby brought the problem "to some ultimate head."
- *"viewing unseen"* — film establishes its connection to reality only by placing us as unseen viewers, at "absolute distance and isolation" — "exactly the price of skepticism."
- *counterfeit vs. artificial* — the distinction Cavell begins at p.196 (developed past the section boundary): fakery meant to fool the camera into taking artifice for reality, vs. artifice whose point is explicitly its non-reality.

---

**End of assigned section (pp. 162–196).** The text breaks mid-argument at p.196 within the "counterfeit vs. artificial" distinction (the second reader, covering pp.196–246, continues with the *Wizard of Oz* / *Caligari* discussion of explicit artifice and the "machinery of fakery").

---

The assigned half is organized by Cavell as a numbered rebuttal: he takes up, in order, the **obvious features of film that his "appeal to reality" might seem to deny or neglect** — (1) the machinery of fakery/special effects; (2) composition within the frame; and (3) the fictionality of film events — and then turns to modernism, the mythological reading, and a concluding act of criticism on *Rules of the Game*.

---

#### Segment: Counterfeiting reality and the machinery of fakery [pp. 196–199]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** He answers the first apparent objection to his "insistence on reality" — that the vast technology of special effects, back-projection, miniatures, and false fronts shows reality to be unnecessary to film.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** The dream-Kansas of Dorothy and the wicked witch on a bicycle and the "counterfeiting" of reality "tap one and the same source of power," which Cavell names "the human craving for reality," or "the craving for our fantasies and reality to complete or to project one another" [p.197]. Counterfeiting reality is the *typical* case. The whole apparatus of movie production — special effects, back-projections, miniatures, false fronts — does *not* show the fact of reality to be unnecessary; "On the contrary, it goes to show just how necessary it is, or how it is necessary," because the *point* of all this machinery is precisely that it "shall serve for reality, from the camera's point of view" [p.197]. Unmasking such tricks (delighting in production shots of half-built tree-trunks and an actor on a box) is a "treat for certain sensibilities," gossip-level pleasure of the "inside dopester"; but such knowledge has no more significance in viewing and assessing a film than knowing a novelist's working habits has in assessing a novel [pp.197–198]. Yet Cavell will not confine technology's interest to gossip, nor slight that technology plays a role in cinema "unlike its role in any of the other arts" — its machinery can be included in the mise-en-scène. He runs a comparison across the arts: architecture's technology has direct artistic/spectacular appeal; painting's mastery (yoking colors, staining) remains a lifetime's work; the technology of writing is "nothing in comparison," yet the relation of constructing a line to constructing a sentence has occupied poets for a millennium — and the visibility of writing (ink on paper) is more interestingly declared in **Bresson's *Diary of a Country Priest*** (ink identified with wine, the priest's "sustenance and suicide") than in **Mallarmé's *Un Coup de Dés*** [p.198]. The **artistic moral of all the machinery of fakery** equals the moral of photographic projection itself: "only what appears within the projected frame counts, exists for the film. The rest is mere convenience" [p.198]. What you can get away with, or really need, are "empirical matters to be discovered... only by screening it." When a convenience stops being convenient it drops out of use "like an out-house" — though survivors of an older generation will insist only the out-house is proper, and (given the technology and economics that made studios both unnecessary and too expensive) the older generation is "not, in all cases, wrong"; but "mere convenience was never the whole issue" [p.198]. He sharpens: "The insistence on reality is not a matter of ethical purity but of cinematic fact" [p.198]. Whether you build a house from scratch for the camera or tear an actual house apart, the cinematic fact is unaltered. The decisive contrast: on the stage two trees may constitute a forest and two brooms the two trees; "for the screen, this would yield only two brooms"; a stage bare but for platforms can be the Roman Senate or Caesar's wife's bedroom; "on the screen, it is an empty stage with a couple of platforms on it" [p.199]. The screen "contracts for its powers of illusion by destroying incompatible powers." A horizontal view reaches the horizon "not by convention but in fact," unless stopped by an interposed object, atmosphere, darkness, or a frame-filling close-up. You can fool the camera (painting or back-projecting a horizon), but this *trompe l'appareil* is no more essential to cinema than *trompe l'oeil* is essential to realistic painting — and either may serve an art that is not "realistic" [p.199].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "the human craving for reality... the craving for our fantasies and reality to complete or to project one another." [p.197]
- "Doesn't all of this just immediately go to show how unnecessary the fact of reality is? On the contrary, it goes to show just how necessary it is, or how it is necessary." [p.197]
- "only what appears within the projected frame counts, exists for the film. The rest is mere convenience." [p.198]
- "The insistence on reality is not a matter of ethical purity but of cinematic fact." [p.198]
- "On the stage, two trees may constitute a forest, and two brooms the two trees; for the screen, this would yield only two brooms." [p.199]
- "The screen contracts for its powers of illusion by destroying incompatible powers..." [p.199]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** *The Wizard of Oz* (Dorothy, the witch on a bicycle, black-and-white Kansas) — the union of counterfeit and craving for reality; Bresson's *Diary of a Country Priest* — the physical/visible commitment of writing made cinematic (ink = wine = sustenance and suicide); Mallarmé's *Un Coup de Dés* — by contrast, a weaker declaration of writing's visibility.
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *trompe l'appareil* — "fooling the camera" (painting/back-projecting a horizon), coined by analogy to *trompe l'oeil*, and like it inessential to the medium. *Convenience* (vs. cinematic fact) — what production builds/keeps is empirical convenience; only what is within the projected frame is cinematically real.

---

#### Segment: Composition within the frame; the frame as mask, not border [pp. 199–203]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** The second apparent objection — that he neglects how reality is *shaped* on film, the *ordonnance* within the individual frame, with some theorists reading frames as paintings. He concedes this as one possibility of the medium while subordinating composition to the automatism of world projection.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** It is "certainly compatible" with what he wrote that a cinematographer treat the frame as a painting's frame — "one possibility of film, one more fact of the medium whose significance is to be discovered" [p.200]. He recalls his claim in *The World Viewed* that the film frame has the *opposite* significance of the frame in painting: following **Bazin's** suggestion that the screen works as much by what it excludes as by what it includes — that it functions "less to frame than to mask" (which led Cavell to speak of a photograph as "a segment of the world as a whole") — he interpreted the film frame as forming its content not as borders or outlines do "but rather the way looms or molds form" [p.200]. The "frankest acknowledgment" of the camera's exclusion (its continuous implication of what is beyond the frame) is **Cukor's *Adam's Rib***: the camera trained for a whole scene on Tracy and Hepburn's empty bedroom while they shout across the vacant space from dressing rooms just out of frame, the effect being not "speaking across a void" but "an intimacy which does not require visible presence" — our intimacy increased because their "invisible and pervasive presence" puts us in the same relation to each as they bear to one another ("It is not every marriage that one would care to become that intimate with") [pp.200–201]. This is prepared by the prior unmoving three-character table scene where "nothing is significant here but what is, apparently routinely, shown" — the camera can do *this* too "if... you have at your disposal... Katharine Hepburn and Judy Holliday" and know how to open the field by placing a witness against a far wall [p.201]. Cukor's implication-beyond-the-frame is to the *sides*, exploiting "the typical region of cinematic narrative implication, in which a definite visible something is explicitly denied our view" — providing cliché devices for suspense (the camera narrowing on a listening face, widening the area from which something may pounce) or for "cowardly or tactful withdrawals" from sex or violence. In "our freedom," we have grown so suspicious of this mode that any avoidance of the visually explicit may be felt as cowardice — which Cavell takes to be under discussion in **Hitchcock's *Frenzy***, whose long back-tracking camera recoils from implied violence after a door is shut in its face (implication beyond the *blocked horizon*, not the sides), the "horrified wittiness" capped when Hitchcock later shows "practically nothing at all" of the event, again happening beyond/below the frame [p.201; cited at n.61]. His suggestion, once more: composition's significance "can be assessed only on the basis of what it is a composition *of* — in movies, that it is one of a succession of automatic world projections" [p.201]. For "composition" to carry anything like its painterly weight, "the various degrees of motion in the succession of projections will have to be stilled" — camera and subject motions arrested — yet this remains "the merest of analogies," since the current of frames through the projector cannot be stilled (except for analysis); the *liveness* of a motionless camera on a motionless subject is wholly different in significance from the stillness of a still depiction ("trivially declared on film by the possibility of freeze frames") [pp.201–202]. The liveness itself "suggests that what we are shown on the screen is always only one of an endless number of equally possible views, that nothing the camera does can break out of the circle of viewing; we are always outside; there is no perfect, or most significant, view" [p.202]. This is one meaning of the **360-degree track around the embracing couple in Hitchcock's *Vertigo***: we see them from every angle (at a head-high height) and the effect is "to accept as final their privacy with one another" [p.202].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "...the screen works as much by what it excludes as by what it includes... it functions less to frame than to mask..." [p.200]
- "...the frame of a film as forming its content not the way borders or outlines form, but rather the way looms or molds form." [p.200]
- "...here it projects an intimacy which does not require visible presence." [p.200]
- "...any significance it has can be assessed only on the basis of what it is a composition of — in movies, that it is one of a succession of automatic world projections." [p.201]
- "...nothing the camera does can break out of the circle of viewing; we are always outside; there is no perfect, or most significant, view." [p.202]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Bazin — the screen as mask, not frame; Cukor's *Adam's Rib* (Tracy/Hepburn empty-bedroom scene; the Hepburn/Holliday table scene) — the frankest acknowledgment of off-frame implication and intimacy without visible presence; Hitchcock's *Frenzy* — back-tracking recoil, implication beyond the blocked horizon, suspicion of the non-explicit as cowardice; Hitchcock's *Vertigo* (360° track) — the outsideness of every view, privacy accepted as final.
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *frame as loom/mold (vs. border/outline)* — the cinematic frame masks/segments the world rather than bounding a composition. *the circle of viewing* — the camera is always *outside*, every shot is one of endlessly many possible views, with no privileged/complete view. *liveness* (of a motionless camera/subject) — ontologically distinct from a still depiction because the strip keeps running.

---

#### Segment: The contingency of the angle — *Vertigo* as the end of Romance, Dreyer's profile [pp. 202–205]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** He develops "outsideness/contingency of viewing" into a reading of madness and Romance (*Vertigo*) and of the single view's arbitrariness (Dreyer), while warning that any general significance of an automatism (e.g., the 360° track) must be discovered empirically.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** This meaning is "a function of *this* 360 degree track, in *this* context, in *this* film." All 360° tracks doubtless enforce some shared range of significance, but any such commonality "can only be discovered empirically, after the critical analysis of all individual 360 degree tracks" — parenthetically, not literally all, but "a goodly corpus... All that matter... all that native viewers accept as significant. Enough to guide the empirical discovery of the *a priori*." He defends this aspiration: "Criticism, as part of the philosophy of art, must aspire to philosophy. Its goal is the native view; the desophisticated" [p.202]. In *Vertigo* the circling track "cocoons the couple within the man's fantasy, which the woman cannot share but is drawn to realize"; world, couple, and man each circle — "a compounding which befits a climate and climax of vertigo" [p.202]. The man's images are "neither really seen nor merely imagined nor simply remembered": they are "projections and successions of the reality he enacts," invoking the status of film images "but simultaneously violat[ing] this status" — not from a position outside the world with a shareable perspective, not implying the world as a whole, but selecting fragments "whose implication is for him alone." Not meaningless but, "as in madness, destroy[ing] the distinction between meaning and loss of meaning, between possibility and impossibility," thereby declaring "this woman's reality for this man, who invokes and violates her presence" [p.203]. If one thinks of Romance (e.g., *The Winter's Tale*) as "the satisfaction of impossible yet unappeasable human wishes," hence as defining "a presiding wish of movies generally," then *Vertigo* is "a declaration of the end of Romance" — joined by **Rules of the Game, The Children of Paradise, The Earrings of Madame de…, and The Birds** [n.62]. The incompleteness/outsideness/contingency of the viewing angle is "most perspicuously declared... in the work of **Carl Dreyer**" [p.203]. In **The Passion of Joan of Arc**, whose subject "is so much the human face," the *profile* is given special significance: judges in profile are "revealed as conspirators," whispering, hiding expressions; whereas in profile Joan is "most lucid to herself," becomes "the accuser of her accusers," and in profile we last glimpse her Passion at the stake. The general implication: "one view of a face eclipses all others, and... we can be given only one at a time." (A mirror does not defeat this — doubling distracts from either view and may show "that two are just as far from completeness"; the mirror's particular significance arises only case by case.) Because the film is a masterpiece, analyzing it "should result in an analysis of the possibilities of film itself" [pp.203–204].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Criticism, as part of the philosophy of art, must aspire to philosophy. Its goal is the native view; the desophisticated." [p.202]
- "They are projections and successions of the reality he enacts. They invoke the status of film images. But they simultaneously violate this status." [p.203]
- "...as in madness, destroy the distinction between meaning and loss of meaning, between possibility and impossibility." [p.203]
- "...one might think of Vertigo as a declaration of the end of Romance." [p.203]
- "...one view of a face eclipses all others, and... we can be given only one at a time." [pp.203–204]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** *Vertigo* — fantasy that invokes and violates the status of film images; the end of Romance. *The Winter's Tale* — Romance as satisfaction of impossible/unappeasable wishes, paradigm of a presiding wish of movies. *Rules of the Game, The Children of Paradise, The Earrings of Madame de…, The Birds* — further "end of Romance." Dreyer, *The Passion of Joan of Arc* — the profile and the single-view eclipse of the face.
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *empirical discovery of the a priori* — common significance of an automatism is found only after criticism of the corpus of its actual instances; the "native"/"desophisticated" view is criticism's goal. *the end of Romance* — films declaring the exhaustion/impossibility of the wish-satisfying Romance form that otherwise presides over movies.

---

#### Segment: Is film a language? Idioms, not grammar; Dreyer's *Gertrud* and the opacity of self-consciousness [pp. 204–207]
