**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** He addresses how one identifies an "element" of cinematic significance, denying film a lexicon/grammar and proposing the camera as a machine for generating *idioms*; then reads *Gertrud* as the limit-case analogy to painting and develops "opacity vs. lucidity of self-consciousness," extending his earlier praise of the courtesan figures.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Another element of *Joan* is "zoning the screen space" — the left side, especially the upper-left quadrant, "zoned for special intensities of mood" [p.204]. But how does one know what a cinematic "element" is? "You do not know in advance of critical analysis. Nothing — no variation or combination of angle, distance, duration, composition, and motions between — can be ruled out in advance as a sign of significance." Nothing counts as a "nonsense syllable" or "ungrammatical sequence" except perhaps after a semantical analysis. From a linguistic view this is backwards: "It suggests that film has neither a lexicon nor a grammar" [p.204]. Then why think of film as a language? "Because the gestures of the camera are, or are supposed to be, significant, and because there is no known end to the significance they may have." If a grammar is "a machine for generating sentences," one may speak of "the camera and its film as a machine for generating idioms"; when one of its proposals is "in fact used significantly," you get "a specification of the cinematic... not as that which is grammatical but as that which is idiomatic to film." A "welcome consequence of this undisguised nonsense": one would not think the cinematic conveys significance that cannot be conveyed in another medium, but "a particular significance in this (critically specified) way" [pp.204–205]. **Dreyer's last film, *Gertrud*** (≈35 years after *Joan*), "trades on and refines... the fact of the limitedness, or arbitrariness, of the single view," bringing composition within the moving frame "to its maximum analogy (excluding the use of stills) with the composition in paintings": the placed camera is "stilled for long minutes," about as still as a camera following speaking, listening humans can be, while the characters are "about as still as speaking, listening human beings can be." They "pose and repose themselves against a total, simplified environment, as for a painting or a still portrait," yet the result is "not the commemorative or candid significance of paintings or still photographs, but the opacity of self-consciousness" [p.205]. The emotion conveyed is "of theatricality," its cause lying not in the drama of words/gestures but in "our sense that they are characters before themselves, that our views of them are their views of themselves." Their awareness "is of being watched from inside. After God, they are God to themselves. Our views of them come after the fact." This is "one form, of the life of the mind, or the life of the love of the mind"; the idea of love is "itself eroticized," so faithlessness becomes "faithlessness to the idea, i.e., a heresy" [p.205]. Cavell names this "the opacity of self-consciousness," to distinguish it from "the lucidity of self-consciousness" — "the capacity to exist for others, to acknowledge and accept the limitedness of the others' views of oneself" — most perfectly expressed (as in his book) by "the greatest of the courtesan figures of film, Garbo and Dietrich; to them I would now add, in all seriousness, Mae West" [p.205]. *Gertrud* raises the idea that "the condition of privacy, of unknownness, of being viewed — the human condition — is itself the condition of martyrdom, the openness to interrogation and rejection"; the question is whether *refusing* this condition causes greater suffering than martyrdom itself — "whether martyrdom is as necessarily the cause as it is the effect of intolerance" [pp.205–206]. The characters' constriction (condemned to the same views of themselves we are given) produces a sense "that it is *they* who are selecting these views, as if their spiritual energies are exhausted in the effort to find some perfect or complete view of themselves" [p.206]. By contrast, the courtesan figures' freedom "lies in their indifference to such a question... not so much that they are invulnerable as that there is no interrogation or rejection whose terms they cannot dictate, or afford. They may be taken, but never unawares" [p.206].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Nothing... can be ruled out in advance as a sign of significance." [p.204]
- "It suggests that film has neither a lexicon nor a grammar." [p.204]
- "...one will wish to speak of the camera and its film as a machine for generating idioms." [p.204]
- "...not the commemorative or candid significance of paintings or still photographs, but the opacity of self-consciousness." [p.205]
- "...our views of them are their views of themselves... their awareness is of being watched from inside. After God, they are God to themselves." [p.205]
- "...the lucidity of self-consciousness, the capacity to exist for others, to acknowledge and accept the limitedness of the others' views of oneself..." [p.205]
- "They may be taken, but never unawares." [p.206]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Dreyer's *Joan* (upper-left zoning) and *Gertrud* (maximum stillness-analogy to painting; opacity of self-consciousness; love eroticized into idea, faithlessness as heresy); Garbo, Dietrich, and now Mae West — the courtesan figures embodying the *lucidity* of self-consciousness (acknowledgment of others' limited views).
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *idiom (vs. grammar)* — film has no lexicon/grammar; the camera generates idioms, and the "cinematic" is what is critically shown to be idiomatic, conveying a particular significance *in this way*, not the otherwise-inexpressible. *opacity of self-consciousness* — characters who are "God to themselves," watched from inside, our views coming after theirs (theatricality). *lucidity of self-consciousness* — the capacity to exist for others and to acknowledge/accept the limitedness of others' views (acknowledgment).

---

#### Segment: A typology of the courtesan figures — Garbo, Dietrich, West [pp. 206–207]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** A self-standing comparative meditation (added in 1979) elaborating the three figures' distinct "individualities," i.e., their distinct handlings of authority over intrusion and yielding.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Defining their individualities "would require defining their individual handlings of their authority over intrusion, settling the terms upon which they yield." Each has her own relation "to solitude, to dominance, to sensuality, to gazes, to reputation, to laughter, to innocence" — together (thinking of the thirties) they "triangulate the classical possibilities outside of marriage" [p.206]. **Garbo** is the human female "at her most private," **West** "at her most public" (the extreme terms reversible), **Dietrich** "awaiting both — vulnerable to change, to mutual knowledge," at home on the stage but prepared to give it up. Change for Garbo is "world-changing mood, or death"; for West, "repetition." West's songs "are not confessions and not addressed to an unknown one, but are part of her instruction... collect[ing] the options at her disposal." Triadic contrasts follow: "West mocks the glamour of the others; Garbo mocks the familiarity of the others; Dietrich mocks the rigidity of the others, permitting herself indignity if she chooses." "Garbo suffers the solitude that is not overcome by love, Dietrich the solitude that is" [pp.206–207]. Neither Garbo nor West "have secrets" — Garbo because "her secrets are the world's," West because "the world has no secrets." Neither is "subject to gazes" — Garbo "because she is beyond them and determines them, West because she is before them and disarms them." Unlike Dietrich, neither has rivals. "Dietrich's perversity declares her privacy and her willingness to share her tastes," her donning of the man's hat "not an offer of dominance but an act of identification... a term of equality." Their modes of yielding differ: "West yields for the moment, out of interest or sympathy; Dietrich out of trust and for as long as it lasts; Garbo for the yielding itself, and forever." Closing epigram: "West suggests and invites; Garbo recognizes and commands; Dietrich looks and, if she likes, leaps" [p.207].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "They seem... to triangulate the classical possibilities outside of marriage." [p.206]
- "Garbo suffers the solitude that is not overcome by love, Dietrich the solitude that is." [pp.206–207]
- "Garbo because she is beyond them and determines them, West because she is before them and disarms them." [p.207]
- "Her donning of the man's hat is not an offer of dominance but an act of identification. It is a term of equality." [p.207]
- "West suggests and invites; Garbo recognizes and commands; Dietrich looks and, if she likes, leaps." [p.207]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Garbo (privacy, solitude unredeemed by love, beyond gazes), Dietrich (mutual knowledge, the man's hat as identification/equality, solitude overcome by love), Mae West (publicity, songs as instruction, before gazes and disarming them).
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *authority over intrusion / terms of yielding* — the individuality of these figures is constituted by how each dictates the terms on which she may be approached, known, and yielded to.

---

#### Segment: Dreyer's stillness and the rethinking of Eisensteinian montage [pp. 207–209]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** He uses Dreyer's "cinematic stillness" to invert the usual priority of montage: the significance of Eisenstein's montage is grounded not in juxtaposition but in the static, frame-unifying individual image — leading to the claim that montage may be necessary only relative to a certain *species* of frame, and that Eisenstein's theory partly answers to pedagogy.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Dreyer's power with "the stasis of the frame" suggests that one significance of **Eisensteinian montage** lies "fundamentally not in the juxtaposition and counterpoint of images but in the fact which precedes that juxtaposition... viz., that it demands, and is demanded by, individual images which are themselves static" or which compound simple/cumulative movements (the sea, a tremendous construction, a crowd) and which "unify the entire reach of the frame, left to right and bottom to top" [p.207]. If the **Odessa steps** design of light and shadow is "less significant than the fact that this design fills and simplifies the entire frame," then the sense conveyed may be "that any pose of nature or society is arbitrary and subject to human change, that no event is humanly ungraspable, and that none can determine the meaning that human beings who can grasp it are free to place upon it" [pp.207–208]. Suppose this significance is destroyed if the content is "budged from just this frame," and that how long the frame must be held is "determined by just this reach" (analogy: how large a series of canvases must be may be set by "the reach of a continuous hue"). Then the necessity to cut, "discontinuously, to another frame," is determined "not by any inherent necessity of montage for film narrative but by the necessities of this species of frame." It follows that "montage is necessary to film narrative only on the assumption that a certain species of frame is necessary" — clearly subject to experimentation [p.208]. Crucially, a favored form of experiment (taking a strip — the Odessa steps — and rearranging its shots) "cannot be tested" for the deeper question: "what it is about certain species of frames that requires stasis and discontinuity; there is no continuous material of the right kind to contrast with what is on this strip" [p.208]. If he is right, this "suggests itself that Eisenstein's theories of montage are responses as much to the giving of instruction in film making as they are to film making itself" — not surprising, given his remarks on the New Criticism (opening of "The Avoidance of Love") and ordinary language philosophy (close of "Austin at Criticism") in *Must We Mean What We Say?* Because good instruction has goals beyond itself, internally related to its subject's goals, the moral is "not that the methodical pursuit of an art is fruitless, but that the pursuit of no one method will yield everything" — while "the richness of Eisenstein's observations equally suggests that the pursuit of no method will yield nothing" [pp.208–209].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "...not in the juxtaposition and counterpoint of images but in the fact which precedes that juxtaposition... individual images which are themselves static..." [p.207]
- "...any pose of nature or society is arbitrary and subject to human change, that no event is humanly ungraspable..." [pp.207–208]
- "...montage is necessary to film narrative only on the assumption that a certain species of frame is necessary." [p.208]
- "...Eisenstein's theories of montage are responses as much to the giving of instruction in film making as they are to film making itself." [p.208]
- "...the pursuit of no one method will yield everything... the pursuit of no method will yield nothing." [pp.208–209]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Eisenstein / the Odessa steps (*Battleship Potemkin*) — montage re-grounded in the static, frame-unifying image; the rearranged-strip experiment as unable to test the prior question of why certain frames demand stasis/discontinuity. Cavell's own *Must We Mean What We Say?* ("The Avoidance of Love," "Austin at Criticism") — instruction's goals shaping theory.
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *species of frame* — a kind of frame whose self-sufficient reach (filling/simplifying the whole frame) determines its required duration and the necessity (or not) of the cut; montage is contingent on a frame-species, not absolutely necessary to narrative.

---

#### Segment: Fictionality, type, and the non-existence of the screened world; the mythological vs. the fictional [pp. 209–213]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** The third and "most blatant" objection — that movie events are *fictional* and so cannot be "real." He concedes his book says little about cinematic dramaturgy, defends that omission, and argues that film's dramatic mode is better called *mythological* than fictional, re-glossing his paradoxes of pastness, presentness, absence, and the experience of contingency.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** The objection: appeal to reality "neglects the fact that the events of movies are fictional," so they cannot from the beginning be real [p.209]. His book contains "next to nothing about the specific problems of cinematic narration or dramaturgy," justified by the sense that dramaturgy "can only correctly be investigated subsequent to an investigation of the medium of cinema itself." That movies are fictional is not what distinguishes them from operas, plays, novels, etc.; discussing a film's dramaturgy apart from the medium's specific possibilities is "equivalent... to discussing the dramaturgy of an opera apart from the nature and structure of its music" [p.209]. Two preoccupations of his book bear on fiction. (1) The idea of a **type** is "a way of grasping the particular ways in which the human being is fictionalized — which is to say, molded — by film"; the creation of a fictional presence (like the presence of nature) "is not an achievement of the medium of film... but is given by the medium itself" (perhaps also true of puppet/marionette theater) [pp.209–210]. (2) Speaking of "the non-existence of the screened world" is "a way of characterizing part of what is meant by fictionality" — though he does not equate non-existence with "the particular artifice of fiction" (partly because he does not know what that artifice is, partly because his intuition is "that fictionality does not describe the narrative or dramatic mode of film": there are "broad hints... that the mode is more closely bound to the mythological than it is to the fictional") [p.210]. His "formal reasons" turn on "my obsession with the particular mode of presence of the figures on the screen and the particular mode of absence from them of their audience." "Pastness threads through" his books, as do "presentness and... futurity"; he does *not* say this is "the past," or history, but he does say that in viewing a movie "I am 'present... at something that has happened' (p. 26)" — contrasting theater and movie-house audiences, both "present at fictions" [pp.210–211]. Saying the theater audience is "present at something happening" does not imply the stage events take place "in real life" or are set in the present; just so the movie audience "present at something that has happened" does not imply the screen events took place in real life or are set in the past. At "second glance" the words "imply an impossibility: How can one be present at something that has happened, that is over?" — a question equally for fictional or historical events, "the contrast of fiction and history is irrelevant here" [p.211]. (Parenthesis: one can have dreams and hallucinations, but it makes no sense "to speak of being present at dreams or at hallucinations" — "why it is wrong to think of movies in terms of dreams or hallucinations.") To be present at something over "is not to state a falsehood but, at best, to utter a paradox," at worst an "unilluminating or unnecessary paradox"; its illumination/necessity depends on whether the experience is really expressed by it, has the attributed significance, and cannot be better expressed — and on whether it "accurately and memorably encodes" his elicited concepts ("our displacement from the world," "the significance of viewing and exhibition," "the absence and presence of actors and audience") [p.211]. His closing book-sentences ("The actors are there... in your world, but... you cannot go there now.... In a movie house, the barrier to the stars is time") were meant "symbolically, as mythological descriptions of the state of someone in the grip of a movie," not literally; "Not just common sense rules this out, but my repeated emphasis on... the projected world's not existing... my absence from the world, as its being complete without me" — notions meant "(of course, mythologically) to correct or explain... what is wrong and what is right in the idea of the pastness of the projected world," which he relates "most immediately to my passiveness before the exhibition of the world, to the fascination, the uncanniness, in this chance to view the manifestation of the world as a whole" [pp.211–212]. The "barrier to the stars" sentence is preceded by relating the movie house to **Plato's Cave**: presenting yourself to these events "cannot be a matter of walking out and literally going somewhere else." "There is no time for me but the time I am in and there is no specific elsewhere to go" [p.212]. To the imagined objection (why deny what common sense already denies?): "Because common sense is, and ought to be, threatened and questioned by the experience of film itself; and because the nature of our absence from the events on the screen is not the same as the nature of our absence from an historical event or from the events in a cartoon or in a novel or on the stage; and because the differing natures of our absence are internal to the differing natures of the audiences of the different arts" [p.212]. In the closing pages of his book he noted "a certain anxiety" in the film audience's participation/perception and characterized it as "an experience of my contingency" — which "may express itself mythologically as the contingency that I am not there — as though my absence requires an explanation"; writers who call movies hallucinations or dreams are responding to the sense that such an explanation is required, and "if they are speaking literally they are wrong," and mythologically wrong too (though "it is not easy to say why") [pp.212–213]. They are "responding to the fact that I am seeing things, things not there, experiencing them as overwhelmingly present"; you cannot "snap me out of this mood" by saying it is "the projection of photographs" or that I misuse "seeing," because "you do not realize what the projection of photographs is" — "You might as well tell me that I do not see myself in the mirror but merely see a mirror image of myself" [p.213]. Unmythologically, the sense of contingency expresses "that I am here, that it is my fate to exist and while I exist to be one place rather than any other"; if you reject this, "then one of us is wrong" [p.213].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "...the mode is more closely bound to the mythological than it is to the fictional." [p.210]
- "How can one be present at something that has happened, that is over?... the contrast of fiction and history is irrelevant here." [p.211]
- "...it is wrong to think of movies in terms of dreams or hallucinations." [p.211]
- "There is no time for me but the time I am in and there is no specific elsewhere to go." [p.212]
- "...common sense is, and ought to be, threatened and questioned by the experience of film itself..." [p.212]
- "...I am seeing things, things not there, experiencing them as overwhelmingly present." [p.213]
- "You might as well tell me that I do not see myself in the mirror but merely see a mirror image of myself." [p.213]
- "...it is my fate to exist and while I exist to be one place rather than any other." [p.213]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Plato's Cave — the movie house's geography; no "elsewhere" to walk to. The mirror — analogy defending the reality of what is "seen" in projection. "Writers about film" who invoke dreams/hallucinations — rejected (literally and mythologically). His own *The World Viewed* (p.26; closing "barrier to the stars" passage) — re-read symbolically/mythologically.
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *type* — film's way of molding/fictionalizing the human being; fictional presence given by the medium itself, not achieved within it. *non-existence of the screened world* — part of what "fictionality" means, but not equated with fiction's artifice. *mythological (mode of film)* — film's dramatic mode, bound to presence/absence, pastness/presentness; its paradoxes are "mythological descriptions" of the viewer's state, not literal claims. *experience of contingency* — the viewer's anxiety that "I am not there" / "I am here"; that my existence-in-one-place is my fate.

---

#### Segment: The myth of film — skepticism, estrangement, anarchism, and the unliveability of democracy [pp. 213–215]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** He gives the *content*-side reasons (beyond the formal) for calling film's mode mythological, linking film to the post-Hegelian/Nietzschean diagnosis of estrangement (skepticism about the external world and other minds), and articulating "the myth of film/movies" as inherently anarchic and as registering democracy's unliveability.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** Beyond glamour/magical resolution/star-association (wishes movies do excite and pander to, "not alone by movies"), movies "also promise us happiness exactly *not* because we are rich or beautiful or perfectly expressive, but because we can tolerate individuality, separateness, and inexpressiveness. In particular, because we can maintain a connection with reality despite our condemnation to viewing it in private" [p.213]. He invokes, without naming, "a couple of unintelligible German philosophers of the early and middle... nineteenth century" speaking of "the human being's estrangement from the world" at the industrial age's establishment **[재구성: 헤겔 그리고/또는 마르크스를 가리키는 우회적 지시 — Cavell은 이름을 대지 않고 '소외(estrangement)' 테제의 발화자로만 환기한다]**, and "a crazy European philologist" who announced "the Death of God" **[재구성: 니체를 가리킴 — 'philologist'와 '신의 죽음'으로 특정되나 이름은 생략됨]** — meaning "an altered relation in which we have placed ourselves to the world as a whole, to nature and to society and to ourselves" [pp.213–214]. **The myth of film**: "that nature survives our treatment of it and its loss of enchantment for us, and that community remains possible even when the authority of society is denied us" [p.214]. "For movies are inherently anarchic." Their "unappeasable appetite for stories of love" is for stories in which love "must find its own community, apart from, but with luck still within, society at large; an enclave within it"; stories in which "society as a whole, and its laws, can no longer provide or deny love." The **myth of movies** "tells not of the founding of society but of a human gathering without natural or divine backing; of society before its securing (as in the Western) or after its collapse (as in the musical or the thirties' comedy...)" [p.214]. It shares "the wish for origins and comprehension" of any myth; "In myth the past is called before us, reenacted, and in its presence we are rededicated"; but on film "the past which is present is pastness or presentness itself, time itself, visually preserved in endless repetition, an eternal return, but thereby removed from the power to preserve us; in particular, powerless to bring us together" [p.214]. The myth of movies "replaces the myth according to which obedience to law... is obedience to the best of myself, hence constitutes my freedom — the myth of democracy"; in replacing it, "it suggests that democracy itself, the sacred image of secular politics, is unliveable" [p.214]. By opposite means "the myth of democracy is found unliveable (at least without prior, visionary transformation of ourselves) in the best of American literature": there one finds "the absence of romance, of the individual woman and man free to consent to one another," unable "to imagine the bearing of a happy family." "It is the land which had refused its full expression to the novel which gave rise to the first dramatic films of major artistic ambition, and to the greatest of the film comedies, which take their form as satires of authority" [pp.214–215]. "The anarchism of movies is already contained in the condition of viewing unseen. For the polis can be affirmed only in present speech, the members live for one another, each explicit, the city gathered within earshot of itself" [p.215].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "...we can maintain a connection with reality despite our condemnation to viewing it in private." [p.213]
- "The myth of film is that nature survives our treatment of it and its loss of enchantment for us, and that community remains possible even when the authority of society is denied us." [p.214]
- "For movies are inherently anarchic." [p.214]
- "...the past which is present is pastness or presentness itself, time itself, visually preserved in endless repetition, an eternal return, but thereby removed from the power to preserve us..." [p.214]
- "...it suggests that democracy itself, the sacred image of secular politics, is unliveable." [p.214]
- "...the polis can be affirmed only in present speech... the city gathered within earshot of itself." [p.215]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** The unnamed "German philosophers" of estrangement **[재구성: 헤겔/마르크스]** and the "philologist" of the Death of God **[재구성: 니체]** — film's myth set against the modern altered relation to the world (the skeptical/estrangement backdrop). The Western (society before its securing); the musical and thirties' comedy (society after its collapse) — genres as forms of the myth. "The best of American literature" — democracy found unliveable, absence of romance, satires of authority.
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *the myth of film / the myth of movies* — film's mythological content: nature's survival of disenchantment, community without social authority, the anarchic enclave of love. *viewing unseen* — the condition (audience absent/invisible) that already contains film's anarchism, opposed to the *polis* of "present speech" and "earshot." *eternal return* (re Nietzsche) — film's endless repetition of time/presentness that cannot preserve or gather us.

---

#### Segment: Utopian vs. anarchic; modernism reconsidered — modernist vs. avant-garde, arrière-garde, experimental film [pp. 215–219]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** He modifies (without retracting) his book's claim about film's *delayed* modernism. He distinguishes "modernist" from "avant-garde," critiques the military-political image of the avant-garde, addresses experimental film, ties "modernism" to a critique of culture, and offers three alternative formulations of film's relation to modernism.
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** The image of a gathering around a leader, meant as "the city within earshot of itself," prompts unanswerable questions ("what city would this be? Who might these individuals be for one another?"), suggesting "film's natural alternative to an anarchic response to social existence is a Utopian one." He poses the choice: "Which is more important, for us to know our society to be just... or for us to know that even in the absence of justice we may enact and satisfy our private need of one another? Which would you rather have, a mind or a brain?" [pp.215–216]. He now modifies "what I said in my book about the delayed encroachment of modernism upon the art of film." Reacting against "empty and prejudicial announcements appointing film as the major modern art," he had insisted film's historical interest lies "in its condition as the last traditional art... the last to find itself pushing itself to its modernist self-questioning." He was "amply tentative," but "it may well be too soon to be so much as tentative about such a question, too soon conceptually as well as too soon artistically" — the art and "the concepts in which one attempts to grasp its behavior, are too chaotic to allow of any such perspective now — except one taken upon the platform of a manifesto, which is not my business" [p.216]. Change in movies "over the past fifteen or twenty years" might be accounted for in two ways he had not considered: "as a repetition, more or less farcical, of film's first and most serious modernist phase... in revolutionary Russia; or (non-exclusively) as the new emergence of experimental film making" [n.63]. He declines to adjudicate, emphasizing adjudication depends "simultaneously" on the value/understanding placed on the objects *and* on "the concept of modernism." He glosses **modernist**: "the work of an artist whose discoveries and declarations of his medium are to be understood as embodying his effort to maintain the continuity of his art with the past of his art, and to invite and bear comparison with the achievements of his past." It is not "everything... advanced or avant-garde"; on the contrary it breaks into the concept of the avant-garde at three points: (i) the implication "that advanced art looks away from the past toward the future"; (ii) "promiscuous attention to any and all claims to advancement," with a tendency to "cede the concept of art altogether," or to cede "the arts as radically distinguished from one another, which is the sensible significance of the 'pure' in art"; (iii) "the military-political image prompting its title," which suggests an art "can advance, or survive, in some way other than through its faithfulness to itself," and that what prompts this is "a synchronized or imminent social advance." Hence: "*Arrière-garde* is more like it. The modernist critic is not penetrating more territory but more time" [pp.216–217]. These grounds might make Eisenstein's work "avant-garde but not... modernist" — which "would not imply that his work is less good" (and Cavell keeps open the art–politics relation: "One may still hope, almost above all, that the dream of the good city will not be lost, without forgetting that it is a dream") [p.217]. The same grounds make certain recent film experiments "inheritors... not of modernism in the other arts, but of (what I called) their modernizings" [p.217]. He admits insufficient knowledge of this work, but stresses "the role of experimentalism in film making is as specific to it as any other of its features," so its significance cannot be assessed "apart from an assessment of the significance of film as such." Two features "beg for assessment": (1) in the novel, music, painting "contemporary with the establishment of the art of film, the major experimentalists have generally proven to be the major artists of their period" — so one can claim this will hold for film too, that the dominant position of famous directors/stars/mass audiences "will ultimately be shown to have been an aberration... caused and maintained by historical, economic forces essentially external to its autonomous development" ("We should be able to convince ourselves of this about a century from now") [pp.217–218]; (2) "Film has from the beginning aroused... lines of experimentation with its physical basis" (the unique role of its technology), and significantly its early experimenters' results "provided devices for particular kinds of comedy, e.g., for animation and for burlesques of magic" [p.218]. The concept of modernism serves "an art that is in battle not particularly against Goliaths... but against false Davids," called for "in specific stages of culture," its application "prophetic" because it distinguishes "those which play at liberation and those which play for it, those which are documents and symptoms of their time and those which are also valid diagnoses and statements of it." This bears on his book "only more or less eccentrically," mostly as he "felt impelled to make the Hollywood case against... bad briefs against it (finding... Davids in Goliaths' clothing)." The concept will "shed what is then unnecessary in its dogmatism" only within "a believable critique of culture... able to schematize the emergence and the features of a stage of culture that calls for such a concept as modernism at all — a stage in which everything and nothing seems to matter... in which diagnosis has replaced dogma, hence institutions have no inside and culture is an unmasquerade; in which art and politics and religion forget themselves, and will covet one another's leavings; in which conscience seems bad manners, faith an indulgence, and nihilism our amusement" [pp.218–219]. He is "prepared to modify my claims about film's modernism" via three options: (a) movies "from their beginning have existed in a state of modernism... have had to achieve their power by deliberate investigations of the powers of their medium"; (b) movies "from their beginning have existed in two states, one modern, one traditional," running parallel/crossing/interweaving; or (c) "the concept of modernism has no clear application to the art of film." None "need weaken my insistence on film as the last traditional art"; each "would be a way of explaining that insistence" [p.219]. He holds to the critical hypothesis running through both book and continuation: "that pride of place within the canon of serious films will be found occupied by those films that most clearly and most deeply discover the powers of the medium itself, those that give fullest significance to the possibilities and necessities of its physical basis. Placing this significance in individual cases is an act of criticism." Perhaps this "hypothesis is more accurately thought of as a definition of... 'the powers of the medium itself'" — "empty apart from placing its significance in individual cases" [p.219].
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "Which would you rather have, a mind or a brain?" [pp.215–216]
- "...its condition as the last traditional art, which means in part the last to find itself pushing itself to its modernist self-questioning." [p.216]
- "...the work of an artist whose discoveries and declarations of his medium are to be understood as embodying his effort to maintain the continuity of his art with the past of his art..." [p.216]
- "Arrière-garde is more like it. The modernist critic is not penetrating more territory but more time." [p.217]
- "...an art that is in battle not particularly against Goliaths... but against false Davids." [p.218]
- "...institutions have no inside and culture is an unmasquerade... conscience seems bad manners, faith an indulgence, and nihilism our amusement." [pp.218–219]
- "...pride of place within the canon of serious films will be found occupied by those films that most clearly and most deeply discover the powers of the medium itself..." [p.219]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Eisenstein / "revolutionary Russia" — film's first serious modernist phase; possibly avant-garde but not modernist. Experimental/"new" film making (post-1960s) — possible inheritor of "modernizings," not of modernism. Early film experimenters (animation, burlesques of magic) — technology's comic yield. The Hollywood case — Cavell's "Davids in Goliaths' clothing" defense of studio film against "bad briefs."
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *modernist (vs. avant-garde)* — discovery/declaration of one's medium to maintain continuity with, and bear comparison to, the art's past — *not* future-facing advance. *arrière-garde* — Cavell's corrective image: the modernist penetrates *time* (the past), not territory. *false Davids / Goliaths* — modernism battles spurious claimants to liberation, not merely the big/establishment. *unmasquerade* — a cultural stage in which institutions "have no inside," diagnosis has replaced dogma. *the powers of the medium itself / its physical basis* — the canon-defining hypothesis (or definition): serious films discover these, placed only by criticism.

---

#### Segment: Concluding act of criticism — *Rules of the Game*, the final tableau, camera as the death of theater [pp. 219–230]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** The essay's culminating, extended close reading — Cavell's promised "act of criticism" on one frame of *Rules of the Game* — which simultaneously demonstrates his method (facts of a frame are inseparable from understanding; questions raised by one frame are not answerable within it) and his thesis about cinema's relation to theater, society, absence, accident, and acknowledgment. This is where the prose essay ENDS (p.230).
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order):** He concludes "with a sketch of such an act of criticism," directed at "accounting for one of its frames" — the final-sequence frame fronting the château's façade (terrace, balustrade, steps, entrance court). The Marquis at the head of the steps gives "a little speech of explanation" about the "deplorable accident" (Schumacher took Jurieu for a poacher), followed by an "isolating closeup," a servant opening the portals, a medium close-up of the homosexual and the general ("A new definition of the word 'accident.'" / "No, no... La Chesnaye has class. That is a rare thing these days"), and finally the camera holding as "shadows of the guests file across the façade," leaving "the looming balustrade, and... a line of cypresses in their brightened tubs; upon which the film ends" [pp.219–220; n.64]. This "summary epilogue" gathers characters and themes; had the film merely copied (rather than absorbed) eighteenth-century comedy, the sequence would beg pardon, celebrate conversion (*The Marriage of Figaro*) or riddance (*Don Giovanni*), and ask the audience's blessing. "Instead, the Marquis speaks alone, in confusion, to, not for the cast; they face him, their backs to us; and they file away from us... as if their production had not been concluded but been interrupted. As if to declare: this production has from the beginning had no audience, none it has not depicted; no standing group of spectators will have known what they were watching" — inspiring "the most public in-joke," the Marquis's "Corneille, stop this farce!" answered by "Which one?" [pp.220–221]. He emphasizes two features of the tableau: (1) **Schumacher's position** — awkward, isolated halfway up the steps between Marquis and audience, gun strapped to his back, which "happens to be pointing exactly at the Marquis' head" ("I mean no more than 'happens to be'"); (2) **the absence of Octave** (Renoir's part) at this moment of general assembly [p.221]. The terrace had been "established for us as a stage" when Octave re-enacted his old conducting teacher (Christine's father); so the Marquis "standing on that spot" gives "some further sort of performance," his château "a piece of theatrical decor" [pp.221–222]. The Marquis "is constructing a particular form of lie" (the gamekeeper "mistook" the guest, whereas the Marquis had earlier *discharged* him for endangering guests) "but in this lie he is telling a truth" (the gamekeeper had in effect shot a poacher on the preserves, acting on the Marquis's behalf). "Telling the truth by lying is, however, a way of defining fiction: the Marquis is... composing a play... or starting a further game." By tacitly reinstating the gamekeeper, the Marquis "places himself at the mercy of the gamekeeper"; by accepting the gun "as the lawful defense of his domain... he has submitted himself to the gun's dominion" [p.222]. After the shoot, the action centers on "the small telescope or eye-piece," of which we learn three things: it is fun/fascinating; "when you look through it, reality is suddenly revealed... in an otherwise unavailable manner"; "it is deadly, it penetrates to the inner life of living creatures." Cavell asserts: "the eye-piece is a sort of figure of speech, or synecdoche of sight, for both a gun and camera" (the wife spying her husband with his former mistress "is already a kind of shooting accident"). "Outdoors, outside, the camera is boss... What the camera has killed, or breached, is the rule of theater. It uses theater, dominates it, locks it indoors. It participates in the subjugation of the Marquis, the ending of his game." For the Marquis "now... all is but toys. He has become the Midas of amusement... and he has laid hands upon himself." But "The camera... is out of his reach; it is behind his audience, witnessing them as well as his performance for them" — "part of what Octave-Renoir's absence... means. He has taken his place behind the camera. His absence declares his responsibility for what has happened... for interfering by exposing it, which is what finally discomfits this comity" [pp.222–223]. He answers a recent critic (who held that Jurieu was "from the beginning fated to be expelled by this rule-intoxicated society," his riddance testifying to the Marquis' game's unbroken power, the film "merely condemn[ing] the Marquis' society"): this "den[ies] the facts and the mood of the frame." Recollecting "who the Marquis is" — his rules — shows he wished poacher-rabbits kept out "but there were limits": "He would not... permit fences"; he fought for his wife "honorably" yet honored "the rights of love and of friendship" and his wife's wish to leave; "Sincerity was as much a point of honor with him as the safety of his guests" [pp.223–224]. The poacher-rabbit was expelled "according to the rules of Schumacher's game — not his game as the Marquis' gamekeeper but his own game of honor ('A shot in the dark... and no questions asked')." Why does the Marquis accept this "foreign rule of honor"? Not because it fulfilled his wishes ("If that were all, there would have been for him no rules in the first place"). "He accepts Schumacher's rule because he is afraid not to, finally unable to confront him and judge him" — reflecting an inability to confront his own guilt, which predates this act: from the first scene with the literal little poacher "the Marquis is afraid of Schumacher," entering a conspiracy with the poacher "to spite Schumacher," so the poacher later feels free to ask "a little service." "The Marquis thus becomes the servant of his servant," then conspires *with* Schumacher, becoming "an accessory against his own authority" (the little poacher's absence from the final tableau signifies the Marquis can no longer protect him) [p.224]. Methodologically: the critic "seemed to me to 'deny the facts'" — but his reading "shows that facts of a frame, so far as these are to confirm critical understanding, are not determinable apart from that understanding itself," and "that questions prompted by one frame are not answerable from within that frame alone." Take the tubbed cypresses: the concluding shot "undeniably emphasizes these tubs," which "gather the intensest patches of light" and "seem to be the source of the light which draws those shadows." Are we to read in this "allusion to footlights" a "final statement of the terrace as an abandoned stage," and "a capping ambiguity about the source of illumination — a question whether it reveals as for an audience in a theater or for an audience in a movie house?" A convincing answer "would have to take its place among the answers to the other questions this film raises about the relations between society and theater and cinema" [pp.224–225]. "That Rules of the Game is interested in theater is about as obvious as that Marx is interested in money": its adoption of traditional French comedy "depicts the social role of theater at its extremest point, the point at which theater and society are absorbing one another, dissolving in one another." He accounts for this perspective "as one achieved through Renoir's declared faithfulness to the perspective of cinema," summarized dialectically: "When society has become fully theatricalized... cinema reestablishes our sense of reality by asserting its own powers of drama" — "more or less what I say... happened historically (pp. 89-94)" [p.225]. He grants the idea is "very obscure" and excuses voicing it so in order "to counter, or question, the familiar claim that 'cinema has changed our ways of looking at the world'" — questioning it generally (the claim "is no less obscure than any claims of my own, merely unquestionably fashionable") and specifically by claiming "cinema entered a world whose ways of looking at itself — its Weltanschauungen — had already changed, as if in preparation for the screening and viewing of film." "Film's presenting of the world by absenting us from it appears as confirmation of something already true of our stage of existence. Its displacement of the world confirms, even explains, our prior estrangement from it. The 'sense of reality' provided on film is the sense of *that* reality, one from which we already sense a distance" [pp.225–226]. "Documentary immediacy, should it be desired, cannot... deny this condition though it may negate it through acknowledging it" — requiring the filmmaker to define "your position in it" and "the position of the audience"; **Godard's *Two or Three Things I Know About Her*** is "a rich exploration" of the former. He develops a parenthetical analysis of **acknowledgment**: registering knowledge "that there is tardiness in the world" is not acknowledgment of *your* being late, nor is registering it "to the company you are leaving rather than to the company you have kept waiting"; "It must not be an excuse; it need not be an apology"; if you wish to acknowledge "something about *us*, then you must define our positions accordingly, e.g., presume an acknowledgment from me of your right to speak for me" — proceeding without it, "rebuking me for my ignorance or my cowardice," you "risk, or seek, my rebuke" and may "achieve dissociation from me... through a betrayal" ("What cause justifies you — not in leaving, but in leaving this way?") [p.226]. He names two further issues any convincing account must settle: (1) the significance of the camera's **frontality/perpendicularity** in the terrace tableau, and (2) the significance of the **danse macabre** that ends the indoor theatrical [p.227]. On (1): frontality (as in *Grand Illusion*) "is the angle of theater, both of its establishment and of its questioning." *Grand Illusion* twice cuts "from full front to full back, across 180 degrees, on a centered figure" — first on Rosenthal (Dalio, "who will play the Marquis"), conveying his "pride in his providence" yet, at his back, "his vulnerability, his humanity, his sincere willingness to please" ("opposes any hurried contrast between theater and sincerity"); second on Carette (the little poacher in *Rules*) during his song, making explicit "the frontal acknowledgment of theater and the camera's declaration... of its absorption and command of theater." In *Rules*, "perpendicularity more openly defines the ascension of cinema over theater," central evidence being "the great tracking shot of the beaters, headed by Schumacher," shot "from the wings," as if Schumacher (hunched, gun on back) "is not so much guiding the action as following it, tracking it, filming it" — a point Cavell expects "to be resisted" ("Must I, as a reader, leave this point to the sheer agreement of others, or can it sensibly be argued?") [pp.227–228]. On (2): the indoor danse macabre, "where the camera is at its freest and the characters spill from the stage into the audience," reads as the film's "way of declaring its cinematic perspective" — the depicted audience "kinaesthetically assaulted," "not quite knowing whether this is or is not a performance," the scene "as pure a realization of the cinema's vaunted 'patterns of light in motion'... as a movie is likely to admit." Yet "we know this dance to be a piece of theater," so it reads "as a parody of a particular theory of cinema": "if kinaesthetic patterns of light and motion is what you want, theater can satisfy you as well as cinema" (a parody of a theory of theater is heard in "Get Corneille to deal with the formalities") [pp.228–229]. The simultaneous "spilling over" of stage and below-stage into the audience — "the simultaneous break-down and break-up of both lively and deadly realms of artifice, forming a chaos of artifice" — exemplifies and comments on the film's theme of "accident" [pp.228–229]. Then the **ontological** climax: in a theater "the actors appear in person... anything can happen to break the spell"; "The abyss between actor and audience is not bottomless, unless convention is bottomless." But "In a movie house, the actors are not present in person and the screen is metaphysically unbreachable; the abyss between actor and audience is as bottomless as time." This does not abolish accident — "everything caught by film is accident, contingency" — but "every accident on film becomes permanent (like the existence of the one world, in the midst of all possible worlds)." "These are consequences of the ontological fact that two screenings of the same film bear a relation to one another absolutely different from the relation borne... by two performances of the same production of a play... a screening of a film is not a performance of it." "There is no essence of the difference between stage and screen. They are essentially different, as like one another, and systematically unlike, as tragedy and comedy, or as two stages of society" [p.229]. It "can seem an accident of culture" that a theater director relinquishes influence over a production "whereas it seems a matter of physical law that the movie director call his work finished and step absolutely aside" — though one could imagine otherwise (audiences invited only to "rehearsals," directors showing only "rushes"). In *Rules*, "the movie director's absolute absence from his work (accented if, per accidens, he appears in it) is fictionalized as Octave's departure from its scene of accident." Following his teacher-reenactment, Octave told Christine of "his longing to have had a public as a 'conductor'"; "As a movie director, absence from the public view is accounted for," so "Octave's departure can declare Renoir's success as a director of movies." Is it also "Renoir's confession that he knows himself to be a public failure as a director of movies? Evidently there is a wave of self-pity here" [pp.229–230]. The essay closes: "as usual in the history of comedy, the clown's self-pity functions within the highest ambitions and achievements of his art: it is the natural tendency of his pity for the world, and his pitilessness towards it, refusing his own exemption from it; it expresses his knowledge of the cost in its falling just to him to embody his displacement from society as society's disfigurement of itself." Specifically, self-pity is "an acknowledgment of the writer's hectic sense, at least since the eighteenth century, of the burden of his art, not knowing whether it is the root or the height of his displacement, nor whether it signifies his greater purity or impurity, measured against the state of his public." Renoir "identifies himself through Octave as both cause and casualty of the accident, and therewith... through Jurieu — the artist as poacher and poached, groundling flyer, a soloist." "The loss of a public is in fact the artist's withdrawal from his public, as a consequence of his faithfulness to his art": the public is lost "because they are readying themselves for war, for life by the gun," and "because of art... because art maintains itself against their assaults, and because, almost against its will, it unsettles the illusions by means of which civilized people conduct themselves. It is in this loving brutality that Renoir declares film's possession of the power of art" [p.230]. **[This sentence is the last line of the prose essay; the Notes begin on p.231.]**
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "...the Marquis speaks alone, in confusion, to, not for the cast; they face him, their backs to us; and they file away from us..." [p.220]
- "Telling the truth by lying is, however, a way of defining fiction..." [p.222]
- "...the eye-piece is a sort of figure of speech, or synecdoche of sight, for both a gun and camera." [p.222]
- "What the camera has killed, or breached, is the rule of theater. It uses theater, dominates it, locks it indoors." [p.223]
- "...facts of a frame, so far as these are to confirm critical understanding, are not determinable apart from that understanding itself." [p.224]
- "That Rules of the Game is interested in theater is about as obvious as that Marx is interested in money." [p.225]
- "When society has become fully theatricalized... cinema reestablishes our sense of reality by asserting its own powers of drama." [p.225]
- "Film's presenting of the world by absenting us from it appears as confirmation of something already true of our stage of existence." [p.226]
- "...cannot... deny this condition though it may negate it through acknowledging it." [p.226]
- "In a movie house, the actors are not present in person and the screen is metaphysically unbreachable; the abyss between actor and audience is as bottomless as time." [p.229]
- "...every accident on film becomes permanent (like the existence of the one world, in the midst of all possible worlds)." [p.229]
- "There is no essence of the difference between stage and screen." [p.229]
- "...a screening of a film is not a performance of it." [p.229]
- "It is in this loving brutality that Renoir declares film's possession of the power of art." [p.230]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Renoir's *Rules of the Game* (the entire reading: final tableau, Marquis' lying-truth, Schumacher's gun, the eye-piece as gun/camera synecdoche, Octave-Renoir's absence behind the camera, the tubbed cypresses/footlights ambiguity, frontality, the danse macabre, the "Corneille, stop this farce!"/"Which one?" in-joke). *The Marriage of Figaro* / *Don Giovanni* — the conventional comic epilogue *Rules* refuses. Dalio (Rosenthal in *Grand Illusion* → the Marquis) and Carette (the song in *Grand Illusion* → the little poacher) — frontality cuts establishing the Dalio/Carette and theater/cinema connections. *Grand Illusion* — frontality as the angle of theater's establishment and questioning. Godard's *Two or Three Things I Know About Her* — documentary immediacy negated-through-acknowledged; defining the filmmaker's position. Marx ("interested in money") — comic analogy for *Rules*' interest in theater.
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *frontality/perpendicularity* — "the angle of theater," used in Renoir to mark cinema's *ascension over* theater (camera behind/witnessing the depicted audience). *synecdoche of sight (eye-piece = gun + camera)* — the telescope figures the camera's deadly, reality-revealing, outdoor mastery. *the screen as metaphysically unbreachable* — the actor–audience abyss "as bottomless as time," unlike theater's convention-bottomed abyss. *accident → permanence* — film records contingency, but every recorded accident becomes permanent (one world among possible worlds); a screening is *not* a performance. *documentary immediacy as negation-through-acknowledgment* — immediacy cannot deny our prior estrangement but can negate it by acknowledging the filmmaker's and audience's positions.

---

#### Segment: Substantive Notes to "More of The World Viewed" [pp. 231–246]
**What Cavell is responding to / doing here:** The endnotes to the essay (book pp.231–246). Most are citations; the following carry independent philosophical/critical weight within Cavell's argument and fall inside the assigned pp.196–246. The **Index begins p.247 and is excluded.**
**Digest (claim-by-claim, in order, by note number):**
- **n.13 (to p.25), on two-dimensionality and screen scale [pp.232–233].** "The most important feature of the screen format remains... its scale, its absolute largeness"; format variation (CinemaScope) is mostly "convenience... and... fashion," though "the declaration of color as such" may have benefited from wider screens. He defends the claim that "the essential ontological difference between the world as it is and as it is screened is that the screened world does not exist" against the objection that the obvious difference is that "the screened world is two-dimensional": "better a real obscurity than a false clarity." "The world which is screened is not [two-dimensional]; its objects and motions are as three-dimensional as ours." The screen-image relation is not the paint-support relation (the support *limits* the painting in two dimensions; the screen does not so limit the images). "Shadows are two-dimensional, but they are cast by three-dimensional objects — tracings of opacity, not gradations of it... phenomenologically the idea of two-dimensionality is an idea of either transparency or outline. Projected images are not shadows; rather... they are *shades*" [pp.232–233].
- **n.21 (to p.41), Dadaism/Surrealism and film [pp.233–234].** Any close-up of an object may render it *trouvé*; Dadaists/surrealists found "direct confirmation" in film's "capacities for nostalgia and free juxtaposition." But this "might equally be taken to show why film made these movements obsolete, as the world has": "Nothing is more surrealist than the ordinary events of the modern world; and nothing less reveals that fact than a surrealist attitude" [pp.233–234].
- **n.29 (to p.49), TV's monstrous-husband image [p.234].** A meditation on the situation-comedy/commercial image of "the husband as a loutish, gullible, lazy, monstrous child" and the cleansing wife — "as if she is asked to cleanse herself and her environment of the evidence that she has produced this monster," in a mutual "bargain" exchanging her freedom for the power to produce monsters [p.234].
- **n.32 (to p.59), the Western hero and the "bad woman" [pp.235–236].** Drawing on **Jean-Pierre Vernant** on Greek tragedy ("two ideals of conduct clash: the hero necessary for the establishment of justice must disappear after its establishment, and because of it"; women possess individual "character" despite lacking citizenship), Cavell maps this onto Westerns: this holds of "the bad woman, not... the good woman." The hero and bad woman are "real to themselves," their feelings strong enough "to judge the world that thinks to size them up"; the impediment to their marriage "is a psychic incestuousness; they are halves of one another," their relation "a serious modern equivalent of the Platonic." Society "cannot produce the hero; he must find its boon outside its limits" [pp.235–236].
- **n.33 (to p.72), *Mission: Impossible* and impersonation of personality [pp.236–237].** Its episodes "contained no suspense at all"; "the narrative had nothing to do with human motivation; the interest lay solely in following out how the gadgets would act. They were the protagonists." The format's disguises "disguised the fact that these characters were already aliens, disguised as humans." A note on the contingency of his examples: in the past tense, "it is now quite without interest" [pp.236–237].
- **n.37 (to p.105), movies and painting [pp.237–238].** No investigation of specific painters influencing films "is likely to bear much weight until we have an internal history of the relation of photography and painting generally." The "direct inaccessibility of the powers of painting to the powers of movies" is "betrayed in the pretentious ballet which ends *An American in Paris*," whose life-size imitations of Renoir/Utrillo/Lautrec reaffirm "that the technological accomplishments money can buy are unlimited, and that the emotional range of this accomplishment may be roughly that of a cheap gag" [pp.237–238].
- **n.38 (to p.105), "media," and Kuhn's "paradigm" [pp.238–239].** His use of "media" (vs. "paradigm," "formula," "schema") aims to (1) characterize modernist art (the search for a medium replaces the search for a style); (2) characterize the continuity between a modernist art and its past; (3) emphasize "the individual fates and faiths of the individual arts." **Kuhn's "paradigm"** is pertinent in two respects: revolutionary change "may result from conservative motives, from a necessity to conserve the identity of a community's enterprise"; and phenomenologically the new authority "will not present itself... as innovation, but as rededication" ("The Reformation was in the work of a faithful, initially reluctant, priest"). It also goes "against the grain of certain ecumenical ideas about 'the unity of science' and 'the unity of the arts'": an exemplary art "cannot be joined on its high ground... by aping... the paradigms it has won for itself, but only by taking inspiration from that separate realization of human seriousness... to discover the necessities of one's own concern" [pp.238–239].
- **n.40 (to p.110), acknowledgment, presentness, theatricality — Cavell and Michael Fried [p.239].** A crucial methodological/biographical note: "the concept of acknowledgment is immediately related to issues of presentness, and of theatricality, in aesthetic, epistemological, and theological contexts." The concept first showed its significance to Cavell "in thinking about our knowledge of other minds, in such a way as to show... modern philosophy neither defeating nor defeated by skepticism." It showed its significance to **Fried** "in characterizing the medium or enterprise of the art of painting... as the continuation of that art." "Because of this disparity in origin, the resulting confluence of concepts struck me (struck us both...) as something more than a confirmation... It was a provocation to further study" [p.239]. (This note ties the essay's whole vocabulary of acknowledgment/presentness/theatricality explicitly to the problem of skepticism about other minds.)
- **n.42 (to p.113), style vs. medium [pp.239–240].** "To speak of modernist painting as the International Style is, at best, empty." "Creating or adopting a style is, in modernist art, replaced by discovering a new medium, or failing to." "A medium of painting is not a manner in which it is made, but its unearthing." "What each is doing can only be known in finding what each is acknowledging. Two or more may acknowledge flatness, but the force of revelation lies exactly in its being *this* which reveals it." Minimalists/literalists, by contrast, "can be said to share or deal in a style," "because of the way emotion is handled, in particular its mass conversion into mood": "we work our contempt and fear of the present into a nostalgia directed to the future" [pp.239–240].
- **n.52 (to p.140), news vs. story; *Citizen Kane* [pp.242–243].** "The teller of news must let what happens dictate what is worth telling... The novelist must let himself dictate what is worth telling." Welles's imitation *March of Time* in *Citizen Kane* "accounts... for the untrue ending of the film": "Rosebud in flames is the March of Time's conclusion... not the conclusion of Welles's film." Depriving us of the meaning until the end "reduces the suspense to a gimmick" [pp.242–243].
- **n.56 (to p.152), Welles's and Tracy's voices [p.244].** Isolation as Welles's invariable projection — "an air of inner preoccupation, together with that voice." Radio's lyricism (the voice *as* the character) reaches its greatest use "in Polonsky's *Force of Evil*"; "The American star best at" listening/being penetrated by a word "was Spencer Tracy" [p.244].
- **n.60 (to p.157), the arts and attention [pp.244–245].** "Music also exercises an absolute control of our attention... Painting allows attention an absolute freedom... The novel can neither command absolute control nor afford absolute freedom; it operates in the weave between them, as lives do" [pp.244–245].
- **n.62 (to p.203), the end of Romance and Malick's *Badlands* [pp.245–246].** The longest substantive note. The terms of "the end of romance" plus "secular mysteries" — especially "the mismatch between the depth to which an ordinary human life requires expression and the surface of ordinary means through which that life must express itself" — describe **Terrence Malick's *Badlands***, "a film that invokes and deserves the medium's great and natural power for giving expression to the inexpressive" (from Chaplin/Keaton's "enforced social silence" to Bogart/Cooper's "enforceable personal silence, or reserve"). A meditation on expression follows: "in the end something must be said for our lives. We are saying something now, always, or allowing it to be said"; the drug-store card stands for surrogate sentiment. He poses the film's questions: "In what spirit does the killer in Badlands say that he 'has a lot to say'?" while Malick shows "the boy unable even to fill a sixty-second recording in a vandalized Record-Your-Own-Voice booth," and "the girl entrust[ing] the narration of her life to the rack of phrases picked from magazine shelves." He hears a resemblance between "this girl's voice and the occasional voices... taped from some place of violent secession" — citing "Patricia Hearst's message of June 1974" and (a "not entirely unrelated dissociation") "Nixon's farewell speech... August 9, 1974." Closing: "To whom, from where, does one address a letter to the world? To what end does one wish to leave one's mark upon the world?" [pp.245–246].
- **n.63 (to p.216) [p.246]:** an accommodation with **Annette Michelson**'s "Film and the Radical Aspiration" (in Mast & Cohen, *Film Theory and Criticism*, 1974) — the experimental-film alternative he declined to adjudicate.
- **n.61 (to p.201) [p.245]:** credits **William Rothman**'s Harvard dissertation (1973) on the shown/unshown in Hitchcock, whose comments on a late draft caused Cavell "to correct or qualify or expand" at half a dozen points.
**Key verbatim quotes:**
- "...phenomenologically the idea of two-dimensionality is an idea of either transparency or outline. Projected images are not shadows; rather... they are shades." [p.233]
- "Nothing is more surrealist than the ordinary events of the modern world; and nothing less reveals that fact than a surrealist attitude." [p.234]
- "...revolutionary change... may result from conservative motives... this new authority will not present itself... as innovation, but as rededication." [p.238]
- "...the concept of acknowledgment is immediately related to issues of presentness, and of theatricality, in aesthetic, epistemological, and theological contexts." [p.239]
- "...modern philosophy neither defeating nor defeated by skepticism." [p.239]
- "A medium of painting is not a manner in which it is made, but its unearthing." [p.240]
- "What each is doing can only be known in finding what each is acknowledging." [p.240]
- "...the medium's great and natural power for giving expression to the inexpressive..." [p.245]
- "...in the end something must be said for our lives. We are saying something now, always, or allowing it to be said." [p.245]
- "To what end does one wish to leave one's mark upon the world?" [p.246]
**Examples / films / persons invoked:** Jean-Pierre Vernant (Greek tragedy → the Western hero/bad woman); Thomas Kuhn (paradigm → medium/rededication); Michael Fried (acknowledgment, presentness, theatricality; flatness; medium-as-unearthing); *An American in Paris* (painting's inaccessibility to film); *Mission: Impossible* and *The Man From U.N.C.L.E.* (impersonation of personality); *Citizen Kane* / Welles's *March of Time* (news vs. story; the untrue ending); Polonsky's *Force of Evil* and Spencer Tracy (radio lyricism / the listening voice); Malick's *Badlands* (expression of the inexpressive; the end of Romance), with Chaplin, Keaton, Bogart, Cooper; Patricia Hearst and Nixon (dissociated "letters to the world"); Annette Michelson and William Rothman (interlocutors).
**Technical vocabulary clarified/introduced:** *shades (not shadows)* — projected images are not two-dimensional tracings of opacity but "shades"; the screened world is three-dimensional and *non-existent*, not flat. *medium as unearthing (vs. style)* — modernism replaces choosing a style with discovering/unearthing a medium; what an artist does is known by *what he acknowledges*. *paradigm/rededication* — revolutionary change from conservative motives, experienced as rededication not innovation. *expression of the inexpressive* — film's "great and natural power" to give expression to silence/reserve (Chaplin→Cooper), the frame for the *Badlands* reading.

---

---

## 4. Argument map / logical skeleton (for quick recall)

1. **Method.** Film's *nature* and its *importance* are one question [pp.3–4]; the data is one's own remembered experience of movies; the only instruments for a theory of film are "the procedures of criticism" [p.12]; element and significance are mutually constituted — **the cinematic circle** [pp.xiii–xiv].
2. **Datum.** Movies were "naturally" important and once drew high and low to the same screen; film is "the one live traditional art" — it long *escaped* modernism [pp.4–6, 15].
3. **Ontological start.** The medium's basis is photographic; "a photograph is of reality" [p.16]. Reframe: **what happens to reality when it is projected and screened?** [p.16].
4. **Photograph ≠ recording ≠ painting.** A photograph does not "reproduce a sight" the way a record reproduces a sound (objects "don't make sights") [pp.20–21]; and "a photograph is **of** the world" whereas "a painting **is** a world" [p.24].
5. **Automatism (mechanism).** Photographs are *manufactured*; photography "overcame subjectivity … by removing the human agent from the task of reproduction" [p.23] — it *bypasses* the modern isolation that painting must *acknowledge*.
6. **Presentness + absence.** Photography "maintains the presentness of the world by accepting our absence from it"; a world present to me to which I am not present is "a world past" [p.23]. The **screen screens** (hides me from the world, and the world's existence from me); "Existence is not a predicate" [p.24].
7. **The human on screen.** The audience is **mechanically absent**; the screen performer is not an *actor* working into a role but a **type/star** the medium *projects* — "the rest is nonexistent" [chs. 4–5].
8. **Myths and media.** Film realized itself not in *works* but in **media** — cycles, genres, types — that satisfied inherited **myths** (the Woman, the Military Man, the Dandy/Western hero); "a cycle is a genre; and a genre is a medium" [p.36]; film magically satisfies Bazin's "myth of total cinema" by letting us "view the world unseen" [pp.39–40].
9. **End of the myths → modernism.** The classical media no longer "naturally establish conviction in our presentness to the world"; film enters "the modernist environment" of the other arts [p.60].
10. **Medium vs. media.** The medium of film = its material basis, **"a succession of automatic world projections"** [≈p.72]; its *media* are the discovered arrangements through which it has been realized — not given but achieved.
11. **Automatism, two senses (the pivot).** The *photographic* automatism and the *modernist* automatism (an inherited convention) are fused: film was **given** an automatism; modernism is the loss of given automatisms and the task of inventing new ones [ch. 14].
12. **Excursus — modernist painting.** Via Greenberg/Fried (flatness, the support, **acknowledgment**, **presentness**), modernism makes the *conditions* of an art into the art's *subject*; "acknowledgment" is "the home of knowledge" [ch. 15].
13. **Self-reference → acknowledgment.** Film *escapes* modernism's "exhibition" because it is screened, not exhibited; genuine modernism is not *self-reference* (exhibiting the self) but **acknowledgment** — an act admitting the existence of others [ch. 16].
14. **The camera's implication.** The camera is always "the one … left out of the picture: the one working now" [p.126]; modernist film must acknowledge the camera's **outsideness** and the viewer's absence — the cinematic figure of skepticism [ch. 17].
15. **Assertions in techniques.** A device becomes an *assertion* only through the **integrity** (not the technical *integration*) of the whole work: "nothing is a 'possibility of a medium' unless its use gives it significance" [ch. 18].
16. **The acknowledgment of silence.** Film's deepest condition to acknowledge is **silence**; "a silent movie has never been made" (silent film projected *a world of silence*, not a deficient sound film); synchronization redefined as the criterion of *poetry* [ch. 19].
17. **Re-statement (1979).** Reality's role is **photographed/projected/screened/exhibited/viewed**, not "reality as such"; film's dramatic mode is **mythological**, not merely *fictional*; film is **a moving image of skepticism** [≈pp.183–189]; modernism re-described (modernist ≠ avant-garde; the modernist penetrates *time*/the past — an *arrière-garde*) [≈pp.215–219]; closing **act of criticism** on one frame of *Rules of the Game* — "the camera is the death of theater," and Renoir's "loving brutality" "declares film's possession of the power of art" [≈pp.219–230].

---

## 5. Master glossary of Cavell's vocabulary

*(Consolidated; where a term develops across chapters the entry tracks the development. Cavell's terms are notoriously context-bound — treat these as orienting, not as fixed definitions.)*

- **Ontology of film** — the inquiry into what film and its constituents (photograph, screen, projection, the screened human) *are*; for Cavell inseparable from why film matters.
- **A photograph is *of* the world / a painting *is* a world** — the governing contrast: a painting's world is self-limiting at its frame; a photograph is *of* the single, continuous reality, so questions about what lies behind/beyond it have answers in reality [p.24].
- **Automatism / mechanism (photographic sense)** — the "inescapable fact" that photographs are *manufactured*, the human agent removed from reproduction; how photography "overcame subjectivity" [pp.21–23].
- **Automatism (modernist sense)** — *an* automatism: an inherited convention/form/medium ("a something") a tradition lives by and an artist relies on (a genre, the sonata). The **medium of film *is* a set of automatisms**, not the camera/celluloid. Film was *given* its automatism; modernism = having to invent new ones [ch. 14].
- **Element** — Cavell's near-synonym for an automatism in the "cinematic circle" formula: a significance-bearing possibility/necessity of the physical medium, knowable only through the acts of direction and criticism [pp.xiii–xiv].
- **The cinematic circle** — the reciprocity by which an *element* of the medium and its *significance* are mutually constituted; "what constitutes an 'element' … is not knowable prior to these discoveries of direction and of criticism" [pp.xiii–xiv].
- **Presentness** — what painting *wants*: "not exactly a conviction of the world's presence to us, but of our presence to it" [p.22]; in modernist painting, a work's "absolute hereness and nowness" that the viewer must answer with his own presentness [ch. 15].
- **The world's presentness / a world past** — photography "maintains the presentness of the world by accepting our absence from it"; a world present to me to which I am not present "is a world past" [p.23].
- **The screen / to screen** — the screen as *barrier*: it screens *me* from the world it holds (makes me invisible) and screens that *world's existence* from me [p.24].
- **"Existence is not a predicate"** — the projected world differs from reality "in no feature," only in not existing *now*; the difference is modal/tensed, not qualitative [p.24].
- **The frame (phenomenological frame; frame as mold/loom, not border)** — the camera **crops** an "indefinitely larger field" ("the rest of the world is cut out"); the screen has no border but is a *container*; the altering frame is "the image of perfect attention" [pp.24–25]. In "More," the frame *masks/segments* the world rather than bounding a composition.
- **Subjectivity / metaphysical isolation** — the modern condition (intensifying "since the Reformation") of the self cut off from the world; photography *escapes* it by automatism, painting must *acknowledge* it [pp.21–22].
- **Romanticism** — "the natural struggle between the representation and the acknowledgment of our subjectivity" [p.22].
- **Mechanical absence (of the audience)** — film *mechanically* defeats our presence to reality; my invisibility/fixity "no longer needs accounting for," unlike a stage convention [ch. 4].
- **Actor vs. performer / star / type** — a stage *actor* works himself *into* a role; a screen *performer* takes the role *onto himself*, and "the rest is nonexistent." The photographed human is a **star** (the figure created by a set of films) and a **type** — "not the kind [of character] an author creates, but the kind that certain real people are" [chs. 4–5].
- **Individuality** — film's way of "creating individuals": a projected separateness "that takes precedence over the social role"; the recurring self-identity of a star across films [ch. 5]. (Color and interchangeable performers later *negate* it — ch. 13.)
- **Cycle / genre / medium** — the identity chain "a cycle is a genre; and a genre is a medium" [p.36]; the *media of movies* are the historical cycles/types through which film's possibilities were given significance.
- **Myth** — an inherited wish/idea an art satisfies; Bazin's **"myth of total cinema"** = "the world re-created in its own image," which film satisfies *magically* and *automatically*, "from below the world," by letting us **view the world unseen** [pp.39–40]. Film's classical myths: the Woman, the Military Man (the "myth of community"), the Dandy [chs. 7–9].
- **Viewing unseen / invisibility** — the viewer's ontological status; the magical mode by which film reproduces the world; later, "exactly the price of skepticism" [pp.40, 101, ≈196].
