- **Medium vs. media** — *the medium* of film is its material basis as such ("a succession of automatic world projections"); its **media** are the discovered, accepted arrangements (genres/types/individualities) through which the medium is realized — achieved, not given [ch. 11].
- **Material basis (of film) = "a succession of automatic world projections"** — unpacked word by word: *succession* (motion, the running thread, cutting), *automatic* (the mechanical, hand-free fact), *world* (photography's subject is the world itself), *projection* (the phenomenology of viewing) [ch. 11].
- **The world as mortal / absolute age / nostalgia** — film depends on real, aging, dying people; the **absolute age** of a movie is the literal mortality it records (vs. film's unsettled *artistic* age); Hollywood, having lost conviction, can declare recurrence "only … by nostalgia" [ch. 12].
- **Color (as a declared condition of the medium)** — when color becomes constitutive (not packaging/make-believe/private-fantasy symbolism), it *neutralizes* the black-and-white "drama" by which personality and event were comprehended, **de-psychologizing/un-theatricalizing** the world and projecting **futurity** ("What if one day…" replacing "Once upon a time…") [ch. 13].
- **Fantasy** — not "a world apart from reality" but "precisely what reality can be confused with"; the medium through which "our conviction of the worth of reality is established" [ch. 13].
- **Acknowledgment** — Cavell's master modernist term (from Fried): not *reference* but an *act* of the self that admits the existence (and one's denial) of others; "the home of knowledge." A painting *acknowledges* the conditions of all painting; modernist film must *acknowledge* the camera, the medium, silence. Opposed to mere **self-reference** ("the exhibiting of self") [chs. 15–17].
- **Exhibition vs. screening** — modernist art stands "naked" before a viewer it must account for (exhibition); film *escapes* this because it is not exhibited but *screened/distributed/viewed* — each print an equal **manifestation**, not a hierarchy of original/copy [ch. 16].
- **The camera's implication / outsideness** — the camera is always "the one … left out of the picture: the one working now" [p.126]; its ontological position is *outside* its world (modeled on "I am outside my language"); what modernist film must acknowledge [ch. 17].
- **Assertion (in a technique); integrity vs. integration** — a device becomes a meaningful *assertion/acknowledgment* only through the **integrity** (conviction) of the whole work, never by being technically *integrated* — "integration can itself be a device"; "nothing is a 'possibility of a medium' unless its use gives it significance" [ch. 18].
- **Acknowledgment of silence; "a silent movie has never been made"** — silent film projected *a world of silence* (parallel to radio's *world of sound*), not a deficient sound film; film's deepest condition to acknowledge is silence [ch. 19].
- **Synchronization** — the binding of speech to speaker; redefined as the criterion of **poetry** (saying-time = meaning-time): film can deliver "just that creature … saying just that, just now" [ch. 19].
- **Mythological (vs. fictional)** — film's dramatic mode; its paradoxes (pastness, presentness, absence, non-existence of the screened world) are "mythological descriptions" of the *viewer's* state, not literal claims; film's content links to the post-Hegelian diagnosis of **estrangement / skepticism** ["More," ≈pp.209–215].
- **Film as a moving image of skepticism** — film *realizes* skepticism: the senses are "satisfied of reality while reality does not exist," because "viewing it is all it takes" ["More," ≈pp.188–189].
- **Modernist vs. avant-garde (1979 revision)** — the modernist *discovers/declares his medium* to keep continuity with the art's past and bear comparison to it; he penetrates *time* (the past), so is better imaged as an **arrière-garde** than an avant-garde ["More," ≈pp.215–219].
- **Reality's role: photographed / projected / screened / exhibited / viewed** — Cavell's series of verbs replacing "reality as such": reality is not *recorded* (film meets neither criterion of a recording) but *projected* and *viewed in our absence* ["More," ≈pp.166, 183].

---

## 6. Key verbatim quotes (high-value for citation / passage seeding)

**Method & the cinematic circle**
- "I feel that I have been composing a kind of metaphysical memoir — not the story of a period of my life but an account of the conditions it has satisfied." [p.xix]
- "in speaking of a moment or sequence from a film we, as we might put it, cannot quote the thing we are speaking of." [p.ix]
- "what constitutes an 'element' of the medium of film is not knowable prior to these discoveries of direction and of criticism. This reciprocity between element and significance I would like to call the cinematic circle." [pp.xiii–xiv]
- "Reading is not an alternative to seeing but … an effort to detail a way of seeing something more clearly." [p.xii]
- "the only instruments that could provide data for a theory of film are the procedures of criticism." [p.12]
- "the answer to the question 'What is the importance of art?' is grammatically related to, or is a way of answering, the question 'What is art?'" [pp.3–4]

**The ontology of the photographic image**
- "the basis of the medium of movies is photographic, and that a photograph is of reality or nature." [p.16]
- "our question becomes: What happens to reality when it is projected and screened?" [p.16]
- "A photograph does not present us with 'likenesses' of things; it presents us, we want to say, with the things themselves. But wanting to say that may well make us ontologically restless." [p.17]
- "we do not know what a photograph is; we do not know how to place it ontologically." [p.18]
- "we cannot say that a photograph reproduces a sight (or a look, or an appearance)." [p.20]
- "Photographs are not hand-made; they are manufactured. And what is manufactured is an image of the world." [p.21]
- "Photography overcame subjectivity in a way undreamed of by painting … by automatism, by removing the human agent from the task of reproduction." [p.23]

**Presentness, absence, the screen**
- "what painting wanted, in wanting connection with reality, was a sense of presentness — not exactly a conviction of the world's presence to us, but of our presence to it." [p.22]
- "To maintain conviction in our connection with reality, to maintain our presentness, painting accepts the recession of the world. Photography maintains the presentness of the world by accepting our absence from it." [p.23]
- "The reality in a photograph is present to me while I am not present to it; and a world I know, and see, but to which I am nevertheless not present … is a world past." [p.23]
- "A painting is a world; a photograph is of the world." [p.24]
- "A screen is a barrier. What does the silver screen screen? It screens me from the world it holds — that is, makes me invisible. And it screens that world from me — that is, screens its existence from me." [p.24]
- "That the projected world does not exist (now) is its only difference from reality. … Existence is not a predicate." [p.24]
- "The altering frame is the image of perfect attention." [p.25]

**Myths, type, the human on screen**
- "you do not really like the highest instances unless you also like typical ones. You don't even know what the highest are instances of unless you know the typical as well." [p.6]
- "a movie comes from other movies." [p.7]
- "if it is art, it is the one live traditional art, the one that can take its tradition for granted." [p.15]
- "a cycle is a genre; and a genre is a medium." [p.36]

**Automatism, modernism, skepticism, silence**
- "giving significance to and placing significance in specific possibilities and necessities (or call them elements; I sometimes still call them automatisms) of the physical medium of film are the fundamental acts of … the director … and the critic." [p.xiii]
- "Within the last decade film has been moving into the modernist environment inhabited for generations by the other major arts." [p.60]
- "One can feel that there is always a camera left out of the picture: the one working now." [p.126]
- "objects participate in the photographic presence of themselves; they participate in the re-creation of themselves on film; they are essential in the making of their appearances." [pp.xv–xvi]
- "Their presence refers to their absence, their location in another place." [p.xvi]
- "to accept film as an art will require a modification of the concept of art." [p.xvii]

> Page references marked "≈" elsewhere in this note (mainly inside "More of The World Viewed") are approximate to within a page or two and should be re-checked against the book before being printed in a published item.

---

## 7. Recurring examples — films, persons, works (what each is made to illustrate)

**Theorists / philosophers**
- **Panofsky & Bazin** — the realist premise ("physical reality as such"; the "myth of total cinema"; the photographic "mold"). Cavell amends both: *projections of* reality, *our absence*.
- **Michael Fried / Clement Greenberg** — modernism: medium, *acknowledgment*, flatness/the support, *presentness*; the lens through which Cavell reads film's modernist turn.
- **Baudelaire** (*The Painter of Modern Life*) — "modernity"/"presentness"; the iconography (the Woman, the Military Man, the Dandy, cosmetics, fashion) read as a "wish for photography."
- **Heidegger** — *world*, *Dasein*, "knowing as access," "willingness for anxiety"; the "radiance" vocabulary for *Days of Heaven*.
- **Wittgenstein** — "seeing-as"/physiognomy; *truthfulness* vs. truth; acknowledgment.
- **Rousseau** (*Letter to d'Alembert*), **Tolstoy** (*What Is Art?*), **Kant** ("existence is not a predicate"), **Kierkegaard / Nietzsche / Hegel** (estrangement, skepticism, eternal return).
- **Alexander Sesonske** — the critic answered throughout "More of The World Viewed."

**Directors / films Cavell reads closely**
- **Renoir — *Rules of the Game*** — the book's recurring touchstone (the Schumacher gun-as-camera memory; the final tableau; "the camera is the death of theater"; Renoir's "loving brutality"); also *Grand Illusion*.
- **Hitchcock — *Vertigo*** (the distorted stairwell: seeing *that* vs. *how*; "the end of Romance") and ***I Confess*** (the priest's refusal of an alibi; inaccuracy "at the highest level").
- **Dreyer — *Gertrud*** (film as *idiom*, not grammar; opacity of self-consciousness) and Dreyer's "cinematic stillness" (re-grounding Eisensteinian montage).
- **Godard** — depersonalization / de-psychologizing; the extended critique closing ch. 13.
- **Malick — *Days of Heaven*** (the 1979 Foreword's "prescription of a reading"; objects' participation in their photographic re-creation) and ***Badlands*** (the "expression of the inexpressive," in the Notes).
- **Capra — *It's a Wonderful Life*, *Mr. Smith Goes to Washington*** — sentimentality; the "myth of community"; the Pietà mis-memory.
- **Westerns — *The Man Who Shot Liberty Valance*, *Shane*; *The Maltese Falcon*** — the Dandy/Western hero; "passive potency"; "the traumatic births of law in the new world."
- **Stars as types** — Garbo, Dietrich, Mae West (the three "courtesan" individualities); Bogart (the Dandy); Astaire, Bette Davis, Fonda, Monroe, etc. (the ch. 1 "semblance of ecstasy" roll).
- **Eisenstein** — montage, re-grounded on the static frame; theory "partly answers to pedagogy."

---

## 8. Notes for future use (passage-design relevance — neutral observations, standalone)

*(Observations on what in this book is structurally useful for exam/passage design. Per project scope this note draws **no** cross-links to other authors; the following stay strictly inside Cavell.)*

- **Trap-rich distinctions** (high-discrimination because they are easy to collapse): *photograph "of" the world* vs. *painting "is" a world*; *presentness* (our presence to the world) vs. *the world's presentness* (the world's presence to us); *present at* vs. *present to* (the spectator and the screen figure); *actor* (into a role) vs. *performer/type* (onto himself); *medium* vs. *media*; *automatism* photographic vs. modernist; *self-reference* vs. *acknowledgment*; *integrity* vs. *integration* (ch. 18); *mythological* vs. *fictional* ("More"); *modernist* vs. *avant-garde*/*modernizer*; *recording* vs. *projection*.
- **The "existence is not a predicate" hinge** [p.24] is a clean, self-contained logical node: the screened world and reality differ *in no feature*, only in present existence — a modal/tensed difference. Good for an inference item about *what kind* of difference film's unreality is.
- **Two-sense terms** (a single word doing double duty — classic source of misreading items): **automatism** (mechanism / inherited convention) and **screen** (to display / to conceal). Cavell *intends* the ambiguity; an item can test whether a student tracks both senses.
- **Necessary-vs.-sufficient / conditional structure** worth exploiting: in ch. 18, technical *integration* is *not sufficient* for significance; only the work's *integrity* confers it. In "More," belief that film projects reality is defended as a matter of *emphasis*, with the claim that there is no neutral parity between "arrangement" and "reality."
- **Counterintuitive theses good for 〈보기〉 application:** "a silent movie has never been made" (silent film = a *world of silence*, not deficient sound film); cartoons are *not* movies (only "*animated* world projections"); color *de-psychologizes* and projects *futurity*; the camera is "the one working now," always outside its world; film is "a moving image of skepticism."
- **The method itself is a possible object of a passage:** the *cinematic circle* (element ↔ significance), *variable quotability*, and "reading as argument" form a compact, self-contained epistemology-of-criticism that can be excerpted without the film-ontology.
- **Structural caution for any excerptor.** Cavell's prose is deliberately non-linear and its key terms are *context-bound* (his own 1979 essay concedes the book is "sometimes incomprehensible"). Any passage built from it should isolate one move and supply the connective logic the original withholds — the per-chapter **[재구성]** notes above mark exactly the places where that connective logic had to be reconstructed.
- **Densest self-contained ontology** for a short single passage: **ch. 2 (pp.16–23)** + **ch. 3 (pp.23–25)** carry the whole "photograph/painting/screen/presentness/absence" core in ~9 pages. **Ch. 11** (medium vs. media) and **ch. 14** (automatism) are the cleanest statements of the modernist apparatus. The closing **act of criticism on *Rules of the Game*** ("More," pp.219–230) is the book's best single demonstration of method.
